### [1] [Taming Visual Neglect: A Variational Information Bottleneck Framework for Adaptive Attention in Multimodal In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23570)
*Kaito Tanaka,Yuji Nishimura,Keisuke Matsuda,Aya Nakayama*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper analyzes when and why visual context helps in multimodal in-context learning (ICL) for large vision-language models, using an information-theoretic framework to explain and improve their behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although large vision-language models can perform in-context learning, it is unclear under what conditions visual examples genuinely contribute beyond text, and why models sometimes ignore visual inputs altogether. Existing empirical findings show inconsistent use of visual context, revealing a need for a principled explanation and a method to exploit visual information more reliably.

Method: The authors propose VIB-ICL, an information-theoretic framework grounded in the Information Bottleneck principle. They define Cross-Modal Information Gain (CMIG) as the extra mutual information that visual context provides about the target on top of textual context. They derive generalization bounds connecting CMIG to the excess risk of multimodal vs. text-only ICL. Building on this, they formulate an Attention Reallocation Principle that prescribes how to adaptively adjust attention weights over visual vs. textual inputs based on redundancy. They then instantiate this theory as a practical VIB-ICL algorithm, which uses variational bounds to estimate CMIG and dynamically reallocates attention between modalities during ICL.

Result: Theoretically, they show that multimodal ICL has lower excess risk than text-only ICL when visual information is non-redundant, and that ignoring visual context is actually optimal under the Information Bottleneck when visual information is redundant. Practically, their VIB-ICL algorithm yields consistent empirical gains: up to 4.7% accuracy improvement and up to 35% reduction in the number of demonstrations needed across five benchmarks, aligning with the theoretical predictions.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that the behavior of vision-language models in multimodal in-context learning—both effective use and apparent neglect of visual context—can be systematically explained by the Information Bottleneck via the Cross-Modal Information Gain. When visual data is redundant, ignoring it is optimal; when it is informative, properly reallocated attention enables multimodal ICL to outperform text-only ICL. Their VIB-ICL algorithm operationalizes these insights and empirically validates the theory across multiple benchmarks.

Abstract: Large vision-language models exhibit strong in-context learning (ICL) capabilities, yet when and why visual context helps multimodal ICL remains poorly understood. Empirical studies show a puzzling dichotomy: models sometimes effectively leverage visual demonstrations, yet often neglect them entirely. We propose VIB-ICL, an information-theoretic framework that resolves this dichotomy through the Information Bottleneck principle. We introduce the Cross-Modal Information Gain (CMIG), which quantifies the additional mutual information that visual context provides about the target beyond textual context. We derive a generalization bound showing that multimodal ICL's excess risk over text-only ICL is governed by the CMIG, proving that multimodal ICL provably outperforms text-only ICL when visual information is non-redundant. We further prove that visual context neglect, often viewed as a failure mode, is the Information Bottleneck-optimal solution when visual information is redundant, yielding a closed-form Attention Reallocation Principle that prescribes how visual attention weights should be adaptively adjusted. We instantiate this principle in the VIB-ICL algorithm, which estimates CMIG via variational bounds and dynamically reallocates attention. Experiments on five benchmarks demonstrate consistent improvements of up to 4.7\% accuracy gains and 35\% reduction in required demonstrations, validating our theoretical predictions.

</details>


### [2] [From Triage to Discharge: A Survey of NLP Tasks, Methods, and Open Challenges in the Emergency Department](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23627)
*Dipankar Srirag,Aditya Joshi,Salil Kanhere,Padmanesan Narasimhan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Survey of NLP applications across emergency department triage, diagnosis, and disposition, highlighting methods, evaluations, trends, and open challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Emergency departments generate large amounts of multimodal textual data under time pressure, but existing surveys either look at clinical NLP broadly or at narrow task slices, leaving a gap for a focused, phase-based overview of NLP in ED workflows.

Method: Systematic review of 46 papers that apply NLP to emergency department workflows, organised by ED phase (triage, diagnosis, disposition) and task (e.g., triage classification, summarisation, diagnosis, report and discharge generation), with analysis of modelling paradigms, evaluation strategies, and benchmarks/shared tasks.

Result: The review finds a clear progression from bespoke neural models to pretrained language models, increasing exploration of interactive clinical NLP systems, and more emphasis on clinically meaningful evaluation metrics and datasets tailored to ED settings.

Conclusion: NLP for ED care is maturing but still faces challenges in generalisability across settings, robustness to noisy clinical inputs, and integration with real-world workflow constraints; addressing these gaps is crucial for future ED-focused NLP research and deployment.

Abstract: Emergency departments (EDs) operate under time pressure, generating multimodal data such as clinical conversations, triage notes, and discharge documents. Recent advances in natural language processing (NLP), particularly pretrained transformers and large language models, have created new opportunities to support language and time-intensive stages of emergency care. Yet existing surveys map clinical NLP across the broader hospital workflow or focus on specific tasks. This survey analyses 46 papers spanning the three phases of ED: triage, diagnosis, and disposition, covering tasks such as triage classification, clinical summarisation, automatic diagnosis, report generation, and discharge documentation. We examine modelling paradigms, evaluation practices, and emerging benchmarks and shared tasks. Across tasks, we identify common trends, including a shift from task-specific neural architectures to pretrained language models, growing interest in interactive clinical systems, and increasing attention to clinically grounded evaluation. Finally, we detail open challenges such as limited generalisability, noisy clinical inputs, and workflow constraints that inform future ED-NLP research.

</details>


### [3] [Contextual Embedding Evidence for Main--Light Verb Distinctions in Urdu](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23645)
*Farah Adeeba,Miriam Butt*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper uses contextual embeddings from several BERT models to test whether Urdu light verbs are both distinct from and lexically related to their corresponding main verbs, finding strong evidence for both systematic difference and continued relatedness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To empirically test Butt’s theoretical account of Urdu light verbs, which posits that light verbs contribute abstract event-structural meaning yet remain lexically tied to their corresponding main (lexical) verbs. The authors aim to see whether modern contextual language models encode these dual properties in their representations of verb uses in natural text.

Method: They collect 1,126 naturally occurring Urdu sentences containing seven target verbs and obtain contextualized embeddings from three models: UrduBERT, DunbaaBERT, and multilingual BERT. They compare representations of main versus light verb uses for each lemma and model, analyzing centroid distances and separability. They also run a seven-way classification task on light-verb tokens only, masking the target verb to test whether verb identity is still recoverable from context, including a stricter setting where preceding forms are disjoint between train and test to check generalization beyond memorized local patterns.

Result: Across all 21 verb–model combinations, main and light uses form significantly separated clusters in embedding space, indicating systematic representational differences. However, centroids for same-lemma main vs. light uses are consistently closer to each other than to those of different lemmas, indicating maintained lexical relatedness. In the masked prediction task on light uses, UrduBERT achieves high performance (0.866 accuracy, 0.852 macro-F1), and still maintains 0.782 accuracy when trained and evaluated on disjoint preceding forms, showing that the model can generalize beyond specific recurring verb combinations.

Conclusion: Contextual embeddings from BERT-type models encode Urdu light verbs as systematically distinct from their corresponding main verb uses while preserving lemma- and verb-specific similarity. This computational pattern aligns with Butt’s theoretical analysis: light verbs carry schematic event-structure contributions but remain lexically connected to their main-verb counterparts.

Abstract: Urdu light verbs contribute schematic event-structural meaning while remaining lexically related to corresponding main verbs. This study tests representational predictions derived from Butt's analysis using contextual embeddings from UrduBERT, DunbaaBERT, and multilingual BERT across 1,126 naturally occurring sentences containing seven Urdu verbs. Main and light uses show significant representational separation in all 21 verb--model comparisons. At the same time, same-lemma main and light centroids are consistently closer than mismatched main--light lemma pairs, supporting continued lexical relatedness. In a seven-way prediction task restricted to light uses, verb identity remains recoverable after the target is masked, with UrduBERT achieving 0.866 accuracy and 0.852 macro-F1. UrduBERT also retains 0.782 accuracy under a preceding-form-disjoint evaluation, indicating generalization beyond repeated local verb combinations. These findings provide computational evidence consistent with Butt's account that Urdu light verbs differ systematically from their main uses while retaining lemma-specific and verb-specific representational structure.

</details>


### [4] [The Limits of Automatic Evaluation of Creativity in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23705)
*Alessandro Tutone,Giorgio Franceschelli,Mirco Musolesi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that current automatic methods, including LLM-as-a-judge, poorly reflect human judgments of creativity in short stories, often overvaluing AI style and missing key creative qualities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs become able to produce seemingly creative text, researchers and practitioners need scalable ways to evaluate that creativity. Existing automatic metrics and LLM-as-a-judge approaches are widely used but their validity for inherently subjective, multidimensional constructs like creativity is unclear. The paper aims to test whether these automatic methods actually align with human perceptions of creativity in narrative writing.

Method: The authors use short stories (both human- and AI-generated) from the WritingPrompts dataset and collect human ratings along 11 dimensions of creativity (e.g., originality, unpredictability, style-related aspects). They then compute various automatic objective metrics and obtain evaluations from LLM-based judges on the same stories. They compare human ratings with both standard automatic metrics and LLM-as-a-judge outputs using correlation analyses and qualitative comparisons of preferences between human- and AI-written stories.

Result: Automatic metrics, including widely used objective scores, show near-zero correlation with human creativity judgments for both human- and AI-written stories. LLM-based judges are systematically biased toward AI-generated stories: they favor polished stylistic features over traits that humans value as creative, such as unpredictability and certain human-like nuances. Overall, there is substantial misalignment between automatic evaluations and human assessments across the board.

Conclusion: Current automatic approaches to evaluating creativity in text, including LLM-as-a-judge and standard objective metrics, do not reliably reflect human perceptions of creativity and miss important dimensions such as unpredictability. Creativity’s multidimensional, subjective nature resists reduction to simple computational scores, implying that future work must develop richer, more human-grounded evaluation frameworks and be cautious about using automatic metrics as proxies for creative quality.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly capable of generating text that challenges human performance in domains requiring creativity, yet evaluating creativity in LLM-generated content remains a significant challenge. Here, we investigate whether current automatic evaluation methods can reliably capture human judgments of creativity. We collect human evaluations of human- and AI-generated short stories from the WritingPrompts dataset across 11 dimensions of creativity, and compare these judgments with automated objective metrics and LLM-as-a-Judge evaluations. Our experiments reveal substantial misalignment between automatic evaluations and human assessments. In particular, LLM-based judges exhibit a systematic preference for AI-generated stories, consistently favoring their stylistic characteristics over the unpredictability and other qualities of human-authored texts. Furthermore, correlation analyses show that widely used automatic metrics exhibit near-zero alignment with human judgments across both human- and AI-generated stories, suggesting that they fail to capture important dimensions of creativity. These findings highlight fundamental limitations in current approaches to the automatic evaluation of creative text and underscore the difficulty of reducing the multidimensional and subjective nature of creativity to computational metrics.

</details>


### [5] [ADE: Agentic Data Evolution Framework for Human-Centered Objectives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23719)
*Yang Yu,Yilin Jiang,Zexuan Fei,Yiming Luo,Xingkai Song,Kaiyi Huang,Aimin Zhou,Xin Lin,Fei Tan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Agentic Data Evolution (ADE), a framework that iteratively improves synthetic supervision data for aligning large language models using a closed-loop process that robustly filters and upgrades data snapshots, leading to consistent performance gains across benchmarks and models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Aligning large language models to nuanced, human-centered, context-dependent objectives is hard because targets are not directly executable and are weakly verifiable. Synthetic data can broaden coverage, but when verification is weak, the main challenge moves from generating data to reliably selecting high-quality examples. Noisy or low-quality supervision signals then destabilize iterative refinement, causing performance regressions that are hard to detect. The paper aims to create a principled, stable way to evolve synthetic supervision data despite noisy verification and weak signals.

Method: The authors propose Agentic Data Evolution (ADE), a data-centric framework that represents supervision as evolving data snapshots. ADE applies a closed-loop Observation-Variation-Selection (OVS) procedure to these snapshots. In each round, the system observes current data quality, generates variations (new or modified synthetic supervision), and then selects improved examples using a steady-state admission mechanism that acts as a quality ratchet. This mechanism conservatively admits only data that meets or exceeds prior quality, thereby enforcing monotonic or stable improvements across rounds. The framework is evaluated via intrinsic metrics (e.g., win rates on an internal DEV300 set) and extrinsic post-training benchmarks across tasks and model scales.

Result: On the DEV300 dataset, ADE increases the intrinsic win rate from 50% to 75.81% and the extrinsic win rate from 55.20% to 68.86%. These improvements are consistent across diverse benchmarks, models of different scales, and post-training methods, and they generalize beyond the original weakly verifiable educational tasks. Blind expert evaluation shows a 66.11% preference for answers produced from evolved data snapshots, indicating that the method yields outputs that humans consider better more often than not. Overall, ADE delivers robust performance gains under weak supervision conditions.

Conclusion: Agentic Data Evolution provides a robust, data-centric approach for improving synthetic supervision under weakly verifiable, context-dependent objectives. By structuring supervision as evolving snapshots optimized through an Observation-Variation-Selection loop with a conservative steady-state admission rule, ADE achieves stable, cross-round quality improvements and avoids regressions. Empirical results and expert evaluations demonstrate consistent gains in both intrinsic and extrinsic metrics, across methods, model sizes, and tasks, suggesting ADE is a broadly applicable strategy for scalable alignment and post-training of large language models.

Abstract: Aligning large language models to human-centered objectives is difficult when targets are non-executable and context-dependent, limiting reliable verification and scalable supervision. Although synthetic data expands coverage, weak verification shifts the bottleneck from generation to selection. Noisy signals destabilize iterative refinement and can cause silent regressions. We propose Agentic Data Evolution (ADE), a data-centric framework that organizes synthetic supervision as evolving data snapshots. ADE improves data snapshots through a closed-loop Observation-Variation-Selection (OVS) procedure, where a steady-state admission mechanism acts as a quality ratchet that conservatively gates updates for sustained cross-round improvement. We validate these improvements through complementary intrinsic trend tracking and extrinsic post-training evaluation. On DEV300, ADE raises the intrinsic win rate from 50% to 75.81% and the extrinsic win rate from 55.20% to 68.86%, consistent performance gains across diverse benchmarks. Blind expert evaluation further confirms this, with a 66.11% preference for evolved answers. These gains extend across post-training methods, model scales, and tasks beyond the target weakly verifiable educational objectives. Resources are available at https://github.com/ZeroLoss-Lab/Agentic-Data-Evolution.

</details>


### [6] [What Reaches Expert Review? Representation, Structural Screening, and Candidate-Form Dependence in AI-Assisted Item Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23766)
*Christopher Brooks*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that the computational evaluation layer between AI-generated test items and expert review strongly shapes which items and evidence psychometricians ever see, even when global statistics look stable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Psychometricians increasingly use AI to generate large pools of test items, then filter them via computational models before human review. This middle layer is often treated as a neutral, technical step, but it may systematically distort construct representation and the items that reach experts. The authors want to understand how choices in semantic representation, structural modeling, and selection policies affect which items survive and what construct evidence they appear to carry.

Method: The authors run two linked in-silico (simulation-based) studies on 32,000 AI-selected Big Five personality items. They hold fixed the underlying source populations of items, then vary: (1) semantic representations (e.g., different embedding configurations), (2) structural evaluation processes, and (3) selection policies for constructing candidate item forms. They track how items move from semantic space through structural evaluation to final candidate forms and examine global vs. local differences in construct evidence, item survival, and coverage of intended content cells.

Result: Despite broad similarity in global semantic geometry across configurations, local differences are substantial. The same item wording can receive different construct evidence depending on representation, different subsets of items survive filtering, and some intended attributes can vanish from the pool even as overall alignment with community structures (e.g., Big Five) appears to improve. At the final review boundary, different eligibility policies always produce forms that appear complete in terms of content-cell coverage, but the actual wording differs. Under different embedding configurations, inclusive primary forms share a median of only 6 out of 40 items, demonstrating that small upstream representational choices cascade into major downstream differences in what psychometricians see.

Conclusion: The computational evaluator—comprising representation choice, structural modeling, and selection rules—is not a neutral or purely technical layer. It materially shapes measurement design by determining which items and kinds of evidence reach psychometric experts, and its apparent global stability can mask substantial local instability in construct coverage and item content. Therefore, this evaluator should itself be treated as an inspectable, revisable design object within the psychometric workflow, not as invisible infrastructure.

Abstract: Between AI-assisted item generation and expert review sits a computational evaluator whose decisions are usually treated as technical preliminaries. Yet representation, structural reduction, and selection policy determine which items and evidence psychometricians ever receive. Across two linked in-silico studies of 32,000 selected Big Five items, we followed fixed source populations from semantic representation through structural evaluation and candidate-form construction. Broad agreement in semantic geometry concealed consequential local differences: identical wording acquired different construct evidence, different items survived, and intended attributes could disappear even as community correspondence improved. These sensitivities also differed across generated source populations. At the final review boundary, both eligibility policies filled every content cell in every evaluable form, yet they presented different wording. Across embedding configurations, inclusive primary forms shared a median of only 6 of 40 items, reflecting the total downstream consequence of changing representation across structural evidence and ranking. The apparent stability of global summaries and complete forms therefore concealed instability in the content reaching psychometricians. The computational evaluator is not neutral infrastructure between generation and expertise; it is an inspectable and revisable part of measurement design.

</details>


### [7] [When Youth Enter The Chat: An Epistemic Shift in the Validation of LLM-Based Measures of Student Talk](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23780)
*Liliana Santos-Deonizio,James Malamut,Ramón Martínez,Dorottya Demszky*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper critiques current uses of LLMs to measure student discourse and shows, via a case study, that youth perspectives often conflict with LLM-based metrics, arguing that youth must be treated as epistemic authorities in designing and validating such measures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used to code and evaluate student talk (e.g., collaboration, equity of voice), but these methods rely on de-contextualized transcripts and adult expert validation, which may misrepresent or marginalize racially and linguistically diverse youth. The authors are motivated to make these measures more meaningful, just, and aligned with students’ own experiences.

Method: The authors conduct a case study in a multilingual 8th-grade math classroom. They apply multiple ethnographically-oriented methods—participant observation, interviews, focus groups, and member checks—with four focal students. They compare LLM-based measures and coding schemes of student talk with the students’ own interpretations and critiques of these analyses.

Result: The study finds systematic misalignments between LLM-based measures (and their underlying coding schemes) and students’ own interpretations of their math talk. Students actively contest both the LLM classifications and how their talk is being operationalized, revealing that adult-constructed metrics miss important contextual and experiential nuances in youth discourse.

Conclusion: LLM-based measures of student talk, validated only by adult experts and standard metrics (e.g., F1 on held-out sets), are insufficient and potentially inequitable, particularly for marginalized youth. To produce trustworthy and just measures, researchers must re-contextualize classroom conversations and share epistemic authority with youth, involving them directly in defining constructs, validating measures, and interpreting findings.

Abstract: LLMs are being used increasingly to measure aspects of student discourse (e.g. talk moves, collaboration, equity of voice) at scale. Typically, LLM-based measures of student talk use transcriptions of classroom conversations that only include verbal contributions, which de-contextualize student language. Common practices for validating these measures include comparing outputs against expert annotations by adults, using held out evaluation sets and F1 scores. We argue that these approaches are insufficient to ensure that such measures are meaningful and equitable for teaching and learning, particularly for racially and linguistically marginalized youth. In order to center the youth whose talk is being analyzed, re-contextualizing these classroom conversations and engaging youth in the research process is necessary. Sharing epistemic authority with youth, ultimately, centers their point of view and adds crucial nuance to the analysis of their talk that adult experts, researchers, and LLMs cannot provide. In a case study of multilingual youth in one 8th-grade math classroom, we address the epistemic exclusion of youth by employing multiple ethnographically-oriented methods to re-contextualize student conversations and center youth as epistemic authorities in conversation with researchers and LLMs. We conducted participant observations, interviews, focus groups, and member checks with four focal students. Findings reveal that there were misalignments between students' interpretations of their own math talk experiences and the LLM-based measures of their talk. Students contested both the LLM classifications and the coding scheme used to measure their talk, highlighting the need for youth to be involved in the epistemic process of producing knowledge about their experiences.

</details>


### [8] [Inter-dimension Dependence for Multi-Dimensional Evaluation of Open-Ended Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23783)
*Haoyuan Li,Snigdha Chaturvedi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how reliable large language models (LLMs) are when used as multi-dimensional judges of open-ended text, identifies a problem of inter-dimension dependence, proposes a metric (CorrGap) to measure it, and introduces a mitigation method (DimCheck) that improves independence and performance while being cost-efficient with smaller models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are commonly used as automatic judges to rate open-ended text along multiple dimensions (e.g., relevance, coherence, style). For these evaluations to be meaningful, each dimension should be assessed independently, but LLMs may unintentionally let non-target dimensions influence their judgment. There was no clear way to quantify or systematically reduce this inter-dimension dependence, motivating the authors to propose both a metric and a mitigation framework.

Method: 1) Define CorrGap, a metric capturing inter-dimension dependence by comparing correlations between LLM-predicted scores and human ground-truth scores across groups of texts that differ in non-target dimensions.
2) Empirically apply CorrGap to multiple LLMs and open-ended text evaluation tasks to show how pervasively non-target dimensions affect judgments.
3) Propose DimCheck, a step-wise procedure that generates chain-of-thought (CoT) explanations from the LLM judge and then iteratively removes evidence in these CoTs that is unrelated to the target dimension, re-scoring after each refinement.
4) Explore using smaller, trained LLMs within DimCheck to approximate the behavior of larger LLM judges at lower inference cost.

Result: CorrGap reveals that many existing LLM judges display strong inter-dimension dependence when evaluating open-ended text, meaning their scores for one dimension are contaminated by others. DimCheck significantly reduces this dependence, as indicated by improved CorrGap measurements, and yields better alignment with ground-truth scores compared to strong baseline evaluation methods. Experiments across three different LLMs and four different tasks show consistent gains. Additionally, smaller LLMs, when integrated into DimCheck, can closely match the effectiveness of larger models while substantially lowering inference costs.

Conclusion: Inter-dimension dependence is a widespread and previously under-quantified issue in LLM-as-a-judge settings. CorrGap provides a practical metric for diagnosing this problem, and DimCheck offers an effective, general strategy to mitigate it by refining chain-of-thought evidence to focus on target dimensions. This not only improves the reliability and fairness of multi-dimensional LLM-based evaluations but can also be done cost-effectively by leveraging smaller models to approximate more powerful judges.

Abstract: LLM-as-a-judge methods are widely used for evaluating the quality of generated open-ended text. Such evaluations are generally multi-dimensional, since the error patterns in texts can be different for different dimensions. Therefore, reliable LLM judges should evaluate each target dimension independently. To quantify the extent to which LLM judges depend on non-target dimensions when evaluating a target dimension, i.e., inter-dimension dependence, we propose CorrGap. To measure this, CorrGap uses the difference in correlations between LLM-predicted scores and ground truth scores across different groups of texts. Using CorrGap, we show that inter-dimension dependence is pervasive across LLM judges in open-ended text evaluation tasks. To mitigate inter-dimension dependence, we propose DimCheck, a method that iteratively removes unrelated evidence from COTs generated by LLM judges in a step-wise way. We show that DimCheck mitigates inter-dimension dependence and outperforms strong baselines across three LLMs and four tasks. We also show that smaller trained LLMs can approximate larger LLMs in DimCheck, with much lower inference costs.

</details>


### [9] [Giga-Embeddings: Mixture-of-Experts Encoders for High-Throughput Text Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23806)
*Egor Kolodin,Egor Krasnoperov,Evgeniy Kosarev,Fyodor Minkin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces Giga-Embeddings, a family of efficient, high-quality text embedding models (10B MoE, 3B dense, 480M distilled) with strong MTEB performance and high throughput, all released as checkpoints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a need for text embedding models that offer both high retrieval quality and efficient serving across languages and domains, while fitting different compute and memory budgets. Existing strong models are often large and slow, or smaller but with weaker retrieval performance, particularly for languages like Russian and for code; this work aims to bridge that gap.

Method: Design a family of embedding encoders: (1) a large sparse 10B-parameter Mixture-of-Experts model with about 1.8B active parameters per token for high accuracy; (2) a dense 3B encoder as a balanced option; and (3) a compact 480M encoder obtained via distillation. Evaluate all models on multiple MTEB benchmark suites (English, Russian, multilingual, code) and on a vLLM-based throughput benchmark with 1024-token inputs. For distillation, use a dimension-agnostic training objective that matches the similarity distributions between teacher and student embeddings, rather than relying on fixed-dimensional alignment.

Result: The 10B MoE model achieves the best aggregate performance across all four MTEB suites in the family while being highly efficient, reaching 114.5k tokens/s on vLLM with 1024-token inputs—25% faster than the 3B dense model and 1.56–2.65× faster than examined external baselines. The distilled 480M model, trained with the dimension-agnostic similarity-matching objective, achieves a score of 70.98 on Russian MTEB, outperforming the FRIDA model despite using 42% fewer parameters. All three models are released as checkpoints.

Conclusion: Giga-Embeddings successfully combine strong retrieval performance with efficient inference across a spectrum of model sizes. The sparse 10B MoE encoder offers state-of-the-art-like quality with high throughput, the 3B dense model provides a middle ground, and the 480M distilled model demonstrates that substantial parameter reductions are possible without major quality loss, even surpassing some larger baselines. The dimension-agnostic distillation objective is effective for producing compact yet strong embedding models, and the released checkpoints enable practical deployment and further research.

Abstract: We introduce Giga-Embeddings, a family of text embedding models designed to combine strong retrieval quality with efficient serving. Its largest member is a sparse 10B-parameter Mixture-of-Experts encoder with approximately 1.8B active parameters per token. Across English, Russian, multilingual, and code MTEB benchmarks, this model achieves the strongest aggregate performance within the family on all four evaluated suites. In our vLLM benchmark with 1024-token inputs, it processes 114.5k tokens per second, providing 25 percent higher throughput than the dense 3B model and 1.56-2.65x the throughput of the evaluated external systems. The family also includes a dense 3B encoder and a distilled 480M encoder for tighter compute and memory budgets. We train the compact model using a dimension-agnostic objective that aligns teacher and student similarity distributions. The resulting 480M model scores 70.98 on Russian MTEB, surpassing FRIDA while using 42 percent fewer parameters. We release all three model checkpoints.

</details>


### [10] [From Preferences to Principles: Rubric-Based Alignment for Grounded Knowledge Answers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23812)
*Aman Saini,Priyanshu Kumar,Eric Peng,Kai Yuan,Harsh Girase,Wanming Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose a rubric-based, multi-dimensional reward framework for training open-domain QA models, which yields better answer quality than scalar or flat rubric rewards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scalar rewards for open-domain QA are too coarse because good answers must jointly satisfy several distinct quality criteria (like factual grounding, coherence, and following instructions), which are hard to capture with a single, holistic score. This limits the effectiveness of post-training via standard reward models.

Method: They design a rubric-based reward framework that, for each query, retrieves supporting evidence and uses it to generate a query-specific rubric. The rubric is decomposed into multiple explicit quality dimensions (e.g., composition, grounding, instruction-following), and these per-dimension criteria are used to provide fine-grained reward signals during post-training instead of a single scalar score. They compare this to instruction-tuned baselines and to simpler, “flat” rubric variants that lack either evidence conditioning or dimension decomposition.

Result: Across three evaluation axes (composition, grounding, and instruction-following), their method outperforms an instruction-tuned baseline by 6.5% on average and flat rubric variants by 4%, with consistent improvements on all tested datasets. Evidence-conditioned rubrics particularly boost factual support, and multi-dimensional decomposition improves coherence, organization, and adherence to the query requirements.

Conclusion: Grounded, query-specific, and multi-dimensional rubrics provide more effective and fine-grained reward supervision for complex open-domain QA than traditional scalar rewards or simpler rubric designs, leading to systematically better answer quality along several key dimensions.

Abstract: Designing effective reward signals for open-domain question answering is challenging because high-quality responses must simultaneously satisfy multiple aspects of answer quality that are difficult to capture with a holistic scalar objective. We introduce a rubric-based reward framework that generates query-specific rubrics grounded in retrieved evidence and decomposed into multiple quality dimensions, providing fine-grained supervision during post-training. Averaged across three evaluation axes (composition, grounding, and instruction-following), our approach improves over the instruction-tuned baseline by 6.5% and over flat rubric variants by 4%, with consistent gains across all evaluation datasets. Conditioning rubrics on retrieved evidence improves factual support, while decomposing rubrics into quality-specific dimensions further improves coherence, organization, and adherence to query requirements. Our results show that grounded, multi-dimensional rubrics provide more effective reward supervision for complex open-domain question answering.

</details>


### [11] [Beyond Static and Linear: What Attention Constraints Best Fit Human Reading Times?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23818)
*Lanni Bu,Xiulin Yang,Christian Clark,Alex Warstadt,Ethan Gotlieb Wilcox*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper tests different ways of adding human-like memory limits to Transformer attention and shows that content-sensitive constraints best align models with human reading behavior, though they trade off with grammatical competence under some training schemes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard Transformers have effectively unlimited access to past context via attention, unlike humans’ limited working memory. Cognitive scientists use these models to study human language processing, but this architectural mismatch may limit psychological plausibility. Prior studies tried single constraint types in isolation, without a unified comparison. The authors aim to systematically test which attention-based memory constraints make Transformers better models of human behavior while maintaining language ability.

Method: The authors modify Transformers’ attention mechanisms to implement various memory constraints (e.g., distance-based and content-sensitive attention limits). They train models of different sizes on different corpora under either static constraints (fixed strength throughout training) or dynamic memory curricula (constraints that change over training). They then evaluate each variant both on psychometric predictive power (how well the model predicts human reading times) and on grammatical competence (syntactic/linguistic benchmarks).

Result: Content-sensitive memory constraints—where the effective memory depends on the informational content or properties of intervening tokens—consistently produce the best alignment with human reading times, and they outperform simple distance-based constraints. Under dynamic memory curricula, models can show improved psychometric fit but exhibit a dissociation, where gains in human-alignment do not necessarily translate into equal or better grammatical competence.

Conclusion: Incorporating human-like, content-sensitive memory limits into Transformer attention improves their ability to model human reading behavior. However, optimizing Transformers for cognitive plausibility via dynamic memory constraints can degrade or at least decouple standard linguistic performance, implying that no single Transformer configuration can simultaneously optimize for both cognitive modeling and grammatical competence. Thus, Transformers should not be treated as a universal, one-size-fits-all model of human language cognition.

Abstract: Transformer-based language models are widely used as models of human language processing, yet their attention mechanisms allow lossless access to the full preceding context, unlike the limited memory systems of humans. We hypothesize that installing memory constraints into transformers' attention mechanisms can improve their fit to human behavioral data. While previous work has explored individual constraints in isolation, we conduct a systematic comparison of multiple attention-based memory mechanisms across different model sizes and training corpora, evaluating both psychometric predictive power for human reading times and grammatical competence. We additionally compare static constraints, in which the constraint strength is fixed throughout training, to dynamic memory curricula. We find that constraints that are sensitive to the content of intervening tokens consistently achieve the highest alignment with human reading times, outperforming distance-based constraints. We observe a dissociation between psychometric fit and grammatical competence under dynamic memory curricula, suggesting that Transformers cannot serve as a one-size-fits-all cognitive model.

</details>


### [12] [Mitigating Exploration Bias in RL for Multi-Instruction Following](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23830)
*Mian Zhang,Yueqin Yin,Kaiyu He,Peilin Wu,Xinlu Zhang,Mingyuan Zhou,Zhiyu Zoey Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper identifies and mitigates exploration bias in RL-based instruction-following training for LLMs, particularly when prompts contain multiple instructions of varying difficulty.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RL training for LLMs improves instruction following but tends to favor easy instructions in multi-instruction prompts. This leads to under-training on hard instructions, limiting overall model capability. The authors aim to understand and correct this imbalance so that RL better improves performance on challenging instructions.

Method: They first define two quantitative metrics to measure exploration bias in multi-instruction settings. Then they propose a two-stage training framework: (1) Behavioral Bootstrapping, a lightweight rejection-sampling-based fine-tuning step before RL that filters and uses trajectories where hard instructions are successfully satisfied to ensure the model can at least sometimes complete them; (2) Scarcity-Aware Rewards, a modified RL reward function that weights instructions according to their empirical scarcity (difficulty) rather than using a uniform cumulative reward, thereby encouraging exploration and learning on hard, rarely fulfilled instructions.

Result: Experiments demonstrate that the new metrics correlate strongly with final instruction-following performance, validating them as good proxies for exploration quality. The proposed two-stage framework substantially reduces exploration bias and enables better use of RL, with their best models achieving significant gains over standard RL baselines on three verifiable multi-instruction following benchmarks.

Conclusion: Exploration bias towards easy instructions is a key failure mode of current RL training for instruction-following LLMs, especially in multi-instruction prompts. By first bootstrapping behavior on hard instructions and then re-weighting rewards according to scarcity, RL can more effectively train models to handle difficult instructions, leading to consistently better benchmark performance. The approach and metrics provide a general recipe for more balanced and effective RL fine-tuning of LLMs.

Abstract: RL has emerged as a powerful paradigm for enhancing the instruction following capabilities of LLMs. While existing training recipes achieve substantial gains, we find that they suffer from exploration bias towards easy instructions when the training data has multiple instructions in a prompt. This bias is caused by two main reasons: 1) the policy model's initial ability to satisfy hard instructions is too low to trigger successful exploration during RL training, so the optimization is biased towards easy instructions; and 2) canonical RL training recipes typically employ a cumulative reward (the number of instructions fulfilled), treating all instructions equally, which biases the policy model towards fulfilling easy instructions to obtain the same amount of reward. To address these issues, we first propose two metrics to measure the exploration bias in instruction following and then introduce a two-stage framework to alleviate it: 1) Behavioral Bootstrapping, a lightweight rejection sampling fine-tuning stage before RL to activate hard instructions; and 2) Scarcity-Aware Rewards, a new RL reward function that assigns rewards to instructions based on their empirical scarcity. Experiments show that the proposed metrics are highly correlated with model performance, and our methods unleash the potential of RL training: our best models outperform the baselines by a significant margin across three verifiable instruction following benchmarks. We release codes at https://github.com/mianzhang/MulIF.

</details>


### [13] [Does Episodic Memory Help Close the Lexical Frequency Gap in Sensitivity to Syntactic Contrasts? A Test Using Retrieval-Augmented Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23851)
*Jing Liu,Najoung Kim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether adding an episodic memory–like retrieval mechanism to language models reduces their oversensitivity to word frequency in grammaticality judgments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard linguistic theory treats grammatical knowledge as largely independent of how frequent particular words are, but neural language models tend to perform much better on grammatical tests involving frequent words than rare ones. This lexical frequency sensitivity challenges their plausibility as models of human grammatical competence. The authors are motivated to explore whether a cognitive theory—Complementary Learning Systems, in which hippocampal episodic memory supports rapid learning of specific experiences—can explain and potentially fix this weakness in language models.

Method: The authors implement an episodic memory mechanism using k-nearest-neighbor (kNN) retrieval-augmented language models. These models augment a standard parametric LM with a datastore of explicit token-level instances and retrieve nearest neighbors at inference time. They construct syntactic contrast tests whose items are stratified by lexical frequency, and then compare performance of vanilla LMs versus kNN-augmented LMs across multiple syntactic phenomena and training regimes (child-realistic vs. large-scale data). They also manipulate what information is used for retrieval—structural vs. semantic—to assess which aspects matter most for improving grammatical robustness on low-frequency items.

Result: Retrieval-augmented (kNN) language models show reduced performance gaps between high- and low-frequency lexical items on syntactic contrast tests compared to vanilla LMs. The improvement is consistent across different syntactic phenomena and different pretraining conditions. Analyses indicate that using structural information in retrieval is crucial for these gains, while relying mainly on semantic similarity contributes little. Despite these benefits, the lexical frequency gap is only narrowed, not eliminated.

Conclusion: Episodic memory–like retrieval mechanisms can partially compensate for the weak parametric representations that cause neural language models to be overly sensitive to lexical frequency in grammaticality judgments. The findings align with Complementary Learning Systems theory by showing that instance-based memory helps with rare patterns, but current implementations are insufficient to fully match the frequency robustness observed in human grammar. The authors suggest future work on better weighting of retrieved instances, richer structural representations and retrieval strategies, and more flexible storage/retrieval configurations to build more human-like episodic memory in language models.

Abstract: Grammatical knowledge and how it is empirically tested are typically considered robust to the frequency of the lexical items in the expressions. However, neural network-based models of grammaticality exhibit high sensitivity to lexical frequency. We draw upon Complementary Learning Systems theory to test the hypothesis that robustness to lexical frequency can arise via a hippocampal episodic memory mechanism, which enables rapid encoding and retrieval of specific experiences and allows learners to leverage them when processing rare patterns. We use retrieval-augmented language models as an instantiation of such an episodic memory mechanism (specifically, $k$-nearest-neighbor language models that augment parametric models with explicit instance storage), and test whether this augmentation helps close the lexical frequency gap that vanilla language models exhibit in syntactic contrast tests. Using syntactic contrasts with frequency-stratified test items, we find that retrieval augmentation narrows the performance gap between high- and low-frequency items, consistent with episodic memory compensating for weak parametric representations. This benefit is consistent across different syntactic phenomena and across models pretrained on child-realistic and large-scale data. Additionally, we show that structural information is critical for effective retrieval, whereas semantic similarity alone provides little benefit. While these are promising proof-of-concept results supporting our hypothesis, the frequency gap is narrowed rather than fully closed. Based on our analyses, we propose preferential reweighting of retrieved instances, better representations and retrieval strategies for structural information, and flexible configurations of storage and retrieval as promising future directions for improving the implementation of episodic memory in language models.

</details>


### [14] [Names Can Hurt: Spotting Slopsquatting Risks Caused by Package Name Hallucinations in Local Coding LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23897)
*Akash Raj,Sargam Sahu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Paper proposes a two-layer detector and mitigation pipeline to prevent LLM hallucinated Python package names from turning into supply chain attacks (‘slopsquatting’).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Code-generating LLMs sometimes invent non-existent Python packages. If an attacker pre-registers such names on PyPI, hallucinations can become real supply chain compromises. The field lacks systematic defenses tailored to this new “slopsquatting” threat.

Method: Design a two-layer detection system embedded in a LangGraph-based generation pipeline. Layer 1 deterministically checks PyPI for package existence. Layer 2 is a Random Forest classifier using ten features from the package name and its PyPI metadata to flag suspicious or low-quality packages, including lookalikes. An import-name reconciler maps import statements to PyPI distributions (e.g., cv2 → opencv-python) so checks are accurate. The pipeline retries generations with increasing temperature and, if necessary, falls back to a stronger model, with careful measurement of recovery at each stage. A user study evaluates perceived usefulness.

Result: On 300 curated prompts, the pipeline yields hallucination-free code in 76% of runs. The primary model hits its retry limit in 28.7% of cases, but intra-model retries fix about 25% of those failures, and cross-model fallback recovers another 16.5% of the remaining ones. About half of the flagged hallucinations are already-registered low-quality lookalike PyPI packages caught only by the classifier. Hallucination rate grows roughly linearly with prompt adversariality, from near 0–10% on normal prompts to 40–73% on adversarial “slopsquat bait” prompts. The weaker primary model, even without this system, refuses 6/10 direct bait prompts, indicating instruction tuning gives some inherent protection. When primary and fallback models are from the same family, ~84% of primary failures repeat on the fallback, suggesting cross-family pairing is more effective. A user study with 24 participants reports mean satisfaction 4.4/5 and 21/24 say they would adopt the system.

Conclusion: A targeted two-layer detection and mitigation pipeline can substantially reduce LLM-induced package hallucinations and the associated slopsquatting attack surface, especially when combined with cross-family fallback models. Deterministic existence checks alone are insufficient; classifiers that reason about package quality and name similarity catch many real but risky packages. Prompt adversariality and model-family redundancy are key factors, and early instruction tuning already provides some baseline defense. Users perceive the system as effective and are inclined to adopt it.

Abstract: When a code generating language model fabricates a Python package name, an adversary who has pre-registered that name on PyPI can convert that hallucination into a supply chain compromise. This event has been termed as 'slopsquatting'. We propose a two layer detector to counter this issue. The first layer performs a deterministic PyPI existence check. The second is a Random Forest classifier trained on ten features derived from the package name and its PyPI metadata. An import name reconciler bridges the two, resolving cases such as 'import cv2' versus 'pip install opencv-python' without a security bypass. The detector is embedded in a LangGraph state machine that retries at escalating temperatures and, on repeated failure, routes to a stronger fallback model. Across 300 curated prompts, the pipeline produces hallucination free code on 76% of runs. The primary exhausts its retry budget on 28.7%; intra model retries recover roughly a quarter of those, and cross model fallback recovers a further 16.5% of the remainder. Four findings have been observed. First, half of the flagged hallucinations are packages already registered on PyPI, as low quality lookalikes of well known projects, caught by the classifier rather than the deterministic layer (e.g., pil, faiss, tabula, haystack). Second, hallucination rate scales almost linearly with prompt adversariality, from 0 to 10% on routine coding to 40 to 73% on slopsquat baits. Third, the weaker primary refused 6 of 10 direct baits unaided, suggesting recent instruction tuning provides a baseline defense. Fourth, when primary and fallback share a model family, approximately 84% of primary failures recur on the fallback, motivating cross family pairing. A user study (n = 24) reports mean satisfaction 4.4 out of 5 and 21 of 24 stated adoption intent.

</details>


### [15] [Investigating Knowledge Transfer Across Interactive Dialogue Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23969)
*Filippo Momentè,Mir Nafis Sharear Shopnil,Andrea de Varda,Pavel Merinov,Raffaella Bernardi,Oswald Lanz,Alessandro Suglia,Alessandro Torcinovich*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how training large language models on specific dialogue games helps them transfer skills to other games, revealing which games are best sources and targets for transfer and how well similarity measures over finetuned models predict this transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dialogue games require complex cognitive and coordination skills, all expressed through language, so training on one game might develop capabilities reusable in others. However, it is unclear which dialogue games transfer best to which others, and whether model similarity measures can predict such transfer. The paper aims to systematically analyze cross-task transfer in this setting to better understand and exploit shared structure among dialogue games.

Method: The authors finetune large language models on individual dialogue games from the clembench benchmark. They then perform two analyses: (i) they construct a task-transferability graph following Zamir et al. (2018), formulating the selection of source-target task relations as a binary integer optimization problem based on empirical performance when transferring between games; and (ii) they compute task vectors à la Ilharco et al. (2022) for each game—essentially parameter-difference directions in model space—and use similarity between these vectors to study relationships among games and to test whether such similarity predicts transferability.

Result: From the optimization-based transferability graph, they find that certain games gain more from transfer learning than from direct finetuning, and that games in the visuospatial family, such as exploration games, serve as particularly strong sources of transferable knowledge. From the task-vector similarity analysis, they observe that these similarity measures reflect structural relations like roles within games but largely fail to predict which tasks transfer well to which others, indicating a disconnect between simple geometric similarity in parameter space and actual transfer performance.

Conclusion: The study concludes that (1) cross-game transfer in dialogue games is highly asymmetric, with some families—especially visuospatial and exploration games—being better transfer sources; and (2) naive similarity-based metrics built from task vectors, while capturing some relational structure, are inadequate proxies for transferability. This suggests that more sophisticated metrics or representations are needed to reliably forecast and harness transfer across language-based dialogue games.

Abstract: Dialogue games represent a challenging setting where complex cognitive skills are required to accomplish tasks while coordinating with other players. Considering that language represents an interface for both understanding the game rules and executing actions, it is reasonable to assume that training on a specific language game will enhance specific capabilities that might be relevant for other tasks as well. Motivated by this rationale, in this paper, we investigate how knowledge transfers across different dialogue games. We study transferability by finetuning LLM models on games from the clembench suite (Chalamalasetti et al., 2023) and performing two analyses: i) we derive a task-transferability graph using a binary integer optimization program from Zamir et al. (2018), using task performance as the main metric; and ii) we compute task vectors (Ilharco et al., 2022) for each game to study similarities across finetuned models and their task transferability. In our first analysis, we find that some games benefit more from transfer than finetuning, and that the visuospatial family (e.g., exploration games) transfers best. With our task vector analysis instead, we find that similarity-based approaches capture game-role relationships but almost no transferability patterns, suggesting that more complex metrics are required.

</details>


### [16] [AgentSpec: Speculative Decoding for Batch Inference of LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24004)
*Xin Wang,Ziming Miao,Yi Zhu,Hui Shen,Zhongwei Wan,Fan Yang,Mi Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes AgentSpec, a speculative decoding algorithm tailored for LLM-based agents that maintains speedups even at large batch sizes by reducing token rejection and improving token budget utilization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing speculative decoding methods speed up LLM inference but their speedup degrades significantly at large batch sizes, which is problematic for real-world, high-throughput LLM agent applications. The authors aim to understand why this degradation happens and design a method that preserves efficiency without harming output quality.

Method: They first perform a systematic analysis of speculative decoding in LLM agents and identify two key causes of speedup loss: (1) high rejection rates of drafted tokens and (2) poor use of dynamically available token budgets. Based on this, they design AgentSpec with two main components: (a) structure-isolated drafting, which restricts speculation to semantically coherent segments of the agent workflow to avoid drafting along irrelevant paths and lower rejection rates; and (b) redundancy-aware budget allocation, which uses agent-level information to better distribute and exploit dynamic token budgets during inference. They implement AgentSpec in vLLM and test it across multiple workloads and model families.

Result: Across five workloads and four LLMs from different families, AgentSpec achieves higher speedups than existing speculative decoding algorithms in vLLM, especially under large batch settings, while maintaining generation quality.

Conclusion: AgentSpec effectively addresses key limitations of current speculative decoding methods for LLM agents by lowering speculative token rejection and better using dynamic token budgets, resulting in superior, more scalable inference efficiency in real-world agent workloads.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-based agent applications often incur high response time. Speculative decoding is a promising solution to improve the inference efficiency of LLM agents without impacting generation quality. However, state-of-the-art speculative decoding algorithms exhibit substantial speed degradation under large batch sizes, limiting their effectiveness to deploy in real-world agent applications. In this work, we first present a systematic analysis of speculative decoding for LLM agents and identify two dominant factors of speedup degradation: high rejection rate of speculative tokens, and under-utilization of dynamic token budgets.B ased on these observations, we propose AgentSpec, a speculative decoding algorithm that addresses the limitations of existing methods for LLM agents. AgentSpec incorporates structure-isolated drafting that constrains speculation to semantically coherent segments of the agent workflow, reducing the drafts of irrelevant semantic paths and achieving an extremely low rejection rate. Moreover, AgentSpec adopts redundancy-aware budget allocation that exploits agent-level information to better utilize the dynamically-free token budget during the agent inference. We implement and evaluate AgentSpec on five different workloads and four different models from four different LLM families in vLLM. Our results demonstrate the superiority of AgentSpec over state-of-the-arts.

</details>


### [17] [SAGE: From Direct Answering to Evidence-Grounded Inference for Chinese Ancient Document Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24011)
*Yuchuan Wu,Xuan Luo,Yinglian Zhu,Meng Fang,Xiangyang Xue,Bin Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SAGE is a multi-agent, evidence-grounded framework that improves Chinese ancient document understanding over standard single-pass LVLM answering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding Chinese ancient documents requires not only vision and language skills but also historical and contextual reasoning. Existing LVLMs treat the task as a one-shot answer generation problem, often producing overconfident answers that are weakly supported by the document content and external evidence. There is a need for a framework that explicitly grounds answers in retrieved evidence, can revise or abstain when evidence is insufficient, and better reflects the structured reasoning process experts use for such texts.

Method: The authors propose SAGE, a structured, multi-agent framework that turns ancient document understanding into an evidence-grounded inference problem. Instead of a single-pass generation, SAGE orchestrates multiple specialized agents: (1) a planner that decomposes the task into subtasks, (2) tool-using agents that actively acquire evidence (e.g., via retrieval or external tools), (3) a verifier that checks claims against the collected evidence, and (4) a controller that manages bounded replanning and evidence seeking within a constrained shared-state runtime. This architecture supports iterative evidence collection, verification, answer revision, and the option to abstain when grounding is inadequate. The framework is instantiated over several LVLM backbones, including Qwen3.5-9B, and evaluated on the AncientDoc benchmark.

Result: On the AncientDoc benchmark, SAGE consistently outperforms direct-answer LVLM baselines that use the same underlying backbones, demonstrating the benefit of the multi-agent, evidence-grounded design. Notably, SAGE built on the relatively small Qwen3.5-9B model surpasses substantially larger monolithic LVLMs on most evaluation metrics, indicating that structured reasoning and evidence integration can compensate for, and even exceed, the advantages of sheer model size.

Conclusion: The study concludes that reframing Chinese ancient document understanding as evidence-grounded inference, implemented via a coordinated multi-agent framework, yields more reliable and better-grounded answers than standard single-pass LVLM generation. The success of SAGE with a smaller backbone shows that careful system design and explicit evidence handling can be more impactful than further scaling model size, pointing toward future research on structured, tool-augmented reasoning for complex multimodal understanding tasks.

Abstract: Chinese ancient document understanding demands complex visual, linguistic, and historical reasoning. Current Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) typically rely on an opaque, single-pass generation paradigm, often producing overconfident and weakly grounded responses. To address this, we propose SAGE, an evidence-grounded multi-agent framework that reformulates Chinese ancient document understanding as evidence-grounded inference rather than direct answer generation. SAGE coordinates specialized agents for task-aware planning, tool-mediated evidence acquisition, claim-level verification, and bounded replanning under a constrained shared-state runtime. This design supports bounded evidence seeking, answer revision, and abstention when grounding is insufficient. Experiments on the AncientDoc benchmark show that SAGE consistently outperforms matched direct-answering baselines across three LVLM backbones. Remarkably, SAGE with Qwen3.5-9B surpasses much larger monolithic LVLMs on most evaluated metrics, highlighting the importance of structured, evidence-grounded inference beyond model scaling.

</details>


### [18] [Curved Inference II: Sleeper Agent Geometry - Extending Interpretability Beyond Probes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24037)
*Rob Manson*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a geometric, backdoor-free method to detect naturalistic deceptive reasoning in LLMs, showing that semantic patterns of deception are encoded in the curvature and ‘surface area’ of inference trajectories, even when linear probes fail.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work like Anthropic’s Sleeper Agents shows that artificially inserted backdoors can be detected with highly accurate linear probes, but this setup is unlike naturally arising deceptive alignment. It assumes (1) strong linear separability of deception-related representations and (2) simple trigger-response patterns, both of which may be artefacts of the backdoor construction. Sophisticated, real-world deception is likely subtler, distributed, and not linearly separable, making probe-based detection brittle. The authors want a more naturalistic, unsupervised way to detect deceptive reasoning that does not depend on artificial triggers, labels, or linear probes and that can scale to complex multi-turn contexts.

Method: They create multi-turn, naturalistic deceptive scenarios rather than using backdoor triggers. Instead of treating deception as a binary label tied to specific tokens, they let semantic complexity build over an extended context window to approximate realistic reasoning. They then apply their Curved Inference framework to the model’s internal residual stream representations, analysing geometric properties like curvature, salience, and a new metric, semantic surface area (A'). A' is designed to capture both the magnitude of representational change and the directional variation of meaning construction along the inference trajectory, in unnormalised residual space. The authors run multiple deceptive prompt strategies across two model families. They do not supervise the internal states with labels or train probes. Output sequences are weakly labelled post hoc via LLM consensus (semantic classification of whether the model is being deceptive or not), and they compare these semantic classes to the measured geometric quantities, testing for statistically significant differences and robustness under noise.

Result: Across five prompt strategies and two LLM families, geometric structure (especially semantic surface area A') is predictive of whether a sequence is classified as deceptive by external LLM consensus. They find statistically significant differences in surface area between different semantic conditions and prompt strategies, demonstrating that the geometry of inference trajectories encodes semantic information about deception. Crucially, even when naive classification suggests no significant effect (e.g., p = 0.555), improving measurement precision reveals underlying geometric signatures, turning some non-significant results into significant ones (e.g., p = 0.048). This indicates that deceptive reasoning leaves robust geometric traces that may not be linearly separable and can be obscured by label noise or imperfect classifiers, but still exist in the underlying representational dynamics.

Conclusion: The work argues that sophisticated deceptive reasoning naturally shapes the geometry of a model’s inference trajectory in ways that can be detected without artificial backdoors, explicit labels, or linear probes. Their Curved Inference framework and semantic surface area metric show that semantic patterns of deception are reflected in curvature-like properties of residual space, providing an unsupervised and more realistic detection path when linear methods fail. This suggests that the ‘shape of inference’ is a fundamental carrier of semantic information, including deception, and that robust safety tools should focus on geometric and dynamical signatures rather than relying solely on linear, backdoor-style probes.

Abstract: This paper extends Anthropic's Sleeper Agents research [1], which showed artificial backdoors persist through safety training & can be detected by linear probes with >99% accuracy [2]. However, probe-based detection relies on linear separability that may be an artefact of backdoor insertion rather than a property of naturally occurring deceptive alignment. Sophisticated deceptive behaviours emerging through natural training are unlikely to produce such convenient linear signals.
  We introduce a naturalistic methodology using multi-turn context windows that simulates realistic deceptive reasoning without artificial triggers or supervised backdoor insertion. Rather than binary trigger-response patterns, we examine how semantic complexity emerges through gradual context development.
  Building on our Curved Inference framework, we analyse curvature, salience, & introduce semantic surface area (A'), a new metric of representational work capturing both the magnitude & directional change of meaning construction in unnormalised residual space. Without backdoors, labels, or probes, we apply this framework to naturalistic deceptive prompts & classify model outputs via LLM consensus.
  Geometric structure reliably predicts semantic classification, with statistically significant differences in surface area across five prompt strategies & two model families. Critically, measurement precision can reveal geometric signatures hidden by classification noise - some strategies improve from non-significant (p = 0.555) to significant (p = 0.048). This validates that sophisticated reasoning creates intrinsic geometric patterns that persist even when detection appears to fail, suggesting the shape of inference itself encodes semantic patterns regardless of whether models have learned to suppress linear indicators of deception - a scalable, unsupervised path for detection when linear methods fail.

</details>


### [19] [When Less Is More: An Empirical Study of Minimal Responses in Counseling Dialogues and the Behavior of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24080)
*Zhiyang Qi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper analyzes short, minimal counselor responses in counseling dialogues, showing they are common and interactionally valuable in human data but are underproduced and undervalued by current LLMs and evaluation methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In real counseling, brief utterances like backchannels (“I see”, “mm-hm”) and short empathic statements play a crucial role in signaling attention, empathy, and encouraging clients to talk more. However, current counseling dialogue systems and automatic evaluation metrics tend to prioritize long, content-heavy responses, implicitly treating minimal responses as low quality or unhelpful. This mismatch may lead LLM-based counselors to behave unnaturally, over-explain, and disrupt client flow, and causes evaluation pipelines to systematically mis-score interactionally appropriate short turns. The paper aims to fill this gap by systematically characterizing minimal responses in counseling data and examining how well LLMs and evaluation methods handle them.

Method: The authors conduct a cross-lingual, multi-dataset analysis of counseling dialogues. They design a two-stage filtering pipeline to identify minimal responses: first, they screen utterances by length and surface content to find candidate short turns; second, they apply contextual verification with a large language model to judge whether each candidate is a genuine minimal response given the surrounding dialogue. They then compare the frequency and types of such responses between human-collected and LLM-generated counseling datasets. Additionally, they create manually curated test contexts where human counselors originally used minimal responses, and prompt various LLMs—including commercial general-purpose models and counseling-specialized models trained on synthetic data—to respond in those contexts. Finally, they examine how LLM-based automatic evaluators score minimal vs. longer responses in terms of response quality.

Result: The analysis reveals that minimal responses are frequent in human counseling dialogues but are significantly rarer in LLM-generated counseling data. When tested in contexts where humans produced minimal responses, strong commercial LLMs can generate appropriately short replies if explicitly directed to do so, but they are unreliable at autonomously deciding when minimal responses are interactionally suitable. Counseling-focused LLMs trained heavily on synthetic data perform especially poorly: they systematically favor longer, information-dense outputs over brief empathic or backchannel-like utterances. Moreover, LLM-based evaluators tend to assign lower quality scores to minimal responses, even in cases where such brief turns are contextually appropriate and align with human counselors’ behavior.

Conclusion: Minimal responses are a central but under-modeled component of counseling interaction. Current LLM-based counseling systems, especially those trained on synthetic data, underproduce such responses and have difficulty choosing them at the right moments, contributing to unnatural, verbosity-biased counselor behavior. Furthermore, widely used LLM-based evaluation frameworks encode a similar bias, penalizing concise yet interactionally effective turns. The paper concludes that future work on counseling dialog agents and their evaluation should explicitly account for minimal responses—through data collection, modeling strategies, and metric design—so that systems better reflect real counseling practices and support more natural, client-centered conversations.

Abstract: In psychological counseling, effective support is not always delivered through long, information-rich responses. Minimal responses, such as backchannel cues and concise empathic statements, help convey attentive listening, express empathy, and encourage clients to continue expressing themselves. However, existing counseling dialogue systems and evaluation frameworks often favor explicit, content-rich replies, overlooking the interactional value of brief counselor utterances. This paper presents a systematic cross-lingual analysis of minimal responses across multiple counseling dialogue datasets. We develop a two-stage filtering method based on utterance length and content, followed by contextual verification using a large language model (LLM). Our analysis shows that minimal responses are common in human-collected datasets but substantially underrepresented in LLM-generated ones. We further evaluate current LLMs in manually curated dialogue contexts where human counselors used minimal responses. The results show that strong commercial LLMs are capable of generating minimal responses when explicitly instructed, but still struggle to determine when such responses are appropriate. Counseling-specific models trained on synthetic data perform particularly poorly, tending instead to produce longer and more information-rich responses. Moreover, LLM-based response-quality evaluation may undervalue minimal responses, even when they are interactionally appropriate.

</details>


### [20] [PARTAB: Partition-Aware Reasoning with Structured Evidence for Scalable Table Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24082)
*Md Mahadi Hasan Nahid,Davood Rafiei*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents PARTAB, a framework that improves LLM table reasoning by partitioning tables into semantically coherent, query-relevant regions and hierarchically selecting evidence before answering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs struggle with large, complex tables because they must process much irrelevant context and cannot easily localize the small subset of rows and columns needed for reasoning. Existing methods either use the entire table or a single reduced view, which still hides important row–column relationships and scales poorly as tables grow. The paper aims to create a more scalable and precise way for LLMs to interact with large tables.

Method: The authors propose PARTAB (Partition-Aware Reasoning over Tables), which builds a structured evidence interface between an LLM and a table. PARTAB first identifies query-relevant regions of the table and organizes them as semantically coherent, row-linked partitions.
