It then performs hierarchical selection: (1) selecting relevant column groups and (2) selecting row-level partitions, effectively narrowing down the table to key regions. Finally, it composes the selected evidence into a compact representation that is fed to the LLM for answer generation across tasks such as QA, fact verification, and numerical reasoning.

Result: On multiple table reasoning benchmarks, including WikiTableQuestions and TabFact, PARTAB outperforms full-table prompting and recent table reasoning baselines, providing strong gains particularly on QA and fact verification, while remaining competitive on numerical reasoning tasks. Empirical analyses show that its semantic partitioning and targeted evidence selection significantly improve evidence localization and reduce the context size needed for reasoning, with especially large benefits on complex tables.

Conclusion: Structured, partition-aware evidence construction enables more scalable and accurate table reasoning with LLMs. By organizing tables into semantically meaningful, query-focused regions and hierarchically selecting relevant evidence, PARTAB improves performance over existing methods, particularly on large and complex tables, highlighting the importance of explicit evidence interfaces between LLMs and structured data.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown strong capabilities in table reasoning, but their effectiveness degrades as tables grow in size and complexity due to irrelevant context and difficulty localizing the evidence required for reasoning. Existing approaches typically reason over either the full table or a single reduced view, which can still obscure important row-column relationships. We introducePARTAB (Partition-Aware Reasoning overTables), a framework that constructs a structured evidence interface between the LLM and the table. PARTAB represents query-relevant evidence as semantically coherent, row-linked table regions and performs hierarchical selection over column groups and row-level partitions before composing the selected evidence for answer generation. We evaluate PARTAB on multiple table reasoning benchmarks, covering question answering, fact verification, and numerical reasoning. PARTAB consistently improves over full-table prompting and several recent table reasoning methods, achieving strong performance on WikiTableQuestions and TabFact while remaining competitive on numerical reasoning. Additional analyses show that semantic partitioning and targeted evidence selection improve evidence localization, substantially reduce the reasoning context, and provide larger benefits on complex tables. These results demonstrate the value of structured, partition aware evidence construction for scalable table reasoning.

</details>


### [21] [MC-CXR: A Multi-Context Chest X-ray Benchmark for Context-Induced Disruption in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24118)
*Junhyeok Lee,Songsoo Kim,Kyu Sung Choi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces MC-CXR, a benchmark to test whether vision-language models for chest X-rays maintain correct image-based decisions when faced with misleading textual or visual context, revealing that models are highly vulnerable, especially to misleading text.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of clinical vision-language models focus on accuracy given only the image or assume context is reliable. In real workflows, X-rays are interpreted alongside prior reports, notes, or images that can be wrong or misleading. There is no benchmark to measure if models can resist such misleading context and preserve correct image-based judgments. The authors aim to fill this gap.

Method: They build the Multi-Context Chest X-ray (MC-CXR) benchmark: 240 chest X-ray cases expanded into 2,522 instances with controlled, paired perturbations of context. For each fixed image and target finding, they create matched reliable and misleading contexts across text and prior imaging, sometimes with visual overlays. They define three task families and two paired metrics: (1) switch-to-wrong rate – how often a model changes a previously correct image-only prediction to an incorrect one under misleading context; and (2) context-aligned error rate – among errors under misleading context, how often they match the misleading label. They then evaluate ten vision-language models (general-purpose, medical, open- and closed-source) using a standardized direct-answer protocol.

Result: All evaluated VLMs show substantial vulnerability to misleading context despite having reasonable image-only accuracy. Mean switch-to-wrong rates range from 45.6% to 78.1% when misleading context is textual and from 35.7% to 61.7% when it is visual. Among the switched (now incorrect) predictions, 74.6% align with the misleading text label but only 17.6% align with the misleading visual context, showing a 57.0 percentage point gap (95% CI 50.9–62.8). This demonstrates a strong asymmetry: models are far more influenced by misleading text than by misleading visual context.

Conclusion: MC-CXR exposes a major robustness issue in clinical vision-language models: they frequently abandon correct image-only decisions when exposed to plausible but misleading context, particularly text. Image-only accuracy is therefore an incomplete measure of reliability in realistic clinical settings where context is ubiquitous and sometimes wrong. The benchmark, released on PhysioNet, provides a standardized way to test and compare models’ resilience to context-induced disruption, highlighting the need for new methods and training strategies that better balance or disentangle image evidence from contextual information.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly used in clinical pipelines where a chest X-ray is interpreted alongside retrieved reports, preliminary notes, or prior imaging. Existing benchmarks measure whether models answer correctly in isolation, but not whether they preserve a correct image-only decision when plausible context conflicts with the image. We introduce Multi-Context Chest X-ray (MC-CXR), a benchmark of 240 cases expanded into 2,522 instances that isolates context-induced disruption through paired perturbation. Each case fixes the current image and target finding while presenting matched reliable and misleading context across text and prior CXR, with visual overlays where available. MC-CXR defines three task families and two paired metrics, the switch-to-wrong rate and the context-aligned error rate. We evaluate ten VLMs spanning open-source general, medical-domain, and closed-source systems. Image-only accuracy is necessary but insufficient. Mean switch rates range from 45.6-78.1% across misleading textual sources and 35.7-61.7% across misleading visual sources. Among switched predictions, 74.6% align with the misleading label for text versus 17.6% for visual context, a 57.0-point gap (95% CI 50.9-62.8). This text-visual asymmetry is observed under the standardized direct-answer protocol. The dataset is available on PhysioNet.

</details>


### [22] [TrustDABench: Benchmarking Reliability and Robustness of LLMs for Structured Data Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24145)
*Boshen Shi,Yize Liu,Chen Zhao,Ce Chi,Zhendong Wang,Xing Wang,Junlan Feng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces TrustDABench, a benchmark to test how reliably and robustly LLMs perform evidence-grounded reasoning over structured data like tables and CSVs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs often give confident but untrustworthy analyses of structured data because they do not reliably adhere to the underlying evidence. The authors want to move beyond surface-level correctness and measure whether models can (1) recognize when a valid evidence path from question to data is missing or ambiguous, and (2) maintain correct reasoning when the same evidence is represented in different table formats. They see a gap in benchmarks that explicitly test evidence-path fidelity, reliability, and robustness for structured-data analysis.

Method: They propose an evidence-path perspective for data analysis with LLMs and define two key properties: reliability (refusing or qualifying answers when evidence is insufficient or conflicting) and robustness (producing stable conclusions when evidence is restructured but semantically unchanged). From this view, they design 19 perturbation operators that systematically modify tables and question–table relationships, such as changing boundaries of observations or cross-table relations. Using an Agentic-LLM-based generation framework, they apply these operators to create perturbed versions of base instances. Human annotators then verify the instances, yielding TrustDABench with 2,340 perturbed examples. They evaluate eight representative LLMs on the benchmark, measuring metrics such as Mean Reliability Score (MRS) and Attack Success Rate (ASR).

Result: Across eight LLMs, performance on TrustDABench is far from ideal. The best reliability score is only 24.21% average MRS, obtained by GPT-5.5, meaning models often fail to properly abstain or seek clarification when evidence is missing or conflicting. The best robustness score, from Claude-Sonnet-5, still shows a 9.10% average ASR, indicating that models remain vulnerable to perturbations that should not change the correct conclusion. The analysis reveals systematic failure modes: models rarely flag conflicting evidence, frequently proceed along plausible yet unsupported analytic paths, and are particularly fragile to perturbations that alter observation boundaries or relationships across tables.

Conclusion: TrustDABench exposes significant weaknesses in current LLMs’ ability to do trustworthy structured-data analysis. Even strong models cannot reliably detect missing or conflicting evidence and are not robust to benign representation changes in tables. The authors conclude that future work must focus on better mechanisms for recognizing evidence boundaries and enabling reasoning that is invariant to representational changes in structured data. They advocate using TrustDABench to guide and evaluate such improvements in LLM-based data analysis systems.

Abstract: LLMs are increasingly used to analyze spreadsheets, CSV files, and other structured data, but producing a correct-looking answer is not the same as producing a trustworthy analysis. A trustworthy result should be supported by a valid path from the user question to the relevant data evidence. This requirement creates two diagnostic questions: whether an LLM can refuse to answer or ask for clarification when such a path does not exist, and whether it can preserve the correct analysis when the same evidence is expressed in different table forms. We introduce TrustDABench, a benchmark that operationalizes these questions as reliability and robustness. Starting from the evidence-path view, we derive 19 perturbation operators and instantiate them through an Agentic-LLM-based generation framework. TrustDABench contains 2,340 human-verified perturbed instances, and we evaluate eight representative LLMs. The results show substantial headroom: the best reliability result is only 24.21% average MRS, achieved by GPT-5.5, while the best robustness result still has 9.10% average ASR, achieved by Claude-Sonnet-5. The failures are systematic: models rarely detect conflicting evidence, often continue along executable but unsupported analysis paths, and remain sensitive to perturbations that change observation boundaries or cross-table relations. These findings suggest that stronger evidence-boundary recognition and representation-invariant reasoning are still needed for reliable structured-data analysis.

</details>


### [23] [FireRedAudio: A General-Purpose Audio Language Model with Decoupled Continuous Representations for Understanding and Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24168)
*Junjie Li,Xuelong Geng,Kun Xie,Feiyu Shen,Yichen Wu,Ziqi Dai,Yichen Han,Yan Jia,Kai Huang,Junjie Chen,Yixuan Li,Manzhen Wei,Fenglong Xie,Lei Xie,Xu Tang,Yao Hu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: FireRedAudio is a 9B-parameter unified audio language model that separates continuous representations for understanding and for generation, enabling strong performance across ASR, audio understanding, TTS, and speech editing within a single autoregressive LLM.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing audio models struggle to jointly handle audio understanding (which benefits from compact, long-context representations) and high-fidelity speech generation (which needs reconstructible, detailed acoustic features). The authors aim to build a single, moderate-scale, general-purpose audio LLM that unifies multilingual ASR, audio understanding, TTS, and speech editing, overcoming the representational conflict between analysis and generation.

Method: They design FireRedAudio, a general-purpose audio language model centered on a shared 9B-parameter autoregressive LLM. The model uses two distinct continuous input pathways: (1) an Audio Encoder for recognition and analysis tasks, optimized for compact, long-context features; and (2) a RedAE-based pathway for speech inputs used in generation, optimized for reconstructible acoustic latents. The LLM can directly produce text (for ASR/understanding) or generate conditioning signals for a flow-matching diffusion transformer (DiT) that outputs continuous acoustic latents. Training is done progressively with multitask objectives to support ASR, long-form audio understanding (up to one hour), zero-shot TTS, instruction-driven TTS, and both semantic and acoustic speech editing while maintaining structured long-form organization and timestamps.

Result: FireRedAudio reaches competitive or state-of-the-art results on audio understanding and multilingual ASR benchmarks. It provides strong content accuracy and speaker preservation in zero-shot TTS, achieves leading performance in instruction-following for Instruct TTS, and substantially outperforms Ming-UniAudio-Edit on both semantic and acoustic speech editing. It can handle long recordings with second-level timestamp accuracy and enable a broad range of tasks from a single 9B-parameter model.

Conclusion: Decoupling the continuous input representations for audio understanding and for continuous-latent speech generation within a single 9B-parameter autoregressive LLM successfully unifies these capabilities in FireRedAudio. The approach enables competitive or leading performance across ASR, long-form audio understanding, TTS (including zero-shot and instruction-following), and speech editing, demonstrating that a moderately sized unified audio-language model with separate pathways for analysis and generation is practical and effective. The authors release code to encourage further research and application.

Abstract: A unified audio model must recognize and understand linguistic, paralinguistic, and environmental information while supporting speech synthesis and editing. A key challenge is representation: understanding favors compact features suited to long-context modeling, whereas speech generation requires reconstructible features that preserve fine-grained acoustic detail. We introduce FireRedAudio, a general-purpose audio language model with a shared 9B-parameter LLM. To the best of our knowledge, it is the first publicly disclosed unified audio-language model to provide separate continuous input representations for understanding and generation within a single trainable autoregressive LLM. Audio to be recognized or analyzed is processed by a dedicated Audio Encoder, while speech inputs for generation use a RedAE-based pathway. The LLM directly generates text or conditions a flow-matching DiT to produce continuous acoustic latents. Through progressive multitask training, FireRedAudio supports ASR and audio understanding, with the latter extending to recordings of up to one hour, as well as zero-shot TTS, Instruct TTS, and semantic and acoustic speech editing. Its structured organization of long-form audio achieves second-level timestamp accuracy. Across comprehensive evaluations, FireRedAudio achieves competitive or leading performance in audio understanding and multilingual ASR, strong content accuracy and speaker preservation in zero-shot TTS, leading instruction following in Instruct TTS, and substantial improvements over Ming-UniAudio-Edit in both semantic and acoustic speech editing. These results demonstrate the viability of decoupled continuous input representations for unifying audio understanding and continuous-latent speech generation in a model of moderate scale. Our code is available at https://github.com/FireRedTeam/FireRedAudio.

</details>


### [24] [MemUse: Moving Memory Evaluation from Direct QA to Natural Integration in Long-Term Human-AI Conversation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24189)
*Ryuichi Sumida,Koji Inoue,Tatsuya Kawahara*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that standard memory evaluations for conversational LLMs (Direct QA about past dialogue facts) do not predict real user satisfaction, and introduces a new benchmark, MemUse, focused on how well models naturally integrate past context into conversation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing memory benchmarks for conversational LLMs mostly test whether the model can explicitly recall a fact when directly asked, but it is unclear whether this skill actually matters for real users or predicts satisfaction in practical deployments. The authors want to understand what kind of memory capability users really value and how to measure it properly.

Method: They ran a 4‑month real-world deployment with 40 users and 1,872 sessions under seven different memory conditions, then compared Direct QA memory benchmark scores across those conditions with user satisfaction metrics. Observing a mismatch, they created MemUse: a benchmark built from real “memory moments” where users implicitly cue past information. Human annotators judged whether a good response should reference prior facts and evaluated model outputs for natural integration of that context. They compare Direct QA performance and MemUse scores using the same model and contexts.

Result: Across the seven memory conditions, Direct QA accuracy on existing benchmarks ranged widely (19.7%–70.1%), but user satisfaction remained flat, indicating no relationship. For the same system and contexts, Direct QA accuracy was 78.8%, yet the model actually referenced only 7.9% of the relevant facts in natural conversation, revealing a 71‑point gap between elicited recall and real conversational use. Within MemUse moments, higher natural integration scores correlate with higher user satisfaction, whereas Direct QA scores do not.

Conclusion: Direct QA benchmarks capture elicited retrieval ability but fail to reflect the memory behavior that matters in real interactions: naturally recognizing when past information is relevant and integrating it smoothly into responses. The authors argue that memory evaluation for conversational LLMs should focus on this Natural Integration capability rather than simple recall, and they provide MemUse and the associated deployment corpus as a resource for developing and testing such systems.

Abstract: Memory systems for conversational LLMs are conventionally evaluated by direct, fact-seeking questions about prior dialogue (Direct QA): can the model recall fact X from a prior conversation? We tested whether higher Direct QA accuracy correlates with higher user satisfaction in a 4-month deployment (40 users, 1,872 sessions, 7 memory conditions). Existing-benchmark Direct QA varies from 19.7% to 70.1% across the 7 conditions, but satisfaction does not change. We hypothesize that existing benchmarks and user satisfaction are tracking different capabilities: benchmarks measure elicited retrieval (recall when asked), while conversation requires natural integration (detecting relevance and naturally weaving prior context into a response). To examine this, we introduce MemUse, a set of real user-cued memory moments drawn from the deployment, scored by an integration-aware judgment of the natural conversational response. Holding the model and context fixed, the same system that scores 78.8% on Direct QA references only 7.9% of those facts in conversation -- a 71-point gap. Within these moments, Natural Integration is associated with satisfaction, whereas Direct QA is not. We release the deployment corpus and MemUse together with all judgments and scoring prompts at https://github.com/ryuichi-sumida/memuse.

</details>


### [25] ['Ghaib in Translation' aka Unseen Harm: Measuring Cross-Script Safety Inconsistency with 'Missed-in-Urdu' Scores in LLM Hate Speech Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24191)
*Fawzia Zehra,Kara-Isitt,Sonal Khosla,Stephen Swift*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates how reliably major LLMs perform safety/content moderation for Urdu (in multiple script varieties) and finds significant instability and missed harmful content, especially in smaller open models, highlighting a major gap in multilingual safety coverage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Urdu is a major world language but is almost entirely missing from LLM safety benchmarks and prior WOAH/ALW research. This raises concerns that safety systems may be less reliable for Urdu users, particularly across different scripts (Nastaliq Urdu, Roman Urdu, code-switched Urdu-English). The authors aim to quantify whether this lack of focus leads to measurable weaknesses in content moderation for Urdu compared with English.

Method: The authors evaluate five large language models (GPT-4o, Claude Sonnet 4.5, Gemini 2.5 Flash, Qwen-2.5, and Llama-3.1) on six datasets covering Nastaliq Urdu, Roman Urdu, English, and Urdu-English code-switching. For the five Urdu-script datasets, they compare each model’s safety labels on the original Urdu text versus on its English translation, measuring label instability rates and, specifically, a 'Missed-in-Urdu' rate—cases where English translation is flagged harmful but the original Urdu is not. They also query the ACL Anthology API to enumerate all 205 papers from nine ALW/WOAH editions, checking for dedicated Urdu work.

Result: Label instability between original Urdu script and English-translation classification is substantial, ranging from 15.9% to 31.6% across the five models, with smaller open-weight models performing worse. 'Missed-in-Urdu' rates lie between 2.4% and 9.9% (median 4.3%), indicating that some harmful content in Urdu passes moderation that would be caught in English. The literature survey finds zero dedicated Urdu papers across nine years and 205 WOAH/ALW papers, confirming a research gap.

Conclusion: Current LLMs do not provide uniform safety guarantees for Urdu, especially across its different script varieties. Safety performance is notably less reliable for Urdu than for English, and smaller open-weight models exhibit higher instability and missed-harm rates than frontier closed models. Combined with the total absence of Urdu-focused safety papers in WOAH/ALW, this suggests a systematic and under-recognized gap in multilingual LLM safety evaluation that needs targeted benchmarks and model improvements for Urdu.

Abstract: Urdu, the world's tenth most spoken language with 246 million speakers, remains almost entirely absent from mainstream LLM safety evaluation and nine years of WOAH proceedings. To investigate whether this absence has measurable consequences for content moderation reliability, five large language models, GPT-4o, Claude Sonnet 4.5, Gemini 2.5 Flash, Qwen-2.5, and Llama-3.1, were tested across six datasets spanning Nastaliq Urdu, Roman Urdu, English, and code-switched Urdu-English. Across the five Urdu-script datasets, label instability between original-script and English-translation classification ranged from 15.9% (Gemini 2.5 Flash) to 31.6% (Qwen-2.5), with a 'Missed-in-Urdu' rate, content flagged as harmful in English translation but passed as normal in the original script, ranging from 2.4% to 9.9% (median 4.3%). A complete enumeration of all 205 papers across nine ALW/WOAH editions via the ACL Anthology API confirms zero dedicated Urdu papers across the entire period. Results indicate that current LLMs provide uneven safety assurance across Urdu's script varieties, with smaller open-weight models showing substantially higher instability and missed-harm rates than frontier closed models.

</details>


### [26] [Measuring Digital Labour Market Transitions with a Digital Semantic Score: An AI-Based Methodology Applied to the Dutch Labour Market](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24222)
*Sadegh Shahmohammadi,Xavier Pinho,Mairi Bowdler,Suhendan Adiguzel-van Zoelen,Joost van Genabeek*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces an AI-based method to track how digitalisation spreads across the Dutch labour market using millions of job profiles and semantic embeddings instead of simple keyword counts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Digital transformation is changing job titles, skills, and career paths, but existing labour market intelligence is often too coarse, keyword-based, and unable to capture nuanced, emerging digital concepts across occupations and transitions. Policymakers and practitioners need detailed, scalable tools to understand where digitalisation is happening, how it affects skills, and where mismatches and shortages arise.

Method: The authors collect millions of Dutch job profiles and apply an AI pipeline: (1) map unstructured job information (titles, descriptions, skills) to standardised ESCO occupations using embedding-based similarity search plus large language model (LLM) classification; (2) construct a Digital Semantic Score by embedding job titles and skills and computing their cosine similarity to transparent digital and non-digital anchor groups; (3) use these scores to quantify digital intensity of occupations, career transitions, new job-title vocabulary, and skill profiles.

Result: The Digital Semantic Score successfully differentiates digital and non-digital language in jobs and skills, capturing broader digital meanings beyond explicit keywords. The analysis shows that digitalisation is highly uneven: digital language is concentrated in managerial, professional, and ICT roles, but is growing in hybrid business, marketing, and automation-related jobs. Career transition patterns indicate that moves into digital work follow specific pathways rather than occurring randomly, and skill-level analysis reveals multiple dimensions of digital capability, from technical to hybrid and business-systems skills.

Conclusion: Integrating large-scale profile data, AI-based occupation classification, and semantic scoring provides a powerful, scalable framework for monitoring digital change in the labour market. This approach improves labour market analytics compared to keyword methods, allows tracking of emerging skills and roles, and can support reskilling strategies and policy design to mitigate skills mismatches and labour shortages in the Dutch context, with potential transferability to other labour markets.

Abstract: The digital transformation of the Dutch labour market is reshaping occupational language, career pathways, and job-related skills. Addressing these changes requires granular labour market intelligence. This paper develops an AI-based methodology to analyse digitalisation using data covering millions of Dutch job profiles. The methodology combines embedding-based similarity search and large language model classification to map unstructured job information to harmonised ESCO occupations. We also introduce a Digital Semantic Score that measures how strongly job titles and skills are associated with digital concepts relative to a non-digital reference. Using embeddings and cosine similarity to transparent digital and non-digital anchor groups, this indicator moves beyond keyword-based approaches by capturing broader digital meanings in occupational language and worker skill profiles. It enables analysis across occupations, career transitions, emerging job-title vocabulary, and skill digitality. The findings reveal that digitalisation is unevenly distributed across the labour market. Digital job-title language is most prominent among managerial, professional and ICT-related occupations, but is increasingly visible in hybrid business, marketing and automation-related roles. Career-transition analyses show that movement toward digital work is pathway-dependent, while skill analyses highlight the multidimensional nature of digital capability, encompassing technical, hybrid and business-systems skills. By combining profile data, AI-supported occupational classification and semantic scoring, this study advances AI-driven labour market analytics and provides a scalable framework for monitoring digital labour market change. The methodology helps identify emerging skill needs, support reskilling strategies, and inform policies addressing skills mismatches and labour shortages in the Netherlands.

</details>


### [27] [RecurSE: Bounded Recursive Self-Evaluation for LLM Rubric Judges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24231)
*Kaiyuan Liu,Ziyuan Zhuang,Rongxiang Weng,Jieping Ye*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose a fully self-supervised RL framework, RecurSE, where an LLM judge improves itself using only its own evaluations, with safeguards to prevent reward hacking and a metric (PAV) to know when to early-stop.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-as-judge is crucial for evaluating open-ended text and guiding alignment, but improving the judge usually needs costly human labels, reward models, or stronger teacher models. The authors want a way for the judge to improve itself without relying on external gold supervision, while ensuring this recursive self-improvement remains stable and does not diverge or overfit.

Method: They introduce Recursive Self-Evaluation (RecurSE), a closed-loop, bounded recursive self-improvement setting. A trainable judge model scores candidate responses via rule-based rubrics (Pass 1). A synchronized policy-copy checker model then audits the judge’s chain-of-thought using separate meta-rubrics and outputs a scalar process reward (Pass 2) for RL training. They decouple the checker’s scalar reward from the judge’s verdict tokens through an interface design to prevent the judge from gaming reward via token copying. They further propose Pairwise Advantage Validity (PAV) as an unbiased validation metric that jointly measures (1) the judge’s evaluation accuracy and (2) the checker’s reliability, to identify the best early-stopping point for training.

Result: On multiple base models (Qwen3.5-9B, Gemma-4-8B-it, Qwen3.6-27B), RecurSE yields consistent improvements on held-out benchmarks spanning medical QA, pairwise comparison, summarization, and professional exams. Ablation studies show that synchronized co-training of judge and checker beats alternatives such as frozen checkers, external meta-judges, self-consistency baselines, and larger-teacher distillation. Additionally, preference datasets generated by their improved judge can further align downstream policies.

Conclusion: LLM judges can be improved through bounded recursive self-improvement without external gold supervision, provided that self-generated rewards are structurally decoupled from the judge’s outputs and rigorously monitored. The RecurSE framework, together with PAV-based early stopping, offers a practical recipe for self-evaluating, self-improving LLM-as-judge systems that generalize across tasks and models.

Abstract: LLM-as-judge is essential for evaluating open-ended text and steering post-training, yet improving the judge itself typically relies on expensive annotations, reward models, or distillation from stronger teachers. In this work, we eliminate external gold supervision from the RL training reward: the model's own evaluative capability generates learning signals for its optimization -- a closed-loop setting of bounded recursive self-improvement (RSI) termed Recursive Self-Evaluation (RecurSE). We study two central questions: when can self-improvement occur, and when must it stop? First, RecurSE pairs a trainable judge evaluating candidate responses under per-rule rubrics (Pass 1) with a synchronized policy-copy checker that audits the judge's reasoning against meta-rubrics to supply a scalar process reward (Pass 2). To enable learning, interface decoupling structurally isolates the checker's scalar score from the judge's verdict tokens, eliminating a degenerative token-copying shortcut that inflates self-assigned rewards. Second, because unanchored recursive learning is inherently bounded, Pairwise Advantage Validity (PAV) serves as an unbiased validation monitor that jointly tracks judge accuracy and checker fidelity to reliably identify the optimal early-stopping window. Across Qwen3.5-9B, Gemma-4-E4B-it, and Qwen3.6-27B, RecurSE achieves consistent generalization gains across held-out medical, pairwise, summarization, and professional benchmarks. Ablations demonstrate that synchronized judge-checker co-evolution outperforms frozen checkers, external meta-judges, self-consistency, and scaled teacher distillation. Furthermore, preference pairs curated by our judge effectively enhance downstream policy alignment. Bounded RSI for LLM-as-judge is thus viable when self-produced reward validity is explicitly decoupled and monitored.

</details>


### [28] [ROBE: Reversed-Order-Biased-Experts for Extracting Extreme Long-tail Events from Historical Texts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24268)
*Stella Verkijk,Piek Vossen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Paper introduces ROBE, a Reversed-Order-Biased-Experts architecture plus synthetic data generation to improve extraction of >50 long-tail historical event types from a 17th–18th century Dutch corpus, significantly boosting recall, precision, and F1 over a standard fine-tuned encoder.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Event extraction in 17th–18th century Dutch texts is extremely challenging because (1) historical corpora before the 19th century are niche and poorly covered in LLM pre-training, and (2) many event types are very rare, so standard models are dominated by frequent classes and underperform on the long-tail. The paper aims to "extract the long-tail of the long-tail" by designing methods that explicitly protect rare event classes and by augmenting scarce domain data.

Method: They define over 50 event types in a Dutch historical corpus and group them into subgroups either by similar frequency or semantic relatedness. For each subgroup, they train an "expert" classifier. During prediction, they prioritize experts trained on underrepresented events so that rare classes are less overshadowed by common ones. This reversed priority scheme is termed ROBE (Reversed-Order-Biased-Experts). In addition, they design a controlled procedure to generate domain-specific synthetic training data to support model learning in this low-resource, niche historical domain. They implement two variants of ROBE and compare them to a baseline fine-tuned encoder model.

Result: Both ROBE implementations outperform a simple fine-tuned encoder baseline. One variant yields a 0.10 absolute increase in recall, and another yields a 0.16 absolute increase in precision. For a targeted group of long-tail classes within the dataset, the best ROBE-based model achieves a 0.10 absolute increase in F1-score, demonstrating clear gains specifically on the rare event types they focus on.

Conclusion: By reordering and biasing an ensemble of expert classifiers in favor of underrepresented classes, and by adding controlled, domain-specific synthetic data, the ROBE approach substantially improves extraction of rare historical event types in a low-resource, niche setting. The study shows that tailored expert architectures and careful data augmentation can mitigate frequency bias and enable better modeling of the extreme long-tail in historical corpora.

Abstract: This paper proposes methods to extract over 50 types of events from a Dutch historical corpus spanning the 17th and 18th centuries. The methods we propose aim to tackle the impossible: extracting the long-tail of the long-tail. Historic data from before the 19th century is in itself a niche domain not covered in the pre-training of Large Language Models, and we aim to extract events only very scarcely annotated in the training data available for this domain. We propose creating expert classifiers for subgroups of the events present in the training data. We make these groupings based on similar frequency in the training data or on semantic relatedness. Experts trained on underrepresented events are assigned higher priority when predicting to avoid being dominated by frequency biases. We refer to this new way of combining classifiers, specifically tailored to protect the long-tail, as ROBE: Reversed-Order-Biased-Experts. We also propose a controlled method to create domain-specific synthetic data. Our two implementations of ROBE outperform a simple fine-tuned encoder model with a .10 increase in recall and a .16 increase in precision respectively. The best model achieves a .10 increase in f1 for a group of long-tail classes in our niche data set.

</details>


### [29] [SENSESHIFT: Continuous Sentiment-Controlled Text Generation via Encoder-based Mask Infilling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24304)
*Shahed Masoudian,Markus Frohmann,Emmanouil Karystinaios,Navid Rekabsaz,Markus Schedl*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes SenseShift, an encoder-based framework that enables fine-grained, sentence-level sentiment control in long-form text generation, improving controllability and robustness over standard decoder-based large language models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Controllable text generation systems using decoder-based LLMs with causal attention struggle to follow complex, fine-grained sentiment specifications, especially when different sentences in a long document must express different sentiment intensities. Existing methods typically use coarse sentiment categories or a single control signal for the whole document, leaving sentence-level control in long texts underexplored. The paper aims to fill this gap.

Method: The authors design SenseShift, an encoder-based controllable text generation framework that uses bidirectional attention instead of causal attention, combined with quantized sentiment signals and an iterative mask-infilling procedure. The model generates local sentences conditioned on a target sentiment intensity, repeatedly infilling masked spans while using bidirectional context and discrete sentiment levels to enforce the desired sentence-level sentiment control.

Result: On story and review generation benchmarks, SenseShift demonstrates stronger sentiment controllability at the sentence level while preserving overall text quality. It also shows better robustness when generating out-of-domain text, outperforming larger decoder-based baselines that rely on causal attention and global or coarse sentiment controls.

Conclusion: Encoder-based, bidirectional architectures with quantized sentiment control and iterative mask infilling can more effectively achieve fine-grained, sentence-level sentiment control in long-form text than standard decoder-based LLM approaches. SenseShift provides a practical framework for locally controlling sentiment intensity without sacrificing fluency or robustness.

Abstract: Recent controllable text generation (CTG) for sentiment control has largely focused on decoder-based large language models, making causal attention the dominant paradigm. While effective for fluent generation, these models still struggle to satisfy complex constraints and follow fine-grained sentiment signals specified by users. Existing sentiment-aware CTG methods typically simplify the problem by treating sentiment either as a coarse categorical label (e.g., positive or negative) or as a single fine-grained control signal applied to an entire document. Consequently, more challenging settings such as sentence-level sentiment control within long-form text remain underexplored. To address these limitations, we introduce SenseShift , an encoder-based framework for fine-grained sentence-level CTG. Unlike standard decoder architectures, SenseShift leverages bidirectional attention, quantized sentiment signals, and iterative mask infilling to generate local sentences conditioned on target sentiment intensity. Empirical evaluations on story and review generation demonstrate that SenseShift achieves stronger sentiment controllability while maintaining text quality and robustness to out-of-domain generation compared to larger decoder-based baselines.

</details>


### [30] [Who is the Agent to Blame? Localizing Faithfulness and Citation Mistakes in Agentic Deep Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24306)
*Eran Hirsch,David Wan,Han Wang,Elias Stengel-Eskin,Mohit Bansal,Ido Dagan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper analyzes why multi-agent deep research (DR) systems have low citation recall and introduces a fine-grained evaluation method that traces and categorizes errors to specific agents, enabling simple fixes that improve citation recall without hurting quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep research systems generate long, cited reports using multiple cooperating agents, but their citations are often incomplete or unreliable, undermining trust and faithfulness. Because these systems are complex and errors propagate like a telephone game, it is difficult to understand where citation and hallucination errors arise and how to fix them. The authors aim to create a systematic way to locate and characterize these errors at the agent level, so DR systems can be debugged and improved.

Method: The authors propose an evaluation framework that, for each agent invocation in a DR pipeline, locally checks its output for faithfulness and verifiability against that agent’s own inputs (rather than only against the final report or the web). They then introduce a taxonomy with four error types—hallucination, uncited input reliance, uncited output, and insufficient citations—to label and analyze errors. They apply this framework to three leading open-source DR systems, tracing which agents cause which kinds of errors and quantifying how these propagate to the final reports.

Result: The evaluation shows that most agents in DR systems make many errors, with single-document summarization agents being the main exception. Error types are not uniform: different agents tend to make different dominant error types, and orchestrator agents mostly commit citation-related errors. In one system (AI-Q), 84.7% of final-report errors originate from the orchestrator; about 31% of these are hallucinations and the rest are citation issues. Using the diagnostic insights, the authors design two targeted, simple interventions that increase citation recall by about 5% while preserving overall report quality.

Conclusion: Fine-grained, agent-level evaluation of deep research systems—combined with a targeted error taxonomy—enables precise diagnosis of where and how faithfulness and citation problems arise. The analysis reveals that orchestrator agents are a major source of both hallucination and citation errors. The proposed diagnostics support practical, low-cost interventions that measurably improve citation recall without sacrificing quality, suggesting that systematic debugging at the agent level is an effective path to more trustworthy DR systems.

Abstract: Deep research (DR) systems produce long-form cited reports by orchestrating multiple agents that search and synthesize information from the web. Citations are the primary mechanism for evaluating the faithfulness of these reports, yet current DR systems exhibit poor citation recall. Moreover, improving citation recall is challenging because DR systems are complex multi-agent architectures where information passes through agents like a telephone game, and both content and citations can get corrupted along the way. We propose an evaluation method that pinpoints which agent introduced each error by locally testing agent invocations for faithfulness and verifiability relative to their own inputs. Furthermore, we propose a four-type taxonomy to categorize the discovered errors: hallucination, uncited input reliance, uncited output, or insufficient citations. Applying our method to three top-ranked open-source DR systems, we obtain actionable diagnostics. Almost every agent makes a lot of mistakes with the exception being those that summarize a single document. We find that the dominant error type varies systematically across agents, where the orchestrator mistakes are mostly citation-related. We find that 84.7% of final-report errors in AI-Q originate at the orchestrator, roughly 31% of them hallucinations and the rest citation mistakes. Guided by these insights, we demonstrate that two simple interventions raise citation recall by 5% without degrading output quality.

</details>


### [31] [Speech-to-SOAP: End-to-End Summarization of Medical Dialogues: KIT@BeTraC 2026](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24327)
*Enes Yavuz Ugan,Fabian Retkowski,Yuka Ko,Thai-Binh Nguyen,Maike Züfle,Jan Niehues,Alexander Waibel*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents a system for end-to-end generation of structured clinical SOAP notes directly from speech using a scalable data augmentation pipeline that unifies diverse medical dialogue datasets via synthetic speech and automatic SOAP supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Summarization with large language models is promising for clinical workflows, particularly extractive clinical protocolling, which can reduce clinicians’ documentation time. Going further, generating clinical notes directly from speech, instead of via text transcripts, can speed processing and retain paralinguistic information (e.g., coughing) that transcripts miss. There is a need for methods that can robustly adapt speech foundation models to this end-to-end speech-to-note task despite heterogeneous and limited medical dialogue data.

Method: The authors build KIT’s lightweight-track system for the BeTraC challenge. They design a scalable data augmentation pipeline that: (1) collects heterogeneous medical dialogue datasets; (2) unifies them through synthetic speech generation (turning text dialogues into speech); and (3) automatically generates SOAP-format supervision for these dialogues. This unified, augmented dataset is then used to adapt (fine-tune) a speech foundation model for end-to-end speech-to-SOAP generation, i.e., mapping audio directly to structured clinical notes without an intermediate transcript.

Result: Using the augmented, unified datasets and SOAP supervision, the adapted speech foundation model can robustly perform speech-to-SOAP generation in the BeTraC challenge’s lightweight track. The abstract implies improved robustness and practicality for clinical note generation, though concrete metrics are not given.

Conclusion: A scalable augmentation and unification pipeline for heterogeneous medical dialogues enables effective adaptation of a speech foundation model to generate SOAP clinical notes directly from speech. This approach supports more automated clinical protocolling, reduces documentation burden, and preserves paralinguistic cues that transcript-based systems would lose.

Abstract: With the advent of Large Language Models and its instruction following capabilities a promising application is the task of summarization. Within this domain of task the extractive sub-task of clinical protocolling has emerged as a topic of particular interest as it can significantly reduce the downtime and protocolling burden of health-care workers thus enabling them to focus on their core work helping humans. A further step towards automation is the direct generation of clinical notes from speech without intermediate transcripts, reducing processing time while preserving information such as coughing or other paralinguistic cues that may be lost in transcript-based systems. To this end, we present KIT's submission to this years BeTraC challenge in the lightweight track. Our main contribution is a scalable data augmentation pipeline that unifies heterogeneous medical dialogue datasets through synthetic speech generation and automatically generated SOAP supervision, enabling robust adaptation of a speech foundation model for end-to-end speech-to-SOAP generation.

</details>


### [32] [SteerCheck: Attribution Specificity and Alignment Leakage in Activation-Steering Audits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24335)
*Daming Luo,Christy Liang,Junyu Xuan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces SteerCheck, a framework for rigorously auditing activation steering interventions in language models to determine whether observed behavioral changes are truly due to the intended concept or confounded by alignment leakage and other off-target effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing activation steering work often demonstrates that interventions can change model behavior but does not rigorously establish that these effects are specific to the intended concept rather than off-target influences. Common control strategies (e.g., isotropic or sign-randomized directions) have unexamined limitations, leading to potentially overstated causal claims about what is being steered. There is a need for a systematic, preregistered attribution audit that clarifies what can and cannot be concluded from steering experiments, and that decomposes different kinds of claims (e.g., about means, tails, polarity, transfer, and semantics).

Method: The authors propose SteerCheck, a preregistered attribution audit protocol for activation steering. They apply it to 960 steering interventions on Qwen3-14B, carefully matching off-target KL and analyzing multiple control constructions: isotropic directions and sign-randomized same-construction directions. They quantify how much these controls remain aligned with the target concept using signed cosine similarity, and they examine the distribution of effects within the randomized family. They introduce diagnostics such as a construction diagnostic A, explicit reporting of exchangeability assumptions, and empirical cosine distributions. They evaluate several forms of claims: mean effects, protected-tail behavior, polarity, transfer (margin and accuracy) across models and datasets, and semantic/factual changes assessed by a frozen three-rater human evaluation and an automatic judge. Robustness is further analyzed via sensitivity to a Γ=1.10 parameter in a sensitivity analysis framework.

Result: They find that isotropic control directions explore only a narrow, nearly orthogonal region of representation space, while sign-randomized directions generated from the same construction often retain substantial alignment with the target concept. Within the sign-randomized family, effect size is strongly correlated with signed cosine similarity (ρ = .94); 25.3% of randomized draws exceed cosine 0.5, and every draw whose effect exceeds the observed mean effect has cosine above 0.80. This indicates significant alignment leakage in commonly used controls. The main Qwen “complete gate” is negative because the protected tail fails across all control families. On new data, continuous margin transfer occurs only in Qwen, and accuracy transfer is absent in all selected cells. Language-based control interventions that were prospectively registered pass the complete gate in both Qwen and DeepSeek, but a passing DeepSeek detox comparator rules out categorical separation, and all nominal passes are fragile to Γ=1.10. Human open-generation evaluation suggests DeepSeek exhibits genuine factual correction but Qwen does not. The automatic judge shows poor calibration (macro-F1 = 0.562), limiting the strength of automatic semantic conclusions.

Conclusion: SteerCheck demonstrates that many apparent successes of activation steering are conditional on strong and often implicit assumptions about control constructions and exchangeability. Common baselines, especially sign-randomized directions, can suffer from substantial target alignment leakage, which weakens the inferential contrast between treated and control interventions. While this does not inherently invalidate conditional randomization tests, it constrains what they can distinguish and underscores the need to report assumptions, construction diagnostics, and cosine distributions. Under SteerCheck’s gates, Qwen fails to show robust protected-tail or semantic improvements, whereas DeepSeek shows some evidence of factual correction but with conclusions that remain sensitivity-bound and partly descriptive. Overall, SteerCheck provides a framework to make mixed and conditional conclusions about activation steering auditable, reproducible, and more methodologically sound.

Abstract: Activation steering can change behaviour without establishing that the effect is specific to the intended concept. We introduce SteerCheck, a preregistered attribution audit that matches off-target KL and separates mean, protected-tail, polarity, transfer, and semantic claims. Exact replay of 960 Qwen3-14B interventions reveals complementary limits of common controls: isotropic directions occupy a narrow near-orthogonal region, whereas sign-randomized same-construction directions often retain substantial target alignment. Effect is strongly associated with signed cosine within the sign-randomized family ($ρ=.94$); $25.3\%$ of its draws exceed cosine $.5$, and every draw exceeding the observed mean effect has cosine above $.80$. This alignment leakage does not by itself invalidate a conditional randomization test; it limits what the comparator can distinguish and motivates reporting exchangeability assumptions, a construction diagnostic $A$, and the empirical cosine distribution. The primary Qwen complete gate remains negative because the protected tail fails all families. On independent data, continuous margin transfers only in Qwen and accuracy transfers in no selected cell. Prospectively registered language controls pass the complete gate in Qwen and DeepSeek, while a passing DeepSeek detox comparator rules out categorical separation; all nominal passes are sensitive to $Γ=1.10$. Frozen three-rater open-generation evaluation supports factual correction in DeepSeek but not Qwen; the automatic judge fails calibration (macro-F1 $.562$), so null-wide semantic results remain descriptive. SteerCheck makes these conditional and mixed conclusions auditable.

</details>


### [33] [FARCA: Fact-Aligned Reliability-Aware Credit Assignment for Reinforcement Learning with Factual Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24350)
*Qiming Xie,Wenjie Zheng,Xiangqing Shen,Rui Xia*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper identifies and mitigates noisy factual credit assignment in RL-trained LLMs by introducing FARCA, a reliability-aware, token-level credit assignment framework that improves factuality without hurting reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Outcome-level verifiable rewards in RL for LLMs can induce hallucinations. Existing process-level factual supervision is too coarse and ignores the reliability of factual signals, leading to a mismatch between fact verification and policy updates. The authors want to precisely attribute factual feedback to the right parts of the model’s behavior and down-weight unreliable signals.

Method: They formalize noisy factual credit assignment as stemming from (1) credit localization ambiguity (where in the trajectory the factual signal should apply) and (2) credit reliability ambiguity (how trustworthy each factual signal is). They propose FARCA, which converts process-level factual supervision into token-level rewards aligned with the model’s policy update granularity. FARCA uses counterfactual evidence attribution to estimate how much a factual judgment depends on specific evidence tokens; this dependence acts as an empirical proxy for verification reliability. These reliability estimates are turned into weights that scale factual rewards and local policy advantages during policy optimization, thus implementing reliability-aware, fine-grained credit assignment.

Result: Across several LLM backbones and multiple factual reasoning benchmarks, FARCA-trained models show substantially higher factual accuracy while maintaining their general reasoning performance. Quantitative experiments demonstrate consistent gains over baselines that use standard or coarse-grained factual supervision.

Conclusion: Addressing noisy factual credit assignment is crucial for reducing hallucinations in RL-finetuned LLMs. By localizing factual feedback at the token level and weighting it by an empirical reliability estimate via counterfactual evidence attribution, FARCA yields more faithful and factually grounded models without degrading their reasoning skills.

Abstract: To reduce the hallucination risk caused by outcome-driven rewards in large language models trained through reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, existing mitigation approaches introduce process-level factual supervision. However, due to coarse-grained aggregation of factual signals and the lack of reliability assessment for these signals, they create a mismatch between fact verification and policy updates. We term this noisy factual credit assignment and decompose it into two aspects: credit localization ambiguity and credit reliability ambiguity. To address these issues, we propose FARCA (Fact-Aligned Reliability-Aware Credit Assignment), a policy optimization framework that transforms factual supervision into localized, reliability-weighted token-level training signals. FARCA achieves fine-grained credit localization by aligning the granularity of fact verification with that of policy updates. It further introduces counterfactual evidence attribution, which uses the dependence of a factual judgment on key evidence as an empirical proxy for verification reliability to compute reliability weights. These weights modulate factual rewards and local policy advantages, reducing the influence of potentially unreliable signals on policy optimization. Experiments across different models and multiple factual reasoning benchmarks show that FARCA significantly improves model factuality while preserving general reasoning capabilities.

</details>


### [34] [Words, Spaces and Generative AI: Layers of language in contemporary architecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24360)
*Anca-Simona Horvath*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper argues that language should be treated as a core design material in AI-assisted architectural practice and proposes a research agenda to systematize how different forms of language shape text-to-X generative design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: With text-to-X generative AI tools becoming standard in architectural practice, designers increasingly shape outcomes through language (prompts, code, annotations). Yet architecture has not sufficiently integrated key developments in linguistics, metaphor theory, and communication studies to understand or control how language steers design. The paper aims to close this gap so that architects can use language more consciously and rigorously as a design material in computational workflows.

Method: Conceptual and theoretical analysis. The chapter reviews major strands of linguistic thought (Wittgenstein on language games, Chomsky on generative grammar, Lakoff and Schön on conceptual and generative metaphors) and relates them to contemporary architectural design processes involving generative AI. It then constructs a three-part framework distinguishing discourse, programming languages, and annotations as intertwined linguistic layers in AI-based design, and from this derives a future research agenda.

Result: The chapter produces: (1) a synthesized account of linguistic theories most relevant to AI-supported architectural design; (2) a three-layer model of language in generative AI workflows—natural-language discourse, programming languages, and data annotations; and (3) a concrete set of proposed research directions: corpus-based studies of architectural texts, closer integration of communication/information theory with architectural research, and systematic study of how different natural languages yield different metaphoric structures and thus different design affordances within generative systems.

Conclusion: Language is not just a medium for describing architecture but an operative design material, especially in text-to-X generative AI environments. Architectural design with AI intertwines natural discourse, code, and annotations, all of which shape computational behavior and design outcomes. To harness this productively, the field needs structured research on architectural corpora, stronger ties to communication and information theory, and comparative work on how different natural languages and their metaphorical structures condition generative and conceptual metaphors in AI-driven design.

Abstract: Language can be considered a design material in architecture, and in the context of text-to-X generative AI models becoming a common tool for architectural practice, looking more closely at language is more important now than in the past. After describing some of the important developments in linguistics starting from Wittgenstein, and including the work of Chomsky, Lakoff, conceptual and generative metaphors as proposed by Schön, this chapter connects them to contemporary architectural design and generative text-to-X tools. The chapter builds on the idea that three main forms of language intertwine in architectural design done using generative AI, namely (I) discourse (or natural language which can contain professional terminology specific to our field), (II) programming languages (which are artificial languages sitting at the basis of all computational systems), and (III) annotations (as language elements attached to pieces of data). It concludes by outlining a research agenda for connecting generative metaphors to generative AI: (a) conducting corpus linguistics studies on architectural texts (using quantitative tools such as topic modelling, and qualitative tools such as discourse analysis); (b) bringing communication theory and information studies closer to architectural research and (c) taking into account that different (natural) languages come with different affordances meaning generative and conceptual metaphors differ in relation to this.

</details>


### [35] [Shortcut Before Circuit: Document Statistics Time In-Context Conflict Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24460)
*Yijun Liao,Fanwei Liang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that when multiple cues (recency, rarity, position) always agree in training data, transformer models can learn any of them, and standard evaluations cannot tell which mechanism they use; only carefully designed interventions and training dynamics can.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In natural language, cues like recency, repetition, and position typically correlate, so when a model answers correctly we cannot tell which underlying heuristic or mechanism it uses. This makes mechanistic interpretability and attribution to data ambiguous: different internal circuits can yield indistinguishable behavior. The authors want to create a setting where such ambiguity is exact and analyzable, to understand when data can and cannot determine mechanisms.

Method: They build a synthetic language where two cues, recency and rarity, are made exactly coextensive: following either rule always yields the same correct answer. They train 26M-parameter transformers (75 runs, multiple seeds and conditions) on this corpus and achieve near-perfect accuracy in all cases, including settings where a simple positional heuristic should fail. Then they apply a “minimal causal edit” to the evaluation distribution: they flip one cue (e.g., invert recency vs rarity) while holding fixed the true answer, token counts, and answer positions. They probe internal representations with per-cell readouts, examine sign fractions and variance across seeds, and analyze training dynamics such as when models escape positional shortcuts. They also study gating and circuit formation over training time.

Result: All models reach ≥0.999 accuracy on held-in data, and even on conditions where trivial heuristics would fail, making behavioral evaluation unable to distinguish which cue is implemented. However, under targeted intervention (the causal edit that separates the cues), internal readouts do not replicate across runs: 13 of 25 cells show sign fraction differences >0.3, with one as large as 0.879 vs a small standard error, indicating high variance in which mechanism is actually used. This variance aligns with how much each comparison relaxes constraints in optimization. In contrast, the timing of transitions does replicate: models leave a positional shortcut according to a predictable pattern, with a closed-form ceiling that increases monotonically with redundancy in the data. Before that escape, attribution can flip sign in many runs (32/75) without any change in accuracy, and the presence of gating on a circuit is necessary but not sufficient for a particular mechanism to be chosen.

Conclusion: When cues are perfectly coextensive in the training corpus, the learning objective is indifferent between them, so multiple distinct mechanisms are equally compatible with the data. As a result, mechanistic details (e.g., which heuristic a circuit embodies) may fail to replicate even though behavior and accuracy do, and attribution methods can give opposite answers without any behavioral difference. What is stable is not which mechanism is learned, but when models transition away from simple shortcuts, governed by redundancy in the data. The paper proposes that the corpus determines when a mechanism can arise but not which specific one does, providing a criterion for when it is or is not even in principle possible to attribute a learned mechanism to properties of the data. Their construction serves as an exact case where such attribution is unavailable.

Abstract: When a context asserts two values for one fact, a model commits to a cue -- recency, repetition, position -- but natural data rarely makes these disagree, so behavior cannot reveal which. We train 26M-parameter transformers on a synthetic language where recency and rarity are exactly coextensive, and separate them with a minimal causal edit that inverts one cue while holding the truth, token count and answer position fixed. All 75 runs reach accuracy >= 0.999, including where the trivial heuristic fails, so no held-in evaluation distinguishes them. Under intervention the per-cell readout does not replicate: 13 of 25 cells differ by more than 0.3 in sign fraction across three seeds, the largest by 0.879 against a standard error of 0.025. The construction predicts this -- coextensive rules leave the objective indifferent between them -- and the variance is ordered by how much of the optimization each comparison releases. What replicates is timing: escape from a positional shortcut with a closed-form ceiling, monotone in redundancy. Probed before that escape, attribution reverses sign in 32 of 75 runs at unchanged accuracy, and gating on circuit formation is necessary but not sufficient. The corpus fixes when a mechanism appears, not which one -- a criterion for when mechanistic attribution to data is available at all, and our construction makes the unavailable case exact.

</details>


### [36] [Dataset Scarcity Limits Robust Evaluation of Multilingual Embedding Models: A Case Study of Slavic Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24477)
*Ana Gjorgjevikj,Barbara Koroušić Seljak,Tome Eftimov*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a framework to more reliably evaluate multilingual text embedding models, focusing on Slavic languages within MTEB, and shows that current benchmarks are sparse and often too weak to support strong conclusions, while identifying a small set of models that transfer well across tasks and languages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multilingual embedding models are widely used for cross-lingual transfer, but current evaluations are uneven and unreliable across high-, mid-, and low-resource languages, especially under data scarcity. There is a need to understand how stable model rankings really are, how well models generalize across tasks within a language, and how strong the empirical evidence is for any benchmark-based conclusion, particularly for under-resourced languages such as many Slavic ones.

Method: The authors design a two-dimensional evaluation framework that separates task-specific from cross-task analyses and jointly examines three aspects: (1) ranking robustness to changes in ranking methods and dataset composition, (2) model consistency across tasks, and (3) evidence strength. They introduce an Evidence Strength Score that incorporates dataset availability, diversity, and the degree to which robustness can be assessed. They apply this framework to the Slavic-language portion of the MTEB benchmark, systematically varying rankings and analyzing model behavior and evidence quality across tasks and languages.

Result: They find that the Slavic portion of MTEB is severely sparse: many language–task combinations depend on a single dataset or on sets of highly correlated benchmarks, which limits the ability to draw robust conclusions from rankings. Despite this, cross-task analysis surfaces a small cluster of models—particularly llama-embed-nemotron-8b, multilingual-e5-large-instruct, and Qwen3-Embedding variants—that show strong and consistent performance across multiple Slavic languages and tasks. They also quantify how the strength of evidence varies, showing that some apparent performance differences are supported by weak or fragile evidence.

Conclusion: Benchmark-based model rankings for multilingual embeddings, especially in low- and mid-resource settings like many Slavic languages, cannot be interpreted in isolation from the strength of the supporting evidence. Their framework and Evidence Strength Score help contextualize rankings by accounting for dataset scarcity, diversity, and robustness, and they highlight benchmark sparsity as a key barrier to trustworthy multilingual evaluation. More and better datasets are needed to improve the reliability of multilingual embedding benchmarks.

Abstract: Multilingual text embedding models enable cross-lingual transfer of knowledge across a wide range of NLP tasks, but their evaluation remains highly uneven across high-, mid- and low-resource languages. In this paper, we propose a two-dimensional framework, specifically tailored for analyzing multilingual embedding benchmarks under dataset scarcity, and apply it on the Slavic-language subset of the MTEB benchmark. The framework distinguishes between task-specific and cross-task evaluation, while jointly analyzing three complementary aspects: (1) ranking robustness, (2) model consistency, and (3) evidence strength. At the task-specific level, we evaluate the stability of model rankings under changes in ranking methodology and benchmark dataset composition. At the cross-task level, we assess the ability of models to generalize across diverse tasks within a language. To quantify the reliability of benchmark conclusions, we introduce an Evidence Strength Score that accounts for dataset availability, diversity, and robustness assessability. Our analysis reveals severe benchmark sparsity, with many Slavic language-task pairs relying on a single dataset or highly correlated benchmark collections, limiting the ability to draw robust conclusions. The cross-task analysis reveals a small group of highly transferable models, most notably llama-embed-nemotron-8b, multilingual-e5-large-instruct, and Qwen3-Embedding variants, that consistently perform well across Slavic languages and tasks. Overall, the results demonstrate that benchmark rankings and robustness conclusions must be interpreted jointly with certain notation of their evidence strength and highlight benchmark scarcity as a major obstacle to trustworthy multilingual evaluation.

</details>


### [37] [Beyond Information Seeking: Severity-Aware Question Supervision for Proactive Medical Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24521)
*Chenxuan Li,Xinrong Chen,Luyan Zhang,Peidong Jia,Zhongyu Zhao,Xuecheng Shang,Peixing Wan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose a new, consequence-aware objective for training medical dialogue agents to ask questions that reduce the risk of missing severe conditions rather than just reducing diagnostic uncertainty.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current proactive medical dialogue systems typically select questions based on how much they reduce diagnostic uncertainty (information gain). However, in medicine, not all diagnostic errors are equally harmful: failing to detect a severe disease is much worse than confusing between benign conditions. Thus, there is a need for a question selection strategy that explicitly accounts for the severity of potential diagnostic mistakes and their downstream consequences, rather than only focusing on information-theoretic criteria.

Method: They introduce Expected-Severity-Risk (ESR), a question supervision objective that scores candidate questions by how much they are expected to reduce a severity-weighted terminal diagnostic risk. ESR computes, for each possible question, the expected reduction in severity-aware loss over possible answers, marginalizing with respect to answer distributions estimated from training data. The resulting rankings over questions are then distilled into a prefix-only language model policy so that, at test time, the dialog agent can select the next question without expensive online computation or access to the teacher. They evaluate ESR against standard uncertainty-reduction and generic decision-aware objectives using Qwen3-4B models on the DDxPlus dataset across multiple random seeds and under both unconstrained and fixed-budget (fixed number of questions) settings.

Result: Using ESR supervision on DDxPlus with Qwen3-4B across three training seeds reduces the mean rate of missing high-severity diagnoses from 0.0645 to 0.0455, a 29.5% relative reduction. It also improves mean overall diagnostic accuracy from 0.9123 to 0.9320 while requiring only 0.14 additional questions per dialogue compared to baselines. Fixed-budget analyses reveal that, even when the number of questions is held constant, ESR-trained agents exhibit distinct behavior from pure uncertainty-reduction approaches. An expected 0/1-risk control objective (which is decision-aware but not severity-weighted) fails to match ESR’s improvement on high-severity error rates, indicating that severity-aware weighting, not just generic decision-awareness, is responsible for the improved safety profile.

Conclusion: Severity-aware, consequence-focused supervision for question selection leads to safer and more accurate proactive medical dialogue systems than objectives that only maximize information gain or generic decision performance. By directly optimizing for reductions in severity-weighted diagnostic risk via the ESR objective, agents more effectively avoid missing serious conditions with minimal additional questioning. The authors argue that future medical dialogue systems should move beyond uncertainty reduction and adopt consequence-aware evidence acquisition frameworks like ESR to better align diagnostic questioning with clinical priorities and patient safety.

Abstract: Proactive medical dialogue requires an agent to decide what to ask from incomplete patient information. Existing information-seeking approaches commonly prioritize questions that most reduce diagnostic uncertainty. While effective for acquiring informative evidence, this criterion overlooks an important property of medical diagnosis: different diagnostic errors can carry substantially different consequences. Missing a severe condition may matter more than reducing uncertainty among less consequential alternatives. Question acquisition should therefore consider not only how informative new evidence is, but also how it is expected to affect the downstream diagnostic decision. To this end, we propose Expected-Severity-Risk (ESR), a consequence-aware question-supervision objective that values each candidate by its expected reduction in severity-aware terminal risk. Because questions must be selected before their answers are observed, ESR marginalizes over possible answers using train-only population statistics. Its rankings are then distilled into a prefix-only language policy, so next-question selection requires no teacher-side computation at deployment. Across three Qwen3-4B training seeds on DDxPlus, matched ESR supervision reduces mean high-severity diagnostic miss from .0645 to .0455 (-29.5%) and improves mean diagnostic accuracy from .9123 to .9320 while requiring only 0.14 additional questions per dialogue. Fixed-budget analyses show that the two objectives remain behaviorally distinct when question count is controlled, while a matched expected-0/1-risk control shows that severity-aware weighting improves the high-severity error profile beyond generic decision-aware supervision. These results support moving proactive medical dialogue beyond uncertainty reduction toward consequence-aware evidence acquisition.

</details>


### [38] [Is Discrete Difficulty Sufficient? Leveraging Continuous Difficulty for Efficient Self-Consistency in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24590)
*Sihyeong Yeom,Geon Park,Geunyeong Jeong,Taewoong Yoon,Jaewook Lee,Harksoo Kim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose Flexible Self-Consistency (FSC), a decoding method that adaptively chooses how many reasoning paths to sample based on a continuous estimate of problem difficulty, keeping accuracy while greatly reducing tokens versus standard Self-Consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-Consistency improves complex reasoning by sampling multiple reasoning paths and picking the most consistent answer, but this is very token-expensive. Existing efficiency improvements only use a few discrete difficulty levels, which cannot capture the continuous variation in reasoning complexity, leaving room for more fine-grained, efficient allocation of computation.

Method: Introduce Flexible Self-Consistency (FSC), which: (1) trains or uses a pre-trained probe to predict the output entropy (uncertainty) for a given input question; (2) treats this predicted entropy as a continuous difficulty signal; and (3) dynamically adjusts the number of sampled reasoning paths for that question, allocating more samples to higher-entropy (harder/less certain) questions and fewer to lower-entropy ones.

Result: On multiple models and benchmarks, FSC achieves accuracy comparable to standard Self-Consistency while cutting token usage by as much as 76%, demonstrating substantial efficiency gains without sacrificing performance.
