Conclusion: By using a continuous difficulty estimate (predicted output entropy) to control the number of sampled reasoning paths, FSC preserves the accuracy benefits of Self-Consistency while significantly reducing computational cost, showing that fine-grained, uncertainty-based allocation of reasoning budget is an effective strategy for efficient LLM decoding.

Abstract: Self-Consistency (SC) is a decoding strategy that samples diverse reasoning paths and selects the most consistent answer, demonstrating strong performance on complex reasoning problems. However, the excessive token consumption incurred by generating multiple reasoning paths has been identified as a major limitation of SC. To improve computational efficiency, several studies have proposed strategies that adjust the number of reasoning paths or allocate resources differentially according to problem difficulty. Nevertheless, most existing methods categorize difficulty into a few fixed levels, failing to fully capture the continuously varying nature of reasoning complexity. In this work, we propose Flexible Self-Consistency (FSC), which estimates problem difficulty as a continuous signal and dynamically adjusts the number of generated reasoning paths accordingly. FSC predicts the output entropy of an input question using a pre-trained probe and leverages it as an indicator of model uncertainty to flexibly control the sampling budget. Experimental results show that, across various models and benchmarks, FSC maintains accuracy comparable to SC while achieving token savings of up to 76%.

</details>


### [39] [Quantization Effects on Bangla Language Understanding in Large Language Models: A Systematic Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24615)
*Ismail Hossain,Nafi Ullah Shafin,Mohammad Abdullah Al Mumin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how different post-training quantization schemes affect LLM performance on Bangla NLU benchmarks, showing that quantization can work well but its impact depends strongly on both model architecture and quantization method.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most quantization studies focus on English, leaving uncertainty about whether similar benefits and trade-offs hold for morphologically rich, low-resource languages like Bangla. Practitioners deploying LLMs on constrained devices in Bangla lack guidance on which models and quantization formats remain reliable.

Method: The authors evaluate three LLM families (Qwen-2.5-7B, LLaMA-3.1-8B, GPT-OSS-20B) in both full precision and three post-training quantization formats (GPTQ-Int8, GPTQ-Q8, GGUF-W8A16). They run zero-shot evaluations with lm-evaluation-harness on five Bangla NLU benchmarks (Bangla MMLU, CommonsenseQA-BN, OpenBookQA-BN, PIQA-BN, BoolQ-BN) to measure accuracy changes under different quantization settings.

Result: GPT-OSS-20B shows substantial performance degradation on reasoning-heavy tasks under GGUF-W8A16, losing up to 57.35% accuracy, whereas Qwen-2.5-7B and LLaMA-3.1-8B remain stable under GPTQ-based quantization and sometimes even slightly outperform their full-precision versions. BoolQ-BN, a comprehension benchmark, is robust across all models and quantization formats.

Conclusion: Post-training quantization is generally viable for Bangla NLU deployment, but its success is not determined by bit width alone. Instead, the interaction between model architecture and quantization scheme is critical, and practitioners should choose combinations carefully when targeting constrained hardware.

Abstract: Post-training quantization lowers the memory footprint of Large Language Models (LLMs) and speeds up inference, which is why it is now common for on-device deployment. Most of what we know about its effects, however, comes from English benchmarks. It is not clear whether the same holds for morphologically complex, low-resource languages such as Bangla, and this gap is what we address here. We evaluate three model families---Qwen-2.5-7B, LLaMA-3.1-8B, and GPT-OSS-20B---in full precision and in three quantized formats (GPTQ-Int8, GPTQ-Q8, GGUF-W8A16) across five Bangla natural language understanding benchmarks (Bangla MMLU, CommonsenseQA-BN, OpenBookQA-BN, PIQA-BN, and BoolQ-BN), using zero-shot evaluation through lm-evaluation-harness. To our knowledge this is the first controlled comparison of quantization formats on Bangla NLU. The three families do not respond the same way: GPT-OSS loses up to 57.35% accuracy on reasoning-heavy tasks under GGUF-W8A16, while Qwen and LLaMA hold steady under GPTQ, and in a few cases the quantized version edges out the full-precision one. BoolQ-BN, a comprehension task, stays stable across all three families regardless of format. Taken together, these results suggest quantization can work well for Bangla deployment, but the choice of architecture and quantization method matters more than the bit width alone. We discuss what this means for practitioners choosing a model to run on constrained hardware.

</details>


### [40] [Beyond Semantic Accuracy: Consequence-Aware Evaluation for Safety-Critical Language Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24621)
*Yujing Chang,Thinh Pham,Van-Phat Thai,Chunyao Ma,Yash Guleria,Pham Nhut Huy,Sameer Alam*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that standard semantic metrics overestimate the safety and reliability of language models in air traffic control tasks, and introduces consequence-aware evaluation that better reflects real operational risk.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In safety-critical domains like air traffic control, even small language misunderstandings can have catastrophic consequences, but current NLP evaluation relies on semantic similarity metrics (e.g., F1) that do not distinguish between harmless and dangerous errors. The authors want to understand whether these standard metrics meaningfully reflect operational reliability and to create an evaluation framework that accounts for asymmetric, consequence-dependent risk of errors.

Method: The authors build a controlled diagnostic benchmark for air traffic control communication, grounded in official aviation standards and expert feedback from 40 air traffic controllers in three countries. They then define a consequence-aware evaluation framework that scores model outputs based on the operational severity of different error types rather than just semantic overlap. Using this benchmark, they evaluate eight language models and also apply risk-aware fine-tuning to test whether training can reduce high-consequence errors.

Result: Across eight evaluated models, standard semantic metrics report high performance, but consequence-aware evaluation reveals a substantial gap: models that appear reliable via F1 still make critical, high-risk mistakes such as misreading key parameters or omitting essential conditions. Risk-aware fine-tuning reduces some of these dangerous errors and narrows the discrepancy between semantic and consequence-aware scores, but it does not eliminate the gap.

Conclusion: Standard NLP semantic metrics substantially overestimate the reliability of language models in safety-critical tasks such as air traffic control. Consequence-aware evaluation is essential to capture operational risks and must be used alongside conventional metrics before claiming readiness for real-world safety-critical deployment. Even with risk-aware fine-tuning, residual safety gaps remain, indicating that current models are not yet trustworthy for unsupervised use in such environments.

Abstract: Can language models be trusted in safety- critical operations? In such settings, strong per- formance on semantic metrics does not guaran- tee operational reliability: a misread altitude, a dropped execution condition, or a confused call- sign may score well under standard F1 yet carry sharply asymmetric operational consequences. We study this problem in air traffic control (ATC), where controller-pilot communication demands near-zero error tolerance, and use consequence-aware evaluation to test whether semantic scores misstate operational reliabil- ity. The framework is instantiated in a con- trolled diagnostic ATC benchmark grounded in aviation standards and feedback from 40 air traffic controllers across three countries. Evaluating 8 models, we uncover a system- atic semantic-safety gap: conventional scores give substantially higher performance estimates than consequence-aware evaluation, even for models that appear reliable under standard met- rics. Risk-aware fine-tuning narrows but does not close this gap, showing that consequence- aware evaluation is a necessary complement to standard NLP metrics before any real safety- critical deployment claim

</details>


### [41] [Expectation, Backlash, Recovery, and Excitement: How Model Releases Shape Reddit Perceptions of Conversational AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24654)
*Vahid Rahimzadeh,Yury Zhauniarovich,Savvas Zannettou*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how public perceptions of conversational AI systems evolve over time by analyzing large-scale Reddit discussions around major model releases from several providers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most research treats user perceptions of conversational AI as static, even though these systems change rapidly through new models, features, and safety or access-policy updates. The authors want to understand how those ongoing interventions shape evolving user sentiment and discourse across different AI providers.

Method: They run a long-term, large-scale analysis of Reddit posts and comments about multiple conversational AI providers. Using sentiment classification and thematic concept analysis, they track how sentiment and discussion themes shift around specific model releases and policy changes for Anthropic, OpenAI, Grok, and DeepSeek.

Result: They find that perceptions are dynamic and sensitive to release interventions: Anthropic sees clear positive sentiment associated with Claude Code and strong product–model fit; OpenAI shows a “backlash then recovery” pattern around GPT-5 and GPT-5.1; Grok-3 perceptions are heavily influenced by the provider’s brand and surrounding political discourse; DeepSeek-R1 attracts both engineering admiration and worries about censorship, access limitations, and reliability.

Conclusion: Model releases function as social and experiential interventions, not just technical upgrades. Each release reshapes user sentiment, expectations, and the broader public conversation, implying that providers must consider perception dynamics and communication strategy when planning and deploying new models or policy changes.

Abstract: Conversational AI systems (CAISes) continuously change through model releases, feature updates, safety interventions, and access-policy shifts, yet user perceptions are often studied as static snapshots. We conduct a long-term, large-scale analysis of Reddit discussions to examine how users perceive CAIS model release interventions across providers. By combining sentiment classification and thematic concept analysis, we show that CAIS perceptions are dynamic and intervention-sensitive. Anthropic exhibits the clearest positive release profile through Claude Code and product-model fit, OpenAI shows backlash-and-recovery dynamics around GPT-5 and GPT-5.1, Grok-3 is shaped by provider identity and political discourse, and DeepSeek-R1 combines engineering praise with concerns about censorship, access, and reliability. These findings show that model releases are not merely technical updates, but user-facing interventions that reshape sentiment, expectations, and public discussion.

</details>


### [42] [From local kernels to global form: modeling the emergence of musical content](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24660)
*Francesco Vitucci,Michele Lorusso,Francesco Scagliola*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes an observation-driven, sliding-window approach to estimate time-varying Markov transition kernels from a single musical sequence and tests its ability to detect structural boundaries in Debussy’s Syrinx via Jensen–Shannon divergence, showing cross-validated boundary sensitivity but also wide plateaus that limit its use as an automatic, standalone segmenter.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional Markov models for symbolic music either use homogeneous kernels or rely on externally given formal segmentations to define non-homogeneous (time-varying) kernels, limiting their ability to discover structure directly from data. The authors want a data-driven way to infer how local musical behavior (pitch and duration transitions) changes over time from just one piece, and to see whether these inferred changes correspond to musically plausible formal boundaries without presupposing them as ground truth.

Method: They construct a non-homogeneous Markov representation by sliding overlapping windows of fixed length L over a single symbolic sequence and estimating a local transition kernel for each window. They then compare successive kernels using the Jensen–Shannon divergence to obtain a trajectory of change over time. Using Debussy’s Syrinx (273 note events) and the commonly suggested A–B–A′ segmentation as a reference, they compute this kernel-change trajectory separately for absolute pitch and notated duration for different window lengths L. They analyze where JS divergence peaks relative to the reference boundaries and compare the width of high-divergence plateaus to a theoretical maximum determined by window geometry (1/sqrt(L−1)). Finally, they run 500 resynthesis experiments from the fitted models to measure how far synthesized sequences depart from the original in both dimensions and to reveal degeneracies such as exact-copy behavior at small L.

Result: At window length L = 6, both hypothesized A–B and B–A′ boundaries coincide with maxima of Jensen–Shannon divergence for both pitch and duration kernels, indicating strong local changes in transition behavior at those positions. However, the divergence plateau is much broader for pitch (high values in 210 of 267 window comparisons) than for duration (64 of 267), meaning many nearby positions also show similarly high change. Given the theoretical upper bound 1/sqrt(L−1) for consecutive-window divergence imposed by the sliding geometry, the observed pitch plateau and its peak at L = 6 are not sufficient on their own to claim precise boundary detection. Resynthesis experiments demonstrate how well the learned models reproduce the source statistics and reveal an exact-copy degeneracy at L = 2, where the model can replicate the source sequence without meaningful generative variability.

Conclusion: The observation-driven sliding-window estimation of non-homogeneous Markov kernels is sensitive to structural boundaries in a musical piece, particularly when considering alignment across pitch and duration dimensions. Nonetheless, broad divergence plateaus and geometric constraints imply that neither pitch nor duration alone should be used as a fully automatic segmenter; boundary evidence is strongest when multiple musical dimensions agree. The analysis also highlights practical limits on window length, with very short windows (e.g., L = 2) prone to trivial, exact-copy behavior that undermines the generative purpose of the model.

Abstract: Markov models are established tools for symbolic music, including non-homogeneous formulations. The narrower contribution examined here is an observation-driven estimation mechanism: overlapping sliding windows derive a trajectory of local transition kernels from one symbolic sequence rather than from an exogenous formal partition. We test this mechanism on 273 logical note events from Debussy's Syrinx (1913), using the often-proposed A-B-A' reading as a reference rather than ground truth. We apply the same validation to absolute-pitch and notated-duration kernels. At $L=6$, both reference boundaries attain the Jensen--Shannon maximum in both dimensions; the duration plateau is substantially narrower (64 of 267 comparisons) than the pitch plateau (210 of 267). Because the theoretical maximum for consecutive sliding-window comparisons is set by window geometry and equals $1/\sqrt{L-1}$ for maximal turnover of the entering/leaving transition, the pitch value at $L=6$ and its broad plateau are not, by themselves, strong evidence. Their cross-dimensional alignment is consistent with boundary sensitivity, while the broad plateaus preclude treating either curve alone as a unique automatic segmenter. Five-hundred-draw re-synthesis experiments quantify departure from the source in both dimensions and expose an exact-copy degeneracy at $L=2$.

</details>


### [43] [One Timeline, Many Renderings: A Wolfram Language Paclet for heterogeneous musical output](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24683)
*Francesco Vitucci,Michele Lorusso,Francesco Scagliola*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents Temporal System, a Wolfram Language paclet that unifies multiple music-technology outputs (Csound, MusicXML, OSC, rehearsal click) from a single, immutable, beat-based timeline, keeping all artifacts synchronized.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Algorithmic compositions often require multiple representations—Csound scores, engraved notation, real-time control data, and rehearsal materials—that are typically authored separately. Because each has its own timeline and conversion to real time, they can drift out of sync, and maintaining consistency becomes error‑prone. The authors want a way to describe musical time and structure once, then reliably generate all necessary artifacts from that common source.

Method: They design Temporal System, a paclet for the Wolfram Language, which maintains an immutable store of typed musical entities on a rational beat-based timeline. Backends implement explicit rendering contracts that map these abstract entities to specific targets: Csound synthesis (via stable, named instruments in external .orc files and k‑rate control curves mapped to p‑fields), beta MusicXML 4.0 notation, OSC control streams, and rehearsal click tracks. All real-time quantities (seconds, samples, hertz) are computed only at render time, ensuring temporal consistency. They also formalize three layers of the system: temporal (beat/meter/tempo model), semantic (musical entities and meaning), and rendering-contract (backend implementations and trade‑offs), and they document limitations arising from integrating a proprietary authoring environment into an otherwise open-source toolchain.

Result: The system can generate synchronized Csound scores, notation (via MusicXML 4.0), OSC control data, and rehearsal click tracks from a single abstract representation. The click backend shares the same meter/tempo information and Csound serialization logic as the other outputs, demonstrating code reuse and temporal coherence. The archived supplement provides example outputs produced by the current implementation, even though the paclet itself is not yet publicly released.

Conclusion: A single immutable, beat-based temporal store with typed entities and backend-specific rendering contracts can reliably unify diverse musical outputs for algorithmic composition, preventing timeline drift between synthesis, notation, control, and rehearsal artifacts. While effective in practice and integrated with open-source tools like Csound and MusicXML, the approach is constrained by its reliance on a proprietary Wolfram-based authoring environment; nonetheless, the published outputs illustrate the feasibility and practical benefits of this architecture.

Abstract: One algorithmic composition may require a Csound score, engraved notation, real-time control, and a rehearsal click. Authored separately, their timelines drift. Temporal System is a Wolfram Language paclet that instead compiles one immutable store of typed entities on a rational beat timeline through backend-specific contracts. It emits Csound synthesis, beta MusicXML 4.0, OSC control, and click artifacts that remain synchronized because they share that store. Conversion to seconds, samples, or hertz occurs only at render time. Csound notes use stable named instruments in external .orc files; curves become k-rate signals declared against score p-fields. The click backend derives rehearsal audio from the same meter and tempo and reuses the Csound serializer. We describe the temporal, semantic, and rendering-contract layers, their practical trade-offs, and the limits of this proprietary authoring environment within an otherwise open-source ecosystem. The archived supplement exposes the reported outputs pending paclet release.

</details>


### [44] [The Annotation Bottleneck in Persian Text NLP: Persian as an Annotation-Scarce Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24698)
*MohammadHossein Mortazavi,Mostafa Salehi,Hadi Veisi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper argues that Persian is not truly a low-resource language overall, but specifically annotation-scarce in uneven ways across tasks and domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Persian is frequently labeled as a low-resource language in NLP, yet this label hides important distinctions between raw data availability and task-specific annotated resources. The authors want to clarify how resource limitations for Persian actually manifest, so that research efforts and funding can be better targeted instead of treating the language as uniformly under-resourced.

Method: The authors review 34 major Persian text resources available by July 2026 and perform three quantitative checks: (1) they use web statistics (W3Techs and Common Crawl CC-MAIN-2026-30) to estimate Persian’s share of web content; (2) they survey existing speech corpora from early datasets like FARSDAT to more recent, large-scale corpora; and (3) they compare the volume of task-specific Persian annotations (for syntax, NER, NLI, etc.) against English, normalizing by each language’s share of Common Crawl web content.

Result: Web statistics place Persian among the ~20 most visible content languages online, with around 0.7–0.9% of measured web content. Speech resources have grown from early small corpora to several hundred or thousand hours. When annotation volume is normalized by web presence and compared to English, some tasks (e.g., syntax and news-domain NER) have relatively dense Persian annotation, while others (e.g., natural-language inference) fall well below the web-proportional baseline. The data contradicts a blanket characterization of Persian as broadly lacking labeled resources.

Conclusion: Persian’s main limitation is not a global shortage of data or annotations but an uneven and fragmented annotation landscape. Scarcity appears in gaps across tasks and domains, divergent annotation schemes, barriers to access and documentation, and weak coverage of specialized domains, preference/supervision data, and non-standard varieties. The authors propose ‘annotation-scarce’—defined ecologically in relation to the NLP resource environment—as a more accurate description than ‘low-resource language’ for Persian.

Abstract: Persian (Farsi) is often described as a low-resource language in natural language processing, but that label collapses distinct shortages into a single category. This paper argues that Persian is more precisely described as annotation-scarce, provided that the term is understood as a property of its NLP resource ecology rather than an intrinsic property of the language. The review covers 34 representative Persian text resources available by July 2026 and adds three quantitative cross-checks. First, independent web measurements place Persian among roughly the twenty most visible content languages: W3Techs reports Persian on about 0.9% of websites with a known content language, while Common Crawl CC-MAIN-2026-30 identifies Persian as the primary language of 0.7039% of HTML pages. Second, a selective speech review shows a long resource trajectory from FARSDAT to recent corpora containing hundreds or thousands of hours of speech. Third, a matched Persian-English comparison normalizes task-specific annotation volumes by relative Common Crawl web presence. The resulting ratios vary sharply: Persian syntax and news NER are comparatively dense, whereas natural-language inference falls below the web-proportional baseline. The evidence therefore does not support a simple claim that Persian is globally deficient in labeled volume. Instead, annotation scarcity is expressed through uneven task and domain coverage, incompatible schemes, access and documentation friction, and limited supervision for specialist domains, preference data, and varieties beyond standard Iranian Persian.

</details>


### [45] [Lost in Speech: Trilingual Spoken Hallucination Detection Across Audio and Transcripts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24707)
*Meruyert Aristombayeva,Jason S. Lucas,Chaewan Chun,Dongwon Lee*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: First multilingual benchmark for detecting hallucinations in spoken news across English, Russian, and Kazakh, showing transcript-based methods beat direct audio and that synthetic hallucinations partly transfer to real fake news but include confounding machine-style artifacts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hallucination detection has focused on text and high-resource languages, leaving a gap for spoken content and low-resource languages like Kazakh, despite growing use of speech interfaces and multimodal models that can hallucinate in audio as well as text.

Method: Construct a multilingual spoken hallucination benchmark with 12,013 news items in English, Russian, and Kazakh containing controlled hallucinations of three types and three severities, each with original and hallucinated versions in both text and TTS/ASR-based audio. Augment this with 290 real fake news items in Russian and Kazakh, translated and passed through the same TTS-ASR pipeline. Evaluate fine-tuned multilingual encoders and multimodal decoder models under transcript-based and direct-audio settings, including zero-shot in-context use of decoders, and analyze performance and confounds such as ASR error and machine-style signals.

Result: Transcript-based hallucination detection generally outperforms direct audio processing; performance loss in audio-based binary detection correlates with ASR error by language. Detectors trained on synthetic hallucinations transfer well to real-world fake news in text form (macro-F1 0.82–0.88). Analysis of Russian data shows that models partly rely on machine-style artifacts and veracity cues, indicating that synthetic benchmarks are confounded by non-semantic signals introduced by the generation pipeline.

Conclusion: The benchmark demonstrates that transcripts are currently more reliable than raw audio for multilingual spoken hallucination detection and that synthetic hallucination training can generalize to real fake news, but it also exposes key confounds—especially machine-style artifacts—within synthetic benchmarks. Future work should design datasets and methods that better disentangle true hallucination signals from such artifacts and reduce ASR-related degradation, particularly in low-resource languages.

Abstract: While text-based hallucination detection has been extensively studied, spoken hallucination detection remains largely unexplored, particularly for low-resource languages. We present the first multilingual spoken hallucination benchmark comprising 12,013 news samples across English, Russian, and Kazakh with controlled hallucinations of three types and three severity levels. Samples comprise original articles and aligned hallucinated counterparts in text and audio. We complement the synthetic corpus with 290 fact-checked fake news items collected natively in Russian (225) and Kazakh (65), translated into the other language and rendered through the same TTS-ASR pipeline. We assess fine-tuned multilingual encoders and, in zero-shot in-context settings, multimodal decoder models on transcript-based versus direct audio processing. Transcript-based detection generally outperforms direct audio processing, with binary-task degradation for strong encoders tracking per-language ASR error. On real-world fakes, synthetic-trained detectors transfer strongly (macro-F1 0.82-0.88 on original text), while Russian provenance analysis reveals both veracity-related and model-dependent machine-style signals, quantifying a key confound in synthetic hallucination benchmarks.

</details>


### [46] [Arbitrary Polygon Oscillator: Generalizing Polygonal Synthesis to Arbitrary Shapes, Morphing, and Three-Dimensional Polyhedra](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24726)
*Antonio Argentieri,Francesco Scagliola*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They present a generalized polygonal audio synthesis engine driven by constant arc-length traversal that works with arbitrary 2D/3D geometry, supports smooth morphing between dissimilar polygons, and includes efficient, geometry-aware antialiasing for real-time use.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior polygonal synthesis methods assume regular, parametrically defined polygons and constant angular velocity, which limits timbral variety, shape sources, and control; there is also a need for smooth morphing between shapes with different vertex counts and for practical, antialiased, real-time implementations that can exploit more complex 2D/3D geometry.

Method: They redesign the polygonal oscillator to traverse with constant arc-length speed and to read arbitrary vertex lists from an external buffer, decoupling the DSP core from shape generation. They introduce a hybrid interpolation scheme that morphs between polygons with unequal numbers of vertices by constructing intermediate, non-parametric shapes. They extend the idea to 3D by rotating a convex polyhedron in 3D, taking the cross-section with a horizontal plane, and using the resulting slice polygon as the waveform. Implementation is done in RNBO with geometry caching to avoid per-sample geometry computations. For antialiasing, they combine adaptive oversampling with a four-point polyBLAMP transition correction whose Bézier-based tangents are computed at runtime from the local geometry, so no closed-form, per-shape analysis is needed.

Result: They obtain a unified, real-time-capable synthesis engine that can use arbitrary 2D polygons or 3D polyhedral cross-sections as waveforms, smoothly morph between shapes even when vertex counts differ, and maintain audio quality via efficient, geometry-adaptive antialiasing without expensive per-sample or per-shape analytic derivations.

Conclusion: Generalizing polygonal synthesis around an arc-length-driven core and geometry-agnostic DSP pipeline enables much richer timbral control, including morphing across heterogeneous shapes and 3D-driven cross-sections, while remaining practical for real-time use thanks to caching and a polyBLAMP-based antialiasing scheme that automatically adapts to arbitrary vertex configurations.

Abstract: Polygonal synthesis generates audio by traversing the perimeter of a polygon with a phasor; prior work uses a constant angular velocity, whereas the proposed system adopts constant arc-length (perimeter) velocity. Existing formulations operate on regular, parametrically defined polygons, producing smooth timbral transitions within a single family of shapes.
  This paper generalizes polygonal synthesis around a unified arc-length engine: vertex data of any origin feed the same DSP pipeline. First, we adapt the oscillator to accept arbitrary vertex configurations from an external buffer, opening the possibility for a broad class of closed polygons -- regular, irregular, or star-shaped -- to function as a waveform generator. Second, a hybrid interpolation algorithm enables smooth morphing between polygons with unequal vertex counts, passing through intermediate shapes that have no parametric description. Third, we extend the paradigm to three dimensions: a convex polyhedron rotated about three axes is sliced by a fixed horizontal plane, and the resulting cross-section yields a continuously variable polygon controlled by the solid's orientation. The system runs in RNBO (Cycling~'74) with a geometry caching strategy that avoids per-sample recomputation. Antialiasing combines a four-point polyBLAMP correction derived from runtime Bézier tangents with adaptive oversampling, adapting the correction geometrically to general vertex configurations without per-shape analytical derivation.

</details>


### [47] [SkillForge: Evolving Verifiable Skills for Reinforcement Learning Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24747)
*Shidong Yang,Ziyu Ma,Tongwen Huang,Xucong Wang,Renda Li,Yiming Hu,Yong Wang,Xiangxiang Chu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SkillForge is a framework that lets LLM agents continually verify, refine, and grow a skill bank through RL-guided interaction, outperforming prior skill-based methods like SkillRL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RL-trained LLM agents are episodic and fail to accumulate reusable knowledge, and prior skill-based methods only grow an append-only skill bank without checking if stored skills stay effective over time.

Method: SkillForge makes skill usage explicit during agent-environment interaction so RL can jointly optimize primitive actions and skill invocations; it adds evidence-based skill verification mechanisms and multi-pathway skill induction to refine, discard, or add skills as the agent keeps interacting with environments.

Result: On benchmarks including ALFWorld, WebShop, and AppWorld, SkillForge-trained agents consistently outperform those using SkillRL, indicating that continuously verified skills yield stronger LLM agents.

Conclusion: Continuously evolving and verifying a skill bank during RL training leads to higher-quality, reusable skills and better overall performance for LLM agents on complex decision-making tasks.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents are trained with reinforcement learning (RL) for complex decision-making tasks. However, most RL-trained agents remain episodic and cannot accumulate reusable knowledge across episodes. Recent skill-based approaches, such as SkillRL, attempt to address this issue by extracting skills from raw trajectories, but treat the skill bank as an append-only repository without verifying whether stored skills remain effective. In this paper, we propose SkillForge, a framework for continuous skill evolution that enables skills to be verified and refined through environment interaction. By making skill usage explicit during agent interaction, RL can directly optimize both environment actions and skill invocation decisions. SkillForge further introduces evidence-based skill verification and multi-pathway skill induction, allowing the skill bank to continuously grow while maintaining its quality. Extensive experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and AppWorld show that SkillForge consistently outperforms SkillRL, demonstrating the effectiveness of continuously verified skills in training stronger LLM agents.

</details>


### [48] [The RAT: A Unified Bayesian Model for RAG Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24753)
*Pius von Däniken,Felix Matthias Saaro,Mark Cieliebak,Jan Deriu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A Bayesian evaluation framework for RAG that decomposes retrieval, abstention, and answer correctness to better understand system behavior and error propagation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard RAG evaluation focuses on end-to-end correctness and ignores how retrieval and generation components interact, how errors propagate, and whether the generator’s behavior is appropriate given retrieval outcomes. There is also a need to use limited annotation budgets more efficiently and to integrate noisy LLM-judge signals with human labels.

Method: Propose a Bayesian probabilistic model that factorizes the RAG pipeline into latent variables for retrieval success, generator abstention behavior, and answer correctness. Fit this model across multiple RAG configurations (datasets, retrievers, generators). Use conditional probabilities to analyze system behavior, and study an annotation allocation problem using information-theoretic tools to compare the value of labeling retrieval success vs task success. Extend the model to treat LLM-as-a-judge scores as calibrated noisy observations within the same Bayesian framework.

Result: Across 27 RAG configurations, the conditional decomposition reveals large behavioral differences between systems that look similar under standard aggregate metrics. The analysis shows that annotating retrieval success yields more information than annotating task success for estimating whether systems follow desired policies. The extended model successfully incorporates LLM-judge annotations as noisy but calibratable signals alongside human labels.

Conclusion: Decomposing RAG evaluation into retrieval success, abstention, and correctness via a Bayesian framework provides a more diagnostic view of system behavior and error propagation than marginal metrics. Targeting retrieval-success annotations is a more efficient use of annotation budget for policy adherence estimation, and LLM-as-a-judge signals can be consistently integrated with human judgments in this probabilistic setting.

Abstract: Evaluating Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems requires assessing not only end-to-end correctness but also how individual components interact and how errors propagate through the pipeline. We introduce a Bayesian evaluation framework that jointly models retrieval success, abstention behavior, and answer correctness, factorized according to the pipeline's information flow. The model distinguishes task success. Whether the user received a correct answer (from generator success) and whether the generator behaved appropriately given the retrieval outcome. We apply the framework to 27 RAG configurations across three datasets, three retrievers, and three generators, and show that the conditional decomposition reveals substantial behavioral differences between systems that appear equivalent under marginal metrics. We further analyze the annotation allocation problem, demonstrating that retrieval-success annotations are more informative than task-success annotations for estimating policy adherence, and provide an information-theoretic explanation for this asymmetry. Finally, we extend the model to incorporate LLM-as-a-judge annotations as calibrated noisy observations, enabling practitioners to combine limited human judgments with cheaper automated assessments within a unified probabilistic model.

</details>


### [49] [ExpConCAD: Experience-Guided Text-to-CAD Generation from Shape Descriptions with Implicit Spatial Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24760)
*Jingyao Liu,Jinkang Tang,Chen Huang,Wenqiang Lei,See-Kiong Ng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces ExpConCAD, a framework that turns text into executable CAD programs by inferring missing spatial constraints using construction-structure understanding and retrieved prior design experience.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world natural-language descriptions of CAD designs are often incomplete and omit crucial spatial constraints, making it hard for current text-to-CAD systems to produce valid, executable CAD programs. Existing methods largely assume fully specified inputs and ignore how human designers reuse prior design experience and construction structures to fill in gaps.

Method: The authors propose ExpConCAD, an experience-enhanced text-to-CAD framework. It (1) recovers the intended construction structure and identifies constraint scopes from the input description, (2) retrieves relevant past examples or experience about how similar scopes were constrained to infer missing spatial constraints, and (3) uses these explicit and inferred constraints to generate executable CadQuery programs. The system includes a memory or retrieval component for experience, a construction-structure understanding module, and a program generator targeting CadQuery.

Result: Through extensive experiments (details not in the abstract), ExpConCAD outperforms existing methods on spatial constraint completion and text-to-CAD program generation quality. The results show that incorporating construction-structure understanding and experience retrieval leads to more accurate and executable CAD programs when the text description is underspecified.

Conclusion: Implicit spatial constraints in text-to-CAD can be effectively completed by combining construction-structure reasoning with reusable design experience. ExpConCAD demonstrates that understanding constraint scopes and leveraging past constraint-completion cases significantly improves the robustness and validity of CAD program generation from natural language.

Abstract: Text-to-CAD aims to generate executable CAD programs from natural-language descriptions. However, real-world descriptions are often underspecified and omit critical spatial constraints required for valid CAD construction, a challenge that has been largely overlooked by existing methods. In this paper, we argue that missing spatial constraints should be inferred with respect to the underlying construction structure and informed by reusable design experience. Based on this insight, we propose ExpConCAD, an experience-enhanced framework for implicit spatial constraint completion. ExpConCAD first recovers the intended construction structure and constraint scopes, then retrieves relevant constraint-completion experience for similar scopes to complete the missing spatial constraints, and finally generates executable CadQuery programs. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of ExpConCAD and provide insights into the role of construction structure understanding and experience memory in spatial constraint completion. Our code is available at: https://github.com/Hotjiashell/ExpConCAD.

</details>


### [50] [Linear Probing Provides Robust and Efficient Detection of Machine-Generated Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24780)
*Gerrit Quaremba,Hanqi Yan,Elizabeth Black,Denny Vrandecic,Elena Simperl*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that simple linear probes on language model representations can detect machine-generated text more robustly and with far fewer labeled examples than existing detectors, thanks to a shared “machineness” direction in latent space.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing supervised detectors for distinguishing machine-generated text from human-written text often fail to generalize to out-of-domain data and need large, diverse training sets. As large language models become more prevalent and their text is easier to generate and misuse, we need detection methods that are both robust across domains and sample-efficient. The authors suspect that differences between machine and human text are encoded linearly in representation space and can be exploited with simpler models.

Method: The authors analyze latent representations of machine-generated and human-written text from language models and study their linear separability in low-dimensional spaces. They examine differences in representation quality and use this to justify linear structure between MGT and HWT. Based on these insights, they design and train two variants of simple linear probes on top of pretrained model embeddings. They evaluate these probes across four benchmarks and compare them with 16 existing detection baselines, measuring performance (AUC) and data efficiency (samples needed for near-peak performance). They also explore the geometry of the learned probe vectors to show that they correspond to a direction that encodes a continuous notion of “machineness.”

Result: The authors find that MGT and HWT latent representations are strongly linearly separable in a low-dimensional space, with systematic differences in representation quality that explain this separability. Their linear probes consistently outperform a wide variety of detectors on four benchmarks, improving out-of-domain detection by about +11 AUC on average. They achieve near-peak detection performance using fewer than 100 labeled training samples. Analysis shows that the learned probe vectors align with a shared latent direction capturing MGT across diverse settings, which generalizes well and supports continuous scoring of how “machine-like” a given text is.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that simple linear probes on top of language model representations are powerful, robust, and sample-efficient detectors of machine-generated text. The effectiveness of these probes stems from a shared, approximately linear “machineness” direction in latent space that is stable across domains and generation settings. This suggests that many complex detection architectures are unnecessary and that latent-space analysis can provide both practical detectors and conceptual insight into how machine and human texts differ. The learned probes can support fine-grained estimation of AI involvement in text, not just binary detection, and thus are promising tools for monitoring and auditing AI-generated content.

Abstract: Distinguishing machine-generated text (MGT) from human-written text (HWT) becomes increasingly important due to potential misuse. However, most supervised detectors often degrade out-of-domain (OOD) and require large, diverse training sets. In this work, we analyze the linearity and quality of MGT representations and show that simple linear probes outperform a wide range of detectors while being substantially more sample-efficient. We first show that MGT and HWT latent representations are linearly separable in low-dimensional space, and provide a plausible explanation for this separability through systematic differences in their representation quality. Motivated by these insights, we train two variants of simple linear probes and evaluate them across 4 benchmarks against 16 baselines. Probes consistently improve OOD detection (+11 AUC), requiring solely ${<}100$ samples to reach near-peak performance. We show that this transferability arises because probes recover a shared latent MGT direction that generalizes across diverse settings. Finally, we demonstrate that probing vectors capture a continuous spectrum of ``machineness'', highlighting their potential for fine-grained estimation of AI-edited text. Overall, our work provides insights into latent-space differences between MGT and HWT and demonstrates the potential of linear probes as as robust and sample-efficient MGT detectors. We release our code on~\href{https://github.com/gerritq/mgt_probes}{github}.

</details>


### [51] [Structurally-bounded Agentic Graph Exploration for Evidence-Grounded Scholarly DeepSearch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24809)
*Rima Hazra,Sayan Layek,Somnath Banerjee,Soumen Chakrabarti,Animesh Mukherjee*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Crase is a constrained, transparent scholarly search system that outperforms deep research agents on large arXiv benchmarks while being cheaper.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep research agents for academic search often rely on open-ended search loops and proprietary models, making them expensive, hard to bound, and difficult to inspect or reproduce. There is a need for a more controlled and transparent method that still achieves strong retrieval performance over large scholarly corpora.

Method: Crase performs a single initial query to a search engine to obtain seed papers, then expands to their 1.5-hop citation neighborhood. It then prunes citation edges whose underlying claims are not entailed by supporting text, and finally ranks the remaining candidate papers using a recency-aware random walk. The entire candidate set, the justification for each paper’s inclusion, and the stopping condition are determined before inference, ensuring bounded and inspectable behavior.

Result: On LitSearch and an additional benchmark built over a 500K-paper arXiv corpus, Crase achieves up to three times higher recall@50 compared to deep research agents based on proprietary models, while operating at about one-third of their computational cost.

Conclusion: A bounded, inspectable citation-graph-based search process like Crase can deliver superior recall and lower cost than open-ended deep research agents, suggesting that carefully structured, transparent retrieval pipelines are a promising alternative for scalable scholarly search.

Abstract: We present Crase, a bounded and inspectable alternative to deep research agents for scholarly search. Instead of an open-ended search loop, Crase queries a search engine once for seed papers, expands them along their 1.5-hop citation neighborhood, prunes citation edges whose claims lack entailment support, and ranks the remaining papers with a recency-aware random walk. This makes the candidate set, the reason each paper is kept, and the stopping condition explicit and fixed before inference. On LitSearch and one further benchmarks over a 500K-paper arXiv corpus, Crase outperforms deep research agents built on proprietary models by up to 3$\times$ recall@50 at roughly a third of the cost.

</details>


### [52] [Reading Is Not Using: Retrieval, Judgment, and the Design of AI Financial Research Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24842)
*Miao Liu,Zhizhe Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that large language models, when used as AI analysts for financial disclosures, can accurately retrieve risk information but often fail to let that information influence their investment judgments in long contexts, and that workflow design is as important as model capability to fix this.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs are increasingly used in finance to read long documents like 10-K filings and support investment decisions, current evaluations mostly check whether they can retrieve specific information, not whether that information actually changes the model’s final judgment. The authors suspect that in long-context settings, models may read and even correctly recall disclosures but still effectively ignore them when making decisions, which could undermine their reliability as AI analysts.

Method: The authors run controlled experiments where they hold information about a focal firm constant while varying unrelated surrounding context length from 2,000 to 128,000 tokens in financial-analysis tasks. They test various LLMs and judgment tasks, including settings where real risk disclosures are removed from actual 10-Ks. They measure both direct retrieval accuracy of the risk disclosures and the extent to which those disclosures affect investment judgments. They also perform causal memory interventions to trace how information flows from source text through compressed summaries and lookup steps into final decisions, and compare different workflow architectures (e.g., chunk-and-summarize pipelines versus targeted, structured restatements placed next to the decision prompt).

Result: They find a clear retrieval-integration gap: as context length increases, the measurable influence of risk disclosures on investment judgments declines to the noise floor, even though the model can still accurately retrieve the disclosures when asked directly. This pattern holds across model families and types of judgment tasks and persists even when using real 10-K risk sections. More capable models delay the onset of this gap to longer contexts but do not eliminate it. Causal memory analyses show that only when risk information is both compressed appropriately and made accessible through source-text lookup does it reliably affect judgments. Chunk-and-summarize workflows tend to evict or overcompress relevant information, while a targeted, structured restatement placed adjacent to the decision prompt restores the effect of disclosures on judgments.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that AI analyst performance is jointly governed by both LLM capability and workflow architecture. Long-context abilities and retrieval metrics alone are insufficient: systems can be certified as competent based on retrieval tests yet systematically ignore crucial information in their investment judgments. Effective financial-analysis workflows must ensure that important disclosures are explicitly carried through summaries and positioned near the decision prompt, for example via structured restatements plus source-text access, to close the retrieval-integration gap in long-context financial analysis.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as AI analysts to process financial disclosures and support AI-assisted investment decisions. Yet such systems are usually evaluated by what they can retrieve, not whether retrieved information affects their judgments. We identify a retrieval-integration gap in long-context financial analysis. Holding focal-firm information fixed and varying only unrelated context from 2,000 to 128,000 tokens, we find that a risk disclosure's influence on investment judgments falls to the experimental noise floor even as direct retrieval remains accurate. The pattern replicates across model families and judgment tasks and in experiments removing real disclosures from actual 10-K filings. More capable models postpone but do not eliminate the gap. Causal memory interventions show that compressed summaries and source-text lookup jointly transmit disclosures into judgments. Workflow architecture determines whether this transmission succeeds: chunk-and-summarize pipelines evict relevant information, whereas a targeted, structured restatement adjacent to the decision restores its influence. AI analyst performance is therefore jointly determined by model capability and workflow architecture. Retrieval-based evaluations can certify systems whose investment judgments ignore information they demonstrably retrieved.

</details>


### [53] [BrowserForge: Scaling Web Episode via Parallel Browser Sandboxes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24848)
*Fei Tang,Huawen Shen,Zhiqiong Lu,Zhengxi Lu,Pengyuan Lyu,Chengquan Zhang,Weiming Lu,Jun Xiao,Yueting Zhuang,Yongliang Shen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: BrowserForge is a framework to automatically generate large-scale, diverse web interaction trajectories from real open-web sites using parallel browser sandboxes and a dual-agent loop, enabling better training of pixel-based web agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pixel-based web agents avoid the brittleness and token cost of parsing HTML/DOM, but require many high-quality interaction trajectories for training. Existing datasets are small, limited to narrow, fixed sets of websites, and recent automated pipelines still rely on constrained tutorial or curated site lists, leading to poor diversity and scalability. There is a need for a scalable way to collect verified trajectories from a broad open-web distribution.

Method: The authors design BrowserForge, composed of three main components: (1) an open-web sourcing stage that automatically discovers and surfaces hundreds of thousands of real, openly reachable websites; (2) a sandbox cluster manager that orchestrates and schedules hundreds of browser instances in parallel for high-throughput data collection; and (3) a Proposer-Solver dual-agent loop, where one agent converts a raw page into an explicit, executable task and the other agent attempts to solve the task on the rendered page, producing an interaction trajectory. A rule-plus-model post-processing pipeline filters failed runs and normalizes the remaining reasoning into a unified chain-of-thought format. Structural signals like the accessibility tree are used only during synthesis to guide data collection, while the trained agent itself operates solely on screenshots.

Result: Using BrowserForge, the authors build a corpus of 203,238 verified trajectories, each from a distinct website, substantially larger and more diverse than prior web-interaction datasets. Fine-tuning a compact multimodal model on this corpus improves its success rate on the live Online-Mind2Web benchmark from 25.66% to 33.33% and yields consistent gains in step accuracy on the static Multimodal-Mind2Web benchmark. The improvements become larger as the size of the BrowserForge corpus increases.

Conclusion: Large-scale, open-web-sourced interaction trajectories substantially enhance the performance of pixel-based web agents. BrowserForge demonstrates that it is feasible to automatically construct a massive, diverse dataset from real websites using parallel browser sandboxes and a dual-agent Proposer-Solver pipeline, while relying only on pixels at training/inference time. Analyses indicate that exposure to a broad distribution of websites and open-web sourcing are central to the observed performance gains, suggesting that future web agents should be trained on similarly wide and realistic data distributions.

Abstract: Web agents that act from rendered pixels avoid the fragility and heavy token cost of reading a page's HTML or accessibility tree, but training them depends on large amounts of high-quality interaction trajectories, and how to produce such data at scale remains an open problem. Public datasets typically contain only a few thousand trajectories drawn from a fixed and narrow set of websites, and even recent automated synthesis pipelines stay bound to predefined site lists or tutorial sources, so the number of distinct websites the agent ever sees barely grows. We present BrowserForge, a framework that generates web interaction data at scale by driving many browser sandboxes in parallel over the open web. BrowserForge couples three components: an open-web sourcing stage that exposes the agent to hundreds of thousands of real, openly reachable websites; a sandbox cluster manager that schedules hundreds of concurrent browsers with high utilization; and a Proposer-Solver dual-agent loop that turns a raw page into an executable task and then collects a verified trajectory for it. A rule-plus-model cleaning pipeline removes failed runs and rewrites the surviving reasoning into a single unified chain-of-thought style. Page structure such as the accessibility tree is used only as a synthesis-time signal; the agent we train and release acts purely from the screenshot. The resulting corpus contains 203,238 trajectories, each collected from a distinct website, larger and more diverse than prior trajectory datasets. Fine-tuning a compact multimodal model on this corpus raises its success rate on the live Online-Mind2Web from 25.66% to 33.33% and consistently improves step accuracy on the static Multimodal-Mind2Web, with the gain growing as the corpus scales. Controlled analyses further confirm that open-web sourcing and broad website coverage are key contributors to the observed improvement.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [54] [RENDER: Controlling Reader-Facing Evidence in LLM Memory Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23568)
*Yuan Si,Simeng Han,Daming Li,Jialu Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows that how past information is rendered to a model (as raw dialogue, summaries, typed records, etc.) strongly affects QA and memory performance, even when the underlying conversation and token budget are fixed, and proposes RENDER to systematically evaluate this effect.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing memory and retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) evaluations usually treat the model’s input format as an irrelevant implementation detail, focusing instead on what information is present. However, real systems present history in very different ways (raw dialog, ChatGPT-style entries, LangChain summaries, MemGPT-style typed records), and these may affect model behavior. There is a need for a benchmark that holds the underlying conversation constant while varying only the user-visible representation to understand how much answer quality depends on the rendering itself.

Method: The authors introduce RENDER, a benchmark control framework. It keeps the underlying conversation fixed but varies the “reader-facing artifact” via deterministic templates that mimic several common memory/RAG styles: (1) ChatGPT-style conversation entries, (2) LangChain-like summaries, (3) MemGPT-like typed records or formal ledgers, and (4) raw dialog excerpts. They use a five-level packet ladder to localize when answer-bearing content enters the model’s context and compare performance across packet levels, representations, and different models. Experiments are conducted on 500 LongMemEval questions with nine LLMs, using matched token budgets and comparing them to standard recency-truncated raw dialog. They also test robustness under retrieval noise and transfer to HotpotQA, using both automatic and judge-based rescoring.

Result: Across nine models and 500 LongMemEval questions, packet-based representations with matched token budgets outperform recency-truncated raw dialogue by 42.4–72.6 percentage points. Among realistic/deployed-style templates, the gap between the best and worst rendering style per model is 24.6–48.8 points. Under the primary automatic scorer, ChatGPT-style entries generally outperform raw dialog, winning on 7 of 9 models. Human or judge rescoring confirms the overall positive effect of better renderings, though for individual models the statistical significance is mixed. Notably, three models that achieve 0% accuracy when given formal ledger-style packets can answer the same questions with 45.4–53.4% accuracy when the identical facts are rendered as natural-language entries. The performance differences persist even with retrieval noise and extend to HotpotQA.

Conclusion: The way memory or retrieved information is rendered to an LLM has a large and systematic impact on performance, independent of the underlying content or token budget. RENDER demonstrates that different reader-facing artifacts—such as natural-language ChatGPT-style entries versus formal typed records—can change accuracy by dozens of percentage points. Because these effects are robust across models, tasks, and retrieval conditions, evaluations of memory and RAG systems should explicitly report, control, or standardize the input representation rather than treating it as an unimportant implementation detail.

Abstract: Memory and RAG evaluations often treat the answering model's input as an implementation detail, even though systems may render the same history as a memory entry, summary, typed record, or raw excerpt. We introduce RENDER, a benchmark control that fixes the conversation while varying the reader-facing artifact. RENDER combines a five-level packet ladder, localizing when answer-bearing content enters the input, with deterministic templates approximating ChatGPT-style entries, LangChain summaries, MemGPT-style typed records, and raw conversation. On 500 LongMemEval questions and nine models, matched-budget resolved packets beat recency-truncated raw dialogue by 42.4-72.6 points. In deployed-style templates, best-worst spread is 24.6-48.8 points per model; under the primary scorer, ChatGPT-style entries have higher point estimates than raw conversation on 7 of 9 models. Judge rescoring preserves the positive aggregate effect, but model-specific significance is mixed. Three models scoring 0 percent on formal ledger packets answer the same facts from natural-language entries at 45.4-53.4 percent. The effect persists under retrieval noise and transfers to HotpotQA, suggesting that memory/RAG evaluations should report or control the reader-facing artifact.

</details>


### [55] [ESQ-Bench: A Multi-Tier Enterprise Oracle Benchmark for Evaluating NL2SQL Dialect Generalization and Silent Semantic Divergence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23569)
*Sanjay Mishra,Divya Chukkapalli,Ganesh R. Naik*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces ESQ-Bench, a realistic, Oracle-first NL2SQL benchmark with complex enterprise schemas and shows that current frontier and open models perform far worse and exhibit high silent-divergence compared with their reported performance on academic benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing NL2SQL benchmarks like Spider and BIRD use simplified academic schemas and open SQL dialects that do not capture the complexity, constraints, and vendor-specific features of real enterprise databases. Models appear to perform very well (>89% execution accuracy) on these benchmarks, but it is unclear whether this performance transfers to production-like environments with complex schemas and silent failures. There is a need for a more realistic, vendor-aware benchmark, along with metrics that expose wrong-but-executing queries (silent divergence), to properly assess NL2SQL systems for enterprise use.

Method: The authors design ESQ-Bench, an NL2SQL benchmark centered on Oracle but ported to PostgreSQL, MySQL, and SQL Server. They create six fully populated schemas (465 tables, 164,682 rows, with no empty tables) grouped into three complexity tiers. They then collect and gold-validate 550 natural language question–SQL query pairs across these tiers. They define a four-metric evaluation harness: exact match (EM), execution accuracy (EX), success rate (SR), and silent divergence (SD). Using this setup, they systematically test multiple models—GPT-4o (schema-linked prompting and zero-shot), Claude Sonnet 4.6 (schema-linked), and local Llama 3.2 (schema-linked)—and measure performance and error modes, especially wrong-result semantics that still execute successfully.

Result: Schema-linked GPT-4o shows monotonic degradation in execution accuracy across increasing schema complexity tiers: 79.8%, 60.3%, and 57.2% EX on executed queries, with EM below 7% for all tiers. Operational silent-divergence is very high—73% to 99% among EX-passing queries—indicating that many queries execute but return incorrect results. An earlier 142-question pilot slice had substantially higher EX (75.6%, 80.4%, 95.8%), highlighting over-optimism from small-slice evaluations. Claude Sonnet 4.6 with schema-linked prompts outperforms GPT-4o on all tiers, achieving 87.4%, 74.9%, and 68.7% EX. GPT-4o zero-shot appears to perform well (78.7%, 73.5%, 77.8% EX) but this is partly due to lower execution rates and survivor bias compared to schema-linked prompting. Local Llama 3.2 schema-linked achieves only 13.3% EX across the full benchmark, emphasizing the large gap between open-weight and closed API models on complex enterprise schemas.

Conclusion: ESQ-Bench reveals that current NL2SQL systems, especially when evaluated on realistic enterprise-like schemas and across vendors, perform substantially worse than suggested by results on academic benchmarks such as Spider and BIRD. Execution accuracy drops sharply with schema complexity, exact-match rates are extremely low, and silent divergence—queries that execute but yield incorrect results—is pervasive, particularly at higher complexity tiers. These findings call into question the readiness of state-of-the-art NL2SQL models for enterprise deployment and underscore the need for benchmarks, evaluation metrics, and model improvements that directly address complex schemas and silent failure modes. ESQ-Bench, with its cross-vendor schemas, tiered complexity, and four-metric harness, is proposed as a more realistic and stringent standard for future NL2SQL research and evaluation.

Abstract: State-of-the-art Natural Language to SQL (NL2SQL) models report execution accuracy exceeding 89 percent on established benchmarks such as Spider and BIRD. However, these benchmarks rely on simplified academic schemas and open-source SQL dialects that do not reflect the complexity of enterprise database environments. We introduce ESQ-Bench, an Oracle-first NL2SQL benchmark with systematic complexity tiers and silent-divergence evaluation across three enterprise schema complexity tiers. We constructed and released six populated schemas (465 tables, 164,682 rows, zero empty tables) with identical seed data on Oracle, PostgreSQL, MySQL, and SQL Server, a four-metric evaluation harness (EM, EX, SR, SD), and 550 gold-validated question-query pairs (Tier-1: 95; Tier-2: 228; Tier-3: 227). Schema-linked prompting with GPT-4o shows monotonic execution-match degradation across tiers: 79.8, 60.3, and 57.2 percent EX on executed queries (June 2026), versus 75.6, 80.4, and 95.8 percent on an earlier 142-question pilot slice. EM stays below 7 percent tier-wide; operational silent-divergence reaches 73 to 99 percent among EX-passing queries. Failure analysis shows wrong-result semantics dominate at higher tiers. Claude Sonnet 4.6 with schema-linked prompts reaches 87.4, 74.9, and 68.7 percent EX (executed queries), exceeding GPT-4o schema-linked on every tier. GPT-4o zero-shot EX on executed queries (78.7, 73.5, and 77.8 percent) inverts schema-linked at Tiers 2 to 3 due to lower execution rates and survivor bias in the zero-shot versus schema-linked analysis. Local Llama 3.2 schema-linked reaches only 13.3 percent bank-wide EX (73 out of 550), underscoring the gap between closed API models and open-weight baselines on enterprise Oracle schemas.

</details>


### [56] [LLM Agents Perform Controlled Experiments Using Simulation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23622)
*Yuchen Xia,Michael Weyrich,Nasser Jazdi,Johannes Stümpfle,Johannes Sigel,Akshay Narla,Gavin K. Reynolds,Anna Jawor-Baczynska,Pol Llopart*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They build a multi-agent LLM system that runs controlled simulation experiments for pharma process design, so answers are based on intervention and comparison rather than text-only reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLMs are good at reasoning and code generation but weak at tasks that require understanding how systems change under interventions, which typically needs controlled experiments. In pharmaceutical process design, practitioners must tune process parameters based on how a simulated system responds, and existing language-only tools do not integrate tightly with high-fidelity simulations for this purpose.

Method: They design a multi-agent framework where LLM-based agents take a user query and a baseline process configuration, convert them into a structured task description, plan and design controlled experiments, run comparative simulations, analyze the outcomes, and then synthesize recommendations for process parameter optimization. The core idea is to tightly couple LLM agents with domain-specific, high-fidelity simulation models in an iterative loop of intervention, comparison, and observation.

Result: In an industrial pharmaceutical application, the system generates recommendations that are more specific and actionable than those from language-only LLM reasoning, and human users rate them higher in correctness and helpfulness. Additional ablations and visual case studies show that simulation coupling and the experimental reasoning procedure each contribute meaningfully to the performance gains.

Conclusion: Integrating LLM agents with scientific simulators in a multi-agent experimental framework enables intervention-driven reasoning, leading to more precise, useful, and trustworthy guidance for process optimization than text-only LLM approaches. This demonstrates a practical path for using LLMs as experiment planners and analysts alongside domain simulators in real industrial settings.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have shown strong capabilities in reasoning, planning, and tool use, but many scientific and engineering tasks require more than plausible text and code generation. They require understanding how a system responds to intervention, which in practice depends on controlled experimentation. In this work, we propose a multi-agent framework that enables LLM agents to conduct controlled experiments with scientific simulation models for pharmaceutical process design. Given a user query and a baseline configuration, the system constructs a structured task representation, designs experiments, executes comparative simulation, interprets the resulting outcomes, and synthesizes evidence-based recommendations for process parameter optimization. By coupling language models with high-fidelity simulation models in an interactive agent framework, the proposed system supports reasoning through intervention, comparison, and observation. As a result, it produces more specific and actionable outputs than language-only reasoning. In an industrial application setting, this advantage is reflected in higher output specificity as well as improved user-rated correctness and helpfulness. Ablation studies and visualized case analyses further demonstrate the effectiveness and practical utility of simulation-integrated experimental reasoning.

</details>


### [57] [A survey detection channel overrides the pixels in an astronomical foundation model, and biases tomographic mean redshifts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23626)
*Ihor Kendiukhov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper audits an astronomy foundation model (AION-1) and finds that it has inherited systematic incompleteness from survey catalogues, strongly relying on detection metadata rather than the image content itself, which biases downstream cosmological inferences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Astronomical foundation models are trained jointly on imaging data and catalogue products that are known to be incomplete and biased. These catalogue imperfections risk being silently baked into powerful models and then propagated into precision cosmology (e.g., photometric redshifts, weak lensing), but the extent and mechanisms of such biases are poorly understood and rarely quantified. The authors aim to causally probe how much a state-of-the-art multimodal model actually depends on potentially flawed catalogue metadata versus the raw pixels, and how that dependence impacts scientific measurements like redshift distributions.

Method: They study AION-1, a large 39-modality transformer trained on >200M objects. Using causal interventions, they hold the image tokens fixed while editing only the survey segmentation map and other metadata, and compare the resulting output changes with matched placebo interventions. They analyse detection gating (whether a source is marked at the field centre) versus the detailed light distribution, and test the model’s behaviour on real blended sources. They also simulate the impact of known survey incompleteness (from the Legacy Survey: 3.68% of targets missing segments) on tomographic redshift estimates under realistic conditions, including magnitude-dependent missing rates and observed positional errors. Additionally, they probe limitations of the tokenizer (image vs spectrum codecs, redshift quantisation) and evaluate the reliability of sparse dictionary features as causal levers by measuring recovery rates and sensitivity to random seeds.

Result: They find that altering only the segmentation map, while keeping images byte-identical, changes key outputs (flux, size, ellipticity, redshift) by factors of 110–4400 relative to placebo edits, demonstrating extreme sensitivity to detection metadata. The dominant mechanism is a detection gate based on presence at the field centre (correlation r = 0.47) rather than the enclosed light (r = 0.30). For 322 real blends, the model effectively ignores how the pipeline partitions the light (R = -0.006). When catalogue photometry contradicts the pixels, the model performs nine times worse than when no metadata is given at all. Propagating the 3.68% segmentation-miss rate into tomographic analyses yields median shifts in bin mean redshifts of 0.71× the LSST DESC requirement across 40 tomographic setups, and exceeds this requirement in 12 cases; when realistic positional errors are included, the worst bin violates the requirement by a factor of 8.3. These effects persist when misses are drawn according to their magnitude dependence. The bias disappears if spectroscopy is available or if the detection channel is removed (with no measurable performance cost), and the pathology strengthens with model scale. They also show that the image tokenizer has only ~28 effective states on sources compared to 934 for spectra, and the redshift head is limited by quantisation. Sparse-token dictionary interventions yield only 26–75% recovery and can shift outcomes by up to 18 percentage points depending on random seed, indicating they are weak causal instruments.

Conclusion: AION-1, representative of current astronomy foundation models, has learned to rely heavily on survey catalogue detection metadata, inheriting and amplifying catalogue incompleteness as a systematic error that can materially bias cosmological inferences such as tomographic redshift distributions. The dominant cue is a crude detection gate rather than detailed image information, and even sophisticated token-based causal editing is unreliable due to tokeniser and dictionary limitations. Removing or rethinking the detection channel, improving image tokenisation, and incorporating robust spectroscopic supervision are necessary to mitigate these biases. More broadly, the work demonstrates the need for causal auditing of multimodal scientific foundation models to avoid silently embedding survey-systematic errors into downstream science.

Abstract: Foundation models for astronomy are trained on survey pixels together with the catalogue products derived from those pixels. Those catalogues are incomplete at a measurable rate, and a model trained on both inherits that incompleteness as a systematic. We audit AION-1, a 39-modality transformer trained on more than 200 million objects, using causal interventions on its inputs.
  Holding the image tokens byte-identical and editing only the survey segmentation map changes every quantity the model reports -- flux, size, ellipticity, redshift -- by 110-4400 times a matched placebo. The mechanism is detection gating, presence at the field centre (r = 0.47), not the light the mask encloses (r = 0.30); across 322 real blends the model ignores how the pipeline partitioned the light (R = -0.006). Nor is the preference specific to that channel: contradicted catalogue photometry leaves the model nine times worse than supplying no metadata at all.
  The Legacy Survey pipeline leaves 3.68% of targets with no segment covering their position. Propagating that rate, with a miss represented by the fields the pipeline actually returns, shifts tomographic mean redshifts by a median 0.71 times the LSST DESC requirement over 40 assignments and exceeds it in 12; observed positional errors take the worst bin to 8.3 times. Drawing the misses by their measured magnitude dependence rather than uniformly does not change it. Spectroscopy removes the effect, withholding the detection channel removes it at no measurable cost, and the effect grows with model scale.
  Two further limits lie in the tokeniser: its image codec resolves 28 effective states on source patches against 934 for the spectrum codec, and the redshift readout is quantisation-limited. Sparse dictionaries are unreliable causal handles: across 15, recovery spans 26-75% and moves up to 18 points on the seed alone.

</details>


### [58] [TRACE: Transition-Aware Residual Control for Multi-Objective Materials Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23631)
*Kang Zhou,Yujia Tong,Yong Tao,Jingling Yuan*

Main category: cs.AI
