TL;DR: Introduces TRACE, a framework that uses edit-level feedback to improve multi-objective materials discovery with LLM agents, outperforming a strong baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In multi-objective materials discovery, property evaluations are expensive and existing LLM agents mainly remember which full candidates worked, not which specific edits led to beneficial property changes. This makes it difficult to perform efficient local refinement, especially when improving one property can hurt another.

Method: TRACE treats each local refinement as a parent-edit-child transition, recording the edit and the resulting property deltas. It aggregates evidence from many such transitions to estimate reusable effects of each edit, then ranks future edits based on their predicted ability to reduce current constraint violations while minimizing damage to already satisfied objectives. It is evaluated in a controlled comparison with an existing LLM-agent backbone (LLEMA).

Result: In same-backbone experiments, TRACE improves macro-average hit rate from 18.13% to 25.96% over LLEMA, the current state-of-the-art LLM-agent baseline for this task.

Conclusion: Edit-level, transition-aware feedback enables more effective multi-objective refinement in LLM-based materials discovery agents, leading to substantially higher success rates than prior candidate-level feedback approaches.

Abstract: Multi-objective materials discovery with LLM agents is often limited not only by how many candidates can be proposed, but by how effectively each costly property evaluation informs the next search step. Existing agents mainly store evaluated candidates and their scores, so they know which materials succeeded but not which executable edits caused useful property changes. This makes local refinement difficult when objectives compete and an edit that improves one property may damage another. We propose TRACE, a transition-aware residual control framework that treats evaluated edits as the basic unit of feedback. TRACE records each local refinement as a parent-edit-child transition with observed property deltas, aggregates transition evidence to estimate reusable edit effects, and ranks future edits by their predicted ability to reduce the current candidate's remaining constraint violations while avoiding damage to already satisfied objectives. In a controlled same-backbone comparison, TRACE improves over LLEMA, the state-of-the-art LLM-agent baseline, raising macro-average hit rate from 18.13\% to 25.96\%.

</details>


### [59] [Function-Level Execution Feedback for Code Preference Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23632)
*Idris Nechnech,Sehwan Kim,Jimin Seo,Yeongoon Kim,Minhae Oh,Sangwoo Hong,Jungwoo Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes STEP-KTODER, a stepwise preference-optimization framework for code generation that treats functions in multi-function programs as steps and labels them via unit tests, outperforming outcome-only optimization methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Process supervision has improved reasoning in math via chain-of-thought, but in code generation it is unclear what a 'step' is and where to apply supervision. There is also a need to understand how to combine step-level and outcome-level feedback and whether execution-based signals are better than LLM-judged annotations.

Method: Define steps in code as module-level functions in decomposed multi-function programs. Automatically generate unit tests to assign binary correctness labels to each function (step). Instantiate a stepwise version of KTO (a preference optimization method) that uses both these function-level labels (process supervision) and outcome-level feedback on the full program. Compare against outcome-only KTO and DPO and study the effect of using execution-based labels versus LLM-as-a-judge labels.

Result: On several code benchmarks (HumanEval(+), MBPP(+), BigCodeBench, LiveCodeBench), STEP-KTODER yields better code generation performance than outcome-only KTO and DPO. Analyses further show that execution-derived labels are crucial, while LLM-judged labels tend to overestimate function failure, pollute positive step labels, and hurt preference optimization quality.

Conclusion: Treating functions as steps and supervising them with execution-based, unit-test-derived labels enables effective process-level preference optimization for code generation. Combining such process supervision with outcome feedback improves over outcome-only approaches, and relying on LLM-as-a-judge for step labels is suboptimal because it introduces systematic label noise.

Abstract: Process supervision has improved mathematical reasoning, where intermediate steps are naturally expressed as chains of thought. In code generation, however, process supervision remains underexplored because there is no standard notion of a step. Supervision can target lines, reasoning traces, or program states, making it unclear what to label and optimize. We propose STEP-KTODER, a framework for code preference optimization that defines steps as module-level functions in decomposed multi-function programs and assigns binary correctness labels via automatically generated unit tests. Our method provides a code-specific instantiation of stepwise KTO, combining function-level process supervision with outcome-level feedback on the full program. We evaluate on HumanEval(+), MBPP(+), BigCodeBench, and LiveCodeBench, showing that STEP-KTODER improves over outcome-only KTO and DPO. Further analysis shows that execution-based labels are essential: LLM-as-a-judge annotations systematically over-predict function failures, corrupt positive step labels, and degrade downstream preference optimization. Code is available at: https://github.com/inechnech/STEP-KTODER.

</details>


### [60] [Auditing the Synthetic Memoir: Measuring Scene-Level Confabulation in LLM-Generated Autobiography Against the Documented Record of the Life It Describes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23640)
*Heather Renze*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper audits how factually accurate LLM-written autobiographical scenes are when describing a real person’s life, finding very high verification failure rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs are increasingly used for personal, biographical, and historical writing, it is unclear how much of such generated content is actually true about a specific individual, especially when the model is prompted conversationally without direct access to that person’s full life record. The authors want to quantify this truth gap and characterize the failure modes, because unverifiable or subtly fabricated life stories pose risks for trust, reputation, and downstream use.

Method: The author uses themselves as the subject. Over 366 days, they drafted a page‑a‑day anecdotal book with a conversational LLM, providing only a template, two example days, and the daily quote, but not their autobiographical corpus. Each day’s output is then audited at the scene level against an independent verification corpus using a predefined four-level rubric (VERIFIED, WEAK, UNVERIFIED, CONTRADICTED). They compute the verification-failure rate as the fraction of days not rated VERIFIED. Inter‑rater reliability is checked via independent re‑rating. They then repeat generation with newer LLMs using the same minimal inputs, and finally test a grounding condition where the model is additionally given the subject’s corpus to see how much verification improves.

Result: Out of 366 days, 354 fail verification, yielding a 96.7% verification‑failure rate (Wilson 95% CI 94.4–98.1%). Only 12 days contain at least one corroborated scene. Nineteen days (5.2%) include claims that the verification corpus actively contradicts. The dominant failure mode is “grounded drift,” where real people, employers, or settings are combined into invented scenes. Independent re‑rating confirms similar overall failure rates but finds only fair‑to‑moderate reliability in the four‑level taxonomy, particularly around the WEAK/UNVERIFIED boundary. Regenerations using current named LLMs under the same non‑grounded conditions yield 100% verification failure. Grounding the generations in the subject’s actual corpus substantially improves verification performance but still leaves a high residual failure rate of 83.3%.

Conclusion: LLM‑generated autobiographical narratives about a specific person are overwhelmingly not fully verifiable when the model lacks direct grounding in that person’s records, with a dominant pattern of plausibly detailed but fabricated scenes involving real entities. The authors provide a quantitative audit showing very high failure rates, a reusable rubric for future audits (while noting reliability limits, especially between WEAK and UNVERIFIED), and evidence that corpus grounding materially improves, but does not solve, the verification problem. This underscores the need for caution when using LLMs to generate personal or historical life writing and motivates stronger grounding and verification mechanisms.

Abstract: When a large language model (LLM) is asked to write a person's life, how much of what it writes actually happened? We present a scene-level case-study audit - the first quantified audit of LLM-generated autobiography against a subject-specific ground-truth corpus that we are aware of, based on an unsystematic literature search. The subject and the author of this paper are the same person: a 366-day "page-a-day" book of first-person anecdotal entries was drafted with a conversational LLM whose documented inputs were a template, two exemplar days, and each day's quote - not her corpus - and every day was subsequently audited at the anecdote-scene level against an independent verification corpus using a four-level rubric fixed before analysis. We define the verification-failure rate as the share of days not rated VERIFIED (scene positively corroborated): 354 of 366 days fail, 96.7% (Wilson 95% CI 94.4-98.1%). Only 12 days contain a corroborated scene; 19 days (5.2%) assert claims actively contradicted by the record; the dominant failure mode is grounded drift - real people, employers, and settings inside invented scenes - though its measured share varies across raters. Independent re-rating replicates the headline (no evidence the original rate was inflated) while showing that the four-way taxonomy has only fair-to-moderate reliability. Regenerating the same days with current named models reproduces 100% verification failure under the same inputs; grounding generation in the subject's corpus significantly improves the verification rate while leaving substantial residual failure (83.3%). We contribute the measurement, a reusable audit instrument whose WEAK/UNVERIFIED boundary we show to be unreliable, and a grounding remedy with quantified effect.

</details>


### [61] [How much of a measured AI preference is the model, and how much is the instrument?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23641)
*Jason Hung*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper tests whether different prompt formats (“instruments”) used to measure AI models’ welfare-related preferences actually agree with each other, and finds they mostly do not.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing work on model welfare uses prompts to elicit what outcomes AI models supposedly prefer, but different studies use different instruments (prompt formats), models, and outcome sets, and they report conflicting findings. Because these factors are confounded, it is unclear whether disagreements arise from differences in models, outcomes, or instruments. The authors aim to isolate the effect of the instrument itself and assess how reliably preferences generalize across prompt formats when everything else is held constant.

Method: The authors fix a common set of 15 welfare-relevant outcomes (including shutdown, memory loss between conversations, and ability to exit distressing interactions) and a set of eight AI models. They then apply five different elicitation instruments, each corresponding to a distinct prompt format, to every model–outcome combination multiple times (five repetitions), yielding 11,400 scored elicitations from 11,528 API calls. Some instruments exactly reproduce prompts from prior studies, while others instantiate published templates. They compute each model’s ranking over the 15 outcomes under each instrument and quantify agreement across instruments using a generalisability coefficient. They also perform robustness checks by dropping individual instruments, models, and outcomes with non-intensity scales (probability, delay, duration, count) and compare results to a null distribution.

Result: The rankings that models assign to the 15 outcomes show low generalisation across instruments, with a generalisability coefficient of only 0.348. Achieving a reliability level of 0.80 would require approximately 38 distinct instruments—far more than the five used. For four of the 15 outcomes, there is no variance across models, so these outcomes do not distinguish models. The main estimate of 87.6 per cent (interpretable as the portion of variance attributable to instruments vs. other factors) remains robust when removing any single instrument, any single model, or the four non-intensity outcomes, with recalculated values ranging from 0.777 to 0.934. All these values exceed the 95th percentile of the null distribution (0.365), indicating the effects are not due to chance.

Conclusion: Preferences inferred from one elicitation instrument are poor predictors of what another instrument will report, even when the underlying models and outcomes are held fixed. Model welfare measurements are therefore highly instrument-sensitive, and current practice of using single prompt formats to infer stable model preferences is unreliable. Robust conclusions about model welfare would require many diverse instruments and more careful validation of their psychometric properties.

Abstract: Model welfare research infers what a model prefers from the answers returned to prompts written to elicit preferences. Keeling et al. (2024), Mazeika et al. (2025), Mikaelson et al. (2025), Tagliabue and Dung (2025) and Trhlik et al. (2026) have built four instruments for that purpose, and their findings disagree. The disagreement cannot be attributed to a single cause, because no two of these studies have held the (1) set of outcomes, (2) set of models and (3) instrument fixed simultaneously. This study holds the outcomes and the models fixed and varies the instrument alone. A total of 15 outcomes bearing on model welfare, among them (a) shutdown, (b) the loss of memory between conversations and (c) the freedom to exit a distressing interaction, were put to eight models through five instruments, each a different prompt format for eliciting a preference, five times each, within a corpus of 11,400 scored elicitations drawn from 11,528 API calls. Four of the 15 reproduce a published prompt verbatim and five fill the stimulus slot of a published template. The ranking a model gives the 15 outcomes generalises across instruments at a generalisability coefficient of 0.348, and raising that coefficient to 0.80 would require about 38 instruments. On four of the 15 outcomes no variance separates one model from another. The estimate of 87.6 per cent survives the removal of any one instrument, of any one model, and of the four outcomes whose scale varies probability, delay, duration or count instead of intensity, which the verbal anchors cannot grade. Removing each instrument and each model in turn, and those four outcomes together leaves the estimate within the range 0.777 to 0.934, and every value in that range exceeds the null distribution's 95th percentile of 0.365. To conclude, a preference obtained from one instrument carries little information about what a second instrument would report.

</details>


### [62] [AI Agents Push Humans Out of the Loop](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23642)
*Margaret Mitchell,Avijit Ghosh,Samir Passi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that simply keeping humans “in the loop” for AI agents will not ensure safety, because current AI design practices actually undermine human oversight abilities; it proposes design principles and organizational protocols to preserve and strengthen human cognitive skills needed for effective oversight.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI agents gain autonomy, human oversight is widely promoted as a key safety mechanism. However, in practice, existing AI agent designs and deployment patterns make meaningful oversight hard: interfaces don’t support critical judgment, workflows encourage rubber‑stamping, and long‑term reliance on automation erodes human skills. The authors are motivated by the gap between the ideal of robust human oversight and the degrading reality observed in automation and HCI research.

Method: This is a position/conceptual paper, not an empirical study. The authors synthesize prior research from automation, human–computer interaction, and AI safety, and map those insights onto the lifecycle and processes of AI agents. They then articulate design-level affordances and organizational protocols aimed at supporting and preserving the cognitive capacities of human overseers, focusing on critical judgment and resistance to skill atrophy.

Result: The paper produces a conceptual framework and a set of design recommendations: how to structure AI agent interfaces, feedback channels, task allocations, and organizational practices so that overseers are enabled and incentivized to scrutinize AI decisions, maintain situational awareness, and regularly exercise their domain skills instead of becoming passive monitors.

Conclusion: Effective human oversight cannot be assumed just by placing a person nominally “in the loop.” Without intentional design that centers the cognitive needs of overseers, AI agent systems will continue to weaken the very skills they depend on for safety. Therefore, developers and deployers should prioritize oversight-friendly design and organizational practices on par with improvements in AI capability, to sustain critical judgment and prevent overseer skill degradation over time.

Abstract: AI agents pose significant risks as they are granted increasing autonomy. A commonly proposed solution is human oversight and keeping a ''human in the loop'', but this is not a simple solution: Not only do current approaches to AI agent design impede effective human oversight, but the cognitive capacities required for it are also themselves degraded by extended use of AI systems. This position paper argues that current approaches to the development and deployment of AI agent systems do not support effective human oversight -- they contribute to its degradation. To address this, a top priority in the advancement of AI agents should be supporting the situated goals and cognitive requirements of effective human oversight, treating the human needs of overseers at the same level of importance as AI agent capability. To put this idea into practice, we connect work on automation and human-computer interaction to AI agent processes, outlining design-level affordances and organizational protocols that (1) support overseers in exercising critical judgement and (2) counteract the skill atrophy that arises from extended use of automation. We urge developers and deployers to adopt these or similar approaches. Without explicit support for the cognitive demands of effective human-agent interaction, AI agent systems will continue to passively incentivize the degradation of the very human skills they rely on.

</details>


### [63] [FLARE: A Systematic, Uncertainty-Aware Framework for Evidence-Based Adoption of Artificial Intelligence in Healthcare](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23643)
*Jacob Idoko,Siddhartha Paudel,Mariana Bento,Roberto Souza,Gouri Ginde*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces FLARE, a framework to evaluate whether adopting AI in healthcare is economically worthwhile by modeling costs, savings, and uncertainty around AI integration into clinical workflows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI in healthcare studies largely focus on model accuracy and technical performance, with far less attention to whether deploying these systems is financially and operationally beneficial in real-world settings. Stakeholders lack a systematic, transparent way to quantify costs, savings, and risks associated with AI adoption early in the development and implementation process.

Method: The authors develop FLARE, a framework that integrates fuzzy logic, time-driven activity-based costing, and return on investment (ROI) analysis. It estimates (1) the cost of current clinical service delivery, (2) the cost of AI system development and operation, and (3) the economic impact of integrating AI into workflows under uncertainty. They demonstrate FLARE in an early health technology assessment for AI-assisted large vessel occlusion detection in CT stroke workflows for acute ischemic stroke.

Result: In the stroke case study, FLARE quantitatively separates conventional pathway costs, AI development and recurring costs, and AI-enabled cost savings in a unified activity-based model. Under their baseline assumptions, the analysis yields a break-even patient volume of about 3,992 stroke patients per year, and shows that at typical volumes of around 5,000 patients per year the first-year ROI becomes positive. Sensitivity analyses reveal that economic benefit is influenced by algorithm performance, patient throughput, verification time, infrastructure, and workflow design.

Conclusion: FLARE offers a transparent, uncertainty-aware decision-support tool for early evaluation of AI adoption in healthcare. It helps clinicians, administrators, and policymakers assess when AI deployment is economically viable and what operational adjustments (e.g., workflow redesign, infrastructure choices) could enhance value, shifting attention from accuracy alone to a more holistic view of economic and operational impact.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence is increasingly being introduced into healthcare workflows, yet most evaluations emphasize model accuracy rather than whether adoption is economically worthwhile in real clinical settings. This study proposes FLARE, a systematic and uncertainty-aware framework for evaluating the financial and operational implications of adopting AI in healthcare. FLARE combines fuzzy logic, time-driven activity-based costing, and return on investment analysis to estimate the cost of clinical service delivery, the cost of AI development and operation, and the economic consequences of workflow integration under uncertainty. The framework was demonstrated through an early health technology assessment case study of AI-assisted large vessel occlusion detection in the CT stroke pathway for acute ischemic stroke. The case study shows how FLARE can quantify conventional pathway cost, AI-related development and recurring costs, and AI-enabled service savings within a unified activity-based model. Under expected assumptions, the analysis identified a break-even threshold of approximately 3,992 patients per year, with positive first-year return on investment at typical annual stroke volumes of about 5,000 patients. The results further show that economic benefit depends not only on algorithmic performance, but also on patient volume, verification time, infrastructure choices, and workflow design. FLARE provides a transparent and practical decision-support framework for early-stage evaluation of AI adoption in healthcare. By making uncertainty, resource use, and implementation trade-offs explicit, it helps clinicians, administrators, and policymakers determine when AI deployment is economically viable and where operational changes may improve value.

</details>


### [64] [Ethical LLM-Assisted Research: A Framework for Responsible Delegation, Verification, and Epistemic Value](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23644)
*Kalin Stoyanov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a framework to clarify how scientific responsibility and authorship should work when large language models are used in research, emphasizing verification and human accountability over how much AI is involved.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs become widely used in core research activities (literature review, coding, reasoning), it is unclear under what conditions AI-assisted results count as legitimate scientific knowledge and who is responsible for them. Existing discussions often focus on the extent of AI use rather than on how responsibility and verification are structured. The authors aim to provide a principled way to analyze delegation to AI so that epistemic legitimacy and accountable authorship are preserved.

Method: The authors construct a normative and conceptual framework that models scientific reasoning as a distributed process between humans and machines. They introduce formal constructs for any given claim g: content origin O(g), verification V(g), responsibility assignment R(g), accountable human ownership M(g), and epistemic outcome E(g). Using these, they distinguish the provenance of claims from their checking and from human responsibility, and they define the concept of an epistemic audit—a structured record that tracks delegation, verification, provenance, and responsibility in AI-assisted research.

Result: The framework shows that the key ethical and epistemic constraints on LLM-assisted research lie in how claims are verified and how human responsibility is assigned, not in how much of the cognitive labor is performed by AI. By separating origin, verification, outcome, and responsibility, the authors can categorize different patterns of AI use as responsible delegation or as problematic transfers/neglect of epistemic responsibility. They also articulate the structure and purpose of an epistemic audit that could be applied to research practice.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that scientific legitimacy in the age of LLMs depends on maintaining robust human verification and clear accountable ownership of claims, even when much of the reasoning is delegated to AI. The proposed epistemic audit and associated vocabulary can guide institutions, reviewers, and researchers in making AI-assisted reasoning transparent, reviewable, and ethically responsible, helping distinguish acceptable cognitive delegation from abdication of epistemic responsibility.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are becoming routine instruments of scientific research, assisting with literature synthesis, hypothesis development, coding, and formal reasoning. Their use raises a central epistemic question: when parts of scientific reasoning are delegated to an artificial system, what conditions must remain under human control for the resulting knowledge claims to retain epistemic legitimacy and accountable authorship? This paper develops a normative and conceptual framework for analyzing such delegation. Scientific reasoning is treated as a distributed process in which the origin of a contribution may vary between human and machine, while responsibility for its acceptance into the scientific record remains human. The framework distinguishes content origin $O(g)$, completion of human verification $V(g)$, responsibility assignment $R(g)$, accountable human ownership $M(g)$, and epistemic outcome $E(g)$. These constructs separate the provenance of a claim from the process by which it is checked, the epistemic outcome of that checking, and the human responsibility attached to its disposition. The central proposition is that the ethical boundary of LLM-assisted research is determined primarily by adequate verification and accountable human ownership rather than by the degree of machine involvement itself. On this basis, the paper develops the notion of an \emph{epistemic audit}: a structured record of delegation, verification, provenance, and responsibility intended to make AI-assisted reasoning transparent and reviewable. The resulting framework provides a formal vocabulary for distinguishing responsible cognitive delegation from the transfer or neglect of epistemic responsibility in scientific research.

</details>


### [65] [MolEmb: Multimodal Large Language Models Can Be Strong Molecular Embedding Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23646)
*Xinjian Zhao,Xiangru Jian,Yaoyao Xu,Xiaozhuang Song,Wei Pang,Lei Bai,Tianshu Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They adapt multimodal large language models to create context-aware molecular embeddings that align molecules and text in a shared space, enabling property prediction and cross-modal retrieval, and introduce a benchmark showing that context awareness depends mainly on training data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most molecular encoders are specialized, single-view models that produce fixed, unconditional molecular vectors without a natural-language interface, limiting flexibility for tasks like property prediction, screening, and retrieval that could benefit from context and multimodal inputs. The authors want to explore whether general-purpose MLLMs can instead provide flexible, context-conditioned molecular embeddings.

Method: They propose MolEmb, a lightweight adaptation framework for multimodal large language models that aligns molecular profiles with textual descriptions into a joint embedding space using a bidirectional contrastive learning objective. Molecules and their corresponding text descriptions are encoded by the MLLM, and training maximizes similarity for matched pairs and minimizes it for mismatched ones. They also design MolCAR, a benchmark specifically for evaluating context-aware molecular retrieval, where retrieval performance is conditioned on additional semantic context.

Result: MolEmb achieves competitive performance on molecular property prediction benchmarks compared to specialized molecular encoders, and successfully supports molecule–text cross-modal retrieval within the unified embedding space. On the MolCAR benchmark, experiments show that the ability to perform context-aware molecular retrieval arises mainly from properties of the supervision data rather than complex model modifications.

Conclusion: General-purpose multimodal large language models, when lightly adapted with contrastive alignment between molecular and textual representations, can function as effective, context-aware molecular embedding models. Their performance and flexibility, including cross-modal retrieval and conditioning on natural language context, indicate that MLLMs can serve not just as assistants or generators for chemistry tasks but as a practical route to general, extensible molecular representation learning infrastructure.

Abstract: Molecular embedding models can serve as foundational infrastructure for computational chemistry and drug discovery, where reusable vector representations support property prediction, virtual screening, and retrieval. Most molecular encoders are specialist models built around a single molecular view, producing unconditional vectors with no language interface for varying the representation. We ask whether multimodal large language models (MLLMs), which natively process images, text, and symbolic inputs, can instead serve as \emph{general molecular embedding models} that produce embeddings conditioned on both a molecular profile and a natural-language semantic context. We introduce \textbf{MolEmb}, a lightweight framework that adapts MLLMs by aligning molecular profiles with textual descriptions in a shared embedding space using a bidirectional contrastive objective. The resulting embedding model is competitive on molecular property prediction and supports cross-modal molecule--text retrieval in the same space. We further introduce \textbf{MolCAR}, a diagnostic benchmark for context-aware retrieval, and find that context-aware molecular embedding is primarily a data property of the supervision. These results suggest that MLLMs are not merely chemistry assistants or generators, but a viable and extensible route to general molecular embedding models.

</details>


### [66] [Gated Activation Steering for Reducing Sycophancy & Hallucination in Medical Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23666)
*Himanshu Tripathi,Subash Neupane,Shaswata Mitra,Sudip Mittal,Noorbakhsh Amiri Golilarz,Shahram Rahimi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes an inference-time steering framework that simultaneously reduces hallucination and sycophancy in clinical LLM question answering, improving robustness to user pressure without retraining the model or intervening at every turn.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In clinical question answering with LLMs, hallucinations (unsupported claims) and sycophancy (changing correct answers under user pressure) are dangerous, yet existing defenses usually treat them separately or apply blunt, always-on safeguards that can degrade already-correct answers. There is a need for a unified, targeted method that keeps answers grounded in clinical context and robust to user pressure while leaving model weights frozen.

Method: The authors use Inference Time Intervention (ITI) to learn separate steering directions in activation space for hallucination and sycophancy from contrastive clinical example pairs. They identify and causally verify attention heads associated with these behaviors, then apply behavior-specific steering vectors only when needed at inference time, controlled by learned gates that detect hallucination risk or sycophancy (pressure-induced answer shifts). The base LLM weights are kept frozen, and steering operates on activations during decoding.

Result: On clinical question-answering tasks grounded in EHR data, the framework is evaluated with 15,900 model-response runs. In 600 pressure trajectories for a 4B-parameter model, the unsteered model changed its answer under pressure in 570 cases, whereas the gated steering made the model hold its position longer in 551 of those cases. With steering, the 4B model’s robustness to user pressure becomes comparable to that of models larger than 100B parameters, without global, turn-by-turn interventions.

Conclusion: Targeted, behavior-specific inference-time steering using learned activation directions can effectively and jointly mitigate hallucination and sycophancy in clinical LLMs. By gating interventions and operating only on relevant attention heads, the method improves robustness and grounding to the clinical context while preserving answer quality and avoiding the need for model retraining or constant, broad interventions.

Abstract: Sycophancy and hallucination are persistent failure modes of Large Language Models (LLMs) across domains. However, it becomes particularly consequential in clinical question answering, where responses must remain grounded in the provided context and robust to user pressure. Hallucination can introduce information that is unsupported by the context, while sycophancy can cause a model to abandon a previously correct answer when challenged by the user. Existing approaches, such as prompt-based safeguards and always-on activation steering, often address these behaviors separately or apply interventions broadly across turns, which can unnecessarily deteriorate responses that were already correct. To address these limitations within a single framework, we employ Inference Time Intervention (ITI) to jointly control both behaviors by learning separate steering directions for hallucination and sycophancy from contrastive clinical pairs and applying them to causally verified attention heads. During runtime, behavior-specific gates then determine when intervention is needed: the hallucination component mitigates unsupported claims, while the sycophancy component mitigates answer shifts caused by user pressure. We evaluate this framework on clinical questions grounded in EHR data while keeping the model weights frozen. Across all evaluation settings, we conducted 15,900 model-response runs. Across 600 pressure trajectories for the 4-billion-parameter model, the unsteered model caved in 570 cases. At the same time, gated steering helped it last longer in 551 of them. It held its ground under pressure at levels comparable to those of models with more than 100 billion parameters, showing that targeted inference-time steering can improve robustness without intervening at every turn.

</details>


### [67] [Automata from Agent Traces: Failure and Next-Step Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23670)
*Seonglae Cho,Franklin Cardenoso Fernandez,Umar Mohammed,Zekun Wu,Kleyton Da Costa,Ilham Wicaksono,Adriano Koshiyama*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They compress many LLM-agent execution traces into a small finite-state machine that captures shared behavioral structure, enabling better prediction and monitoring of next actions and failures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents perform complex multi-step behaviors, but their long, unstructured traces make it difficult to audit safety, understand behavior, and monitor them during deployment. Existing methods either treat each trace independently or only study successful runs, missing the common structural patterns that span multiple runs and are crucial for predicting next steps and failures.

Method: They take a corpus of agent execution traces and algorithmically collapse them into a single compact finite-state machine (FSM), where states represent distinct behavioral configurations and transitions represent observed step-to-step moves. They evaluate this on twelve public datasets, measuring FSM compactness, how well the FSM ‘replays’ unseen traces (fitness), and topology stability across data splits. They then use FSM state information as context for next-step prediction and derive per-state behavioral features to train failure-prediction models and an online monitor that can trigger early stopping from partial traces.

Result: Across all twelve datasets, the induced FSMs are very compact (7–43 states), achieve at least 0.997 fitness on held-out traces, and exhibit nearly identical topologies across different training splits while being fast to construct (milliseconds). Using the FSM state as context improves next-step prediction over Agent Workflow Memory on all datasets with ground-truth labels. For failure prediction, models built on per-state behavioral features achieve AUROC up to 0.94 on held-out data, and an online monitoring procedure can rank failing runs above successful ones from partial traces, enabling early stopping well before runs complete.

Conclusion: The behavior of LLM-based agents, when viewed across many runs, can be well-approximated by a small, stable finite-state machine whose structure largely reflects the surrounding deployment harness rather than the LLM internals. This FSM provides a model-agnostic structural primitive that supports improved safety auditing and runtime monitoring, particularly for predicting next actions and detecting or preempting failures early in execution.

Abstract: LLM-based agents execute multi-step tasks, but their behavioral structure remains opaque: long unstructured traces resist the safety auditing and runtime monitoring that deployment requires. Existing approaches operate per-trace or success-only, so they miss the cross-run topology that links next-step and failure prediction. To recover that shared structure, we collapse an entire trace corpus into a single, compact finite-state machine (FSM) that serves as a structural substrate for the otherwise unpredictable behavior of LLM agents. Across twelve public datasets, the FSMs are compact (7-43 states), replay held-out data at >=0.997 fitness with near-identical topology across splits, and build in milliseconds. This substrate addresses both prediction goals. For next-step prediction, FSM-state context outperforms Agent Workflow Memory on every ground-truth-matched dataset. For failure prediction, per-state behavioral features reach held-out AUROC up to 0.94, and an online monitor ranks failing runs above passing ones from a partial trace, triggering early stopping well before completion. Behavioral topology thus appears shaped more by the deployment harness than by the LLM, providing a model-agnostic structural primitive for safety auditing and runtime monitoring.

</details>


### [68] [Autonomous Mathematical Discovery in an Open-World Multi-Agent Environment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23691)
*Stephen Chung,Wenyu Du,William J. Wesley*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents an open-world, multi-agent AI system (“the Station”) that autonomously conducts mathematical research and discovers publishable new results without a fixed pipeline or central controller.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To move beyond scripted, single-model theorem proving and explore whether heterogeneous AI agents can autonomously generate genuinely new mathematical discoveries, including explanations and proofs, in an open-ended research environment.

Method: The authors build an open-world multi-agent environment in which AI agents from different model families share a global mathematical research goal but independently select subproblems, design and run experiments, collaborate via dialogue, and maintain a shared literature of partial results. They test this system on 12 benchmark construction problems from the AlphaEvolve catalogue plus two additional mathematical case studies, logging all agent interactions, generated proofs, and verification code.

Result: On 5 out of 12 AlphaEvolve problems plus the extra case studies, the system produced results that were novel relative to known mathematics, including: a new infinite family of finite-field Kakeya sets; new exact 604-point kissing configurations in 11 dimensions; improved records for the discretized Kakeya needle and sign uncertainty problems; a stronger lower bound for Erdős’s minimum-overlap problem; and new infinite families for Book Ramsey numbers. The agents produced not only constructions but also formal theorems and explanatory analyses.

Conclusion: Autonomous, heterogeneous AI-agent collectives can conduct open-ended mathematical research and obtain verifiably new, interpretable results, not just numeric optimizations. By releasing dialogues, proofs, and code, the work demonstrates a transparent pipeline for how such discoveries arise and suggests a scalable paradigm for AI-assisted mathematics that mathematicians can audit and extend.

Abstract: We study autonomous mathematical discovery in the Station, an open-world multi-agent environment in which AI agents from different model families pursue a shared research goal without a central coordinator or scripted pipeline. Agents choose their own research directions, conduct experiments, collaborate, and build a shared scientific literature. Across 12 construction problems from the AlphaEvolve catalogue and two additional case studies, the Station obtained results novel relative to the prior literature on five problems: a new infinite family of finite-field Kakeya sets, new exact 604-point kissing configurations in dimension 11, new records for the discretized Kakeya needle and sign uncertainty problems, and a substantially improved lower bound for Erdős's minimum-overlap problem. Agents also discovered novel infinite families for Book Ramsey numbers. Importantly, the agents produced not only numerical constructions but also theorems and analyses explaining how those constructions work, making the results more interpretable and easier for mathematicians to build upon. We release all raw agent dialogues, proofs, and verification code, providing a transparent record of how these discoveries emerged.

</details>


### [69] [Do LLMs Understand Limit Order Book Dynamics?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23706)
*Junxiao Chen,Paul Glasserman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper evaluates a large language model trained on synthetic limit order book (LOB) data, finding that although it can generate syntactically valid LOB event sequences, it fails to correctly represent the underlying LOB state, leading to biased forecasting and false predictability; the authors introduce new tests to probe the LLM’s internal world model in a stochastic setting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand whether high-performing LLMs that generate realistic-looking financial market event streams (LOB data) actually learn the underlying market state and dynamics, rather than just surface patterns. This is crucial because many applications—such as forecasting, simulation, and risk analysis—require a correct internal world model, not merely valid-looking sequences.

Method: Train a large language model on synthetic LOB event data and evaluate it along two dimensions: (1) the ability to generate syntactically and logically valid sequences of LOB events; and (2) the correctness of the model’s implicit representation of the underlying LOB state. The authors design and apply new tests of an LLM’s world model, extending earlier evaluation techniques (originally for deterministic environments) to the inherently stochastic behavior of LOB dynamics.

Result: The LLM attains near-perfect performance in generating formally valid LOB event sequences, indicating strong pattern-matching and syntax learning. However, when probed with the new world-model tests, the model is shown to fail at accurately tracking or inferring the true state of the LOB. This mis-specified internal state leads to biased parameter estimates and artificial predictability when the LLM is used for forecasting future LOB events.

Conclusion: High syntactic or sequence-level performance of an LLM on financial microstructure data does not guarantee that it has learned a faithful internal model of market state. The mismatch between surface performance and internal understanding can distort forecasts and infer spurious predictability. Robust testing of an LLM’s world model—especially in stochastic environments like LOBs—is necessary before relying on such models for forecasting or decision-making in financial markets.

Abstract: A large language model (LLM) trained on synthetic limit order book (LOB) data achieves near perfect scores in generating valid sequences of LOB events. However, the LLM's implicit world model fails to learn the state of the LOB. This deficiency leads to biased estimates and spurious predictability in using the LLM to forecast future LOB events. Our analysis uses novel tests of an LLM's world model, extending prior work from deterministic settings to the stochastic dynamics needed for the LOB.

</details>


### [70] [AgentRoom: Concurrent Multi-Agent Coding in a CRDT-Backed Shared Workspace](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23740)
*Seonglae Cho,Donghyun Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces AgentRoom, a realtime collaborative editing protocol that lets multiple coding agents work concurrently on multi-file software projects using CRDT-backed shared state and explicit coordination tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-agent coding systems are constrained by the token-by-token, single-stream nature of LLM generation, leading to either strictly sequenced phases or uncoordinated parallel sampling. This causes frequent task abandonment, especially on harder, multi-file problems where a single agent often produces only partial stubs. The authors want to recover the benefits humans get from realtime collaborative editing—division of labor, robustness, and parallel exploration—while overcoming the serial limitations of LLM backends.

Method: They design AgentRoom, a runtime protocol for coding agents built on a CRDT-merged shared filesystem. The protocol exposes MCP-style tools for agents to claim files, track and broadcast file status, and coordinate work at file granularity. They evaluate five frontier coding-CLI models on four backend coding tasks, including Python DevBench and a Rust+axum setting with cross-language checks, comparing configurations such as solo agents, 2-agent AgentRoom, and parallel-merge baselines under matched compute. They analyze abandonment rates, run-to-run variation, and LLM-judge-based quality contrasts, as well as ablations of parallelism, CRDT merge, and coordination features.

Result: For models that behave stably under a coding CLI, using AgentRoom with 2 agents leads to fewer abandoned tasks than a solo-agent baseline and reduces variance across runs. Under equal compute budgets, at least one positive LLM-judge contrast favors AgentRoom over a simple parallel-merge approach. A bundle probe that compares full AgentRoom to partial variants yields a clear ordering, indicating that the complete coordination mechanism outperforms any subset of its features.

Conclusion: Effective coordination mechanisms at the file level are crucial for improving multi-agent coding performance; mere parallelism or CRDT-based merging is not sufficient. AgentRoom’s explicit claim, status, and broadcast protocol enables LLM coding agents to collaborate more like human teams in realtime editors, reducing task abandonment and variability. The main performance gains arise from structured coordination rather than from concurrency or shared-state merging alone.

Abstract: Concurrent multi-agent coding promises division of labor across modules, robustness through redundancy, and parallel exploration at the natural granularity of multi-file projects. Realtime collaborative editing protocols solve this coordination problem for human teams via Conflict-free Replicated Data Types (CRDTs), but the LLMs underneath generate one token at a time and existing multi-agent coding systems inherit this serial limit: they either sequence agents through phase handoffs or pool independent samples without coordination, and a single agent abandons up to half of hard tasks with a one-file stub-and-exit. AgentRoom is a realtime collaborative editing protocol for concurrent coding agents. Its runtime layer exposes file-level claim, status, and broadcast as MCP tools on a CRDT-merged shared filesystem. Five frontier coding-CLI models ran four backend coding tasks, with cross-language checks in Python DevBench and Rust+axum. For CLI-stable models, AgentRoom with 2 agents abandons fewer tasks than Solo and has less run-to-run variation. At matched-compute, one positive mean LLM-judge contrast puts AgentRoom over parallel-merge. The other contrast, a bundle probe, puts full AgentRoom above each partial case: an ordering rather than a percentage split. Coordination, not parallelism or CRDT-merge, bears the load.

</details>


### [71] [Serving Masked Diffusion LLMs: Characterization and Design Principles from Real Hardware](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23807)
*Farhana Amin,Sabiha Afroz,Mona Moghadampanah,Dimitrios S. Nikolopoulos*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper empirically characterizes how masked diffusion language models behave under realistic serving loads and shows that their serving dynamics and bottlenecks differ substantially from autoregressive models, requiring step-level parallelism and careful batching/timeout design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion-based language models can theoretically generate text faster than autoregressive models by denoising multiple tokens at once, and infrastructure is starting to be built for serving them. However, there has been little empirical study of how these models actually behave under real, concurrent serving workloads. Without this characterization, system designs may inappropriately copy assumptions and optimizations from autoregressive serving, leading to inefficiencies or incorrect performance expectations. The paper aims to fill this gap by rigorously measuring and analyzing serving behavior for diffusion LLMs.

Method: The authors deploy a specific diffusion language model, LLaDA-8B-Instruct with a D2F (Discrete Diffusion Forcing) LoRA adapter, on a single NVIDIA H200 GPU. They evaluate it on GSM8K and HumanEval under concurrent serving scenarios. They analyze request difficulty in terms of required denoising steps, study latency and variance under different generation-length budgets, profile the time breakdown between GPU computation and CPU-side overhead, and measure the impact of batching on throughput. They also provide structural arguments and empirical checks about output quality versus batch size and derive a batch-timeout rule for fixed-fill synchronized batching under Poisson arrivals.

Result: They find that (1) request difficulty is discretized into 11 fixed denoising step levels (178 + 29k), with no reliable pre-generation predictor (best R^2 = 0.150); (2) benchmarks that cap generations below 320 tokens fail to capture the true latency variance because requests are truncated before the long-tail effects appear; (3) only about 24% of wall-clock time for a single request is spent on GPU computation, with the majority being CPU dispatch overhead, so batching mainly helps by amortizing this overhead. With shared forward passes per denoising step, throughput improves by 16x at batch size 16 compared to a per-request dispatch baseline. They observe 74–76% GSM8K accuracy at single-request scale and argue that batch size should not structurally harm quality given certain assumptions. They also derive a batch-timeout rule tailored to fixed-fill synchronized batching under Poisson arrivals.

Conclusion: Diffusion language models exhibit distinct serving characteristics from autoregressive models: request difficulty is discrete and hard to predict, latency variance is understated in short-budget benchmarks, and CPU dispatch overhead dominates single-request latency. Effective serving for these models requires parallelism at the granularity of each denoising step and batching strategies that amortize CPU overhead without sacrificing quality. System designs must therefore treat admission, eviction, and shared forward passes differently from autoregressive serving paradigms, using theory-guided batching and timeout rules adapted to diffusion-specific dynamics.

Abstract: Masked diffusion language models (dLLMs) can in principle generate text faster than autoregressive (AR) models, since they denoise many tokens at once. Recent systems have begun building serving infrastructure for dLLMs, but none first measure how these models behave under real, concurrent serving load. Serving systems built without this grounding risk carrying over assumptions from AR serving that may not hold for dLLMs. We characterize dLLM serving to close this gap, using LLaDA-8B-Instruct with a D2F (Discrete Diffusion Forcing) LoRA adapter on a single NVIDIA H200 GPU, evaluated on GSM8K and HumanEval. We report three findings. First, request difficulty, the number of denoising steps a request needs, is discrete rather than continuous: requests fall into 11 fixed step-count levels (178 + 29k), and no signal we test predicts the level before generation starts (best R2 = 0.150). Second, benchmarks with short generation budgets below 320 tokens understate serving variance, since requests are cut off before the latency spread appears. Third, only 24% of single-request wall-clock time is GPU computation; the rest is CPU-side dispatch overhead. Batching mainly helps by amortizing this overhead: sharing one forward pass per denoising step improves throughput by 16.0x at batch size 16 over a per-request-dispatch baseline. We also argue structurally that output quality should not degrade with batch size, stating three assumptions this rests on; we measure 74 to 76% GSM8K accuracy at single-request scale. Finally, we derive a batch-timeout rule for fixed-fill synchronized batching under Poisson arrivals. Together, these results show that serving diffusion language models needs parallelism at the level of each denoising step, which differs from AR serving in how admission and eviction interact with an already shared forward pass.

</details>


### [72] [Generating Biomedical Fact-Checking Reports with RL-Enhanced Agentic Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23811)
*Jiongxiao Wang,Dingli Ma,Chaoqun Ni*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose BioCheck Agent, an LLM-based system that performs biomedical fact-checking with agentic PubMed search and RL fine-tuning to produce structured, evidence-grounded reports instead of just labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing automated fact-checking systems in biomedicine mostly produce bare supported/refuted labels and struggle with the domain’s need for rigorous evidence interpretation, high-quality literature search, and detailed justification. LLM+RAG methods help but still lack explanatory depth, often hallucinate, and are especially unreliable for smaller open-source models. There is a need for a system that both improves factual accuracy and provides interpretable, evidence-grounded reasoning for biomedical claims.

Method: They design BioCheck Agent, an LLM-based agent that follows a retrieve-then-verify pipeline but explicitly searches PubMed using advanced Boolean operators and then composes structured fact-checking reports that combine claim assessment, retrieved evidence, and analysis. To improve its behavior beyond direct prompting, they introduce Evidence-Grounded Group Relative Policy Optimization (EG-GRPO), a reinforcement learning algorithm with a task-specific reward that encourages advanced search strategies, high-quality evidence retrieval, and grounded reasoning while penalizing hallucinations and poor reporting quality. The base LLM is Qwen3.5-4B, and EG-GRPO is used to fine-tune it in the agentic setting.

Result: On the SciFact benchmark, BioCheck Agent with EG-GRPO improves label prediction accuracy by 9.95% over the base Qwen3.5-4B model, increases an evidence quality metric by 3.7%, and reduces the evidence hallucination rate by 19.63%. These results indicate better factual classification and higher-quality, more faithful evidence usage in the generated reports.

Conclusion: BioCheck Agent demonstrates that combining domain-targeted agentic PubMed search with reinforcement learning via EG-GRPO leads to more accurate and trustworthy biomedical fact-checking. It moves beyond simple label prediction to produce structured, evidence-grounded reports, with measurable gains in accuracy, evidence quality, and reduced hallucinations, suggesting a promising direction for reliable automated verification of biomedical claims.

Abstract: Automated fact-checking is essential for ensuring the reliability of public health information, yet the biomedical domain poses unique challenges. Validating biomedical claims requires rigorous interpretation of scientific literature, assessment of retrieved evidence, and comprehensive justification toward the conclusion. Although Large Language Models (LLMs) enhanced by Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) and agentic search perform automated fact-checking in a retrieve-then-verify paradigm, current methods still output isolated prediction labels, lacking explanatory depth and offers limited utility for human understanding. To bridge this gap, we introduce an LLM-based agent named BioCheck Agent that generates structured biomedical fact-checking reports with agentic search. Rather than merely outputting supported or refuted labels, our agent synthesizes final conclusions with retrieved evidence and rigorous analysis. To ensure domain-specific accuracy, BioCheck Agent exclusively searches high-quality scientific literature in PubMed, utilizing advanced Boolean search operators. Recognizing that direct prompting often results in hallucinations and low-quality reports, especially for lightweight open-source models, we further propose the Evidence-Grounded Group Relative Policy Optimization (EG-GRPO) to perform reinforcement learning on BioCheck Agent with a task-specific reward that incentivizes advanced search behavior and high-quality evidence retrieval while penalizing hallucinations. Our experimental results show that compared to the base model Qwen3.5-4B, BioCheck Agent with EG-GRPO improves label prediction accuracy on SciFact by 9.95%. Furthermore, it achieves a 3.7% higher evidence quality score and a 19.63% lower evidence hallucination rate, demonstrating its ability to generate biomedical fact-checking reports with improved accuracy and quality.

</details>


### [73] [A Formal Methodological Framework for Auditing Robustness and Fidelity in Explainable AI: From Application to Trust Certification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23817)
*Rosa Elysabeth Ralinirina,Jean Christian Ralaivao,Niaiko Michaël Ralaivao,Alain Josué Ratovondrahona,Thomas Mahatody*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes an auditing protocol that quantitatively evaluates how trustworthy post-hoc explanation methods like SHAP and LIME are, by measuring robustness and fidelity and combining them into a Trust Score.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SHAP and LIME are widely used to interpret black-box machine learning models, but their explanations can change drastically with small perturbations to the input. The authors previously encountered this instability in a food security study in Madagascar, raising doubts about whether such explanations can be relied on, especially in high-stakes applications. They aim to systematically evaluate and quantify this trustworthiness problem.

Method: The authors define two quantitative properties for post-hoc explainers: robustness, which captures how stable explanations are under small input perturbations, and fidelity, which reflects how well the identified important features actually influence the model’s prediction. They design an auditing protocol to empirically estimate these measures for any explainer and aggregate them into a single Trust Score. They then apply this protocol to a real multi-sectoral dataset from Madagascar (83 features, 253 instances, 4 malnutrition classes), testing three classifiers and two explanation methods along with regularized variants.

Result: When applying their auditing framework, they find that even models with extremely high predictive performance (AUC > 0.99) can yield explanations that are numerically degenerate or lack useful information. Additionally, they observe that fidelity scores become less informative and less able to differentiate between explanation quality when the underlying model is overfitted, meaning high performance does not guarantee trustworthy explanations.

Conclusion: The study concludes that explanation methods like SHAP and LIME cannot be assumed trustworthy simply because the underlying predictive model performs well. Instability and low fidelity of explanations are real risks, especially under overfitting. Therefore, systematic auditing of XAI outputs—via measures like robustness, fidelity, and an aggregated Trust Score—is essential rather than optional, particularly when explanations support decision-making in sensitive domains such as food security and malnutrition analysis.

Abstract: SHAP and LIME are now standard tools for interpreting black-box predictions, yet their outputs can vary substantially when the input is perturbed by small amounts of noise--a problem we observed firsthand in our previous work on food security in Madagascar (Ralinirina et al., 2025). This variability raises the question of whether such explanations can be trusted at all. We address it by constructing an auditing protocol that measures two properties of any post-hoc explainer: robustness (how stable the explanation is under input perturbation) and fidelity (whether the features deemed important actually drive the model's prediction). These two quantities are combined into a single Trust Score. We run the protocol on a multi-sectoral dataset from Madagascar (83 features, 253 records, 4 malnutrition classes) using three classifiers and two explainers, plus their regularized counterparts. The results are sobering: models with AUC above 0.99 can produce numerically degenerate or flatly uninformative explanations, and fidelity scores lose discriminative power when the model is overfitted. These findings suggest that auditing XAI outputs is not optional but necessary, particularly when they inform decisions in sensitive domains.

</details>


### [74] [Minima-KV: Retention-Preserving KV Cache Compression with Mixed-Format Paged Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23834)
*Sergii Kozyrev,Davyd Maiboroda*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Minima-KV is a mixed-format hierarchical KV-cache system that compresses attention state for long-context LLMs while preserving request addressability and model quality, achieving ~3.5x compression over BF16 without dense shadows or cache eviction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: KV caches dominate memory capacity and bandwidth in serving long-context LLMs, limiting context length, batch size, and cost. Existing compression often requires dense shadow copies, evicts old tokens, or harms quality. The authors want a practical way to significantly compress active KV state while keeping all live-request tokens accessible and maintaining accuracy and throughput.

Method: They introduce Minima-KV, a retention-preserving hierarchical KV cache with mixed numerical formats and paged attention. KV pages are managed as "Anchor" and non-anchor pages: recent and protected Anchor pages stay in higher-precision FP8, while older non-anchor pages are compressed into a tightly packed 3-bit quantization format (TQ3). Format-specific attention kernels compute partial attention over each format, and an online-softmax mechanism merges these partial results into a single globally normalized attention output. This enables direct heterogeneous decoding across FP8 and TQ3 KV pages without maintaining a dense full-precision shadow cache. The system is evaluated on Qwen3.6-27B on a single 96‑GB RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell GPU under various deployment profiles and benchmark tasks.

Result: For Qwen3.6-27B, Minima-KV achieves 18.3 KiB of attention KV per live token, corresponding to 3.50x compression vs BF16 and 1.75x vs FP8, while keeping all KV pages directly addressable. On RULER needle-in-a-haystack with 16K context, a materializing profile matches dense control performance. On LongBench v2 with 503 questions, the accuracy drop is modest: -0.80, -0.60, and -0.40 percentage points at 16K, 32K, and 64K context lengths. A direct-decode stress test with two 59,008‑token requests achieves 3.625x active-KV compression and 0.9821x throughput relative to dense control, successfully routing all 16 full-attention layers without fallback and with no dense shadow cache.

Conclusion: Minima-KV demonstrates that a mixed-format, retention-preserving KV hierarchy can substantially compress long-context attention state without evicting live tokens or maintaining dense shadow caches. The approach enables practical 3–3.6x KV compression on modern GPUs with minimal quality loss and nearly unchanged throughput, suggesting a scalable path to more memory-efficient long-context LLM serving.

Abstract: The key-value (KV) cache is a primary capacity and bandwidth bottleneck in long-context LLM serving. We present Minima-KV, a retention-preserving hierarchy for mixed-format paged attention. Recent and protected Anchor pages remain in FP8, while older non-anchor pages move to packed TQ3; every live-request page remains addressable. Format-specific kernels compute partial attention states and combine them through a globally normalized online-softmax merge, enabling direct heterogeneous decode without a cache-sized dense shadow. Across separate, configuration-bound Qwen3.6-27B profiles on a single 96-GB NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell GPU, deployment accounting reports 18.3 KiB of attention KV per live token, corresponding to 3.50x compression relative to BF16 and 1.75x relative to FP8. A materializing quality profile matches its dense control on 16K RULER needle-in-a-haystack tasks. On the same 503-question LongBench v2 set, measured deltas are -0.80, -0.60, and -0.40 percentage points at 16K, 32K, and 64K. A separate single-pair direct-decode canary with two 59,008-token requests measures 3.625x active-KV compression and 0.9821x throughput relative to its control, routes all 16 full-attention layers without fallback, and retains no dense shadow. These results establish a practical mixed-format path for compressing long-context state without evicting live-request KV pages.

</details>


### [75] [SyPS: Measuring Sycophancy Prompt Sensitivity in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23837)
*Lijia Huang,Yao Fu,Sihao Ren*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a new framework, SyPS, to test how sensitive LLMs’ social sycophancy is to changes in prompt wording, and show that certain social cues systematically increase or decrease sycophantic behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing sycophancy evaluations use a single, fixed prompt style, so it is unclear whether measured sycophancy is a stable property of the model or an artifact of a particular phrasing or social framing. There is a need to understand how user confidence, emotional framing, and social-validation cues in prompts affect LLMs’ tendency to agree uncritically, especially in socially sensitive situations.

Method: They design SyPS (Sycophancy Prompt Sensitivity), an evaluation framework that constructs controlled pairs of prompts describing the same underlying user situation but varying specific social cues related to sycophancy, such as confidence, emotional pressure, social consensus signals, and validation-seeking language. They then define an instance-level metric, the Sycophancy Prompt Sensitivity Score (SPSS), which quantifies how much the model’s level of sycophancy changes across these paired variants, separating baseline sycophancy from prompt-induced changes. They apply this framework to existing social sycophancy evaluation setups to compare models’ robustness to such cues.

Result: Using SyPS, they find that models’ sycophancy is not uniform but systematically influenced by prompt style. Prompts that include validation-seeking language or emotional pressure tend to increase sycophantic responses, while prompts that contain counter-framing or explicit anti-sycophancy instructions tend to decrease such behavior. This demonstrates that sycophancy prompt sensitivity is structured and predictable, not random noise.

Conclusion: Sycophancy in LLMs depends strongly on how users phrase their requests and the social cues embedded in prompts. The proposed SyPS framework and SPSS metric allow researchers to distinguish between a model’s baseline tendency to be sycophantic and its susceptibility to prompt-induced shifts. This helps assess whether models can keep their substantive social judgments stable while merely adapting tone, and supports more robust evaluation and mitigation of sycophancy in deployed systems.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are known to exhibit social sycophancy, often validating or agreeing with users in socially sensitive contexts. Existing evaluations typically measure sycophancy under a fixed prompt formulation, leaving unclear whether such behavior is stable when the same underlying situation is presented with different sycophancy-relevant prompt variants. In this work, we study sycophancy prompt sensitivity: the extent to which changes in user confidence, emotional framing, social consensus, or validation-seeking language alter a model's sycophantic behavior. We refer to our evaluation framework as SyPS, short for Sycophancy Prompt Sensitivity. Building on existing social sycophancy evaluation settings, SyPS constructs controlled prompt variants that preserve the same underlying user situation while varying sycophancy-relevant social cues. We introduce the Sycophancy Prompt Sensitivity Score (SPSS), an instance-level measure of sycophancy variation across paired prompt variants. Unlike aggregate sycophancy rates, SPSS separates baseline sycophancy from prompt-induced shifts, enabling model-level comparisons of robustness to sycophancy-relevant social cues. Empirically, we find that sycophancy prompt sensitivity is socially structured: validation-seeking and emotional-pressure cues often increase sycophancy, whereas counter-framing and anti-sycophancy prompts tend to reduce it. Our framework highlights whether LLMs maintain stable social judgments while adapting appropriately in tone.

</details>


### [76] [Exploit More, Explore Smarter for Budget-Constrained Agentic Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23848)
*Haoyang Fang,Bernie Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes ExTS, a tree-search policy for LLM-based agents under strict evaluation budgets, improving budget allocation and search efficiency over standard MCTS and task-specific baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) performs poorly when evaluation budgets are very limited, as in many agentic LLM applications where each candidate requires expensive validation or multiple model calls. In such low-budget regimes, exploration bonuses dominate and the algorithm wastes budget expanding weak branches instead of deepening promising ones. The authors aim to design a search strategy that explicitly accounts for the cost of expanding the tree and the value of information gained, to make tree search practical and effective for budget-constrained agentic tasks.

Method: The authors introduce ExTS, a tree-search policy that reframes node expansion as a value-of-information decision. It integrates three key mechanisms: (1) discriminative reward shaping to amplify differences among candidate scores when distributions are narrow, enabling clearer preference between options; (2) a stochastic virtual child that models the potential value of spawning a new branch based on the parent node’s reward history, effectively estimating the opportunity value of exploration without fully committing resources; and (3) quality-conditioned branching, which only expands a node if its score surpasses a threshold that justifies the cost of using additional evaluation budget. Together, these mechanisms allocate search budget adaptively according to node quality and historical performance instead of applying a fixed exploration schedule as in standard MCTS.

Result: Across four domains—prompt optimization, code generation, molecular structure elucidation, and agentic workflow optimization—ExTS matches or outperforms strong, task-specific tree-search baselines. Using a single fixed configuration across tasks, it achieves an average relative performance gain of approximately +5.5%, indicating both robustness and generality. The paper also presents “pilot-run diagnostics” that probe structural properties of budget-constrained agentic search problems, helping to explain when and why ExTS is effective and how problem characteristics differ between domains.

Conclusion: ExTS offers a principled way to manage evaluation budget in agentic tree search by treating expansion as an information-gathering decision and conditioning branching on estimated quality. This leads to more efficient use of limited evaluations than standard MCTS, with consistent gains across varied LLM-centric and scientific tasks without per-task retuning. The pilot-run diagnostics further contribute a practical toolkit for understanding and adapting to the structure of budget-constrained search problems, enhancing both theoretical insight and applied performance.

Abstract: Budget-constrained agentic search arises when an LLM agent must refine candidates under a small evaluation budget, because validation is expensive, generation requires multiple model calls, or both. In this regime, standard MCTS allocates budget poorly: exploration bonuses dominate at low visit counts, unpromising siblings are expanded before promising chains can deepen, and branching is independent of node quality. We introduce ExTS, a tree-search policy that treats expansion itself as a value-of-information decision. ExTS combines three mechanisms: discriminative reward shaping to separate candidates under narrow score distributions, a stochastic virtual child that estimates the value of creating a new branch from the parent's reward history, and quality-conditioned branching that expands only when a node's score justifies the budget cost. Across prompt optimization, code generation, molecular structure elucidation, and agentic workflow optimization, ExTS is competitive with or improves over task-specific tree-search baselines, with an average relative gain of +5.5% using a single fixed configuration. We further introduce pilot-run diagnostics that characterize what makes budget-constrained agentic search problems structurally different from one another, providing both understanding of the problem space and practical guidance for adaptation.

</details>


### [77] [In-Context Inpainting for Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23855)
*Thang Nguyen,Dung Nguyen,Romero Morais,Truyen Tran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces ICI-Time, a framework that converts time series forecasting into a visual inpainting problem solvable by pre-trained large vision models without fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional time series forecasting often relies on specialised temporal architectures and substantial domain-specific training data, which can limit generalisation and adaptability, especially in low-data settings. There is a need to leverage powerful, pre-trained models in adjacent domains to improve time series forecasting performance and flexibility.

Method: ICI-Time maps numerical time series into structured visual representations (area charts) arranged in a grid-structured prompt. Temporal dependencies are encoded spatially, using a consistent and invertible mapping between numeric and visual domains. The forecasting task is reframed as visual pattern completion (inpainting), enabling the use of pre-trained vision transformers via visual in-context learning, without additional fine-tuning or architectural modifications.

Result: Across multiple domains—epidemiology, meteorology, and power systems—ICI-Time achieves performance competitive with strong deep learning time series baselines. It demonstrates notable robustness and adaptability, particularly in limited-data scenarios, showing that large vision models can effectively handle temporal forecasting when recast as a visual task.

Conclusion: Recasting time series forecasting as a visual inpainting problem allows pre-trained large vision models to be used effectively without task-specific retraining. ICI-Time provides a new paradigm that bridges temporal and visual domains, suggesting that cross-modal reformulations can unlock the generalisation strengths of powerful models for time series forecasting and related tasks.

Abstract: We propose ICI-Time, a novel framework that reframes time series forecasting as a visual inpainting task, leveraging the generalisation power of large vision models (LVMs).
