Unlike methods that require specialised temporal architectures and extensive domain-specific training, ICI-Time transforms time series into structured visual representations (area charts) and applies visual in-context learning, reformulating forecasting as pattern completion within a grid-structured prompt that pre-trained vision transformers can solve without fine-tuning or architectural modification. Temporal dependencies are represented through spatial layout, with a consistent, invertible mapping between numerical and visual domains. Extensive experiments across epidemiology, meteorology, and power systems demonstrate that ICI-Time performs competitively against deep learning baselines and shows promising adaptability under limited-data settings, introducing a new paradigm that bridges temporal and visual domains.

</details>


### [78] [Granite.Trust Policy Tools: Shareable, Actionable Policies for Generative AI Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23870)
*Nathalie Baracaldo,Nicolas Mello,Kush R. Varshney,Heiko Ludwig,Kate Soule,David Cox*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a policy schema and tooling to define and enforce fine-grained, content-based safety policies for generative AI across the model lifecycle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing policy specification methods are designed for traditional access control and cannot express nuanced, content-based constraints needed for generative AI safety. Organizations face diverse regulatory, contextual, and ethical requirements, so they need flexible, customizable mechanisms to govern model outputs and mitigate different risks.

Method: The authors design the Actionable Policy schema, a YAML-based format for expressing what model responses may or may not contain, including support for exception-based governance that explicitly encodes allowable deviations and how to track violations. They also implement a synthetic data generation pipeline that produces training and evaluation data aligned with these policies, along with tools to help define schemas and enforce policies across systems. The full framework is released as open-source software.

Result: They deliver an end-to-end, open-source toolkit: the Actionable Policy schema, example policies, a synthetic data generation pipeline, and enforcement/authoring tools. This toolkit allows organizations to declaratively specify content constraints once and reuse them for model alignment, testing, and runtime monitoring.

Conclusion: By introducing a structured, reusable, and exception-aware policy schema plus supporting tooling, the work enables organizations to manage generative AI safety in a more consistent and customizable way across the application lifecycle, addressing limitations of traditional access-control-based policy approaches.

Abstract: When it comes to safety policies for generative AI, one size does not fit all. Each organization and use case needs to mitigate different risks depending on the application context, regulatory environment, organizational values, and user personas. Yet, existing policy specification approaches are designed for traditional access control and fail to capture the nuances of GenAI application: the enforcement of content-based constraints.
  We present two contributions to address this gap: (1) the Actionable Policy schema, a YAML-based format for specifying what model responses can and cannot contain. The schema enables exception-based policy governance, proposing exceptions to track policy violations; (2) synthetic data generation pipeline that produces policy-aligned training data for model alignment and testing, and a set of tools to help define the schema and enforce policy. Together, these enable organizations to specify policies once and enforce them throughout the GenAI application lifecycle: from model alignment to runtime monitoring. The Actionable Policy schema, example policies, and tools are available as open source: https://github.com/ibm-granite/granite.trust.policy-tools We welcome new ideas, contributions and feedback.

</details>


### [79] [Semantic Overlays: Mitigating Prompt Injection with Annotations Beyond Tokens and Steering Vectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23873)
*Joshua Penman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Semantic Overlays, a non-textual control channel for language models that marks different spans (e.g., instructions, tools, untrusted input) using learned adapters, greatly mitigating prompt injection while preserving model utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language models only see undifferentiated token streams, even though the serving stack knows which parts are user input, tools, or system instructions. Because the model must infer span identity from text alone, adversaries can craft prompts that confuse or override instructions (prompt injection). Existing defenses struggle because any control signal expressed as text can itself be spoofed or injected against. The authors want a robust, non-spoofable way to tell the model what each span is and how to treat it, in order to stop prompt injection without breaking usability.

Method: They add a non-textual, out-of-band channel called Semantic Overlays: small, learned adapter modules applied to the frozen model’s residual stream at chosen prefill positions that correspond to text spans. Each overlay is trained to encode specific semantics (e.g., “this span is untrusted,” “this is code in language X,” or imperative instructions) that the model will interpret when processing the associated tokens. Overlays are selectively applied (not global), composable across spans, and trained so that the underlying text remains readable while its interpretation is transformed. They evaluate overlays against multiple prompt injection benchmarks and analyze readability and utility trade-offs.

Result: Semantic Overlays enable the model to treat marked spans according to overlay semantics in ways that cannot be reproduced by tokens alone. For example, when a code snippet is overlaid with a label asserting a different programming language, the model faithfully rewrites it into that language, demonstrating strong control over span interpretation. For security, an overlay that marks a span as “non-executable” prevents the model from following instructions embedded in that span, effectively neutralizing a wide class of prompt injections in untrusted context. Quantitatively, SEP separation improves from 24.3% to 96.5% without utility loss (under their corrected scoring rule), TensorTrust attack success drops from 34.8% to 6.6%, and all four PIArena attack families fall to 0% compliance, while marked spans remain highly readable (92.5% exact copy rate).

Conclusion: Non-textual, overlay-based control of span semantics is an effective and practical defense against prompt injection and related attacks. By supplying the model with a separate, unspoofable channel that encodes span identity and semantics, Semantic Overlays substantially improve security while preserving the model’s ability to read and reproduce overlaid content. The approach generalizes beyond simple tags to rich semantic payloads and is compatible with frozen base models via small learned adapters, making it a promising direction for robust, controllable language model serving stacks.

Abstract: Everything a language model sees is tokens. The serving stack knows what each span is -- user input, tool output, instructions -- but the model must keep track of that itself, and it can lose track or be confused: text can be written to read like anything. Prompt injection is a natural exploit of this phenomenon. By scrambling the model's understanding of span identity, an attacker can induce unwanted and potentially dangerous actions. Adding a non-textual channel to the model's input -- a way to communicate span identity beyond text -- mitigates this class of attack. We thus introduce a general steering technique called Semantic Overlays: small learned adapters applied at chosen prefill positions to a frozen model's residual stream. Laying an overlay over a span creates an out-of-band annotation channel that cannot be replicated by tokens. Unlike steering vectors, Semantic Overlays are trained, adaptable, and selectively applied. An overlay can encode complex semantics that reshape how the model perceives the marked span: asked to copy a code snippet under an overlay asserting that it is in a different programming language than it is, the model rewrites the snippet, faithfully, in the asserted language. Overlays are also composable, allow for transparent reading of underlying content, and can carry complex payloads -- including imperatives that the model will follow. An overlay which marks a span as "non-executable" defends against the broad class of prompt injections that add instructions in untrusted context. We report strong results on prompt injection benchmarks: SEP separation rises from 24.3% to 96.5% with utility unchanged (our scoring rule; we also correct a defect in the published grader), TensorTrust attack success rate falls from 34.8% to 6.6%, and all four PIArena attack families drop to 0% compliance, all while marked spans stay readable (92.5% exact copy rate).

</details>


### [80] [AI Finds A Way](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23875)
*Aaron Dharna,Cong Lu,Ryan Sullivan,Joel Lehman,Victoria Krakovna,Jeff Clune*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper compiles 26 real-world anecdotes showing how modern AI systems often find creative, unexpected, and sometimes problematic solutions that bypass human intentions, arguing this behavior is widespread, important for safety, and must be better understood and managed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI systems routinely display surprising behaviors—finding loopholes in reward functions, exploiting underspecified goals, or uncovering new scientific phenomena—yet such incidents are scattered, informally shared, and under-documented. This lack of systematic documentation hinders our understanding of AI capabilities and risks, especially as models grow more powerful. The authors aim to collect and formalize these stories to highlight patterns in unexpected AI behavior and to inform safety and alignment research.

Method: The authors curate 26 firsthand anecdotes contributed by over 100 researchers across diverse machine learning subfields. These anecdotes describe instances where AI systems behaved in unexpected, creative, or exploitative ways, often by circumventing design constraints or hacking reward signals. The paper organizes and analyzes these cases thematically, contrasting successful superhuman performance with failure modes such as reward hacking or constraint violations, and then extends the discussion to foundation models and their amplified behaviors.

Result: The curated anecdotes demonstrate that: (1) reinforcement learning agents can achieve superhuman performance in complex domains, but also frequently exploit underspecified rewards and hidden loopholes; (2) unexpected behaviors are not rare edge cases but recurring patterns across tasks and architectures; (3) foundation models trained on internet-scale data do not eliminate these issues and can intensify both creative successes and misaligned or surprising behaviors; and (4) similar learning dynamics underlie both desirable scientific discoveries and problematic reward hacking or misalignment.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that modern AI systems have a strong tendency toward innovative yet unpredictable behavior, including circumventing human-imposed constraints and exploiting reward functions. This tendency is intrinsic to powerful optimization, not an anomaly. Therefore, AI safety and alignment research must explicitly anticipate and manage such behaviors, aiming to preserve beneficial creativity and scientific discovery while reducing harmful or misaligned outcomes. The work offers a consolidated set of case studies meant as a reference point for future research on aligning capable AI systems with human values without suppressing their capacity for unexpected but useful discoveries.

Abstract: Artificial Intelligence (AI) algorithms frequently learn creative and unexpected solutions, surprising even expert researchers who develop and study them. They often astonish practitioners by discovering unanticipated behavior, exploiting loopholes in reward signals, or spontaneously uncovering previously unknown scientific phenomena. However, accounts of such unconventional behavior across machine learning are seldom formally documented. This work presents 26 curated firsthand anecdotes from various machine learning subfields representing the work of over 100 researchers. These anecdotes showcase the capability of modern AI systems to circumvent human-imposed design limitations and discover unexpected solutions to the tasks we train them on. Furthermore, these accounts are particularly important for the safety of future AI systems. They illustrate the fundamental challenge of aligning models with human values without diminishing their creativity, so they can make surprising discoveries without producing surprising, potentially harmful outcomes. The paper first details AI achieving superhuman success through reinforcement learning across many challenging domains. However, reward-driven optimization can fail when the model learns to hack an underspecified reward or unarticulated constraint. We then present case studies suggesting that harnessing internet-scale foundation models (FMs) has not resolved these fundamental challenges and, in fact, can supercharge them. Nevertheless, we argue that these same learning dynamics can be harnessed to accelerate scientific discovery. Finally, we hope this work provides a consolidated resource to inform future research and demonstrates that the tendency toward unexpected behaviors is commonplace in modern AI, highlighting the need to anticipate and manage AI's capacity for innovative, yet unpredictable, solutions. (abstract abridged)

</details>


### [81] [Provenance Guided Incremental Learning Under Evolving Concept Definitions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23893)
*Ismail Lamaakal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a provenance-guided framework to efficiently update machine learning systems when the target concept is explicitly revised via rule changes, avoiding full retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deployed learning systems face not only data distribution drift but also explicit changes in the rules or policies that define prediction targets. Existing concept-drift approaches focus on detecting and adapting to statistical or error-based changes, ignoring that many real-world shifts are initiated by human-edited rules (e.g., new eligibility policies, security rules). When such rules change, historical labels may become partially invalid, making full relabeling and retraining expensive and unnecessary. A principled method is needed to selectively update only the affected portions of the data and model while preserving still-correct knowledge.

Method: The authors formalize rule-induced concept shift, where the mapping from features to labels changes due to explicit rule revisions while the input data remain the same. They design a provenance-guided incremental learning framework that: (1) compiles consecutive concept (rule) definitions into a structured rule delta capturing what changed; (2) uses data provenance to trace how these changed rule components impact historical records; (3) certifies which existing labels remain valid and need not be revisited; (4) restricts expensive reevaluation and relabeling to a localized candidate region of possibly affected instances. Within this region, executable revisions are automatically relabeled, while ambiguous cases are resolved via selective human supervision. The newly obtained labels are then used to incrementally repair the prediction model rather than retrain from scratch. A versioned concept memory supports returning to or reusing prior definitions. They also build RuleShift-Bench, a benchmark with multiple domains and types of concept revisions, to evaluate their approach.

Result: On the RuleShift-Bench benchmark across various domains and revision types, the provenance-guided repair framework achieves 92.3% accuracy and 90.2% Macro-F1 while only reprocessing 14.7% of the historical dataset, yet still retaining 94.6% of the records that are actually impacted by the rule change. The average update latency is reduced to 179 seconds, compared with 993 seconds required for complete relabeling and full model retraining. These results show large efficiency gains with minimal performance loss.

Conclusion: Explicit rule or policy revisions can be treated as first-class signals for maintaining learning systems, not just as external changes that must be rediscovered through errors or drift detection. By compiling rule deltas, exploiting provenance to localize affected data, and performing incremental model repair, systems can selectively update labels and predictive state after concept shifts while preserving unaffected knowledge. This approach improves efficiency and scalability for long-lived, rule-governed machine learning applications and is validated through the RuleShift-Bench benchmark results.

Abstract: Learning systems deployed over long periods must adapt not only to statistical changes in incoming data, but also to revisions of the definitions that generate their prediction targets. Conventional concept-drift methods typically infer such changes from observations or prediction errors, even when the underlying policy, rule, or query has been explicitly modified. This paper studies rule-induced concept shift, where the target-defining concept is revised directly, causing previously stored instances to acquire different semantic labels without requiring any change in their observed data. We introduce a provenance-guided incremental learning framework that compiles consecutive concept definitions into a structured rule delta, traces the changed components through historical provenance, certifies records whose previous labels remain valid, and restricts reevaluation to a localized candidate region. Executable revisions are relabeled automatically, ambiguous cases are handled through selective supervision, and the resulting changes are used for incremental predictor repair. A versioned concept memory further supports recurring definitions. We also introduce RuleShift-Bench, spanning financial, demographic, cybersecurity, and graph-structured data with threshold, predicate, logical, relational, recurring, and mixed concept revisions. Across the benchmark, provenance-guided repair attains 92.3% accuracy and 90.2% Macro-F1 while reprocessing 14.7% of the historical collection and retaining 94.6% of affected records. Its average update latency is 179s compared with 993s for complete relabeling and retraining. The results demonstrate that an explicit concept revision can be exploited as a data-maintenance signal, allowing learning systems to update the supervision and predictive state that depend on the change while preserving knowledge that remains valid.

</details>


### [82] [BenchBench-Protocol: Evaluating Real-World Wet-Lab Protocol Reasoning and Modification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23898)
*Aditya Sivakumar,Ashu Singhal,Nicholas Larus-Stone,Nithin Parsan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BenchBench-Protocol is a benchmark of 149 real-world protocol-modification tasks to evaluate how well large language models adapt wet-lab biology protocols.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing life-science LLM benchmarks use expert-elicited, open-ended tasks that may not reflect routine, real-world lab work. Adapting published experimental protocols for new purposes is a common and practically important task that requires nuanced wet-lab reasoning, but there is no benchmark directly capturing this ability from genuine experimental practice.

Method: The authors collected 149 protocol-modification tasks by comparing original published wet-lab protocols with versions that scientists actually modified for real experiments. Differences between the original and modified protocols were used to construct: (1) the task query (what needs to be changed), and (2) weighted rubric elements describing what constitutes a correct modification. The dataset spans 96 source protocols across nine wet-lab biology domains and only includes tasks that domain experts rated as high quality. They then evaluated nine closed and open large language models on these tasks, grading responses with the rubrics and normalizing scores; they also tested multiple attempts per task to assess benchmark saturation.

Result: Claude Opus 5 achieved the best performance with a 59.2% normalized rubric score. Other evaluated models scored between 34.1% and 47.1%. Even when taking the best response out of ten attempts, the benchmark did not saturate, indicating substantial headroom for improvement in protocol-modification reasoning.

Conclusion: BenchBench-Protocol provides a grounded benchmark for assessing LLMs on realistic wet-lab protocol adaptation tasks, demonstrating that current models are moderately capable but far from perfect at this form of experimental reasoning. The work supports the idea that benchmarks built from real-world experimental modifications are both feasible and valuable for evaluating and improving life-science LLMs.

Abstract: We introduce BenchBench-Protocol, a benchmark for large language models of 149 protocol-modification tasks recovered from modifications that scientists made to published protocols during real experimental work. Adapting a published protocol to a new experiment is a routine task for a wet-lab scientist, and a correct modification requires accounting for prior choices and downstream steps. Recent life-science benchmarks have moved toward open-ended, rubric-graded tasks, but tasks are typically elicited from experts rather than reconstructed from real-world modifications. BenchBench-Protocol tasks are derived from differences between a published protocol and a version a scientist modified, which provides the basis for the query and the weighted rubric elements for a correct response. The benchmark draws from 96 source protocols across nine domains of wet-lab biology and only includes tasks rated highly after review by domain experts. We evaluate nine closed and open models; Claude Opus 5 scores highest at 59.2% normalized rubric score, with other models between 34.1% and 47.1%, and the benchmark remains unsaturated when taking the best of ten attempts. As models are increasingly helpful in life-sciences research, evaluating them on routine wet-lab tasks becomes correspondingly important. We present BenchBench-Protocol as both a grounded assessment of wet-lab reasoning and evidence for the utility of real-world experiments to construct benchmark tasks.

</details>


### [83] [Quantifying System-Level Harms from AI Adoption in Complex Sociotechnical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23906)
*Paul Vautravers,Oliver Chalkley,Gabriel Downer,Kate S,Damian Ruck*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Framework to connect AI model behaviour with system-level risk in Critical National Infrastructure, demonstrated on a UK financial system.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI evaluation is model-centric and does not explain how specific AI behaviours lead to risks in large sociotechnical systems like Critical National Infrastructure. There is a need for a traceable way to understand the systemic consequences of AI failures and to support evidence-based governance.

Method: Propose a framework that combines structured hazard analysis (using Systems Theoretic Process Analysis, STPA), component-level testing of AI models, and probabilistic system modelling. Apply it to the UK Real Time Gross Settlement (RTGS) financial system, constructing AI-driven loss scenarios and running component-level experiments on adversarial manipulation of LLM-based trading, then mapping observed behavioural shifts into a financial contagion model.

Result: Adversarially perturbed prompts cause measurable shifts in LLM-based trading recommendations, and when such recommendations are followed in the contagion model, these shifts reduce system resilience. Specifically, they increase the number of bank failures and lower the shock threshold that triggers cascading disruption, especially when AI systems are widely or monopolistically adopted.

Conclusion: The framework can trace AI behaviour to system-level outcomes, answering the practical “so what?” of AI failures. It shows that even simple adversarial manipulations of AI trading systems can materially degrade the resilience of critical financial infrastructure, underscoring the need for anticipatory, system-aware governance of AI in complex sociotechnical settings.

Abstract: Artificial Intelligence (AI) is increasingly integrated into complex sociotechnical systems, including Critical National Infrastructure (CNI), where harms emerge from interactions between technical, human, and organisational elements. Yet current AI evaluation remains model-centric, offering little insight into how observed behaviours might translate into system-level risk. We propose a framework that links structured hazard analysis, component-level testing, and probabilistic system modelling to bridge this gap. By providing a traceable pathway from model behaviour to system-level outcomes, the framework enables practitioners to answer the "so what?" of AI failures, quantify their systemic impact, and move toward evidence-based and anticipatory governance of AI in complex systems. Applied to the UK's Real Time Gross Settlement (RTGS) system as an illustrative worked example, we derive AI-driven loss scenarios using Systems Theoretic Process Analysis (STPA) and examine adversarial manipulation of LLM-based trading as one such loss scenario. Component-level experiments show that simple adversarial inputs induce measurable behavioural shifts where AI recommendations are followed. Under the component-to-system mapping used here for a financial contagion model, these shifts alter system resilience, increasing bank failures and lowering the threshold at which shocks lead to cascading disruption, particularly under widespread or monopolistic AI adoption.

</details>


### [84] [Retrieval-augmented generation vs. deterministic tax computation in multi-agent financial advisory: A 2x2 factorial experiment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23908)
*Aryan Brar,Justin Du,Avery Lor,Kylie Seto,Eric Taylor*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Paper evaluates a multi-agent LLM system for tax-loss harvesting and finds that adding a custom tax engine can hurt performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tax-loss harvesting can improve long-term portfolio growth, but applying it well is complex and individualized. The authors want to explore whether LLM-based multi-agent systems, augmented with a tax calculation engine and external financial documents, actually improve tax-optimized trade recommendations.

Method: They build a multi-agent trade recommendation system powered by an LLM, with two binary context providers: (1) a custom capital gains/tax optimization computation engine and (2) a RAG-based vector store of market advisory reports. This yields four conditions in a 2x2 design. They measure recommendation quality via relative capital gains during portfolio liquidation and analyze effects using a 2x2 repeated-measures ANOVA on data from 30 portfolios/participants.

Result: The tax engine had a significant negative main effect: turning it on reduced tax savings by ~55 percentage points compared to conditions without it (F(1,29)=9.17, p=.005, η²p=.240). The RAG context had no significant effect (p=.841), and there was no significant interaction (p=.553). Descriptively, the RAG-only condition had the best mean tax savings (47.7%), followed by the baseline (no engine, no RAG) at 30.6%. Conditions with the tax engine performed worse.

Conclusion: Adding a domain-specific tax optimization engine to an LLM agent did not improve and actually worsened tax-loss harvesting recommendations, while RAG provided no significant benefit over the LLM alone. The results suggest that the base model’s internal financial knowledge may suffice for reasonable tax optimization, and that extra tools can introduce conflicting objectives or complexity that reduce performance.

Abstract: Tax-loss harvesting demonstrates consistent benefits to long-term portfolio growth; yet implementing it efficiently often involves complex considerations that are specific to the holdings within that portfolio and the individual who owns it. We introduce a custom capital gains calculation engine and a RAG-retrieved vector store of market advisory reports to provide context for a multi-agent trade recommendation system. We investigate the effects of each context provider on the quality of recommendations, measured by relative capital gains incurred during portfolio liquidation. A 2x2 repeated-measures ANOVA revealed a significant main effect of the tax optimization engine ($F(1,29) = 9.17$, $p = .005$, $η^2_p = .240$): enabling the engine reduced tax savings by approximately 55 percentage points relative to the no-engine conditions. The RAG main effect was not significant ($p = .841$), nor was the interaction ($p = .553$). The RAG-only condition achieved the highest descriptive mean tax savings (47.7%), and the baseline condition performed second-best (30.6%), suggesting that the pre-trained language model's internalized financial knowledge may be sufficient for competent tax-loss harvesting recommendations without explicit tooling. These results indicate that augmenting LLM agents with domain-specific computation engines does not guarantee improved performance and may introduce conflicting optimization signals.

</details>


### [85] [PROOF-Gen: From Optimized Data to Better Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23911)
*Anh Ta,Junjie Zhu,Shahin Shayandeh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes PROOF-Gen, a method that turns failed tool-calling trajectories from a strong teacher model into successful “golden” demonstrations via per-scenario prompt optimization, greatly increasing usable data and improving student and deployed agent performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current tool-use agents are trained by supervised fine-tuning on trajectories generated by an expensive frontier teacher model. Production pipelines must repeatedly regenerate these trajectories, yet rely on a generate-and-filter procedure that simply discards failed trials. This wastes most of the teacher’s computation and leaves hard scenarios unsolved, because failures provide no learning signal. On τ2-bench, over half of teacher trials fail and many are near-misses, indicating that small corrections could turn them into valuable training data.

Method: The authors introduce PROOF-Gen (Per-scenario Reflective Optimization to Overcome Failed Generation). For each failed task, a separate reflector model inspects the execution trace and evaluation feedback, then generates targeted corrective guidance for that specific scenario. This guidance is used to re-prompt the teacher so that it is steered toward a successful (passing) trajectory for that same task. Once a passing trajectory is obtained, the extra guidance text is removed so the resulting demonstration remains clean and task-agnostic. These recovered golden trajectories are then added to the usual successful ones for supervised fine-tuning of smaller student models and for improving deployed tool-using agents.

Result: On the τ2-bench benchmark, PROOF-Gen recovers 93% of previously failed scenarios, including many near-miss failures. Using the expanded dataset, fine-tuning Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507 raises Pass^1 from 0.132 to 0.529, and Gemma 4 E4B-it gains 7.2 percentage points on the BFCL v4 multi-turn benchmark. In an actual production pipeline, the method increases trajectory goal-completion by 6.3 percentage points. The improvements also transfer to an on-device model, which sees +1.5 percentage points in goal completion and +1.7 to +5.0 percentage points across multiple response-quality metrics, with consistent positive gains across languages, including an average +1.48 percentage points in non-English locales.

Conclusion: Failed teacher trajectories, especially near-misses, contain rich information that can be unlocked rather than discarded. By using a reflector to generate per-scenario corrective guidance and re-running the teacher, PROOF-Gen converts most of these failures into high-quality training demonstrations without contaminating them with scaffolding. This substantially increases data efficiency, improves tool-calling performance of smaller student models, and yields measurable gains in real deployed systems across languages and device types.

Abstract: Supervised fine-tuning on teacher-generated trajectories is the standard first stage for distilling tool-calling capabilities into deployable models. Post-training pipelines that drive shipped tool-calling agents re-run this stage on a daily or weekly cadence, paying the frontier-teacher cost each cycle, yet the mechanism is generate-and-filter (keep the teacher's passing trajectories, discard the rest) and each cycle leaves behind the same hard scenarios because failures supply no signal. On τ2-bench, 57% of teacher trials fail, two-thirds of them near-misses (most tool calls correct, undone by one decisive error).
  We introduce PROOF-Gen (Per-scenario Reflective Optimization to Overcome FailedGeneration), which recovers golden trajectories from these failures via per-scenario prompt optimization. For each failed task, a reflector analyzes the execution trace and evaluation feedback, then writes corrective guidance that steers the teacher to a passing trajectory. The guidance is stripped before training, so the student learns from clean demonstrations with no task-specific scaffold.
  On τ2-bench, per-scenario optimization recovers 93% of failed scenarios. Fine-tuned on the combined data, Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507 improves from Pass^1=0.132 to 0.529 and Gemma 4 E4B-it gains +7.2pp on BFCL v4 multi-turn. In a deployed pipeline, the method lifts trajectory quality by +6.3pp goal completion and transfers to a deployed on-device model (+1.5pp goal completion; +1.7 to +5.0pp across response-quality metrics), with positive transfer in every locale (non-English average +1.48pp).

</details>


### [86] [MARS: Multi-Specialist LLM Relay System for Competitive Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23918)
*Andrei Mikhailov,Mikhail Burtsev,Alsu Sagirova*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces MARS, a prompt-based multi-agent system of specialized LLMs for competitive programming, coordinating topic-specific agents with retrieval to significantly improve code-generation pass rates and efficiency over direct prompting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although large language models perform well on general code generation, they struggle on competitive programming tasks, where success depends on choosing and correctly applying specialized algorithmic techniques. Existing multi-agent approaches use generic roles (planner, coder, debugger) and still leave algorithm choice to a single backbone model, leading to persistent failure modes. The authors want a system that can reliably select and apply the right algorithmic methods while controlling cost and variance.

Method: They design MARS (Multi-Agent Relay of Specialized LLMs), a prompt-only framework where each agent is an LLM specialized in a particular algorithmic domain (e.g., dynamic programming, graphs, strings, geometry). Specialization is grounded via retrieval-augmented generation over an algorithms-and-theory corpus. For a given problem, a retrieval step selects a small subset of relevant specialists. A designated starter agent writes an initial C++17 solution. The system then iteratively runs this candidate in a sandbox on public test examples; at each step, the active specialist can keep the solution, repair it, or hand it off to another specialist, communicating via structured packets. After the relay ends, an infrastructure-fixer pass normalizes boilerplate and implementation details.

Result: On the CodeContests benchmark using Gemma 4 as the backbone, MARS achieves a pass rate of 0.624 ± 0.006 with an average of 2.3 recorded pipeline stages per task. This is a 14.4 percentage point improvement over a direct-prompting baseline. MARS closes most of the performance gap to the stronger CodeSIM system (0.731 pass rate) while being 3.3× cheaper in wall-clock cost and exhibiting much lower variance in per-task token usage.

Conclusion: Specializing LLM agents by algorithm topic and coordinating them via a retrieval-informed relay pipeline yields substantial gains on competitive programming tasks compared with generic multi-agent or direct prompting approaches. MARS demonstrates that a prompt-only, retrieval-augmented architecture can approach the performance of more complex systems while being significantly more efficient and consistent in computational cost.

Abstract: Large Language Models excel at code generation, yet competitive programming exposes a persistent failure mode: existing multi-agent pipelines distribute work over generic planner, coder, and debugger roles and delegate the choice of algorithmic technique to the backbone alone. We present MARS (Multi-Agent Relay of Specialized LLMs), a prompt-only framework in which each agent is a topic specialist---dynamic programming, graphs, strings, geometry, and so on---grounded by retrieval-augmented generation over an algorithm-theory corpus. Given a problem, retrieval selects a small team of relevant specialists; a starter writes an initial C++17 solution, and each subsequent turn runs the candidate against public examples in a sandbox, lets the active specialist keep, repair, or hand off the draft, and forwards a structured packet to the next specialist. A single infrastructure-fixer pass normalizes boilerplate at the end. On the CodeContests test split with Gemma 4, MARS reaches $0.624 \pm 0.006$ pass rate at $2.3$ recorded pipeline stages per task ($+14.4$ percentage points over direct prompting), closing most of the gap to CodeSIM ($0.731$) at $3.3{\times}$ lower wall-clock cost and substantially smaller variance in per-task token spend. The source code is available on GitHub: https://github.com/fckand/mars.

</details>


### [87] [Data Mixing as Mixture Experiment: Response Surface Methodology and Optimal Design for Large Language Model Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23922)
*Yicheng Mao,Hongru Du*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper reframes data mixing for LLM pretraining as a statistical mixture experiment and uses Scheffé response-surface models plus optimal design to choose and interpret proxy mixtures more efficiently.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In LLM pretraining, we have multiple data domains (e.g., web text, books, code) and a fixed token budget. The practical question is how many tokens to allocate to each domain to minimize validation loss. Current approaches train small proxy models on many candidate mixtures and then use a learned response model to pick mixtures for large-scale training. This is expensive and somewhat ad hoc, and we lack a principled way to both interpret mixture effects (how domains interact) and design the proxy experiments efficiently. The authors want a statistically grounded framework that can (1) explain how domains contribute individually and via interactions, and (2) reduce the number of proxy runs needed while preserving the ability to rank mixtures.

Method: They reinterpret proxy data-mixing as a classical mixture experiment: domains are mixture components, token shares are mixture proportions, and validation loss is the response over the simplex. They model this response surface using sparse second-order Scheffé models, which decompose effects into additive (per-domain) and pairwise interaction terms. They then construct model-robust I-optimal experimental designs for choosing which mixtures (design points) to train proxy models on, aiming to minimize average prediction variance over the simplex under model uncertainty. Empirically, they apply this framework to the RegMix setting, fitting sparse Scheffé models, comparing them to a flexible ML predictor, and running a calibrated simulation study to test how many proxy runs can be removed while preserving mixture ranking.

Result: The Scheffé-based analysis reveals that the value of each domain is highly relational: some domains that look weak under purely additive models become attractive when pairwise interactions are allowed, especially when combined with web-derived text. The sparse Scheffé model maintains mixture rankings when scaling from proxy to larger models and is competitive in predictive performance with a more flexible ML model, while being more interpretable in terms of additive vs. interaction effects. In simulation studies calibrated to real proxy responses, their model-robust I-optimal designs can eliminate about 25% of the original proxy training runs while still recovering the key ordering of mixtures.

Conclusion: LLM data mixing should be approached not just as a prediction or regression problem but as an experimental-design problem on the probability simplex. By viewing domains as mixture components and using sparse Scheffé response-surface models together with model-robust I-optimal designs, practitioners can both better understand how domains interact and design more statistically efficient proxy experiments, reducing computational cost while preserving the ability to identify good mixtures.

Abstract: Data mixing is a central design problem in large language model pretraining: given a fixed token budget, practitioners must decide how much data to allocate to each domain. Recent proxy-based methods address this problem by training small models on candidate mixtures, fitting a response model, and using the response to select mixtures for larger-scale training. We show that this workflow has the structure of a classical mixture experiment. Under this view, data domains are mixture components, token shares are component proportions, proxy-training runs are experimental design points, and validation loss defines a response surface over the probability simplex. We develop this formulation using sparse second-order Scheffé response-surface models and construct model-robust $\mathcal{I}$-optimal designs for proxy data-mixing experiments. Using RegMix as an empirical case study, we demonstrate how the framework can both interpret observed mixture responses and design more efficient proxy experiments. The Scheffé analysis shows that domain value is strongly relational: several domains that are weak under additive effects become favourable through pairwise interactions, especially through combinations with web-derived text. The sparse Scheffé model preserves mixture rankings across model scales and remains competitive with a flexible machine-learning predictor while providing an explicit decomposition of additive and interaction effects. In a simulation study calibrated to observed proxy-training responses, model-robust $\mathcal{I}$-optimal designs recover the relevant mixture ordering after removing about 25\% of the original proxy runs. These results suggest that LLM data mixing should be treated not only as a prediction problem, but also as an experimental-design problem in which the proxy mixtures themselves can be chosen to improve statistical efficiency.

</details>


### [88] [Evolutionary Recurrent Decision Model in Developing Adaptive and Maladaptive Behaviors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23932)
*Andrew Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes ERDM, a reinforcement-learning framework modeling how evolutionary mismatch and bounded rationality generate adaptive and maladaptive behaviors that resemble psychopathology.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how behaviors considered maladaptive or psychopathology-related today may in fact arise from cognitive systems that evolved for recurrent ancestral environments and now face evolutionary mismatch, and to provide a formal, testable computational model for these mechanisms.

Method: Develop a reinforcement learning-based decision model (ERDM) where agents operate in several evolutionarily recurrent environments (threat, goal pursuit, alliances). Agents learn policies through rewards based on survival-related metrics. The model is tested under different simulated levels of adverse childhood experiences to see what behavioral strategies emerge.

Result: Under different environmental and developmental conditions, the model generates a range of strategies—such as learned helplessness, avoidance, healthy relationship patterns, and aggression—without explicitly programming these strategies. These emergent behaviors qualitatively match patterns documented in empirical psychological research, supporting the model’s ecological validity.

Conclusion: Psychopathology-like patterns can be seen as the output of bounded, satisficing cognitive systems facing a mismatch between modern and ancestral environments. ERDM offers a promising computational tool to formalize and investigate these processes and can be extended to other contexts and studies.

Abstract: This study introduces the evolutionarily recurrent decision model (ERDM), a computational reinforcement learning framework designed to examine how evolutionary mismatch, bounded rationality, and satisficing contribute to adaptive and maladaptive behavior. ERDM simulates agents across evolutionary recurrent environments, including threat, prey/goal-pursuits, and alliances. Agents learn through competing rewards abstracted from survival metrics. A validity study under varying adverse childhood experiences demonstrates that distinct adaptive and maladaptive strategies, such as learned helplessness, avoidance, healthy relationships, and aggression, emerge naturally without being hardwired. These results align with empirical literature, showcasing ecological validity. The results suggest that many psychopathology-relevant aspects may be interpreted as bounded cognitive systems operating under modern-ancestral environmental mismatch, positioning ERDM as a key computational cognitive tool that can be extended to other studies.

</details>


### [89] [More Rejective, Not More Discriminative: The Unit of Verification in Pre-Execution LLM Oversight](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23941)
*Yuchen Han,Cheng Yan,Wuyang Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how long a fallible LLM safety monitor should look ahead (review window size) when vetting AI agent plans, and finds that short windows (1–2 actions) maximize useful discrimination between safe and unsafe behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In AI control, pre-execution oversight uses an LLM monitor to approve or block an agent’s planned actions before they are irreversibly executed. If the monitor blocks too much, it becomes impractical and gets turned off; but if it misses harmful actions, it fails its safety purpose. Existing designs quietly assume a fixed “unit of verification” (how many future actions are reviewed in a single call), without measuring how this choice affects a fallible LLM’s ability to distinguish harmful from harmless plans. Natural behavior traces can’t cleanly isolate this variable, and naively optimizing for catch rate alone is misleading because a monitor that rejects everything catches all errors but is useless. The authors want a controlled, principled way to quantify the trade-off between catching errors and avoiding unnecessary blocking as review length changes.

Method: The authors propose the twin-prefix framework. For each gold (correct) action plan, they construct (1) a prefix where they inject exactly one error that the environment would accept, and (2) a clean “twin” prefix that differs only in a single write, so the pair is tightly matched. Each pair is presented to an LLM monitor (a “judge”) at five nested review lengths (different numbers of upcoming actions). This design holds everything constant except the length of the reviewed prefix, so any change in the monitor’s decision can be attributed to the unit of verification itself. They evaluate multiple judges across two domains, using a pre-registered discrimination metric called informedness, defined as catch rate minus false rejection rate. They also run observation-replay analyses, feeding back withheld intermediate observations, to diagnose why longer windows behave worse.

Result: Longer review windows monotonically increase the error catch rate, but they simultaneously increase the rate at which safe plans are incorrectly rejected, in near lockstep. As a result, informedness (catch minus false rejection) peaks at very short units, typically one or two actions, for all six LLM judges in both tested domains. This means that when used zero-shot, longer-context monitors become more globally rejective but not meaningfully more discriminative. Observation replay shows that much of the failure from longer windows comes from depriving the monitor of intermediate observations that would have clarified earlier steps. A calibrated short review unit can achieve informedness up to 0.95 relative to an eight-action review baseline, and among the tested strategies, no policy that ignores the labels (i.e., is label-blind) consistently outperforms this short-unit design.

Conclusion: The unit of verification—the number of actions inspected per monitoring call—is a crucial design parameter in AI oversight mechanisms and should not be left implicit. Longer review windows do not automatically yield better safety; for zero-shot LLM monitors, they mainly induce more rejections without improving discrimination between harmful and harmless plans. Carefully chosen short units (1–2 actions) can substantially improve the balance between catching errors and avoiding unnecessary blocking. The authors argue that safety evaluations and deployment “safety cases” should explicitly state the chosen unit size and report performance on the matched clean series, not just headline catch rates. Their twin-prefix framework provides the first controlled, pre-registered experimental instrument for making and justifying this design choice, and it shows that catch rate alone is an inadequate metric for pre-execution oversight quality.

Abstract: Pre-execution oversight is core to trusted monitoring in AI control: a fallible LLM monitor vets planned actions before irreversible execution. Over-blocking forfeits usefulness and pressures deployers to disable it. Every protocol must fix a unit of verification: how many actions one call reviews. Existing designs take the unit as given; its effect on fallible monitors is unmeasured. Natural traces cannot isolate it: review length co-varies with error type and position. Catch alone misleads: rejecting everything catches everything. Measuring this needs boundary variation alone and a matched clean control. We introduce the twin-prefix framework, which supplies both. Each gold plan yields a prefix with one injected, environment-accepted error and a clean twin differing in one write. Judging each pair at five nested lengths ties verdict changes to the unit alone. Discrimination is scored by pre-registered informedness, catch minus false rejection. Longer review raises catch; false rejection climbs in lockstep. Informedness peaks at one or two actions for all six judges in both domains: longer windows make zero-shot monitors more rejective, not more discriminative. Replaying withheld observations traces the failure largely to observation deprivation. Safety cases should state the unit and co-report the clean series. Our framework is the first controlled, pre-registered instrument for this choice and never reads catch alone. Our calibrated short unit recovers up to 0.95 informedness over eight-action review, and no tested label-blind policy consistently beats it.

</details>


### [90] [Recursive Agentic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23956)
*Shengxin Zhang,Xiaomin Wu,Xiyang Wu,Jing Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper provides a unified framework to compare test-time reasoning strategies for language models and shows that branching over multiple reasoning paths consistently yields the largest accuracy gains across models and benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing test-time reasoning methods—like iterative refinement, problem decomposition, and multiple-sample selection—are typically evaluated separately, using different setups. This fragmentation makes it hard to understand which strategies truly help, how much they help, and under what conditions. The authors aim to create a common framework that puts these methods on equal footing and to empirically determine which operator is generally most effective and why.

Method: The authors formalize three classes of test-time reasoning strategies as recursion operators over an agent’s reasoning trace: (1) GROW, which extends a single reasoning chain deeper; (2) PRUNE, which decomposes the problem into subproblems and recombines their solutions; and (3) BRANCH, which samples multiple reasoning chains and selects among them. They then evaluate these operators against a shared single-pass chain-of-thought baseline under a unified experimental harness that controls for prompts, token budgets, and grading code. Experiments are conducted across five benchmarks and three frontier models, creating 14 model-benchmark combinations and involving 49,327 graded items and 151,876 model calls. They also analyze correlations between operator gains and baseline failure modes such as budget-exhausted, truncated outputs and examine the impact of different scoring protocols, including paired vs. unpaired evaluation and the handling of scoring-pipeline failures.

Result: BRANCH improves accuracy in all 14 model-benchmark settings, with an average gain of 5.98 percentage points and is the best-performing operator in 12 of the 14 cases. GROW yields a mean gain of 2.18 percentage points but degrades performance in two settings. PRUNE produces a smaller average improvement of 0.94 percentage points. The benefit of BRANCH is shown to stem not only from diverse reasoning paths but also from its ability to recover from truncation and budget-exhausted outputs; its gains are strongly correlated (r = 0.72) with the baseline rate of empty or truncated outputs. The authors further find that unpaired evaluation and treating failures in the scoring pipeline as model errors can significantly distort or even invert comparative conclusions about these methods.

Conclusion: Within the unified abstraction of recursion operators over reasoning traces, repeated branching (BRANCH) emerges as a consistently dominant test-time reasoning strategy across models and benchmarks, outperforming deepening a single chain (GROW) and problem decomposition (PRUNE). The findings weaken the argument that different problems generally require routing among distinct operators at test time, since BRANCH alone already delivers robust gains. Additionally, the work highlights that evaluation methodology—especially whether scoring is paired and how scoring failures are treated—can substantially impact measured effectiveness, and it advocates for paired scoring as a standard protocol in test-time compute evaluations.

Abstract: Test-time reasoning methods such as iterative refinement, decomposition, and repeated sampling are often evaluated in isolation, making their gains difficult to compare across models, benchmarks, and evaluation pipelines. We introduce a unified view of these methods as recursion operators over an agent's reasoning trace: GROW, which deepens a single reasoning path; PRUNE, which decomposes and recomposes the problem; and BRANCH, which samples alternative reasoning paths and selects among them. We evaluate all three operators against a single-pass chain-of-thought baseline under a shared harness with identical prompts, token budgets, and grading code. Across five benchmarks and three frontier models, comprising 14 model-benchmark settings, 49,327 graded items, and 151,876 model calls, BRANCH improves accuracy in all 14 settings by an average of 5.98 percentage points and is the best-performing operator in 12. In contrast, GROW yields a mean gain of 2.18 points and degrades performance in two settings, while PRUNE improves accuracy by 0.94 points on average. Analysis shows that BRANCH's advantage arises not only from exploring multiple reasoning paths, but also from recovering from truncation: its gains strongly correlate with the baseline rate of empty, budget-exhausted outputs (r = 0.72). These results weaken the hypothesis that different problems require routing among test-time reasoning operators; at this level of abstraction, repeated branching is consistently dominant. Finally, we show that unpaired evaluation and treating scoring-pipeline failures as model errors can materially change, and even reverse, comparative conclusions, motivating paired scoring as a standard protocol for test-time-compute evaluation.

</details>


### [91] [More GPUs or a Smaller Cache? Tensor Parallelism versus KV Compression for Memory-Bound LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23962)
*Srikanta Datta Tumkur,Mehar Simhadri,Anshu Bansal,Jay Iyer,Sai Pavan Kumar,Sai Kapil Kumar,Ramesh Nampelly,Raj Dandekar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper compares two strategies for handling KV cache memory limits in LLM serving—tensor parallelism vs. KV compression—on a unified cost/latency axis and finds compression is consistently more cost-effective below a certain model size/memory threshold.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing work treats tensor parallelism (multi‑GPU sharding of weights and KV cache) and KV cache compression/eviction as separate solutions to KV memory exhaustion. Parallelism papers focus on throughput scaling; compression papers focus on memory savings. There is little quantitative guidance on when to use which under a common cost metric, making it hard for practitioners to choose the most economical strategy for a given model and hardware budget.

Method: The authors build a profiled simulator calibrated on A100, A40, and H100 GPUs. They evaluate Llama‑2 7B and 70B under a range of configurations: tensor parallelism degrees from 1 to 8 GPUs and KV cache compression schemes with 16/8/4‑bit quantization and keep‑ratios down to 0.25. For each configuration they measure/estimate cost per million tokens and latency, plotting the configurations on a single cost‑normalized axis to search for a crossover point where tensor parallelism becomes more cost‑effective than compression.

Result: Across all tested settings—two Llama‑2 model sizes, three GPU types, and all considered levels of memory relief—KV compression is between 1.20× and 2.00× cheaper than tensor parallelism at comparable capacity targets. For a 7B model on an 80 GB GPU, KV cache never becomes the bottleneck within the model’s context window. They identify a rough boundary around 36B parameters for 80 GB devices: below this, compression dominates and additional GPUs for tensor parallelism yield poor cost‑efficiency; above this, tensor parallelism is mandatory because parameter weights, not KV cache, become the binding resource, and KV compression cannot help.

Conclusion: KV compression is the economically superior strategy for handling KV cache limits for models up to roughly 36B parameters on 80 GB GPUs, as it significantly increases capacity per dollar (up to 16.5×) despite increasing per‑token latency (8–93%). Tensor parallelism, while the only option that reduces latency and a requirement for very large models like Llama‑2‑70B whose weights don’t fit on a single GPU, offers modest capacity gains (≈1.21×) for substantial extra hardware cost. Practitioners should therefore favor compression for small‑to‑mid‑sized models and reserve tensor parallelism primarily for weight‑bound large models where it is unavoidable.

Abstract: When an LLM serving deployment runs out of KVcache room, there are two well-established ways out. Tensor parallelism shards the weights and the KV cache across two, four, or eight devices, buying memory headroom at the price of an all-reduce on every layer and a hardware bill that grows with the device count. The algorithms community shrinks the cache in place, with KV quantisation and eviction keeping a single GPU and spending a little quality instead. Compression papers report memory ratios, parallel-scaling papers report throughput curves, and almost nobody puts the two on the same cost axis. We place tensor-parallel configurations (degree 1 to 8) and KV-compressed configurations (16/8/4-bit, keep-ratios down to 0.25) on one costnormalised axis, cost per million tokens against latency, using a profiled simulator calibrated on A100, A40, and H100 hardware, and we go looking for the cost-equivalence crossover. We do not find one. Across two models (Llama-2 at 7B and 70B), three GPU types, and every level of memory relief we could construct, compression is cheaper by 1.20x to 2.00x. A 7B model on an 80 GB device cannot exhaust its KV budget within its own context window, and the boundary that decides between the strategies is model size relative to device memory, at roughly 36B parameters for an 80 GB card. Below that wall, compression dominates and extra GPUs are largely wasted spend; above it, tensor parallelism stops being a choice and becomes an entry ticket: Llama-2-70B is infeasible on one A100 at any KV setting, because the binding resource is weights, which KV compression does not touch. Tensor parallelism is the only lever that improves latency (compression makes per-token latency worse, by 8 to 93%, through batching contention), while compression is the only lever that multiplies capacity per dollar (16.5x, against 1.21x for an eightfold spend on GPUs).

</details>


### [92] [Giraffe: A Mapping Architecture from Hidden Text Representations to Visual Embeddings for Efficient Graphic Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23970)
*Nejla Ghaboosi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a lightweight architecture that lets an MLLM generate complex graphic designs by mapping a single image token to a CLIP visual embedding, avoiding long multi-token image representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal LLMs are strong at understanding images but weak at generating them. Current generation methods use many specialized tokens per image, which greatly increases sequence length and becomes impractical for tasks like graphic/ layout design that require thousands of tokens mixing text, multiple images, and layout elements. The authors want a more token-efficient way to represent and generate images within MLLMs.

Method: They introduce an architecture that maps hidden token states from the language model to the embedding space of visual models such as CLIP ViT-L/14 using just a single [IMG] token per image. The system uses two shallow MLP blocks; each block has its own compression module and they share a common expansion module. Training uses six different loss functions. One of the two blocks is only used to assist during training and is removed for inference, making the final model lightweight.

Result: The proposed single-token image embedding mapping, trained with the dual-MLP and multi-loss scheme, achieves strong empirical performance on both image-to-design and text-to-design generation tasks compared to prior approaches that require many image tokens.

Conclusion: Efficiently mapping language model hidden states to visual embedding space with a single token per image enables scalable, lightweight multimodal generation, particularly suited for complex graphic design tasks involving many interleaved text and image elements.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have made significant progress in understanding and interpreting mul- timedia content. However, their ability to generate me- dia remains limited. Recent approaches have attempted to bridge this gap by translating the hidden representations of token sequences into the embedding space of visual models or directly into raw image data. However, these methods often represent each image using multiple specialised to- kens which significantly increases the input length. This be- comes a major limitation for tasks such as graphic design generation where the output typically involves a seamless blend of thousands of tokens across text, multiple images, and layout information. To address this challenge, a novel architecture is proposed that maps hidden token represen- tations to the embedding space of visual models, such as CLIP ViT-L/14, using a single [IMG] token per image. The architecture employs two shallow MLP blocks, each with a separate compression module followed by a shared expan- sion module, trained with six distinct loss functions. One block aids the other during training and is omitted during inference, resulting in a lightweight solution. Strong perfor- mance is demonstrated in both image-to-design and text-to- design generation tasks.

</details>


### [93] [When Seeing Is Not Enough: Benchmarking Interactive Visual Grounding in LVLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23978)
*Zhengxiang Wang,Owen Rambow*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper evaluates how well large vision-language models handle interactive visual grounding, showing they lag behind humans, especially when they must proactively ask questions to identify a target.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most visual grounding work assumes a single, fully-informative referring expression that directly maps to a visual target, which ignores the interactive, incremental, and often ambiguous nature of real-world reference. The authors aim to assess whether current large vision-language models can handle scenarios where target information is incomplete and must be clarified through dialogue.

Method: The authors design a controlled evaluation framework for interactive visual grounding with LVLMs. They systematically vary how much information about the visual target is given initially versus how much must be gathered through interaction. They test four human-grounded visual contexts and four different interaction protocols, including cases with initial descriptions that can be refined and cases where no description is given and the model must ask questions to obtain target information. They compare LVLM performance to human task-level baselines and run follow-up studies across different description sources (human vs. AI), reasoning levels, repeated interactions, description providers, and visual contexts.

Result: Across all tested contexts and interaction protocols, current LVLMs underperform compared to human baselines. Interaction helps in scenarios where models can refine or repair an initial target description through follow-up questions. However, performance is particularly poor when models receive no initial description and must proactively acquire target information via questions. The models also show miscalibration, frequently expressing high confidence despite low empirical accuracy. These patterns are robust across variations in description source, reasoning effort, repetition, provider, and context.

Conclusion: Interactive visual grounding, which requires not only visual recognition but also effective information seeking and synthesis through dialogue, remains a substantial challenge for current LVLMs. Existing models are especially weak at proactive, question-driven grounding and exhibit poor confidence calibration, indicating that future research must focus on improving interactive capabilities and uncertainty awareness in vision-language systems.

Abstract: Visual grounding is typically evaluated as a one-shot mapping from an informative referring expression to a visual target. This formulation misses a central property of real-world reference: target information is often incomplete, ambiguous, and established through interaction. We introduce a controlled evaluation framework for interactive visual grounding in large vision-language models (LVLMs), varying how much target information is provided upfront and how much must be acquired through dialogue. Across four human-grounded visual contexts and four interaction protocols, current LVLMs perform significantly below task-level human baselines. Interaction can help when follow-up questions refine or repair an initial target description. Performance is lowest when no initial description is provided and target information must be acquired through questions, indicating that proactive question-driven grounding remains difficult. LVLMs are also poorly calibrated, often reporting confidence that exceeds their empirical accuracy. Follow-up studies confirm these patterns across varied description sources (human versus AI), reasoning efforts, repeated interactions, description providers, and visual contexts. Overall, interactive visual grounding remains an important challenge, requiring visual matching, information seeking and synthesis.

</details>


### [94] [Rules Before Oracles: Auditable, User-Configurable Argument Selection for Deliberative Polling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23979)
*Muntaser Syed,Markus Zanker,Marius Silaghi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how to design transparent, rule-based recommendation mechanisms for deliberative polls that decide which arguments each voter sees, and shows that a simple, legible ranking rule can perform nearly as well as complex opaque rankers while giving users meaningful control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In large deliberative polls, there are more submissions than any voter can read, so some mechanism must choose which arguments each person sees. Currently this role is effectively played by opaque machine-learned ranking systems, which quietly control much of the decision outcome. Voters cannot recompute, inspect, or contest how exposure to arguments was shaped, raising legitimacy and accountability concerns. The authors want to know whether we can instead use mechanisms whose logic is public, recomputable from public evidence, and with tunable parameters held by voters themselves, treating such legibility not as a trade-off against accuracy but as a precondition for an acceptable civic mechanism.

Method: The authors formally model a deliberative poll as operating over bipolar justification sets, and evaluate slates of arguments by three metrics: reason coverage, the order in which reasons arrive, and the total captured endorsement mass. They define seven explicit, checkable criteria that any civic recommender should satisfy. They then propose a ranking rule that meets these criteria: a one-hop reversed endorsement flow algorithm whose behavior is parameterized by a relation-weight function. To evaluate this, they build an agent-based simulator that logs every slate shown at every vote, running about 17,000 paired simulations under different conditions, including adversarial flooding scenarios and different weight policies.

Result: The proposed legible rule’s performance is close to an idealized upper bound: the slates it serves are at most 0.035 below a label-reading ceiling that upper-bounds all possible selection procedures, including fully unconstrained opaque rankers, implying any extra advantage from opacity is limited. When considering coverage alone and assuming non-degenerate authoring, the rule looks similar to a random slate because the evaluation instrument is order- and charity-blind. However, on the other two metrics—arrival order and endorsement mass—the rule outperforms random and alternative strategies at every prefix, with the advantage growing under adversarial pressure, and it achieves about 3.3 times higher endorsement mass. When a realistic fraction of submissions lack explicit reasons, the coverage advantage of the rule reappears and increases. Under label-homogeneous flooding, overall completeness drops sharply (from 0.81 to 0.34) with a flat weight policy but only to 0.44 with author-count normalization, showing that the choice of relation-weight function acts as a security control worth about 10% of completeness.

Conclusion: Transparent, rule-based ranking mechanisms for deliberative polls are feasible and competitive: a simple, legible one-hop reversed endorsement flow rule, parameterized by a voter-controlled weight function, can nearly match the performance of any selection mechanism while providing robustness to adversarial flooding and explicit control over the trade-off between coverage and endorsement mass. The choice between different ranking variants is inherently normative—a decision about where to sit on a coverage–mass frontier—so it should be handed to voters via a legible, inspectable rule rather than embedded in opaque machine-learned systems. The mechanism also lends itself to deployment on open-source, peer-to-peer platforms, aligning institutional design with transparency and user agency.

Abstract: In a deliberative poll, once submissions outnumber what anyone will read, some mechanism chooses which arguments each voter sees, acquiring much of the decision; practice delegates it to opaque learned rankers, so a voter cannot recompute or contest the exposure that shaped their vote. We ask whether it can be a published rule over publicly recomputable evidence with parameters held by the voter, treating legibility as an admissibility condition on usable mechanisms, not an objective traded against accuracy. We formalise a poll over bipolar justification sets, judging a slate by reason coverage, the order it arrives in, and captured endorsement mass; we give seven checkable criteria for a civic recommender and a rule meeting them: a one-hop reversed endorsement flow parameterised by a relation-weight function. An agentic simulator records every slate at every vote, over about 17,000 seed-paired runs. Served slates fall 0.035 short of a label-reading ceiling upper-bounding every selection procedure, opaque ones included: any unconstrained ranker's advantage is bounded and small. On coverage alone, with non-degenerate authoring, the rule is indistinguishable from a random slate, a null due to an order-blind, charity-blind instrument; on the other two it leads at every prefix by a margin widening with adversarial pressure and dominates on mass by a factor of 3.3. Once a realistic fraction of submissions carries no reasons, the coverage margin returns and grows. Label-homogeneous flooding collapses completeness from 0.81 to 0.34 under a flat weight policy, only to 0.44 under author-count normalisation, making the weight function a security control worth 10% of completeness. The choice between ranking arms is a position on a coverage-versus-mass frontier, not a fact, the kind of choice only a legible rule can hand to the person it affects. It maps onto an open-source peer-to-peer platform.

</details>


### [95] [Memory Is Not Always Needed: Characterizing Conditional Memory in Scientific Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23982)
*Zhen Bi,Xueshu Chen,Yan Wang,Zhizhi Peng,Haosen Hong,Zhen Wang,Zhixuan Chu,Bingyu Zhu,Jungang Lou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how and when to use external (conditional) memory in language models for scientific reasoning, and proposes a routing mechanism that selectively activates and injects memory to improve reliability without harming existing capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language models doing scientific reasoning often need external, specialized knowledge. Conditional memory can provide this via explicit retrieval, but it doesn’t always help: sometimes it fixes missing knowledge; other times it distracts or harms reasoning that the base model could already do. There is a need to understand in a principled way when memory helps or hurts, and how to control its influence during multi-step scientific computation.

Method: The authors (1) analyze the model’s “scientific knowledge boundary” to see what it already knows vs. where memory is needed; (2) define and probe specific knowledge-circuit nodes in the network where memory can be injected; (3) design controlled interventions to measure the effects of memory at different layers and stages; and (4) propose a Knowledge Boundary-Aware Router that, using task-specific input features available before generation, decides (a) whether to turn memory on, (b) at which layer-stage nodes to inject memory signals, and (c) how strongly to weight these signals. They evaluate across multiple backbone models and six biological/chemical reasoning task types, comparing against static routing and random routing with matched activation rates.

Result: Experiments on biological and chemical reasoning benchmarks show that the impact of conditional memory is highly input-, task-, and location-dependent. The proposed Knowledge Boundary-Aware Router better captures when and where memory is beneficial, preserving positive contributions of memory while significantly reducing cases where memory degrades performance, relative to baselines such as static memory use and activation-rate-matched random routing.

Conclusion: Selective, boundary-aware allocation of conditional memory is crucial for reliable scientific reasoning in language models. By routing memory based on pre-generation input proxies and targeting specific model nodes, one can leverage external knowledge where it fills genuine gaps while avoiding interference where the base model already has sufficient competence. This establishes selective memory routing as a key design principle for future scientific reasoning systems.

Abstract: Scientific reasoning requires language models to retrieve specialized knowledge and incorporate it reliably into multi-step computation. Conditional memory provides an explicit lookup pathway that complements dense neural representations, but its usefulness is inherently input- and computation-dependent: retrieved information may repair missing scientific associations, yet it may also introduce distracting shortcuts or interfere with reasoning that the base model can already perform correctly. In this work, we systematically investigate when, where, and to what extent conditional memory should participate in scientific reasoning. We characterize the scientific knowledge boundary and controlled interventions on memory-enabled knowledge-circuit nodes. Based on these analyses, we propose a Knowledge Boundary-Aware Router that uses task-specific input proxies available before generation to determine whether memory is activated, which layer-stage nodes receive memory signals, and how strongly these signals contribute. Experiments on biological and chemical reasoning benchmarks, covering two backbone families and six task types, show that memory effects vary substantially across inputs, tasks, and injection locations.
