Compared with static and activation-rate-matched random routing, our approach more consistently preserves beneficial memory contributions while suppressing memory-induced regressions, establishing selective memory allocation as an important principle for reliable scientific reasoning.

</details>


### [96] [Diverse by Reasoning: Harnessing the Wisdom of LLM Crowds for Future Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24001)
*Nirupam Chetlapalli,Yiming Liao,Min-Chun Chen,Keke Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a behavior-aware way to build small but effective “crowds” of large language models, showing that carefully chosen, behaviorally diverse subsets can outperform naive voting over many models while being much cheaper.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs are increasingly used for forecasting and other future prediction tasks, practitioners often ensemble multiple models to get a wisdom-of-the-crowd effect. However, different LLMs can behave redundantly, so merely adding more models may increase cost without improving accuracy. There is a need for a principled way to measure and exploit behavioral diversity among models to build efficient, high-performing ensembles.

Method: The authors introduce a framework that (1) collects reasoning traces from many LLMs on a set of independent development benchmarks, (2) represents each model via its behavioral patterns on these tasks, (3) clusters the models using K-means++ based on behavioral similarity, and (4) selects representative models (medoids) from clusters to form a compact crowd. They test 25 LLMs, use seven benchmarks to quantify behavioral diversity, and then evaluate different crowd constructions on two future-prediction benchmarks, comparing clustered medoid crowds against naive voting over all models.

Result: A small, behaviorally representative ensemble (a three-model medoid crowd derived from K-means++ clustering) outperforms majority voting across all 25 LLMs on both future-prediction benchmarks. This approach achieves about an 88% reduction in model calls and roughly 80% lower inference cost, while improving or at least maintaining predictive performance. The experiments also highlight that not all diversity is helpful; behaviorally representative diversity is key.

Conclusion: Carefully constructed LLM crowds based on behavioral clustering can be both more accurate and far more cost-efficient than large, naive ensembles. Effective ensembling depends more on selecting behaviorally representative models than on maximizing the number of models or raw diversity, suggesting that model behavior profiling and clustering should be integral to building LLM-based forecasting systems.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for future prediction, motivating the use of multiple models as a wisdom-of-the-crowd mechanism. However, simply increasing crowd size does not guarantee effective diversity, as different LLMs may exhibit redundant behaviors. We propose a behavior-aware framework for constructing diverse LLM crowds. The framework characterizes models using their reasoning traces on independent development tasks, clusters models by behavioral similarity, and selects representatives for collective prediction. We evaluate 25 LLMs using seven development benchmarks for behavioral diversity modeling and two future-prediction benchmarks for evaluating diverse crowds' performance. Our results show that crowd composition can matter more than crowd size: a three-model medoid crowd based on K-means++ behavioral clustering outperforms conventional voting over all 25 models on both prediction benchmarks, while reducing model calls by 88% and inference cost by approximately 80%. The results further suggest that representative behavioral diversity, rather than simply maximizing diversity, is important for constructing effective LLM crowds

</details>


### [97] [Incorporating Cognitive Load and Knowledge Transfer for Multi-Domain Knowledge Tracing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24005)
*Haotian Zhang,Shucun Wang,Jinze Wu,Liang Ding,Shuochen Liu,Zhenya Huang,Jing Sha,Shijin Wang,Qi Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes LT-MKT, a multi-domain knowledge tracing model that explicitly accounts for cognitive load and knowledge transfer using an LLM-based multi-domain hierarchical graph, leading to state-of-the-art performance on real datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most existing knowledge tracing approaches assume single-domain learning, but real students often learn multiple subjects or domains simultaneously. This creates cognitive load when managing learning sequences across domains, and enables knowledge transfer within and across domains, both of which are typically ignored in KT. The paper aims to better assess student knowledge states in realistic multi-domain environments by explicitly modeling these two factors.

Method: The authors introduce LT-MKT, which first uses large language models to integrate textual information from questions and associated concepts to build a Multi-domain Hierarchical Graph connecting related concepts across domains. On top of this graph, they model cross-domain features over time and knowledge dimensions to capture cognitive load effects when students interact with items from different domains. They further add a knowledge transfer module that simulates how mastery in one concept or domain propagates to related concepts within and across domains. These components are jointly trained for predicting students’ future responses.

Result: On multiple real-world multi-domain datasets, LT-MKT outperforms existing state-of-the-art KT methods in predicting student performance, showing that modeling cognitive load and cross-domain knowledge transfer leads to more accurate knowledge state estimation.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling cognitive load and knowledge transfer in multi-domain learning yields better knowledge tracing performance than traditional single-domain or domain-agnostic KT models. Leveraging LLMs to construct a multi-domain hierarchical graph and incorporating temporal and knowledge-level cross-domain interactions, along with a dedicated knowledge transfer module, enables a more realistic and effective representation of students’ evolving knowledge states. The approach achieves state-of-the-art results, suggesting it is a promising direction for KT in complex, real educational settings.

Abstract: Knowledge Tracing (KT) aims to assess students' dynamic knowledge states from their learning histories. While most existing KT methods focus on single-domain learning with notable success, real-world learning scenarios often involve multiple domains simultaneously, introducing two critical factors: 1) Cognitive load, arising from managing learning across domains in both temporal and knowledge dimensions. 2) Knowledge transfer, where knowledge states in one domain influence related states both within and across domains. In this paper, we focus on exploring these factors to improve students' knowledge state assessment in multi-domain learning scenarios and propose a novel method incorporating cognitive Load and knowledge Transfer for Multi-domain Knowledge Tracing (LT-MKT). Specifically, to bridge isolated domains, LT-MKT first integrates textual information from questions and their associated concepts to construct a Multi-domain Hierarchical Graph, leveraging the advanced representational capabilities of large language models (LLMs). Then, cross-domain features in both the temporal and knowledge dimensions are explicitly modeled to capture the effects of cognitive load. Additionally, a knowledge transfer module is designed to model the propagation of knowledge states within and across domains. By jointly modeling these factors, LT-MKT enables more accurate prediction of students' future performance. Finally, extensive experiments on real-world datasets demonstrate that our method achieves state-of-the-art performance.

</details>


### [98] [Reflection with Action-Induced Visual Differences for Desktop GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24015)
*Yijie Ma,Chaoyue Niu,Fan Wu,Guihai Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They improve GUI agent reflection by separating visual change detection from reasoning, which boosts accuracy and end-to-end task success.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In desktop GUI agents using the Planner-Operator-Reflector (POR) framework, complex, dense interfaces cause subtle or scattered state changes after actions. Current reflectors must both detect these changes and verify action outcomes in one step, relying on implicit evidence and leading to weakly grounded, error-prone reflections. This bottleneck harms objective alignment and task performance.

Method: They propose Evidence-First Reflection (EFR), a two-stage reflector. First, it focuses on detecting action-induced visual differences: it localizes the action region and candidate changed regions using Set-of-Marks annotations, then describes and filters the changes to keep only action-relevant ones. Second, given this explicit, cleaned evidence, it performs outcome verification and final judgment, decoupling evidence collection from reasoning.

Result: On OSWorld-Verified and WindowsAgentArena benchmarks, EFR increases reflector accuracy by 7.11%. This translates into average end-to-end task success improvements of 5.94% on OSWorld-Verified and 4.95% on WindowsAgentArena compared with baselines.

Conclusion: Decoupling evidence extraction from reasoning in GUI reflectors leads to more grounded, reliable reflections and reduces both visual search and reasoning complexity. This improves the overall effectiveness of POR-based GUI agents on realistic desktop benchmarks.

Abstract: The Planner-Operator-Reflector (POR) framework is widely used in GUI agents to maintain objective alignment in complex tasks through modular collaboration. However, desktop GUIs introduce a key challenge: large, dense interfaces often exhibit subtle or scattered state changes, placing most of the burden on the reflector, which must compare pre- and post-action screens, while the planner and operator reason over a single state. Existing reflectors collapse change detection and outcome verification into one step, leaving evidence implicit and yielding weakly grounded decisions. To address this limitation, we propose Evidence-First Reflection (EFR), a two-stage reflector that explicitly decouples action-induced visual differences extraction from outcome verification. EFR identifies the action location and candidate changed regions with Set-of-Marks annotations, describes and filters action-relevant changes, and makes the final judgment from the cleaned evidence. This evidence-reasoning decoupled design makes reflection better grounded in screen transitions, while reducing both visual search complexity and reasoning burden. Experiments on OSWorld-Verified and WindowsAgentArena demonstrate that EFR improves reflector accuracy by 7.11%, yielding average end-to-end task success gains of 5.94% and 4.95% on the two benchmarks, respectively.

</details>


### [99] [Beyond Confidence: Test-Time Scaling for Multi-Turn Search Agents via Retrieval Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24024)
*Hyunho Kook,Junhyuk So,Tianyu Fu,Haizhong Zheng,Beidi Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper finds that confidence-based voting using token log probabilities fails in multi-turn retrieval-augmented LLM agents due to copy inflation, and proposes Retrieval-Grounded Voting (RGV), which uses lexical overlap with retrieved documents to weight rollouts, yielding better accuracy, especially when only a minority of rollouts are correct.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing confidence-based voting methods work reasonably well for single-turn reasoning by using internal LLM confidence signals such as token log probabilities. However, modern LLMs function as multi-turn search agents that retrieve external documents, and it is unclear whether these confidence signals remain reliable in such settings. There is a need to understand and fix why aggregation methods underperform when the model interacts with retrieval, particularly since accurate answer selection from multiple rollouts is critical for real-world systems.

Method: The authors empirically study confidence-based voting in multi-turn retrieval-augmented LLM agents, identifying a phenomenon they call copy inflation: tokens copied from retrieved documents receive systematically higher log probabilities when appended to the agent’s context. This leads to overly uniform confidence scores and weakens weighted voting. To fix this, they introduce Retrieval-Grounded Voting (RGV), which scores each rollout by measuring lexical overlap between its final answer and the specific documents it retrieved, computing this signal outside the LLM’s contaminated context. They evaluate RGV on four benchmarks for search agents and across five LLMs, comparing it against standard confidence-based voting.

Result: The study shows that traditional confidence-based voting degrades in multi-turn retrieval settings due to copy inflation, resulting in less discriminative confidence scores across rollouts. In contrast, the proposed Retrieval-Grounded Voting method, which relies on lexical overlap with retrieved documents rather than log probabilities, achieves consistently higher accuracy on all four evaluated search-agent benchmarks and across five LLMs. The gains reach up to +5.4 percentage points in overall accuracy and up to +35% improvement on minority-correct questions, where only 1–2 out of 8 rollouts contain the correct answer.

Conclusion: Confidence-based voting based on token log probabilities is unreliable for multi-turn retrieval-augmented LLM agents because retrieval causes copy inflation, flattening confidence signals and weakening aggregation. Retrieval-Grounded Voting, which instead scores rollouts using lexical overlap with retrieved documents, avoids this issue while not requiring extra LLM calls. It provides a more robust and effective way to aggregate multiple rollouts in search-agent settings, especially in challenging cases where correct answers are rare among candidates.

Abstract: Confidence-based voting aggregates parallel LLM rollouts by weighting each with internal signals such as token log probabilities, and has been actively studied for single-turn reasoning. However, modern LLMs increasingly act as multi-turn search agents that retrieve and condition on external documents. In this paper, we show that confidence-based voting transfers poorly to this multi-turn setting, and identify the underlying failure reason as copy inflation: when retrieved documents are appended to an agent's context, tokens copied from those documents receive systematically inflated log probabilities. This flattens confidence scores within each question and weakens the resulting weighted vote. To address this issue, we propose Retrieval-Grounded Voting (RGV), which scores each rollout by the lexical overlap between its final answer and the documents it retrieved. By computing the signal outside the contaminated context, RGV sidesteps both token log probabilities and additional LLM calls. Across four search-agent benchmarks and five LLMs, RGV consistently outperforms confidence-based voting, with gains of up to +5.4% accuracy and +35% on minority-correct questions, where the correct answer appears in only 1-2 of 8 rollouts.

</details>


### [100] [Relative Time Intervals Representation for Word-level Timestamping with Masked Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24041)
*Quanwei Tang,Zhiyu Tang,Xu Li,Dong Zhang,Shoushan,Guodong Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper extends Speech Large Language Models to predict fine-grained, temporally aligned outputs by adding relative timestamp modeling with an efficient hybrid fine-tuning strategy and a masked timestamp objective, improving timestamp accuracy without hurting transcription quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing SpeechLLMs are strong at understanding and generating speech content but weak at producing fine-grained, time-aligned outputs, which are crucial for applications like alignment, diarization, and precise captioning. The authors aim to fill this gap by making SpeechLLMs not just content-aware but also explicitly temporal-aware, while keeping models efficient and robust to noisy timestamp annotations.

Method: The authors (1) replace absolute timestamps with a compact relative timestamp representation to improve generalization and reduce vocabulary size; (2) introduce a hybrid fine-tuning scheme where the timestamp-augmented embedding layer and LM head are fully fine-tuned while decoder layers are tuned with LoRA for parameter efficiency; and (3) design a masked timestamp training objective that hides some ground-truth timestamps during training, forcing the model to infer temporal structure rather than overfit to noisy annotations.

Result: Experiments show that the proposed approach significantly improves timestamp prediction accuracy over baselines while preserving strong speech transcription performance. The model remains parameter-efficient due to hybrid fine-tuning and is more robust to real-world noisy timestamp labels thanks to the masked timestamp objective.

Conclusion: By modeling relative timestamps and using a hybrid fine-tuning plus masked timestamp training, the paper successfully transforms SpeechLLMs into temporal-aware models that jointly capture content and timing. This leads to better fine-grained alignment without sacrificing transcription quality, suggesting a practical path to more temporally precise speech applications.

Abstract: Although Speech Large Language Models (SpeechLLMs) excel at speech understanding and generation, their capacity for fine-grained, temporally aligned outputs remains underexplored. Our work addresses this gap by enabling SpeechLLMs to jointly model speech content and temporal structure, effectively transforming them from ``content understanding machines" into ``temporal-aware content understanding machines". Specifically, we replace traditional absolute timestamps with relative timestamps, achieving a more compact vocabulary and stronger generalization capabilities. To efficiently infuse timestamp prediction ability into pre-trained large language models, we introduce a hybrid fine-tuning strategy: full-parameter fine-tuning of the timestamp-augmented embedding layer and language model head, combined with LoRA fine-tuning of the decoder layers. Moreover, we design a masked timestamp training objective, preventing the model from over-relying on ground-truth timestamps, and thereby enhancing robustness against noisy real-world annotations. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our approach achieves significant improvements in timestamp prediction accuracy while maintaining strong speech transcription performance.

</details>


### [101] [Algorithmic Impact Reveals the Hidden Social Choice Structure of Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24046)
*Zachary Wojtowicz,Michelle Si,Finale Doshi-Velez,Ariel Procaccia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper reframes AI alignment with multiple stakeholders as a welfare optimization problem in a convex impact space, enabling the use of welfare economics and mechanism design to design, analyze, and compare alignment protocols with clear social choice guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI alignment methods like reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) focus on matching model behavior to feedback without robustly handling divergent preferences across affected individuals. This leads to weak social choice guarantees and unclear welfare consequences. The authors aim to provide a principled, welfare-theoretic foundation for multi-stakeholder alignment that explicitly accounts for and aggregates heterogeneous preferences in a transparent, analyzable way.

Method: The authors represent the consequences of an AI system as points in a convex impact space, where each dimension corresponds to welfare-relevant outcomes across individuals or groups. They then formulate alignment as a linear optimization problem over this space, subject to constraints that encode social desiderata. Using tools from welfare economics and mechanism design, they (1) analyze how specific alignment protocols map into welfare outcomes and social choice properties, (2) show that voting-by-issues and random-dictatorship mechanisms are strategyproof and unanimous in this framework, and (3) construct new families of alignment protocols that maximize utilitarian social welfare while respecting constraints like limits on individual or group harm.

Result: Theoretically, the paper provides a general transformation between alignment protocols and their welfare consequences via linear optimization in impact space. It proves that certain mechanisms (voting-by-issues, random dictatorship) satisfy strategyproofness and unanimity when used as alignment procedures. It also derives a family of welfare-maximizing alignment rules that can incorporate fairness-like constraints (e.g., bounding harm to individuals or groups). Empirically, it demonstrates how these protocols behave on real preference datasets involving kidney allocation, charitable food distribution, LLM response alignment, and trolley problem scenarios, illustrating concrete welfare trade-offs and constraint effects.

Conclusion: AI alignment with multiple affected parties can be systematically framed as social welfare optimization rather than ad hoc preference fitting. By embedding alignment into a convex impact space and using linear optimization, the authors create a bridge between alignment practice and welfare economics/mechanism design. This bridge enables the design and analysis of alignment protocols with explicit, provable social choice properties and controllable welfare constraints, offering a more principled alternative or complement to standard RLHF for multi-stakeholder AI systems.

Abstract: When an AI algorithm makes decisions that affect more than one person, aligning it becomes a problem of social choice: how should people's divergent preferences about system behavior be reconciled and aggregated into a single coherent model? The standard approach to aligning frontier AI models$\unicode{x2013}$reinforcement learning from human feedback$\unicode{x2013}$largely sidesteps this question and has poor social choice guarantees. However, it remains unclear what alternative should replace it. We show that, by focusing directly on an algorithm's welfare consequences, the alignment problem can be reformulated as linear optimization over a convex impact space, which makes it amenable to the standard toolkit of welfare economics and mechanism design. This reformulation clarifies how alignment protocols translate into welfare consequences and, conversely, how a social planner's desired constraints on welfare consequences can be translated back into alignment protocols. We apply this transformation to show that voting-by-issues and random-dictatorship mechanisms are strategyproof and unanimous. Demonstrating the reverse direction, we also apply the impact representation to derive a family of alignment protocols that maximize utilitarian social welfare subject to various social desiderata, such as bounds on individual or group harm. We illustrate the welfare implications of these alignment protocols empirically using real human preferences over kidney allocation, charitable food distribution, LLM responses, and trolley problems.

</details>


### [102] [Poisoning Agentic Alpha: Adversarial Vulnerabilities Across Roles and Architectures in Multi-Agent Trading Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24069)
*CheolWon Na,Hao Ni,Lukasz Szpruch,Zhangyang Wang,Dhagash Mehta,Saurabh Nagrecha,Alejandro Lopez-Lira,Chanyeol Choi,Yongjae Lee,Jee-Hyong Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper empirically studies how adversarial signals injected via data or prompts can affect LLM-based multi-agent trading systems, showing that no common architecture is intrinsically robust and offering a framework to analyze and compare robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based multi-agent trading systems are being deployed to manage real financial assets, but their reliance on inter-agent communication and external data makes them vulnerable to adversarial manipulation. Existing attack work often assumes unrealistic internal access; this paper instead focuses on practical, low-barrier attacks via the data and prompts that agents naturally consume, aiming to understand and quantify how such adversarial signals propagate through realistic trading pipelines.

Method: The authors decompose a standard LLM-based trading pipeline into four functional roles—Analyst, Researcher, Trader, and Risk Manager—each with a tailored, interface-consistent attack. They consider multiple communication topologies and distinguish between data-level and agent-level attacks. They introduce the Adversarial Signal Preservation Score (APS) to measure how far an injected adversarial signal propagates through the system toward the final trading decision. Experiments span five financial assets, two LLM backbones, and both buy/sell target directions to compare architectures and assess robustness.

Result: Empirical results show that adversarial signals injected at different roles and via different channels can survive multiple stages of processing and significantly influence final trading decisions. APS reveals systematic differences in how communication topologies preserve or dampen adversarial signals, but across settings no evaluated architecture consistently prevents harmful propagation. The study maps which roles and structures are most vulnerable and under what conditions attacks are most effective.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that current LLM-based multi-agent trading architectures are broadly vulnerable to realistic prompt- and data-based attacks, and that no common design choice alone guarantees robustness. Their role-based threat model, attack suite, and APS metric offer a foundation for analyzing and comparing designs, and the findings highlight the need for more principled defenses, such as robustness-aware communication structures, validation layers, and role-specific safeguards in future agentic trading systems.

Abstract: LLM-based multi-agent trading systems, in which specialized agents collaborate through structured communication to produce trading decisions, are moving rapidly from research prototypes to live deployments that control real assets. The same inter-agent communication that makes them effective also exposes them: a corrupted signal can propagate to the final decision and translate into realized financial loss. Unlike prior attacks that presume privileged access to system internals, we restrict the adversary to what is practically reachable---the source data and prompts agents consume---yielding a low-barrier, and thus democratized threat model instantiated as role-specific adversaries.
  We present the first systematic empirical study in the financial domain to characterize how an adversarial signal enters a multi-agent trading system and how far it survives toward the decision. Along the role axis, we decompose a widely-used trading pipeline into four functional roles---Analyst, Researcher, Trader, and Risk Manager---and pair each with an attack matched to its interface. Along the structural axis, we evaluate four communication topologies under data- and agent-level attacks, using the Adversarial Signal Preservation Score (APS) as a post-hoc lens on why some designs are more robust than others. We conduct experiments across five assets, two backbones, and two target directions. A central finding is that no architecture is inherently robust. These findings provide insights for the future design of safer and more robust agentic trading systems.

</details>


### [103] [Compression Trinity: Exploring Sparsity, Quantization, and Low-Rank Approximations for LLM Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24070)
*Mohammad Mozaffari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a unified “Compression Trinity” framework that combines sparsity, quantization, and low-rank approximations to improve the efficiency of large language models while preserving or even improving accuracy, across both pretraining and post-training stages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models are extremely costly to train and deploy due to high computational and environmental demands. Existing compression approaches—sparsity, quantization, and low-rank approximations—are usually used separately and each hits a point where further efficiency gains severely degrade accuracy. There is a need for a principled way to jointly exploit these techniques to push efficiency further without sacrificing performance, and to make both pretraining and post-training more scalable.

Method: The thesis proposes the “Compression Trinity,” a framework that jointly applies sparsity (to cut computation), quantization (to reduce memory bandwidth), and low-rank structure (to regain lost accuracy). It instantiates this idea in several concrete methods: (1) MKOR, an optimizer that uses block-diagonal sparse curvature and low-rank inversion to approximate second-order information while keeping optimizer states quantization-friendly; (2) SLoPe, a training-time method that uses N:M structured sparsity with a doubly-pruned backward pass and low-rank “lazy” adapters applied only near the end of training; (3) OPTIMA, a post-training method that stabilizes static sparsity masks by formulating weight reconstruction as global column-wise quadratic programs; (4) PATCH, which learns dynamic hybrid sparsity ratios during fine-tuning instead of fixing masks; and (5) SLiM, a one-shot scheme that simultaneously applies sparsity and quantization, then uses analytically derived low-rank adapters to recover information lost due to compression.

Result: MKOR reduces curvature update complexity from cubic to quadratic in parameter dimension and speeds up convergence by up to 1.85x versus KFAC. SLoPe yields up to 1.25x training speedup via double-pruned backward passes while maintaining accuracy using late-stage low-rank adapters. OPTIMA increases zero-shot performance of sparsified models by up to 3.97% compared to prior zero-training baselines. PATCH surpasses the performance ceiling of fixed sparsity masks under a fine-tuning budget and provides up to 1.38x inference speedups via learned dynamic sparsity ratios between 0% and 50%. SLiM implements the full Compression Trinity in a single procedure, achieving up to 5.66% accuracy gains over previous state-of-the-art compression methods and even outperforming dense, uncompressed models at equal parameter counts by 0.6%.

Conclusion: Jointly applying sparsity, quantization, and low-rank approximations within a unified framework is more effective than using any of these techniques in isolation. Across optimizers, training-time methods, post-training compression, fine-tuning, and one-shot schemes, the Compression Trinity consistently improves efficiency (speed and memory) while preserving or improving accuracy. This demonstrates that such integrated compression strategies are key to making large language models both scalable and high-performing in practical deployments.

Abstract: Prohibitive computational and environmental costs impede the scalable deployment of Large Language Models (LLMs). Traditional compression techniques (sparsity, quantization, low-rank approximations) are typically applied in isolation, and each hits an accuracy-efficiency wall. This thesis proposes the "Compression Trinity," a unified framework that applies the three pillars jointly: sparsity to reduce computation, quantization to minimize memory bandwidth, and low-rank approximations to recover accuracy. To accelerate pretraining, we apply the Trinity to the optimizer and model architecture. MKOR approximates curvature via block-diagonal sparsity and low-rank inversion, maintaining numerical stability for quantized states; it reduces curvature update complexity from $O(d^3)$ to $O(d^2)$ and accelerates convergence by up to 1.85x over KFAC. SLoPe accelerates training by up to 1.25x via a double-pruned backward pass for N:M sparsity, using low-rank "lazy" adapters in the final 1% of training to recover accuracy. For post-training compression, OPTIMA stabilizes static masks in a zero-training regime by formulating weight reconstruction as globally optimal column-wise quadratic programs, improving zero-shot accuracy by up to 3.97%. Given a fine-tuning budget, PATCH breaks the ceiling of static masks by learning a dynamic hybrid sparsity ratio between 0% and 50%, yielding up to 1.38x speedups. Finally, SLiM realizes the full Compression Trinity in one shot, using mathematically derived low-rank adapters to recover information lost to quantization and sparsity, improving accuracy by up to 5.66% over state-of-the-art methods and outperforming uncompressed dense models at equal parameter budgets by 0.6%. Together, these results show that jointly applying the Compression Trinity is essential for efficient, scalable, high-performance LLMs.

</details>


### [104] [AgentWorld: Personality-Aware Reliability Evaluation for Agentic Information Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24076)
*Gunja Agarwal,Arup Kumar Das,Arun Menon,Jitesh Chandra Mishra,Vignesh Divakaran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentWorld is a framework to evaluate agentic information-retrieval systems using diverse personality-driven users and adversarial stress tests, going beyond standard scripted, uniform evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of agentic information-retrieval agents rely on scripted, uniform users and simple pass@k metrics, which fail to capture how agents behave with diverse user personalities, stateful tool environments, and adversarial perturbations. There is also a lack of systematic ways to expose and quantify trajectory-level brittleness and categorize failure modes in realistic scenarios.

Method: The paper introduces AgentWorld, a simulation framework with four main components: (i) simulated user populations parameterized by Big Five (OCEAN) personality traits interacting with stateful tool-use environments; (ii) an enhanced pass^k consistency metric that includes structured fault classification, partial credit scoring, and dual-control handoff verification; (iii) mechanisms to export training data based on score thresholds in six different fine-tuning formats; and (iv) an adversarial Risk Analyser that records key intermediate states, runs Monte Carlo rollouts with four task-aware perturbation types, and computes risk using ΔP/ΔT scoring, Dempster–Shafer evidence fusion, and Shapley-value-based attribution over attack categories. They demonstrate the framework in three experiments: a conversational analytics agent evaluated across 10 OCEAN personas with 240 human judgments; a customer-support agent tested on 5 tasks × 4 persona variants; and adversarial stress tests of 5 tasks to measure pre-existing brittleness and attribute risk to system vs. action/tool layers.

Result: The experiments show that personality variation meaningfully affects agent performance and surfaces failure modes that uniform testing misses. They observe issues such as cross-domain leakage, contextual drift, a 0.27-point quality gap across personas, and large differences in pass rates (50% vs. 100%) on the same task depending on persona. The adversarial Risk Analyser reveals significant trajectory-level brittleness even without perturbations (minimum value V_min=0.375), and Shapley analysis indicates that most risk is attributable to system and tool/infrastructure layers (46% system, 38% action), rather than other factors.

Conclusion: AgentWorld provides a richer, more realistic, and adversarially-aware evaluation framework for agentic information-retrieval systems by incorporating personality-diverse users, structured fault analysis, and trajectory-level risk quantification. The results indicate that standard uniform, scripted evaluations and simple pass^k metrics are insufficient: they miss important failure modes and underestimate brittleness. Incorporating personality variation and adversarial stress testing is necessary to properly evaluate and improve such agents, and the framework’s export mechanisms can directly support targeted fine-tuning and system refinement.

Abstract: Evaluation of agentic information retrieval remains limited to scripted interactions with uniform users, missing both natural personality diversity and adversarial brittleness. We present AgentWorld, a simulation framework combining (i)Big Five (OCEAN) personality-driven user populations with stateful tool-use environments; (ii)the pass$^k$ consistency metric with structured fault classification, partial-credit scoring, and dual-control handoff verification; (iii)score-thresholded training-data export in six fine-tuning formats; and (iv)an adversarial Risk Analyser that snapshots required-intermediate-state spines, branches Monte-Carlo rollouts under four task-aware perturbation types, and quantifies risk via $ΔP / ΔT$ scoring, Dempster--Shafer evidence fusion, and Shapley attack-category attribution. Three experiments demonstrate the framework: a conversational analytics agent across 10 OCEAN personas (240 evaluator judgments); a customer-support agent across 5 tasks $\times$ 4 persona variants; and adversarial stress-testing of 5 tasks revealing pre-existing trajectory brittleness ($V_{\min}=0.375$ without perturbation) and tool/infrastructure-layer attack dominance (Shapley: 46% system, 38% action). Personality variation surfaces failure modes uniform testing cannot expose---cross-domain leakage, contextual drift, a 0.27-point quality gap, and 50% vs. 100% pass-rate across personas on the same task---while the Risk Analyser quantifies trajectory-level brittleness that pass$^k$ alone cannot measure.

</details>


### [105] [EMRB: A Multi-Level Benchmark for Evaluating LLM Reasoning over Raw Electromagnetic Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24086)
*Mingxu Zhang,Ying Sun,Yuhan Li,Yang Ji,Dazhong Shen,Ke Zhang,Shan Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces EMRB, a benchmark to test whether LLM-based code agents can analyze raw wireless I/Q data, and proposes a structured agent method, ReconPilot, which significantly improves performance across multiple LLM backbones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LLMs are increasingly used as code agents for scientific and engineering tasks, their capability to handle raw physical-layer measurements (specifically, raw I/Q samples in wireless communication) has not been systematically evaluated. Existing benchmarks usually rely on preprocessed, structured features, so they do not test whether models can discover and extract relevant quantities directly from raw measurements. The authors aim to fill this gap with a benchmark that reflects realistic low-level signal analysis tasks.

Method: The authors construct EMRB, a benchmark of 200 tasks built from 11 types of signals, each with verified ground truth. The tasks span 27 question types and five difficulty levels, ranging from basic signal detection and measurement to complex OFDM system design, and they provide only raw I/Q captures. LLMs must write and run code to analyze and interpret the raw data to answer questions. They evaluate 14 LLMs from different model families and also design ReconPilot, a structured multi-stage prompting framework that decomposes the workflow into signal reconnaissance, targeted analysis, and self-verification. ReconPilot is applied on three LLM backbones and multiple combinations to quantify its impact.

Result: Across 14 evaluated LLMs, performance on EMRB ranges from 24.1% to 78.9% overall. Performance strongly depends on task difficulty: average accuracy is 84.9% on basic measurement problems but drops to 21.2% on system design problems, showing that current models struggle with more complex end-to-end reasoning over raw signals. The ReconPilot method consistently improves results: for three backbones, it raises scores by 3.8 to 17.6 percentage points, and out of 15 backbone–configuration combinations, 13 see improvements when using ReconPilot.

Conclusion: LLMs, even when used as code-writing agents, are far from reliable at complex physical-layer reasoning directly from raw I/Q data, especially for higher-level system design tasks. EMRB provides a rigorous benchmark to quantify these capabilities and highlight gaps. The structured ReconPilot workflow demonstrates that carefully designed agentic scaffolding can substantially improve performance but does not fully close the gap on harder problems. The public release of EMRB and code aims to support further research on LLM-based signal analysis and more capable physical-layer reasoning agents.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as code agents for scientific and engineering analysis, but their ability to analyze raw physical-layer measurements remains untested. We introduce \textbf{EMRB} (\textbf{E}lectro\textbf{m}agnetic \textbf{R}easoning \textbf{B}enchmark), which evaluates whether LLMs can analyze raw I/Q data by writing and running code. EMRB contains 200 problems across five difficulty levels and 27 question types, from signal detection to OFDM design, generated from 11 signal types with verified ground truth. Unlike benchmarks built on preprocessed features or structured tables, EMRB provides only the raw capture; the quantities each question refers to must first be discovered through code. We evaluate 14 LLMs spanning proprietary, open-weight, and reasoning-oriented families. Scores range from 24.1\% to 78.9\%, with the mean dropping from 84.9\% on basic measurement to 21.2\% on system design. We also propose \textbf{ReconPilot}, a structured method that separates signal reconnaissance, targeted analysis, and self-verification. Across three backbones, ReconPilot raises the overall score by 3.8 to 17.6 points and improves 13 of 15 backbone-level combinations tested. All data and code are publicly released in \href{https://github.com/mingxuZhang2/EMRB}{\textcolor{blue}{our GitHub repository}}.

</details>


### [106] [Are Android GUI Agents Robust Against Runtime Anomalies? AnTrap: Evaluating Agents in Dynamic Adversarial Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24099)
*Guo Gan,Yilun Zhao,Cong Chen,Jinbiao Wei,Tingyu Song,Zheyuan Yang,Lin Fu,Hong Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AnTrap is a benchmark that injects realistic dynamic anomalies into GUI agent trajectories on Android to systematically test and analyze their robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GUI agents deployed on Android face dynamic anomalies such as unexpected pop-ups and incorrect actions, but benchmarks do not systematically evaluate how robust these agents are to such runtime issues. There is a need for a structured way to characterize these anomalies and a benchmark to test whether current models can handle them.

Method: The authors design AnTrap, a benchmark that perturbs GUI agent execution trajectories with dynamic anomalies while keeping tasks solvable. They propose a 4-layer taxonomy of anomalies (State, Thinking, Action, Round) with 10 subcategories and implement a construction pipeline that injects realistic adversarial conditions. They evaluate 16 state-of-the-art GUI models and also perform GRPO-based adversarial reinforcement learning in both clean and perturbed environments to see which anomaly types can be learned away and which reveal deeper reasoning limitations.

Result: Across 16 leading GUI models, all show strong vulnerability to dynamic anomalies, with even the best models experiencing substantial drops in performance under perturbations. GRPO training improves robustness against many single-step state and action level anomalies, but offers limited gains against more complex, deep contextual traps such as state deadlocks.

Conclusion: AnTrap exposes that modern GUI agents are not robust to realistic runtime anomalies, especially deep contextual traps. While adversarial reinforcement learning can mitigate simpler, single-step anomalies, certain categories of traps reflect intrinsic reasoning limitations that cannot be overcome simply by training in perturbed environments, highlighting the need for more fundamentally robust agent designs.

Abstract: GUI agents often encounter dynamic anomalies when deployed on Android devices, from unexpected pop-ups to action misuse, yet existing benchmarks lack systematic evaluation of agent robustness against runtime anomalies. We introduce AnTrap, a comprehensive benchmark that injects dynamic perturbations into agent execution trajectories. We propose a taxonomy organizing real-world anomalies into four layers (State, Thinking, Action and Round) with ten fine-grained subcategories, and develop a construction pipeline that preserves task solvability while introducing realistic adversarial conditions. Evaluating 16 leading GUI models, we reveal universal vulnerability to dynamic anomalies, with even the strongest models suffering significant performance degradation. Furthermore, we conduct GRPO training in both original and adversarial environments to validate our benchmark, separating environment-learnable anomalies from reasoning-bottlenecked ones. Our findings show that while single-step traps at state and action layers are largely addressable through adversarial reinforcement learning, deep contextual traps, like state deadlock, expose intrinsic limitations that cannot be resolved by training in environments with traps alone.

</details>


### [107] [ACE: A Self-Correcting Agentic Canvas Editor for Multi-Slide Presentation Automation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24103)
*JooYoung Jang,Taegyeong Lee,Jihyeon Park,Nojun Kwak*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ACE is a hierarchical, scene-graph-based canvas editor with specialized tools, paired with a content router (CARE) and a self-correction loop using a ground-truth-free judge, achieving better instruction following, speed, and cost than an HTML-based agentic pipeline for document design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-powered design editors work over legacy, flat document formats that only expose absolutely positioned elements. This forces agents to recompute coordinates, often breaking layouts. Moreover, in design tasks there is no single ground truth, so standard diff-against-reference evaluation unfairly penalizes valid alternative solutions. The paper aims to create an editing and evaluation framework that respects layout structure and can assess quality without relying on a unique reference output.

Method: The authors introduce ACE, an agentic canvas editor operating on a hierarchical scene graph instead of flat coordinates, and equip it with a presentation-focused action space of 98 tools. They pair ACE with CARE, a content-aware routing system that slices each deck and sends only the relevant portion to the LLM, greatly reducing input tokens. They also implement a self-correction loop driven by a ground-truth-free instruction-following judge that critiques the agent’s output in natural language, which is then fed back as the next instruction to refine the design. They benchmark ACE against an HTML-based agentic pipeline using the same LLM backbone, and run human and model-based judging, including out-of-loop judges, to validate performance and control for circularity.

Result: With the same backbone model, a single-turn scene-graph ACE editor already matches the performance of a multi-iteration HTML agentic pipeline. Adding the self-correction loop significantly improves instruction-following scores (4.23 vs 3.81 on a 94-task benchmark, statistically significant with paired p=0.010), while running 1.75× faster and at about 44% lower cost. Visual quality metrics (VQ means) are statistically similar, but human raters prefer ACE outputs overall (58.7% decisive win rate) and especially favor self-corrected outputs (81% preference). Results are consistent across three judge families, and out-of-loop judges retain about two-thirds of the self-correction gain, suggesting limited evaluation circularity. Approximately 66% of cases converge after one pass, and a strict-peak rollback strategy eliminates observed regressions.

Conclusion: Hierarchical scene-graph editing combined with a rich, presentation-oriented toolset and a content-aware routing scheme enables more reliable, efficient LLM-based document editing than flat HTML-style pipelines. A ground-truth-free instruction-following judge can effectively drive self-correction loops, improving alignment with user instructions without relying on a unique reference output and with bounded circularity. The system achieves better instruction following at lower cost and higher speed, with human raters consistently preferring its outputs, indicating that this approach is a practical path toward robust, deployable LLM agents for commercial design platforms.

Abstract: Commercial design platforms increasingly edit documents through large language model (LLM) agents, but two practical problems block reliable deployment: legacy document formats expose only \emph{flat}, absolutely positioned elements, so agents must recompute coordinates and routinely break layouts; and design has no unique ground truth, so diff-against-reference metrics penalize valid-but-different outputs. We present \textbf{ACE}, an agentic canvas editor over a \emph{hierarchical scene-graph} with a presentation-specialized action space (98 tools), paired with \textbf{CARE}, a content-aware router that feeds the agent only the relevant slice of each deck (avg.\ $\sim$89\% input-token reduction), and a \emph{self-correction} loop driven by a \emph{ground-truth-free} instruction-following (IF) judge whose natural-language critique is fed back as the next-turn instruction. With a fixed backbone, a scene-graph editor in a \emph{single turn} already matches a same-backbone \emph{agentic} HTML pipeline that iterates internally; adding self-correction lifts ACE significantly above it on instruction following (IF 4.23 vs.\ 3.81 on the full 94-task benchmark, paired $p{=}.010$, replicated by an out-of-loop judge) at 1.75$\times$ the speed and $\sim$44\% lower cost. VQ means are statistically indistinguishable, but 26 blind raters prefer ACE overall (58.7\% decisive win-rate) and prefer the self-corrected output 81\% of the time; the ranking is invariant across three judge families, and out-of-loop judges retain two-thirds of the self-correction gain, bounding circularity. 66\% of cases halt after one pass, and a strict-peak rollback removes every observed regression.

</details>


### [108] [Scalable Question-Centric Text-to-Image Evaluation: Reliable Ranking, Fine-Grained Diagnosis, and Cost-Aware Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24112)
*Shaoan Zhao,Fang Zhao,Xueqiang Guo,Xinpei Su,Huanlin Gao,Qiang Hui,Ting Lu,Fuyuan Shi,Chao Tan,Bikun Yang,Kai Wang,Shiguo Lian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They introduce QC-T2I-Bench, a question-centric benchmark that decomposes text-to-image prompts into atomic, dependency-structured questions to more precisely evaluate model capabilities, distinguish basic vs. compositional failures, and enable routing between T2I models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing T2I benchmarks give similar overall scores to different models and use coarse prompt-level or category-level metrics. These approaches blur where and why models fail, conflate simple and complex prompts, and cannot clearly distinguish basic capability gaps from failures under additional compositional constraints. The authors want a fine-grained, interpretable evaluation that better supports practical model selection and diagnosis.

Method: They propose QC-T2I-Bench, which transforms open-ended prompts into atomic, attributed questions and organizes them using Davidsonian Scene Graphs (DSGs) that encode dependencies among requirements. They apply hierarchy-constrained question aggregation so that if a prerequisite question fails, dependent questions are excluded and simple vs. complex prompts do not get the same weight. Using the DSG, they compute joint success across all requirements in a prompt, track repeated entities across prompts, and differentiate basic realization errors from failures when more constraints are added. They run this framework on multiple open-source English and Chinese T2I models and also repurpose the resulting question-level records to design a training-free, cost-aware router among models.

Result: Empirical evaluation on several open-source T2I models shows that the question-centric metric yields stable rankings and detailed diagnostic information. They find that joint completion rates drop sharply as compositional complexity increases: from 80.7% for components involving two capabilities to 37.2% when seven or more capabilities are involved. Their cost-aware routing strategy, built from the question-level evidence without extra training, achieves an ERNIE benchmark score of 89.51 while reducing GPU seconds per megapixel (GPU-s/MP) by 21.3% compared to a baseline.

Conclusion: Question-centric, dependency-aware evaluation via QC-T2I-Bench provides more reliable and interpretable assessment of T2I models than prompt-level scores. It reveals how performance degrades with compositional complexity, separates basic from compositional failures, and supports practical tools like efficient routing between models. The approach works across languages (English and Chinese) and can guide both model development and deployment decisions.

Abstract: Modern text-to-image (T2I) models often have similar total scores but different strengths, making practical selection difficult. Fine-grained benchmarks decompose prompts into questions, yet often return them to prompt scores and fixed categories, weakening attribution and ignoring complexity. Related requirements are also scored separately or as one total, obscuring basic versus compositional failure. We present QC-T2I-Bench, a question-centric framework that converts open prompts into attributed atomic questions and organizes their dependencies with Davidsonian Scene Graphs (DSGs). We use hierarchy-constrained question aggregation to exclude downstream questions after a prerequisite fails and to prevent simple and complex prompts from receiving the same total weight. We then use the DSG structure to measure joint success within prompts and compare repeated entities across prompts, separating basic realization failures from failures under additional requirements. We evaluate multiple open-source T2I models on English and Chinese prompts. The resulting question-level evidence supports reliable ranking and fine-grained diagnosis: joint completion falls from 80.7\% for components with two capabilities to 37.2\% for those with seven or more. Finally, we reuse the same records for training-free routing; our cost-aware router matches ERNIE's 89.51-point estimate with 21.3\% less GPU-s/MP.

</details>


### [109] [AHEAD: Adaptive Hindsight with Environment-Augmented Distillation for Agentic RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24114)
*Xiaolong Jin,Dingmin Wang,Vijay Lingam,Varun Kumar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes AHEAD, a step-aware reinforcement learning framework for training multi-turn LLM agents that combines environment feedback with LLM-generated corrective hints specifically on error steps, improving task success and sample efficiency over standard GRPO and prior self-distillation methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Trajectory-level rewards in RL for multi-turn LLM agents give the same credit or blame to all steps, making it hard to pinpoint which decisions caused success or failure. Self-distillation with privileged information can provide step-level supervision, but prior work applies the same kind of guidance uniformly to all steps, overlooking that routine steps need little extra help while critical error steps need strong corrective signals that environment feedback alone cannot provide. The authors want a framework that exploits this asymmetry and provides targeted, step-aware supervision to improve learning efficiency and performance.

Method: They introduce AHEAD, a step-aware RL framework built on GRPO. A teacher policy receives dense environment feedback on every step as a grounded signal, but additionally receives LLM-generated corrective hints specifically on steps identified as errors. Thus, different supervision sources are matched to different step types: routine steps rely on environment feedback, while error steps get extra corrective direction. This setup is integrated into GRPO with minimal modifications, effectively augmenting outcome-based RL with targeted self-distillation signals at the step level.

Result: On three benchmarks—ALFWorld, WebShop, and a search-based QA setting—and across three model scales, AHEAD outperforms standard GRPO and prior self-distillation baselines. For a 7B model, AHEAD improves task success rates by +13.3 percentage points on ALFWorld and +11.0 points on WebShop compared to GRPO, reaches target success levels in fewer training steps, and completes tasks within stricter interaction budgets, demonstrating improved sample efficiency and effectiveness.

Conclusion: Step-aware supervision that distinguishes routine from error steps and allocates different guidance sources accordingly leads to better training of multi-turn LLM agents than uniform trajectory-level rewards or indiscriminate self-distillation. AHEAD shows that combining dense environment feedback with targeted corrective hints on error steps, in a minimally invasive extension of GRPO, substantially boosts success rates and efficiency on diverse interactive benchmarks.

Abstract: Training multi-turn LLM agents with reinforcement learning typically relies on trajectory-level rewards, which assign a uniform advantage to every step and cannot identify which decisions led to success or failure. Self-distillation methods can provide finer-grained supervision by augmenting RL with privileged information. However, existing approaches usually apply the same type of privileged information to every step in an indistinguishable manner, ignoring a key asymmetry: routine steps need little additional guidance, while critical error steps require corrective direction that environment feedback alone cannot provide. We propose AHEAD, a step-aware framework that matches different supervision sources to different step types. The teacher receives environment feedback on all steps as a grounded dense signal, and additionally receives LLM-generated corrective hints on error steps to supply the direction that environment feedback lacks. The method introduces minimal changes to the standard GRPO algorithm. Across ALFWorld, WebShop, and Search-based QA, and across three model scales, AHEAD raises task success (+13.3 points on ALFWorld and +11.0 on WebShop at 7B over GRPO), reaches a given success rate in fewer training steps, and solves tasks within tighter interaction budgets than outcome-only RL and prior self-distillation baselines.

</details>


### [110] [Paritok-4B: Intent-Conditioned Context Compression for Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24188)
*Jiayu Shi,Luzhuo Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Paritok-4B is a 4B-parameter, extractive, intent-conditioned compressor specialized for coding-agent trajectories that cuts context to about one-quarter of its size while preserving most problem-solving quality and eliminating per-token compressor costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coding agents spend most tokens repeatedly sending large code files and tool outputs to LLMs. Existing prompt compressors are prose-oriented: they paraphrase, rename identifiers, and often discard exactly the spans of code the agent must later edit, which breaks tools and degrades performance. There is a need for a code-aware, reliable compressor that keeps structurally critical text (identifiers, paths, numbers) while still achieving strong compression and maintaining solve accuracy, and that is cheap enough to self-host rather than using expensive frontier LLMs as compressors.

Method: The authors design Paritok-4B, a LoRA-adapted Qwen3-4B model specialized for compressing coding-agent trajectories. The model is (1) extractive: it selects subsequences from the input rather than rewriting them, aiming to preserve exact identifiers, file paths, and numbers, and (2) intent-conditioned: it takes as input the agent’s current task or intent and focuses its selection decisions within a key retained segment, choosing which lines to keep instead of drastically changing the total amount of retained content. They distill a gpt-4.1-mini teacher model that provides compression demonstrations over 67,074 real OpenHands coding-agent trajectories, filtering down to 40,606 validated training examples, and fine-tune Qwen3-4B with a LoRA adapter. They then evaluate compression ratio and task performance on SWE-bench Lite, including both idealized inputs and realistic `cat -n` line-numbered agent contexts, and compare against frontier LLMs (gpt-4.1-mini and gpt-5) used as compressors. Statistical tests such as paired comparisons of intent relevance and McNemar’s test on solve outcomes are applied to quantify impact on solution quality.

Result: Paritok-4B is highly extractive: 96.0% of its emitted identifiers, paths, and numbers already appear in its input, with 96.2% on held-out SWE-bench Lite, confirming minimal rewriting. Its intent conditioning yields that retained lines are on average 0.067 points more intent-relevant than removed ones (95% CI [+0.056, +0.078]), showing that the model selectively keeps task-relevant content rather than simply compressing uniformly. Across all 300 SWE-bench Lite instances, it compresses context to 25.7% of the original size—about 2.0× stronger compression than a gpt-4.1-mini-based compressor (50.2%) and 2.4× stronger than gpt-5 (61.9%)—while retaining 86.5% of the uncompressed single-shot solve quality. When evaluated on real agent `cat -n` inputs, it compresses to 27.8% while retaining 89.3% of solve quality, with 30 tasks solved only in the uncompressed setting and 17 only in the compressed setting; a McNemar exact test yields p=0.079, indicating no statistically significant drop in solve rate at this sample size. Economically, Paritok-4B is a 264 MB adapter that runs on a single 24 GB GPU with effectively zero marginal per-token compressor cost, whereas using gpt-5 as a compressor is net-negative at list prices, costing more than the tokens it saves downstream.

Conclusion: A small, specialized, extractive, and intent-conditioned compressor like Paritok-4B can aggressively compress coding-agent context (to about one quarter of its original size) while preserving most task-solving performance and drastically improving cost-efficiency relative to using large frontier models as compressors. Its high reuse of input identifiers and paths makes it particularly suitable for code settings where exact strings matter. Given its open-source release (weights, data, evaluation scripts) and ability to self-host on a single GPU, it offers a practical, economically favorable way to scale coding agents without incurring substantial additional token or model-serving costs.

Abstract: Coding agents re-send large file reads and tool outputs to a frontier LLM every turn, and this context dominates their token bill. General-purpose prompt compressors are trained on prose and suit code poorly: they paraphrase identifiers and drop the exact spans an agent needs to edit. We present Paritok-4B, a 4B LoRA compressor for coding-agent trajectories built on two commitments. It is extractive: it selects spans rather than rewriting them, and 96.0% of the identifiers, paths, and numbers it emits already appear in its input, holding at 96.2% on held-out SWE-bench Lite output. It is intent-conditioned: told the agent's current task, it acts chiefly inside a retained segment, selecting which lines survive (retained lines are +0.067 more intent-relevant than removed ones, paired 95% CI [+0.056, +0.078]) rather than changing how much is retained. We distil a gpt-4.1-mini teacher over 67,074 real OpenHands trajectories into 40,606 validated examples and fine-tune Qwen3-4B. On all 300 SWE-bench Lite instances, Paritok-4B compresses agent context to 25.7% of its size, 2.0x harder than a gpt-4.1-mini compressor (50.2%) and 2.4x harder than gpt-5 (61.9%), while retaining 86.5% of uncompressed single-shot solve quality. Fed the cat -n line-numbered input real agents produce, it compresses slightly less (27.8%) and retains more (89.3%); there the paired test is informative, with 30 instances solved only uncompressed and 17 only compressed, an exact McNemar p=0.079, so at this sample size compressing context to roughly a quarter of its size does not significantly reduce the solve rate. The model is a 264 MB adapter that self-hosts on one 24 GB GPU with no per-token compressor fee, which at list prices decides the economics: gpt-5 as a compressor is net-negative, costing more than the downstream tokens it saves. Weights, data, and evaluation scripts are open (Apache 2.0).

</details>


### [111] [Robust Code RL via Faulty-Code-Driven Test case Synthesis and Dense Reward Shaping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24135)
*Yiwen Zhang,Xiaodong Yan,Zhenyu Huang,Deng Zhao,Liang Jiang,Qing Cui,Zujie Wen,Zhiqiang Zhang,Jun Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes RobustTests, a framework to improve reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (RLVR) for code generation by generating better test cases and rewards, reducing reward hacking and improving LLM coding performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RLVR for code generation relies heavily on automatically generated test cases to validate code. When these tests have poor coverage or quality, models can pass tests with incorrect logic (reward hacking), leading to biased rewards, false positives/negatives, and degraded policies. The authors want to make RLVR more robust by improving test generation and reward design so that models truly learn correct program behavior rather than exploiting weaknesses in the test suite.

Method: RobustTests uses a faulty-code-driven test synthesis strategy: it collects "near-correct" faulty code solutions and uses them to guide generation of new test cases that specifically target latent logical errors. It adds validator agents plus behavioral feature clustering to filter out invalid or redundant synthetic tests. To further reduce noise from imperfect synthetic tests, it introduces a stepwise dense reward function based on test pass rates, giving fine-grained rewards instead of a coarse pass/fail signal. Using this pipeline, the authors construct an augmented test dataset for CodeContests, covering a wider variety of faulty-code behaviors.

Result: Applying RobustTests to RL fine-tuning of Qwen3-32B on a moderately difficult subset of CodeContests leads to an absolute 3% improvement on the LiveCodeBench benchmark compared with baseline RLVR methods. The new test-augmented dataset shows broader coverage of faulty-code scenarios and higher diagnostic power.

Conclusion: RobustTests improves the robustness and effectiveness of reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards for code generation by synthesizing higher-quality, fault-targeting tests, filtering them via validator agents and clustering, and using a finer-grained reward scheme. This reduces reward hacking, better captures logical errors, and translates into measurable performance gains on downstream code benchmarks.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (RLVR) has emerged as a pivotal technique for enhancing the code generation capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). However, the efficacy of RLVR in coding implementations is fundamentally limited by the comprehensiveness of test cases, because insufficient test coverage in code validation often causes false positives, further leading to reward hacking and policy degradation. To mitigate the reward bias stemming from the suboptimal quality of current automated generation methods, we propose the RobustTests framework, which introduces a faulty-code-driven test case synthesis strategy that leverages "near correct" faulty codes to guide the model in precisely capturing latent logical discrepancies and further integrates validator agents with behavioral feature clustering to facilitate the granular filtering of invalid and redundant test cases. To address false negatives caused by inherent hallucination noise in synthetic test cases, RobustTests also incorporates a stepwise dense reward function based on pass rates, bolstering training robustness through fine-grained feedback. By employing this pipeline, we construct a high-quality dataset that augmented the test cases in CodeContests, encompassing a broader spectrum of faulty code scenarios and significantly enhances diagnostic utility. Experimental results demonstrate that, by leveraging a moderately challenging subset of problems from CodeContests for training, RL fine-tuning of Qwen3-32B via RobustTests achieves an absolute 3% performance gain on the LiveCodeBench benchmark compared to baseline methods, confirming the effectiveness of the RobustTests framework in advancing the code generation proficiency of LLMs.

</details>


### [112] [Preference Data Selection for Mitigating the Alignment Tax in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24192)
*Minsu Kim,Jianxun Lian,Xing Xie,Steven Euijong Whang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes BALIGN, a data-selection strategy for alignment that reduces catastrophic forgetting while maintaining alignment quality by filtering harmful preference data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Aligning large language models to human preferences often causes an alignment tax: models lose pre-trained general capabilities due to catastrophic forgetting during preference optimization. Existing works mainly adjust optimization procedures or architectures, but they overlook how intrinsic properties of the alignment (preference) data itself drive this degradation. The paper aims to understand and control those data characteristics to preserve capabilities while still aligning the model effectively.

Method: The authors analyze the gradient dynamics of preference optimization and identify three data-centric factors that influence parameter drift: (1) the log-probability margin under the reference model between chosen and rejected responses, (2) the token length difference between the chosen and rejected responses, and (3) the TF-IDF similarity between preference samples and general capability corpora. They define a composite risk score that combines these orthogonal features, and propose BALIGN, a balanced data selection strategy that filters out high-risk preference samples prior to training. They then run preference optimization using this curated dataset and evaluate alignment and capability retention.

Result: Using standard human preference datasets, BALIGN yields models that retain their foundational, pre-trained capabilities far better than baselines while achieving similar or better alignment performance. The approach consistently attains a superior Pareto frontier between alignment and capability preservation, and does so with minimal extra computational cost due to lightweight pre-filtering of data rather than heavy changes to training or model architecture.

Conclusion: Data-centric alignment via targeted filtering of risky preference samples can substantially reduce catastrophic forgetting in large language models without sacrificing alignment quality. BALIGN offers a simple, low-overhead strategy that balances alignment gains and capability preservation by scoring and excluding preference examples most likely to cause harmful parameter drift.

Abstract: Aligning large language models to human preferences is crucial for real-world deployment but frequently incurs an alignment tax, leading to the catastrophic forgetting of pre-trained general capabilities. While previous works primarily frame this problem as an optimization or architectural challenge, the inherent characteristics of preference data that drive this degradation remain largely underexplored. In this paper, we propose BALIGN, a balanced data selection strategy that explicitly mitigates catastrophic forgetting while optimizing alignment efficacy. Through theoretical and empirical analyses of the preference optimization gradient, we identify three key data-centric features that dictate parameter drift: the reference model's log-probability margin, the token length difference between chosen and rejected responses, and the TF-IDF similarity to general capability corpora. By aggregating these orthogonal features into a unified composite risk score, BALIGN systematically filters out high-risk preference samples that disrupt intrinsic model parameters or provide minimal alignment utility. Extensive experiments on standard human preference datasets demonstrate that BALIGN strongly preserves foundational capabilities without compromising alignment gains, consistently achieving the optimal Pareto frontier with minimal computational overhead.

</details>


### [113] [OmniJudge or OmniBias? Diagnosing Multimodal Judges through Balanced, Decoupled Lenses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24160)
*Guangzheng Hu,Ziyue Jiang,Weixu Qiao,Lixin Zhang,Jianye Kang,Yuru Wu,Rong Bao,Niantong Li,Wei Wang,Ziyi Cheng,Xinfa Zhu,HangRui Hu,Ting He,Bing Zhao,Lin Qu,Hu Wei,Jin Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces D3-Omni, a balanced, fine-grained benchmark to diagnose where multimodal “OmniJudge” models actually fail across text-to-image, text-to-video, and text-to-speech evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal judge models are widely used to score and annotate T2I, T2V, and TTS generations, but current benchmarks and training data are skewed toward positive examples and mix multiple failure types together. This lets judges appear accurate overall while still missing important errors, especially on underrepresented or entangled capability dimensions. The authors want a way to uncover these hidden blind spots with precise, per-capability diagnostics.

Method: They build D3-Omni, a diagnostic benchmark covering 53 orthogonal binary capability dimensions (17 for T2I, 22 for T2V, 14 for TTS) and 10,671 examples in total. Starting from fully verified positive “seed” outputs, they generate negative cases via controlled prompt rewriting and atomic perturbations that each isolate exactly one capability dimension. The dataset is designed to be: (1) Dual-balanced, reducing scarcity of negative samples and strongly balancing labels per dimension; (2) Decoupled, so each sample reflects a single, clearly attributable error type; and (3) Dynamic, constructed to increasingly target underrepresented label regions as generative models improve. They ensure near 1:1 positive/negative balance per dimension and a roughly uniform distribution over total error counts.

Result: Using D3-Omni to evaluate strong existing OmniJudge models, they find several systematic weaknesses: (1) performance is notably worse on modality-specific aspects than on more generic dimensions; (2) models are much better at confirming that requirements are met than at spotting when requirements are violated; and (3) they often collapse multiple, ostensibly distinct attributes into a single decision, indicating poor disentanglement of fine-grained factors. This shows that models can achieve high aggregate accuracy yet still have consistent blind spots.

Conclusion: Balanced, decoupled, and dynamically constructed diagnostics like D3-Omni reveal hidden failure modes of multimodal OmniJudge models that standard aggregate metrics miss. Such benchmarks are necessary to accurately understand and improve fine-grained multimodal understanding, especially around modality-specific reasoning, violation detection, and disentangling distinct attributes.

Abstract: Multimodal understanding models that can jointly judge text-to-image (T2I), text-to-video (T2V) and text-to-speech (TTS) generation are increasingly used as "OmniJudges" for evaluation and automatic annotation. How reliably they understand what they score remains unclear, since existing benchmarks and training data tend to overemphasize positive examples and to conflate distinct failure modes, so a judge may score well without recognizing failures while its capability gaps stay hidden. Motivated by this, we introduce D3-Omni, a balanced and decoupled benchmark for diagnosing fine-grained multimodal understanding, covering 53 orthogonal binary dimensions (17/22/14) and 10,671 samples (3,526/1,998/5,147) across the three tasks. Rather than re-generating outputs, which may leak information across dimensions, we fix verified fully positive seeds and derive negatives through controlled prompt rewriting and atomic, dimension-isolating perturbations. The resulting D3 design is Dual-balanced, which helps alleviate negative-sample scarcity and per-dimension label imbalance; Decoupled, so that each error is attributable to a single capability; and Dynamic, steering construction toward under-represented regions of the label distribution as generative models improve.The suite reaches near 1:1 per-dimension parity and a uniform distribution over all total-score levels. Under this balanced view, even strong OmniJudges tend to struggle on modality-related dimensions, to confirm satisfied requirements far more reliably than they detect violated ones, and to treat nominally distinct attributes as largely a single decision, suggesting that aggregate accuracy may hide systematic blind spots that a balanced and decoupled lens can help expose and, in turn, address.

</details>


### [114] [Constraint-Guided Enterprise Data Mapping with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24218)
*Sebastian Monka,Pramod Anantharam,Thien Vo Minh,Lavdim Halilaj*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Constraint-Guided Mapping (CGM), a neuro-symbolic method that uses hard admissibility constraints to drastically prune the candidate space for enterprise entity alignment, then applies neural ranking and bounded LLM disambiguation within that feasible set, achieving large accuracy and cost gains over LLM-only matching.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise entity alignment across heterogeneous, semi-structured data sources is challenging due to implicit attributes, unit and granularity mismatches, and evolving schemas. Manual or spreadsheet-based matching does not scale, while pure LLM-based approaches, though semantically strong, can violate structural and physical invariants, producing operationally invalid mappings. The paper aims to reconcile high semantic recall with strict adherence to structural constraints in a scalable, auditable way.
