Method: The authors propose Constraint-Guided Mapping (CGM), a three-stage neuro-symbolic framework: (i) formalize schema-grounded admissibility constraints as metadata mc = <tau_c, delta_c>, where tau_c is the constraint type (e.g., key, unit, structural) and delta_c provides executable relation and normalization logic; (ii) use these constraints not just as validators but as operators on the hypothesis space to generate and prune candidate correspondences, employing a cascade relaxation strategy so that even under noise the feasible set is nonempty; and (iii) within this constraint-restricted feasible set, apply neural ranking and limited LLM-based disambiguation to select the final alignment. Constraints are automatically discovered and can be refined by experts, and they are applied as hard filters wherever structural invariants are match-determining.

Result: On a structural-decoy benchmark, applying hard admissibility constraints reduces the candidate space by about 480x while preserving ground-truth matches; ablation shows that this constraint gate, rather than the LLM, is the main contributor to performance, improving F1 from 0.08 to 0.66. The constraint framework is model-agnostic and introduces no additional inference cost, allowing a smaller model with constraints to match the performance of a frontier LLM used without constraints at roughly 28x lower cost. Across seven enterprise datasets ("makes"), the approach achieves macro F1 of 0.70 with automatically discovered constraints, reducing expert effort by roughly 7x relative to spreadsheet-based matching. Additional experiments on the Valentine benchmark provide external validation and show that constraints should be enforced as hard only when structural invariants uniquely determine matches.

Conclusion: Constraint-Guided Mapping (CGM) demonstrates that encoding schema-level structural invariants as hard admissibility constraints, and using them as hypothesis-space operators rather than post-hoc checks, yields more accurate, cheaper, and more auditable enterprise entity alignment than LLM-only methods. The gains are primarily due to constraint-based pruning, not model size, and generalize across multiple enterprise settings with automatically learned, expert-tunable constraints. The work argues for careful, domain-aware use of hard vs. soft constraints, applying hard constraints only where structure decisively determines matches.

Abstract: Enterprise entity alignment must handle semi-structured records, implicit attributes, and unit or granularity mismatches. Manual matching is still common in practice, but does not scale as schemas and providers evolve. LLM-only matching improves semantic recall, yet can violate structural and physical invariants, producing fluent yet operationally invalid correspondences.
  We propose constraint-guided mapping (CGM), a neuro-symbolic method with three stages: (i) schema-grounded admissibility constraints with metadata mc = <tau_c, delta_c>, where tau_c denotes the constraint type and delta_c provides executable relation and normalization logic; (ii) constraint-restricted candidate generation with cascade relaxation to guarantee a nonempty feasible set under noise; and (iii) neural ranking with bounded LLM disambiguation restricted to that feasible set.
  Methodologically, constraints operate as hypothesis-space operators rather than post-hoc validators, enabling controlled degradation under relaxation and auditable, human-guidable decisions. On a controlled structural-decoy benchmark, hard admissibility shrinks the candidate space by ~480x without dropping the GT, and a layer-by-layer ablation shows this gate, not the LLM, is the decisive lift (F1 0.08 to 0.66). The benefit is model-independent and adds no extra inference cost: a small model with constraints matches a frontier LLM used without them at ~28x lower cost. The method, not a single tuned configuration, transfers across seven enterprise makes (macro F1 0.70), each under its own automatically discovered, expert-refinable constraints, and lowers expert effort by ~7x versus spreadsheet workflows. Public Valentine results add an external ranking sanity check and mark the boundary: constraints should be hard only where structural invariants are match-determining.

</details>


### [115] [Task-Adaptive Rubrics for GUI Reward Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24174)
*Tao Xiong,Xavier Hu,Wenkai Wang,Qinzhuo Wu,Changqiao Wu,Pengzhi Gao,Wei Liu,Jian Luan,Shengyu Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes AdaptRubric, a framework that builds task-adaptive rubrics for evaluating GUI agent outcomes, improving reward modeling and leading to better task success in both offline and online settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GUI reward verifiers rely on generic or implicitly learned judging criteria that are not sufficiently tailored to each individual task. This can cause criteria to be too generic, miss explicit constraints in user instructions, or be overly strict by adding unstated requirements. A more task-adaptive way to construct success criteria is needed so that outcome rewards more faithfully reflect whether the agent actually met the user’s instruction for that specific task instance.

Method: The authors introduce AdaptRubric, a coarse-to-fine rubrics framework. First, at a category-level coarse stage, the system routes the user instruction to a GUI task family and retrieves reusable, task-family-specific criteria (coarse rubric retrieval). Then, at an instance-level fine stage, it generates a more detailed rubric that captures concrete values, scopes, and constraints directly from the current instruction (fine rubric generation). This rubric is then used as the judging criteria for assigning outcome rewards to GUI trajectories.

Result: In experiments on offline reward evaluation and online reinforcement learning for GUI agents, AdaptRubric outperforms prior reward verifiers. Quantitatively, it improves F1 score by 3.6 points over the baseline average under a matched image (observation) budget and increases task success rate by 4.23 points when used for online RL optimization.

Conclusion: Task-adaptive rubric construction via the proposed coarse-to-fine AdaptRubric framework leads to more accurate outcome reward modeling for GUI agents. By leveraging both category-level reusable criteria and instance-specific constraints, AdaptRubric provides better-aligned judging standards, which translates into measurable gains in reward evaluation quality and end-to-end GUI task success.

Abstract: Recent studies on GUI agents have increasingly focused on outcome reward modeling, which assigns outcome rewards by judging whether an executed trajectory satisfies the success criteria implied by the user instruction. Existing GUI reward verifiers, however, often under-specify how these criteria should be constructed for each task instance. Whether using generic rubric structures or implicit model reasoning, their judging criteria are not sufficiently task-adaptive: they can transfer checks across tasks, overlook concrete constraints in the current instruction, or become overly strict by enforcing unstated requirements. To address this limitation, we propose AdaptRubric, a Coarse-to-Fine Rubrics Framework that constructs task-adaptive judging criteria through a category-level coarse stage and an instance-level fine stage. AdaptRubric performs category-level coarse rubric retrieval by routing the instruction to a GUI task family and retrieving reusable task-family criteria, then conducts instance-level fine rubric generation to surface compact cues for concrete values, scopes, and constraints in the current instruction. Across offline reward evaluation and online reinforcement learning optimization, AdaptRubric consistently outperforms prior reward agents, improving F1 by 3.6 points over the baseline average under a matched image budget and yielding a 4.23-point task-success gain.

</details>


### [116] [RePolicy: Reinforcement Learning for Safety-Policy Invocation in Agent Safeguards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24275)
*Houcheng Jiang,Boxuan Zhang,Qiyong Zhong,Junfeng Fang,Xiang Wang,Xiangnan He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RePolicy is a reinforcement-learning-based safeguard that learns when and how to invoke safety policies for language model agents, enabling robust, policy-aware safety judgments over entire trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods for enforcing safety policies on language model agents mostly use prompting or supervised fine-tuning, which are brittle: they generalize poorly to unseen trajectories and cannot easily adapt when the applicable policy set or policy wording changes. There is a need for a safeguard that can flexibly interpret and apply a dynamic library of safety policies to full agent execution traces.

Method: The authors introduce RePolicy, a safeguard model trained to process an agent’s full trajectory together with a dynamic policy library. RePolicy selects the relevant policy, then generates a policy-grounded rationale and a safety judgment. The system is first initialized with supervised learning on a new dataset, PolicyTraj-20K, and then optimized with a GRPO-style reinforcement learning procedure using verifiable rewards and perturbations of the policy context to improve robustness in invoking the correct policies.

Result: Across six different agent safety benchmarks, RePolicy shows strong performance in detecting safety issues and demonstrates robust selection and use of the appropriate policies, even when the policy context is varied or perturbed.

Conclusion: Learning safety-policy invocation via reinforcement learning enables a safeguard that can better generalize across agent trajectories and dynamic policy libraries than prompt- or SFT-only approaches. RePolicy provides more reliable, policy-grounded rationales and safety judgments and remains robust under changes to the policy context, indicating its suitability for practical deployment in safeguarding language model agents.

Abstract: Safeguarding language model agents requires assessing complete execution trajectories under context-dependent safety policies. Existing policy-aware safeguards mainly rely on prompting or supervised fine-tuning, limiting their ability to adapt to unseen trajectories and changing policy contexts. We propose RePolicy, an agent safeguard that learns safety-policy invocation through reinforcement learning. Given an agent trajectory and a dynamic policy library, RePolicy invokes the applicable policy and uses its content to produce a policy-grounded rationale and safety judgment. We construct PolicyTraj-20K to support supervised initialization, followed by GRPO with verifiable rewards and policy-context perturbation. Experiments across six agent safety benchmarks show that RePolicy achieves strong overall safety-detection performance and robust policy invocation under varying policy contexts.

</details>


### [117] [The Invisible Editorial Layer: Formalizing Undisclosed Inference-Time Steering, Probability Placement, and the Attribution Problem in Deployed Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24662)
*Augusto Camargo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that modern LLM deployments include an often-hidden inference layer that can systematically modify model outputs after inference but before token sampling, creating framing biases that are not attributable to model weights alone, and it analyzes the technical and regulatory implications of such “inference-time” interventions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations and governance of LLMs mostly assume that model behavior is fully determined by weights, data, and prompts. However, real deployments can introduce additional, opaque control via decoding- and logit-level interventions (e.g., controlled generation, watermarking). The authors are motivated to show that this extra layer can introduce political, ideological, or commercial framing bias, which is largely unexamined in terms of accountability, security, and regulation.

Method: The paper is conceptual and analytical rather than empirical. It (1) formalizes a distinction between the base model and the deployed system; (2) defines and analyzes the Inference Attribution Problem under conditions of limited observability; (3) introduces the notion of Probability Placement as a conceptual advertising mechanism operating via probability shifts; and (4) proposes Inference Policy Transparency as a governance principle, mapping these concepts to existing legal frameworks such as the EU AI Act, the Digital Services Act, and FTC doctrines.

Result: The authors derive three core conceptual contributions: (1) a framework showing why one cannot, in general, causally attribute observed bias to model weights if inference-time interventions are possible and undisclosed; (2) the proposal of Probability Placement as a powerful but potentially covert channel for commercial influence that acts by shifting token probabilities instead of inserting obvious ad content; and (3) an articulated requirement for transparent, auditable inference policies, connected to specific provisions in existing EU and US regulatory regimes.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that LLM behavior cannot be fully understood or governed by focusing only on model weights, training data, and prompts. Undisclosed inference-time interventions can introduce systematic framing bias and create novel vectors for commercial and ideological influence. Therefore, governance and compliance regimes should explicitly recognize the deployment-layer inference policy, address the Inference Attribution Problem, and require sufficient transparency and auditability of inference-time modifications in line with emerging AI and digital service regulations.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are commonly evaluated under the assumption that their observable behavior is primarily determined by model weights, training data, alignment procedures, and user prompts. This view is incomplete. Modern inference pipelines may systematically modify the probability distribution produced by a model immediately before token selection, creating an additional layer of control between frozen weights and observed text.
  While controlled generation (e.g., PPLM, GeDi, DExperts, FUDGE) and text-watermarking systems (e.g., SynthID-Text) demonstrate the technical maturity of decoding- and logit-level interventions, the governance, security, and economic implications of an undisclosed inference policy remain comparatively underexplored. This paper examines the emergence of inference-time framing bias: the systematic modification of generated language toward political, ideological, institutional, or commercial frames via interventions applied after model inference but before token sampling.
  We formalize the operational reality Model != Deployed System and introduce three concepts: (1) the Inference Attribution Problem, characterizing why observed behavioral bias cannot generally be causally attributed to model weights alone under limited observability; (2) Probability Placement, defining a hypothetical advertising primitive in which commercial influence is implemented through systematic shifts in generation probabilities rather than explicit product insertions; and (3) Inference Policy Transparency, a governance principle for making deployment-layer interventions auditable. We examine these concepts in relation to Article 5 of the EU AI Act, the EU Digital Services Act, and FTC doctrines.

</details>


### [118] [Confident at the moment of action: belief miscalibration in LLM play under hidden information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24691)
*Bhushan Kashinath Joshi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Authors study whether AI model self-reported confidence aligns with correctness in a hidden-information chess game. They find extreme overconfidence: moves made with ≥0.5 confidence about the opponent’s hidden royal piece are almost always wrong, and most miscalibration is concentrated in these high-confidence actions. Conventional metrics (legality, cost, latency, completion rate, game outcome) fail to reveal this belief-quality failure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many modern agentic AI systems use their own confidence to decide when to act, escalate, or defer, assuming that higher stated confidence means higher probability of being correct at action time. This is safety- and reliability-relevant. The authors want to test this assumption in a controlled environment with hidden information and dynamic uncertainty, where the model must form and update beliefs about an unobserved game state element (the location of a royal piece in a chess variant).

Method: They design a hidden-information chess-like variant where the opponent’s royal piece can be secretly and repeatedly moved among pieces. Each turn, the model must (1) choose a move and (2) separately report a probability distribution over which piece is currently the hidden royal. After games finish, the true royal positions are reconstructed, enabling calibration analysis: especially the accuracy of moves made when the model states at least 0.5 confidence in a given location. They run two main batches plus four further model/configuration tests (including different provider and different deliberation budgets) and compare belief calibration to conventional metrics like legality, cost, latency, and completion rate.

Result: In the main two batches, when the model makes captures while stating high confidence (≥0.5) about the hidden royal’s location, it is correct only 1 time out of 62. Nearly all calibration error is concentrated in these high-confidence events (≈99% of total miscalibration in both batches). Additional configurations show a similar pattern, though weaker; variation in deliberation budget within the same model can change calibration almost as much as switching to a different model with similar benchmark score. Conventional metrics can show a configuration as strictly better on legality, cost, latency, and completion, while it simultaneously has the worst belief calibration measured.

Conclusion: Self-reported confidence from current models can be extremely miscalibrated precisely in the high-confidence regime where agentic systems tend to rely on it for gating actions. Belief quality can be very poor even when game outcomes and standard performance metrics look strong, so outcome-only or conventional evaluations can miss serious reliability failures. Calibration, especially under hidden and changing information, needs to be evaluated directly rather than assumed from task performance or leaderboard scores.

Abstract: Agentic systems increasingly gate actions on a model's own stated confidence, which assumes confidence tracks correctness at the moment of acting. We test this in a hidden-information chess variant where royal status can be secretly, repeatedly relocated between pieces, and where an agent's stated probability distribution over the opponent's hidden royal piece -- elicited every turn, separately from the move it chooses -- is scored against ground truth recoverable after the game. Across two independent batches, captures made at high stated confidence ($\geq 0.5$) about the hidden piece's location were correct in 1 of 62 cases. The calibration deficit is concentrated almost entirely in these events: 99.3% of it in the original batch, 98.7% in the replication. The same pattern, in weaker form, orders consistently (point estimates only; most pairwise gaps are not statistically distinguishable at this sample size) across four further model configurations spanning a second provider -- reported as scope for the finding, not as evidence that capability predicts calibration: a same-model comparison at a fixed external leaderboard score shows a deliberation-budget change alone moves the metric by nearly as much as a large cross-model gap. In a separate seat, conventional evaluation axes -- legality, cost, latency, completion rate -- can dissociate entirely from belief quality, with the configuration winning on every conventional axis producing the worst belief quality tested. A model exhibiting this pattern can still win the game its belief was about, which is why outcome-only evaluation would not detect it.

</details>


### [119] [MetaRAG: Belief-Action Aligned Policy Optimization for Agentic RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24214)
*Qiuyi Qi,Tian Liang,Jiamu Wang,Jinjian Zhang,Wei Zhou,Pengcheng Zhu,Linjian Mo,Ming Kong,Jie Liu,Qiang Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MetaRAG aligns an agentic RAG model’s search/answer decisions with its own internal belief about answerability, using a verification step and belief probing to improve accuracy–efficiency trade-offs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic RAG systems must decide whether to keep retrieving information or answer immediately. Existing RL-based approaches depend heavily on external supervision signals (e.g., correctness, step counts) and ignore whether the model itself believes it already has enough evidence. This can lead to inefficient search, over-searching, or premature answering. The paper aims to incorporate the model’s internal notion of answerability into the learning objective so that search behavior is both effective and efficient.

Method: The authors define search decision quality as alignment between the agent’s internal belief about answerability and its chosen actions. They propose MetaRAG, a policy optimization framework that uses two key mechanisms: (1) Verify-first Action Generation, which forces the model to explicitly conduct a verification step before committing to an action (continue searching or answer), and (2) Internal Belief Probing, which estimates the model’s own belief about whether the question is currently answerable from the question-history context. From these, they compute a consistency reward that encourages agreement between belief and action, but this reward is gated by answer correctness so that trajectories that are confidently wrong are not reinforced. The belief probe is only used in training and is removed at inference, so it does not add runtime overhead.

Result: Across seven public QA benchmarks, MetaRAG improves both accuracy and efficiency compared to strong RL-based agentic RAG baselines. It achieves a better accuracy-efficiency trade-off, meaning higher accuracy for similar or fewer retrieval steps, or comparable accuracy with reduced search cost. The improvements are robust: they transfer to more challenging deep research scenarios, work with different optimization algorithms, and generalize across multiple underlying language model backbones.

Conclusion: Incorporating an agent’s internal answerability belief into policy learning leads to better search decisions in agentic RAG. By aligning actions with these beliefs and rewarding only belief-action consistency that results in correct answers, MetaRAG provides a practical way to train more accurate and efficient RAG agents without increasing inference-time cost. The framework appears broadly applicable across tasks, optimizers, and model architectures.

Abstract: Agentic retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) requires language models to decide when to continue searching and when to answer. Existing RL-based methods rely on external supervision and overlook the agent's internal belief about whether the current evidence is sufficient. To address this problem, we reformulate the search decision quality as belief-action alignment and propose MetaRAG, a belief-action aligned policy optimization framework for agentic RAG. MetaRAG uses Verify-first Action Generation to elicit an explicit verification process before each actual action, and Internal Belief Probing to estimate the policy model's own answerability belief from the same question-history context. Based on these, MetaRAG derives a consistency reward that is further gated by answer correctness, avoiding reinforcement of internally consistent but incorrect trajectories. The belief probe is used only during training and introduces no inference-time overhead. Experiments on seven public QA benchmarks show that MetaRAG consistently improves the accuracy-efficiency trade-off over strong RL-based agentic RAG baselines, with gains that transfer to deep research settings, different optimizers, and multiple model backbones.

</details>


### [120] [Meta$^n$: Recursive Self-Improvement through Emergent Depth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24735)
*Zae Myung Kim,Young-Jun Lee,Seungyeon Jwa,Dongyeop Kang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Meta^n, a recursive meta-level operation for self-improving LLM agents that refines both answers and the underlying reasoning stack, achieving higher performance than prior methods on multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing self-improving LLM agents mainly refine final answers or add a single fixed meta-level; when they attempt self-editing, they must leave some editing machinery untouched to remain stable, limiting meta-depth to about two layers. There is a need for a more principled way to realize deeper, stable meta-reasoning that improves not just outputs but the entire reasoning process over multiple layers.

Method: The authors introduce Meta^n, centered on a fixed meta-operation Ω that is applied recursively. Ω takes as input the traces of the solver stack and the code that produced those traces, and outputs the next meta-layer in the form of (i) a strategic pre-process and (ii) a library of helpers callable by lower layers. Crucially, Ω itself never changes; only its input grows as more layers are added. The system automatically decides how deep to recurse based on convergence rather than a preset depth, and an evolutionary archive is used to search over chains of such layers.

Result: Across two different LLM backbones, Meta^n outperforms previous self-improving agent architectures on all eight tested benchmark families. On the particularly challenging ARC-AGI-2 benchmark, designed to prevent simple skill memorization, Meta^n is the only system achieving a non-zero score. Empirical ablations suggest that the main benefit of recursion comes from the conditioning and information each layer passes forward to the next, and analysis shows that distinct, specialized layer roles emerge with depth despite the lack of explicit prompting for such roles.

Conclusion: Keeping the meta-operation Ω fixed while recursively feeding it richer input enables stable, arbitrarily deep meta-reasoning in LLM agents. Meta^n improves performance across diverse benchmarks, with especially strong gains on tasks resistant to memorization, and exhibits emergent specialization of layers. This architecture suggests a promising direction for scalable, stable self-improvement in LLM-based agents by recursing on process traces and code rather than continually rewriting the meta-procedures themselves.

Abstract: Self-improving LLM agents refine answers, not the process that produces those answers. Systems that add a meta-level hold that level fixed, and those that edit themselves must leave part of their own editing machinery untouched to stay stable, capping the meta-depth they realize at roughly two. We present Meta$^n$, which keeps the meta-operation fixed and recurses on its input instead. That operation, $Ω$, is applied repeatedly to its own products, reading the traces of the solver stack below together with the code that produced them, then writing the next layer as a strategic pre-process and a library of callable helpers. Because $Ω$ never changes, it cannot destabilize the system, and because its input strictly grows, each layer reasons from a higher vantage than the last. Depth is set by convergence rather than fixed in advance, and an evolutionary archive searches over layer chains. Across two backbones, Meta$^n$ outperforms prior self-improving agents on all eight benchmark families. The sharpest case is ARC-AGI-2, built to resist skill memorization, where it alone scores above zero. Ablations indicate that most of the gain from recursion comes from the conditioning each layer passes to the next, and distinct layer roles emerge with depth although no prompt prescribes them. Code available at https://github.com/minnesotanlp/meta-n

</details>


### [121] [Recursive Experiential-Working Memory Evolution for Long-Horizon Agent Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24876)
*Zhaochen Yu,Yingcheng Wu,Zhenfei Yin,Kaiyuan Chen,Zhe Zhao,Mengdi Wang,Shuicheng Yan,Ling Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Recuris, a recursive Experiential-Working Memory architecture that improves long-horizon agent performance by separating short-term task tracking from long-term skills and enabling bounded recursive self-improvement of skills.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing recursive self-improvement for AI agents struggles on long-horizon tasks because long and noisy interaction histories make it hard to infer the current task state and to invoke the right skills at the right time. Moreover, typical approaches lack a clean way to attribute failures to specific components, limiting targeted improvement. The authors want a scalable way for agents to continuously learn from experience and improve their long-horizon behavior without unbounded retraining or full-history reprocessing.

Method: They design Recuris, an agent architecture with two coupled memories: (1) Working Memory that explicitly tracks task progress and current needs over the course of an episode, and (2) Experiential (Skill) Memory that stores modular skills. During execution, Working Memory guides which skills from Experiential Memory to invoke, grounding decisions in the current state summary rather than the entire history. The structured interaction between these memories produces localized evidence about which skills or memory components caused failures. A fixed Meta-Agent then uses this evidence to perform validation-gated, localized updates to Skill Memory, reshaping future execution while keeping the improvement loop bounded and recursive.

Result: On four long-horizon benchmarks and with ten different models, Recuris improves task success rates in 35 out of 37 model-benchmark combinations. It boosts frontier models to near or state-of-the-art performance: for example, on tau-bench it yields +17.8 points for GPT-5.6 Sol and +15.6 points for Claude Opus 5, reaching 87.9% for Opus 5, and provides +16.6 and +13.5 points for Qwen3.6-27B and 35B on SkillFlow. The gains become larger on longer-horizon tasks, up to +32.2 points for the longest tasks, and it reduces common long-horizon failure modes by up to 80%.

Conclusion: Recuris demonstrates that a recursively evolving memory architecture—with explicit separation and coupling of Working and Experiential (Skill) Memory—enables effective, bounded recursive self-improvement for long-horizon agents. By structuring execution to produce localized evidence and using a fixed Meta-Agent to update skills, agents can continually convert accumulated experience into more reliable and capable long-horizon behavior. This positions recursive memory evolution as a promising, scalable foundation for RSI in practical agent systems.

Abstract: Recursive self-improvement (RSI) remains hard in long-horizon tasks, where growing histories obscure the task state and misalign skill invocation. We introduce Recuris, a recursive Experiential-Working Memory architecture for long-horizon agent harnesses, in which Working Memory tracks task progress and guides skill selection from Experiential Memory, grounding skill use in current needs rather than the full history. This coupling also turns execution into structured evidence that localizes failures to specific memory components. Across tasks, a fixed Meta-Agent turns that evidence into localized, validation-gated updates to Skill Memory that reshape execution and yield new evidence, forming a bounded recursive memory-evolution loop. Across four long-horizon benchmarks and ten models, Recuris improves task success in 35 of the 37 completed model-benchmark pairs, carrying frontier models to SOTA-level task success: on tau-bench it adds +17.8 points to GPT-5.6 Sol and +15.6 to Claude Opus 5, taking Opus 5 to 87.9%, and +16.6/+13.5 points on Qwen3.6-27B/35B on SkillFlow. The advantage widens as the interaction horizon grows, to +32.2 points on the longest tasks, and common long-horizon failures fall by up to 80%. These results position recursively evolving memory as a scalable foundation for RSI, enabling agents to continuously transform accumulated experience into increasingly effective long-horizon behavior. Code: https://github.com/Gen-Verse/Recuris

</details>


### [122] [Evaluating Multiple LLM Generations with Validated Task Coverage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24228)
*Florian Le Bronnec,Rio Yokota*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: New benchmark (VTC-Bench) and metric (Validated Task Coverage) to evaluate how well LLMs produce multiple distinct, useful outputs across several attempts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM evaluations typically consider only one output or compress multiple outputs into a single success indicator, failing to capture whether models produce several genuinely different useful results in multi-sample scenarios common in real applications.

Method: Construct a five-domain benchmark, VTC-Bench, using real-data tasks where both usefulness and task-relevant distinctness of outputs can be automatically and reproducibly validated without relying on model-based judges. Define Validated Task Coverage (VTC) as the number of distinct useful results obtained within k attempts, and systematically compare models and inference settings using this metric versus conventional single-draw quality and simple diversity measures.

Result: Experiments show that models or inference configurations that perform best under standard single-output quality metrics do not necessarily achieve the highest validated task coverage. Additionally, naive measures of output variation fail to align with task-relevant coverage, indicating that they are poor proxies for the benchmark’s notion of distinct, useful outputs.

Conclusion: Finite candidate sets from LLMs should be evaluated as complete objects, using metrics like VTC that account for the number of distinct useful results across multiple attempts. Doing so reveals important differences in model behavior and coverage that conventional per-output or diversity-only evaluations overlook.

Abstract: Many LLM applications are most useful when they provide several candidate outputs for comparison, validation, or combination. Predominant evaluation settings, however, still focus on individual outputs or reduce multiple samples to a single success or selected answer. This can miss whether the outputs include several genuinely different useful results. We introduce VTC-Bench, a five-domain benchmark for this setting, together with Validated Task Coverage (VTC) as its core evaluation quantity. The benchmark is built from carefully selected real-data tasks where both output quality and task-relevant distinctness can be checked automatically and reproducibly, without model-based judges. VTC measures how many distinct useful results are obtained within $k$ attempts. Across multiple models and inference settings, the benchmark leads to different conclusions from conventional evaluation: configurations that look strongest from single-draw quality are not necessarily those with the best coverage, and simple measures of output variation do not reliably recover task-relevant coverage. These results show that finite candidate sets can be evaluated directly as objects of interest, revealing differences in model behavior that are not apparent from conventional per-output evaluation.

</details>


### [123] [TRACE: An Evidence-Grounded Benchmark for Safety Evaluation of Large Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24232)
*Zhenyu Wu,Siyuan Chen,Changchun Yang,Jiaqi Dong,Min Zhou,Ali Almadan,Talal Hammad,Faisal Wahbo,Aminullah Tora,Mona Alshahrani,Xin Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces TRACE, a benchmark to evaluate how well safety/guardrail models detect unsafe content not just in prompts and outputs, but also inside reasoning traces, with evidence-grounded annotations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing safety benchmarks for language and reasoning models mainly assess whether prompts or final answers are unsafe, ignoring the intermediate reasoning traces. However, large reasoning models often generate unsafe content within these traces even when the final answer looks safe. Current benchmarks also use coarse, binary safe/unsafe labels without pointing to the exact text that justifies the label, which limits both diagnosis and model improvement. There is a need for a more fine-grained, evidence-based benchmark that covers the whole inference pipeline.

Method: The authors build TRACE, a benchmark that: (1) covers prompts, reasoning traces, and final responses for large reasoning models; (2) spans two languages, nine risk categories, and ten adversarial attack strategies; (3) uses four LRMs to generate reasoning traces and outputs for each prompt; and (4) provides annotations for the safety of each component (prompt, trace, final response) along with explicit spans of supporting evidence extracted from the text. They then evaluate 18 existing guardrail models on this dataset.

Result: On TRACE, guardrail models perform much worse at judging the safety of reasoning traces than at judging prompts or final responses. They also have difficulty correctly extracting and localizing the text spans that serve as evidence for unsafe judgments. This demonstrates a substantial performance gap in current safety tools when dealing with intermediate reasoning content and evidence grounding.

Conclusion: Safety evaluation must extend beyond prompts and final answers to include full reasoning traces, and it must be evidence-grounded. TRACE shows that current guardrail models are not yet capable of reliably detecting and localizing unsafe content inside reasoning processes. The work motivates the development of new guardrail methods specifically targeted at reasoning traces and precise evidence extraction across the entire LRM inference pipeline.

Abstract: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) generate intermediate reasoning traces that may contain unsafe content, even when their final responses appear safe. Guardrail models are designed to detect and block unsafe content, yet existing benchmarks for unsafe content detection focus primarily on prompts and final responses, leaving reasoning traces largely unexamined. Moreover, these benchmarks typically provide only binary safety labels, without evidence annotations that justify the judgments. To address these limitations, we introduce TRACE, an evidence-grounded safety evaluation benchmark that covers the entire LRM inference pipeline: prompts, reasoning traces, and final responses. TRACE includes prompts in two languages spanning nine risk categories and ten attack strategies. For each prompt, four LRMs generate reasoning traces and final responses, and we annotate the safety of each component and extract supporting evidence from the corresponding source text. Evaluating 18 guardrail models on TRACE reveals that safety judgment for reasoning traces is substantially more challenging than for prompts or final responses, and that current models struggle to accurately extract supporting evidence. These findings highlight the need for guardrail models that can reliably detect and precisely localize unsafe content across the LRM inference pipeline.

</details>


### [124] [STRIVE: Multi-Agent Structured Temporal Reasoning with Integrated Verification for Longitudinal Radiology Report Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24237)
*Junyeong Maeng,Eunsong Kang,Heung-Il Suk*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A new multi-agent framework (STRIVE) improves longitudinal radiology report generation by explicitly structuring clinical reasoning, using progression-aware rewards, and adding verification stages, achieving higher temporal agreement with reference reports.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Longitudinal radiology report generation needs not just current findings but how they change over time. Existing end-to-end models entangle multiple tasks (diagnosis, attribute estimation, temporal comparison, language generation) inside opaque representations, leading to task interference, poor interpretability, and difficulty tracing or correcting errors. They also treat temporal progression labels as independent, ignoring their ordered structure, so minor direction-preserving mistakes are penalized as heavily as serious direction reversals.

Method: The paper proposes STRIVE, a multi-agent framework that decomposes LRRG into three specialized agents: a Diagnosis Agent, an Attribute Agent, and a Temporal Change Agent, each producing explicit intermediate evidence. The Temporal Change Agent is post-trained with Progression-Aware GRPO, a shaped and verifiable reward function that accounts for the ordered nature of progression states, giving partial credit for direction-preserving but imperfect predictions and penalizing direction reversals most. STRIVE includes two verification stages: (1) a deterministic Consistency Gate that reconciles and enforces coherence among agent outputs prior to report generation, and (2) a Validation Agent that checks whether the final generated report is supported by the aggregated clinical evidence. The overall system is trained and evaluated on the Longitudinal-MIMIC dataset.

Result: On the Longitudinal-MIMIC benchmark, STRIVE achieves the strongest clinical performance compared with recent LRRG methods. It more than doubles Longitudinal Change Concordance (LCC), which measures how well the model’s description of temporal changes matches the reference report, relative to the best existing baseline, while also improving overall clinical efficacy metrics.

Conclusion: Explicitly structuring clinical reasoning into multiple agents, enforcing progression-aware temporal modeling, and adding integrated verification significantly improve longitudinal radiology report generation. STRIVE yields more accurate and temporally consistent reports with better traceability and verifiability of clinical decisions, demonstrating the value of structured, multi-agent reasoning for complex clinical NLP tasks.

Abstract: Longitudinal radiology report generation (LRRG) requires identifying both current findings and their changes relative to a prior study. Existing methods jointly model diagnosis, attribute estimation, temporal comparison, and language generation within implicit representations, which can cause task interference, obscure the evidence underlying each decision, and limit error traceability. They also model progression states as independent labels, ignoring their ordered structure and thus treating missed changes and direction reversals equally. We present STRIVE, Multi-Agent Structured Temporal Reasoning with Integrated Verification for LRRG, which decomposes clinical reasoning into specialized Diagnosis, Attribute, and Temporal Change Agents that produce explicit intermediate evidence. In particular, the Temporal Change Agent is further post-trained using Progression-Aware GRPO, a verifiable, shaped reward that assigns partial credit to direction-preserving errors while scoring direction reversals lowest. STRIVE performs verification at two stages: a deterministic Consistency Gate reconciles the agent outputs before report generation, and a Validation Agent checks whether the generated report is supported by the aggregated clinical evidence. On Longitudinal-MIMIC, STRIVE attains the best clinical efficacy among recent methods and more than doubles Longitudinal Change Concordance (LCC), a measure of temporal agreement with the reference report, over the strongest baseline.

</details>


### [125] [SA-Bench: Evaluating Semantic Alignment in LLM-Based Paper Reproduction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24252)
*Xue Hu,Zewei Pan,Zeli Su,Zhou Liu,Wentao Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They build a benchmark (SA-Bench) to measure how faithfully LLM agents reproduce ML papers in code and show current systems drift a lot from the papers’ true specifications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although LLM agents can auto-generate code to reproduce research papers, their implementations often diverge subtly from what the papers actually specify, threatening scientific validity. There was no systematic way to diagnose or quantify this semantic drift in paper-to-code generation.

Method: They curate 30 ML papers from top conferences and decompose each paper’s implementation-relevant specifications into fine-grained, atomic, verifiable statements called Semantic Alignment Units (SAUs). They categorize possible divergences into four types of drift (numerical, methodological, protocol, ordering). Using these SAUs, they evaluate 12 paper-code generator configurations (4 LLMs × 3 scaffolding frameworks) across five ML domains, assigning each SAU a score based on whether the generated repository correctly implements the specified requirement. They then compile error statistics and develop a taxonomy of failure modes.

Result: They construct 1,491 SAUs and run 360 total evaluations over 12 generator configurations. The best-performing setup (Claude + PaperCoder scaffold) attains only 0.301 mean SAU score (on a 0–1 scale); the overall mean across configurations is 0.221, indicating pervasive semantic drift. Analysis shows that agents frequently attempt to implement requirements but do so incorrectly, with implementation mismatches and placeholder stubs causing most zero scores. Scaffolds focused on making code executable do not substantially improve semantic faithfulness to the paper.

Conclusion: Current LLM-based paper reproduction agents are far from reliably faithful to papers’ specifications; they routinely introduce semantic drift even when code runs. SA-Bench offers a structured benchmark, annotation set, and evaluation pipeline for diagnosing where and how these systems fail. Improving scientific reproduction will require agent scaffolds and workflows that explicitly emphasize verifying semantic agreement with paper specifications rather than just producing executable code.

Abstract: LLM agents can generate paper reproduction code, yet often produce scientifically unfaithful implementations. We define this failure mode as semantic drift, where generated code silently diverges from the paper's specifications. We introduce SemanticAlign-Bench(SA-Bench), a diagnostic benchmark covering 30 papers from ICLR, ICML and NeurIPS 2025. For each paper, we decompose its specifications into atomic and verifiable implementation claims, which we call Semantic Alignment Units (SAUs) and evaluate repositories along four diagnostic dimensions spanning numerical, methodological, protocol and ordering drift. In total, we construct 1,491 SAUs across five ML domains and evaluate 12 generator configurations (4 models $\times$ 3 scaffolds). Even the strongest configuration (Claude+PaperCoder) achieves a mean SAU score of only 0.301 out of 1.0, with an overall mean of 0.221 across 360 evaluations. A failure taxonomy reveals that agents attempt most requirements but implement them incorrectly, with implementation mismatch and stubs accounting for the majority of zero-scored claims. Our analysis further indicates that scaffolds optimized for executability provide limited leverage for scientific reproduction; narrowing the gap requires scaffolds that prioritize semantic specification verification. The benchmark, annotations and evaluation pipeline are publicly available.

</details>


### [126] [Beyond Accuracy: A Dual-Judge Evaluation Protocol for Vision-Language Models in Legally Grounded Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24258)
*Su Myat Noe,Ha Thanh Nguyen,May Myo Zin,Ken Satoh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a dual-judge evaluation protocol for legal-style AI tasks, adding a strict binary semantic-equivalence judge alongside a standard 0–10 quality judge, and shows they diverge systematically, especially on degraded visual inputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI used in legally accountable settings needs not only high-performing answers but answers that are demonstrably correct against a formal standard. Existing benchmarks focus on scalar quality scores or LLM-as-judge ratings, which can overestimate reliability, especially when inputs are imperfect. The authors want an evaluation signal that captures whether model outputs actually match an authoritative ground-truth interpretation, not just whether they sound good, and to understand where conventional quality scoring becomes untrustworthy.

Method: They design a controlled regulatory interpretation task: understanding UK traffic signs from images under varying visibility and occlusion. For each input, there is a codified, human-curated reference answer. Every model answer is evaluated by two LLM-based judges: (1) a standard 0–10 quality judge, and (2) a strict binary semantic-equivalence judge that decides whether the answer is meaningfully equivalent to the reference. They run 4,680 evaluations across seven visibility levels and two occlusion modes, compute associations between the two judges’ outputs, analyze disagreement patterns (especially false negatives/Type II–like behavior of the strict judge), and cross-check with a small human study to validate how each judge aligns with everyday readers.

Result: The two judges are moderately correlated (point-biserial r = 0.644) but disagree in systematic ways. About 8.0% of all evaluations show an asymmetric Type II pattern: answers that receive high quality scores but are judged non-equivalent to the reference. This marginal disagreement rate is highest at good visibility (14.2% at v = 0.8) because high scores are more frequent there. However, conditioning on already high-quality answers (score > 7), the probability that they are actually non-equivalent is greatest under heavy occlusion (54–63% at v <= 0.3), indicating that high scalar scores are least reliable when input images are most degraded. A 49-row human check finds the 0–10 judge aligns well with everyday readers (Pearson r = 0.81; r = 0.80 with an LLM accuracy sub-score), whereas the equivalence judge is stricter and tends to only reject, not accept, beyond what humans would. The protocol only adds one extra LLM call per evaluation but reveals quality-vs-equivalence mismatches that standard single-judge setups miss.

Conclusion: A dual-judge evaluation framework—combining a conventional quality-scoring LLM judge with a strict semantic-equivalence judge to a legal-style reference—provides additional, practically important information about AI reliability in legally accountable tasks, especially under degraded inputs. While both judges are individually reasonable (with the scalar judge matching everyday readers), their systematic disagreements, particularly the high-rate of non-equivalent yet high-scoring answers under occlusion, show that single-judge protocols overstate trustworthiness. The authors advocate incorporating such dual-judge signals into future benchmarks and release their prompts, occluded datasets, and evaluation logs to facilitate replication and extension.

Abstract: AI systems are increasingly evaluated for legally accountable settings, where correct outputs must also be justifiable against an applicable legal standard. Existing legal-AI benchmarks and LLM-as-judge protocols provide important infrastructure for measuring task performance and open-ended response quality. We contribute one additional evaluation signal: a dual-judge protocol that pairs a standard 0-10 quality judge with a strict binary semantic-equivalence judge against a human-curated reference.
  We study a controlled, visually grounded regulatory task - UK traffic-sign interpretation, whose meaning is a codified question with a known reference for every input - and measure not merely whether the two judges disagree (by construction they must) but how much and where. On 4,680 evaluations under seven visibility levels and two occlusion modes, the two judges are moderately associated (point-biserial r = 0.644), while revealing an asymmetric Type II pattern affecting 8.0% of all evaluations.
  Its distribution is instructive: the marginal rate peaks at high visibility (14.2% at v = 0.8) simply because high-scoring answers are common there, but conditioned on the answer already scoring above 7, the rate is highest under heavy occlusion (54-63% at v <= 0.3), so a high quality score is least trustworthy when the input is most degraded.
  We are explicit that the signal is a property of this judge and reference: a 49-row human check shows the 0-10 judge aligns closely with everyday-reader judgement (Pearson r = 0.81; r = 0.80 with the LLM accuracy sub-score), while the equivalence judge is fairly but one-directionally stricter. The protocol adds one LLM call per evaluation and surfaces a signal single-judge protocols do not report. We release the prompt template, occluded variants, and full evaluation results.

</details>


### [127] [Real-World Knowledge-Guided Change Data Synthesis for Remote Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24263)
*Yaoyi Qi,Xingxing Weng,Chao Pang,Yongkang Cui,Xiangyu Hao,Xiaokang Zhang,Guibo Zhu,Gui-Song Xia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: KnowChange is a knowledge-guided framework that uses pretrained vision-language models to synthesize realistic change-detection data, improving model performance with compact but high-utility synthetic datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing change data synthesis methods depend on manually designed rules for simulating changes, which cover only a limited set of class transitions and enforce rigid, predefined change patterns. This restricts the diversity, realism, and adaptability of generated data to varied change types, limiting their effectiveness for training robust change detection models. A more flexible and knowledge-aware approach is needed to reason about where and how changes should occur in images.

Method: KnowChange employs pretrained vision-language models as external knowledge sources. Given a pre-change image and a description of desired change types, the vision-language model is used to infer plausible change locations and semantic class transitions. These knowledge-guided change proposals are then combined with generalizable synthesis models to actually generate the post-change imagery. The framework is unified, supporting diverse change types without relying on handcrafted transition rules, and can be plugged into existing synthesis pipelines.

Result: Across extensive experiments, datasets synthesized with KnowChange lead to superior performance compared to those produced by existing rule-based synthesis methods. This holds for both synthetic-to-real transfer scenarios (training on synthetic data, testing on real data) and for using synthetic data to augment limited real datasets. Notably, these gains are achieved even though the KnowChange-generated datasets are relatively compact in size.

Conclusion: Incorporating vision-language model knowledge into change data synthesis enables more flexible, diverse, and effective simulation of real-world changes. KnowChange improves the downstream utility of synthetic data for change detection and can be seamlessly integrated into current synthesis pipelines, suggesting a promising direction for knowledge-guided synthetic data generation in remote sensing and related domains.

Abstract: Change data synthesis provides a cost-effective solution for expanding training data and improving the performance of change detection models. However, existing synthesis methods typically rely on handcrafted rules to simulate changes, where limited coverage of class transitions restricts the diversity of synthesized data, while predefined transition designs limit their flexibility in accommodating varied change types. In this work, we introduce KnowChange, a knowledge-guided change data synthesis framework that leverages pretrained vision-language models as knowledge sources to reason about plausible change locations and class transitions from pre-change scenes and desired change types. By integrating knowledge-guided change simulation with generalizable synthesis models, KnowChange enables flexible synthesis of diverse change types within a unified framework. Extensive experiments demonstrate that KnowChange-generated data consistently outperforms existing synthetic datasets in both synthetic-to-real transfer and synthetic data augmentation, despite being generated at a compact scale. Further analyses show that the knowledge-guided change simulation can be seamlessly integrated into existing synthesis pipelines and enhance the downstream utility of synthesized data.

</details>


### [128] [Matched Excess-Outranker Regularization for Candidate-Set Interference in Continual Knowledge Graph Embedding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24273)
*Hao Ren,Junbin Gao,Jiaojiao Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces MEOR, a new regularization method to mitigate rank degradation in continual knowledge graph embedding caused by new entity admission, improving historical ranking without changing the underlying model or learner.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In continual knowledge graph embedding, as new entities are added over time, prior work focuses mainly on catastrophic forgetting (i.e., maintaining previous scores and relationships). However, even if old entity scores and their relative ordering remain stable, the admission of new entities expands the candidate set for queries, which can cause previously correct or high-ranked answers to be outranked by newcomers. This phenomenon, not captured by standard forgetting metrics, is termed candidate-set interference. The paper is motivated by the need to formally characterize and directly mitigate this distinct source of rank loss, ensuring robust historical performance as the knowledge graph grows.

Method: The authors formalize candidate-set interference as the adverse impact of newly added entities on the ranking of historical answers under an expanded candidate universe. They propose Matched Excess-Outranker Regularization (MEOR), a host-level regularization objective compatible with existing continual learners and embedding architectures. MEOR constructs structurally matched old references—score-blind baselines that reflect acceptable levels of competition from entities similar to the newcomers. It then defines a smooth, answer-relative measure of newcomer pressure: how strongly new entities threaten to outrank historical correct answers. A one-sided penalty is applied only when this newcomer pressure exceeds that of the matched reference, thereby suppressing excessive interference while leaving intact the learning signal for genuinely informative new entities. The method is evaluated on ENTITY-ComplEx and FBInc-S/L setups, compared against replay, persistent calibration, matched maximum regularizer (MMR), and unmatched old regularizer (UOR), along with ablations over MEOR’s reference construction and aggregation components.

Result: On the ENTITY-ComplEx benchmark, MEOR improves historical current-universe mean reciprocal rank (MRR) by 0.0057 over a replay baseline and reduces candidate-set interference by 0.0055, with one-sided 95% lower bounds of 0.0052 and 0.0051, respectively, demonstrating statistically robust gains. MEOR satisfies preservation criteria for both old-universe ranking and newcomer acquisition, meaning it maintains performance on historical entities while still learning useful representations for new ones. Compared with persistent calibration, MMR, and UOR, MEOR yields superior historical current-universe MRR. Ablation studies confirm that each component of the reference construction and aggregation contributes meaningfully to performance. On the FBInc-S and FBInc-L settings across ten host–backbone combinations, adding MEOR consistently improves historical ranking, with all paired 95% confidence intervals excluding zero, indicating reliable, architecture-agnostic benefits.

Conclusion: The study identifies candidate admission as a distinct and measurable cause of rank degradation in continual knowledge graph embedding, beyond classical catastrophic forgetting. By introducing MEOR, a one-sided regularizer based on matched old references and newcomer pressure, the authors demonstrate that candidate-set interference can be substantially reduced without modifying the underlying embedding models or continual learning algorithms. The consistent gains across datasets, architectures, and evaluation setups support MEOR as an effective, general-purpose mechanism for preserving historical ranking quality as knowledge graphs grow over time.

Abstract: Continual knowledge graph embedding updates entity and relation representations as a graph grows. Existing methods primarily address catastrophic forgetting, but entity admission also changes the candidate universe of every compatible query. A historical answer can therefore lose rank even when its score and its ordering among old entities are preserved. We formalize this effect as candidate-set interference and introduce Matched Excess-Outranker Regularization (MEOR), a host-level objective that compares smooth answer-relative newcomer pressure with score-blind, structurally matched old references. Its one-sided penalty acts only when newcomer competition exceeds the matched reference, preserving the host learner's signal for legitimate new entities. Across eight paired runs on ENTITY-ComplEx, MEOR improves historical current-universe mean reciprocal rank (MRR) by 0.0057 over replay and reduces candidate-set interference by 0.0055, with one-sided 95% lower bounds of 0.0052 and 0.0051, respectively. It satisfies the preservation criteria for old-universe ranking and newcomer acquisition and improves historical current-universe MRR over persistent calibration, matched maximum regularizer (MMR), and unmatched old regularizer (UOR). Direct ablations support each component of its reference construction and aggregation. Adding MEOR also improves historical ranking in all ten reported FBInc-S and FBInc-L host and backbone settings, with every paired 95% confidence interval excluding zero. These results establish candidate admission as a distinct source of continual rank loss and show that it can be controlled without replacing the underlying embedding architecture or continual learner.

</details>


### [129] [Eating for a Sustainable Planet: Personalized Sustainable Diet Recommendation via Constraint-Aware Decision-Making Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24274)
*Ying Jin,Weiqing Min,Mingyu Huang,Shuqiang Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a personalized sustainable diet recommendation model that treats sustainability as explicit, learnable constraints instead of user preferences, and validates it on a new large-scale sustainable recipe dataset (SusDiet) showing it can improve sustainability without hurting user satisfaction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most work on sustainable diets focuses on population-level optimization, but real-world impact depends on individual-level behavior change. However, individuals differ widely in tastes, needs, and constraints, making it difficult to enforce sustainability targets while preserving personal food preferences. There is a gap in methods that personalize diet recommendations while rigorously enforcing multi-dimensional sustainability (nutrition, cost, culture, environment).

Method: The authors design a constraint-aware decision-making model for diet recommendation where sustainability aspects are encoded as learnable constraints rather than blended into the user preference model. They also build SusDiet, a dataset of about 150,000 recipes annotated with multiple sustainability indicators (nutritional adequacy, affordability, cultural aspects, environmental impact). The model is trained and evaluated on this dataset to recommend recipes that satisfy sustainability constraints while matching predicted user tastes.

Result: On the SusDiet dataset, experiments demonstrate that the proposed model successfully shifts recommendations toward more sustainable options across the defined indicators, while maintaining alignment with user preferences, i.e., it does not reduce predicted user satisfaction compared with baselines that ignore sustainability constraints.

Conclusion: Encoding sustainability as explicit, learnable constraints in a personalized recommendation system enables more sustainable dietary choices without sacrificing individual preference satisfaction. The new SusDiet dataset and the proposed framework provide a quantitative basis for designing interventions and policies that better align individual eating behavior with planetary health and sustainable development goals.

Abstract: A sustainable diet represents a multi-dimensional synergy among four essential pillars: nutrition adequacy, economic affordability, cultural acceptability, and environmental respect. Despite the prevalence of population-level sustainability modeling, practical implementation relies on effective individual-level adoption. This transition is often hindered by inter-individual heterogeneity, posing a formidable challenge in aligning sustainable diet requirements with individual preferences. To address this issue, we propose a personalized sustainable diet recommendation model based on a constraint-aware decision-making mechanism, where sustainability is incorporated through learnable constraints rather than modeled as user preferences. To systematically evaluate the proposed approach, we construct a sustainable diet dataset named SusDiet with about 150k recipes, characterized by broad coverage of sustainability indicators. Experimental results on this dataset show that our method promotes more sustainable choices without compromising individual preference. This work establishes a framework for aligning individual dietary choices with planetary health, offering quantitative evidence to guide future sustainable diet interventions and policy-making for sustainable development.

</details>


### [130] [ReproAgent: Contract-Guided Paper-to-Code Reproduction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24291)
*Xue Hu,Zewei Pan,Zhongyuan Wang,Zhou Liu,Zeli Su,Wentao Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents ReproAgent, an AI system that converts research papers into executable code repositories using a structured four-stage pipeline with persistent implementation contracts, outperforming comparable scaffolding methods on a benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reproducing research papers as code is hard because the full method specification is fragmented: explicit details (algorithms, metrics, artifacts) may be lost across long agent reasoning chains, while crucial implicit details (framework defaults, inherited conventions, settings from related work) never appear in the paper at all. Existing agentic systems struggle to systematically track obligations from the paper and leverage evidence from related repositories, leading to incomplete or non-faithful implementations.

Method: The authors design ReproAgent, a four-stage Prepare–Plan–Generate–Repair pipeline centered on a persistent implementation contract. This contract has two key channels: (1) an implementation-requirement channel that converts paper snippets into concrete code obligations, organized into work packages and projected into file-level contracts; and (2) a reference-evidence channel that retrieves structural and content evidence from related repositories. Both channels are maintained and consumed throughout planning, code generation, and repair, guiding the agent’s actions and ensuring consistency with the paper and prior codebases.

Result: On the PaperBench Code-Dev benchmark, ReproAgent achieves the best mean performance among scaffolding systems using the same backbone LLMs (Claude-Sonnet-4.5 and Gemini-3-Flash). Channel ablation experiments show that removing either the implementation-requirement or reference-evidence channel degrades performance, and qualitative per-paper case studies further illustrate their importance.

Conclusion: ReproAgent demonstrates that a persistent implementation contract with dual channels for explicit requirements and reference evidence can significantly improve paper-to-code reproduction. The approach yields state-of-the-art performance within its setting and empirical analyses validate that both channels are crucial. The released code and artifacts support further research and practical adoption.

Abstract: Paper-to-code reproduction asks scientific AI agents to turn research papers into executable repositories that preserve the paper's method, protocol and artifacts. This is difficult because the specification is split: explicit paper content such as algorithms, metrics and artifacts is often lost across long agent trajectories, while implicit details such as framework defaults and conventions inherited from related work are absent from the paper. We introduce ReproAgent, a four-stage Prepare--Plan--Generate--Repair pipeline built around a persistent implementation contract with two channels: an implementation-requirement channel that turns paper snippets into code obligations, and a reference-evidence channel that retrieves content and structure evidence from related repositories. Both are bound to work packages, projected into file-level contracts, and consumed across generation and repair. On PaperBench Code-Dev, ReproAgent reaches the highest mean score among same-backbone scaffolds under both Claude-Sonnet-4.5 and Gemini-3-Flash. End-to-end channel ablations and per-paper cases support the contribution of both channels. Code and experimental artifacts are publicly available.

</details>


### [131] [VideoHarness-RSI: Recursive Harness Self-Improvement for Long-Video Understanding with Frozen Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24302)
*Guoyang Xu,Hao Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes VIDEOHARNESS-RSI, a framework that automatically searches for better executable programs that construct model input context for long-video understanding around a frozen VLM, showing that optimizing context construction alone significantly boosts performance and transfers to new benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long videos exceed the context limits of current vision-language models, so systems must compress or select parts of the video. Existing solutions mix context construction with other components (retrieval, memory, agents), making it hard to understand the pure effect of better context construction. The authors want a controlled, reproducible way to study how much performance gain is possible by only improving the program that builds the model’s input context, holding the underlying VLM and interfaces fixed.

Method: They introduce VIDEOHARNESS-RSI, a recursive search framework over executable “harnesses” (programs that decide how to construct context from long video). An outer-loop proposer generates candidate context-construction programs using prior harnesses, their evaluation scores, and execution traces. Each proposed harness is executed end-to-end with a frozen VLM on long-video tasks; better-performing harnesses are retained and used to guide further search. The approach treats long-video understanding as automated harness design: only the program structure for context construction is searched, while the VLM and its API remain unchanged. They run this search from scratch (uniform sampling) and from stronger hand-crafted baselines and then test transfer to other benchmarks.

Result: Recursive harness search starting from uniform sampling reliably discovers context construction programs that outperform weaker manually designed baselines. When initialized from a stronger hand-crafted harness, the same RSI process still finds additional improvements beyond the human design. The best discovered harness generalizes to other long-video benchmarks without needing to redo the search, demonstrating transferability of the learned context-construction strategy.

Conclusion: Executable context construction can be treated as an independent optimization layer around a frozen VLM, and systematically improving it via recursive search yields measurable gains in long-video understanding, even over strong human baselines. The proposed VIDEOHARNESS-RSI provides a controlled and reproducible baseline for future work on automated harness design and on transferring discovered context-construction programs across long-video tasks and datasets.

Abstract: Long-video understanding depends critically on how a limited model context is constructed from a much longer video. Existing approaches improve this process through compression, retrieval, memory, and agentic evidence acquisition, but these mechanisms are typically introduced as part of a manually designed inference system or optimized together with other components. This makes it difficult to isolate a simpler question: how much can be gained by improving the executable context-construction program alone? We study this question through VIDEOHARNESS-RSI, a controlled baseline for recursively searching executable context constructors around a frozen vision-language model (VLM). An outer-loop proposer uses prior programs, evaluation outcomes, and execution traces to generate candidate harnesses, which are executed and evaluated end to end before successful variants are retained for further search. This makes long-video understanding a controlled instance of automated harness design: the searchable object is executable program structure, while the answering model and interface remain fixed. Starting from uniform sampling, recursive harness search consistently finds room for improvement and surpasses several weaker hand-crafted baselines. Starting instead from a stronger hand-crafted baseline, the same RSI process yields a further improvement. The selected harness also transfers to additional long-video benchmarks without further search. Together, these results establish executable context construction as a distinct optimization layer and provide a reproducible baseline for studying harness discovery and transfer around frozen VLMs.

</details>


### [132] [OPDSearch+: On-Policy Distillation with RL Refinement for Search-Augmented Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24310)
*Qinglin Ye,Zhiyuan Gu,Jingjie Xia,Yiheng Zhang,Kaiyan Zhao,Shunchao Zheng,Yuhang Mu,Wenchao Du,Yiming Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces OPDSearch+, a teacher-student distillation framework that improves search-augmented reasoning for small language models without task-specific teacher fine-tuning, combining on-policy distillation with subsequent reinforcement learning to surpass RL-only baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Small language models struggle with search-augmented multi-hop reasoning, especially when relying on dynamic web search. Existing on-policy distillation approaches need expensive task-specific data collection and fine-tuned teachers; using off-the-shelf teachers directly causes instability and caps student performance at the teacher’s level. The authors aim to develop a more scalable, stable, and stronger approach to leverage search and teacher guidance without those costs and limitations.

Method: They propose OPDSearch+, a two-stage framework using a frozen off-the-shelf instruction-tuned model as teacher. In stage one, the student interacts with a live search engine, generating search-augmented reasoning trajectories, and is trained with a per-position forward KL on-policy distillation objective that transfers the teacher’s decomposition and evidence-integration behavior, without any task-specific teacher fine-tuning. In stage two, they apply reinforcement learning starting from the distilled student; the insight is that the teacher reshapes the student’s policy distribution so that subsequent RL can converge to solutions unattainable by RL from scratch. Training is evaluated on multiple QA benchmarks using a 3B-parameter student model.

Result: On seven QA benchmarks, the 3B-parameter OPDSearch+ model consistently outperforms prior 3B RL baselines. Notably, it achieves a 13.1% improvement on HotpotQA and an 8.5% improvement on 2WikiMultihopQA over the best existing 3B RL methods, demonstrating that combining teacher-guided on-policy distillation with RL yields stronger search-augmented reasoning than RL alone.

Conclusion: OPDSearch+ shows that frozen, off-the-shelf instruction models can effectively serve as teachers for search-augmented reasoning without any task-specific fine-tuning, by first reshaping the student policy through on-policy distillation and then refining it with RL. This two-stage process overcomes stability and performance-ceiling issues of naive OPD, is more scalable than collecting high-quality SFT data, and enables small models to achieve state-of-the-art results among comparable RL-based methods on challenging multi-hop QA tasks.

Abstract: Search-augmented reasoning remains difficult for small language models. On-policy distillation (OPD) from trained teachers offers a promising direction, but suffers from two issues: (1) high-quality multi-turn search trajectories depend on dynamic retriever responses, making SFT data prohibitively expensive to collect at scale; (2) task-specifically trained teachers incur substantial training cost, while directly applying OPD with an off-the-shelf teacher without task-specific fine-tuning constrains the student to the teacher's performance ceiling and suffers from severe training instability. We propose OPDSearch+, the first distillation paradigm that requires no teacher fine-tuning for search-augmented reasoning. We investigate the role of a frozen off-the-shelf instruct model as the teacher in on-policy distillation, and reveal a key insight: the teacher reshapes the student's policy distribution so that subsequent RL converges to a superior solution that RL alone cannot reach. In stage one, the student interacts with a live search engine and is distilled via a per-position forward KL objective, transferring reasoning decomposition and evidence integration skills without any task-specific teacher training. In stage two, RL refines the distilled student from a richer behavioral foundation, achieving performance that RL alone cannot reach from scratch. Across seven QA benchmarks, OPDSearch+ with a 3B model consistently outperforms all prior 3B RL baselines, achieving gains of 13.1% on HotpotQA and 8.5% on 2WikiMultihopQA.

</details>


### [133] [Benchmarking LLM Judges for Voice-Agent Evaluation: Reliability, Calibration, and Human Oversight](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24314)
*Anupam Purwar,Shashank Singh,Kritika Srivastava*

Main category: cs.AI
