TL;DR: The paper evaluates how well large language models (LLMs) like GPT-4.1 and GPT-5 can act as automated judges of conversational voice agents, comparing them with human evaluators across quality and safety metrics under different evaluation setups.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assessing conversational voice agents at scale is difficult because it usually requires human evaluators to judge nuanced interaction quality and safety. This is costly and slow, especially when voice systems involve complex pipelines (ASR, reasoning, tool calls, speech generation). The authors want to know if LLMs can reliably replace or complement humans as evaluators, and under what conditions this is trustworthy.

Method: The authors collect telecom and retail voice-agent conversations and have both human evaluators and LLMs (GPT-4.1 and GPT-5) score them on multiple conversational quality and safety dimensions. They test three evaluation configurations (p0, p1, p2) that differ in how the evaluation is set up or prompted. They then compare aggregate agreement, metric-level correlations, evaluator consistency, and systematic patterns of disagreement between humans and LLMs to determine where automated judging is reliable versus sensitive to context or interpretation.

Result: LLM judges show meaningful agreement with human evaluators and can reliably score some conversational attributes, but their performance varies significantly by metric and evaluation configuration. Certain metrics correlate strongly between humans and LLMs, while others show systematic discrepancies, highlighting where automated judgment is less trustworthy. The evaluation setup (p0, p1, p2) also affects LLM scoring behavior, and observed patterns generalize to some extent across different LLM judge models.

Conclusion: LLM-based evaluation is a useful and scalable component for assessing voice agents but is not uniformly reliable across all metrics or setups. The paper provides an empirical framework to identify which metrics are suitable for automation and advocates for hybrid evaluation pipelines where LLMs handle scalable metrics, while human evaluators remain responsible for context-heavy, high-stakes, or ambiguous judgments.

Abstract: Evaluating conversational voice agents at scale re- quires reliable assessment methods that capture both observ- able interaction quality and the contextual judgment typically provided by human evaluators. We investigate LLM-as-a-Judge evaluation by comparing human judgments with GPT-4.1 and GPT-5 on telecom and retail voice-agent conversations, across conversational quality and safety dimensions. The same interac- tions are scored under three evaluation configurations, p0, p1, and p2, to test whether automated judgments are sensitive to the evaluation setup and whether observed patterns generalize across configurations and judge models. Beyond aggregate agreement, we examine metric-level correlations, evaluator consistency, and systematic human-LLM disagreement to identify which conver- sational attributes can be judged reliably by automation and which remain sensitive to interpretation and context. Effective voice-agent evaluation is also shaped by pipeline-level factors such as speech generation, streaming, and error propagation across ASR, reasoning, and tool-calling stages, motivating our focus on comparing how human and LLM judges score the same interactions end to end. Our results show that LLM- based evaluation can serve as an effective component of large- scale voice-agent assessment, but that its reliability is metric- and configuration-dependent rather than uniform. This pro- vides an empirical framework for identifying which metrics suit automated evaluation and supports hybrid pipelines in which LLM judges handle scalable assessment while human evaluators remain engaged for metrics that demand contextual interpretation and higher-confidence judgment.

</details>


### [134] [Can a Dynamic Internal Field Govern a Transformer's Cognition? Certifiability, not Superiority, in Homeostatic Compute Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24319)
*Francisco M. Arrabal-Campos,Ignacio Fernandez,Francisco G. Montoya,Alfredo Alcayde*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether a low-dimensional dynamic internal field can serve as a certifiable controller of computation in intelligent systems and finds it can reliably govern compute and stability but does not enhance core reasoning ability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern intelligent systems not only need to perform reasoning, but also to control how much computation to use, when to stop, and which modules to activate. Existing approaches to this kind of "meta-control" are often ad hoc and lack formal guarantees about stability and safety. The authors want to know whether a single, low-dimensional internal dynamical state with explicit physics and provable stability can play this meta-control role, and whether the particular physical type of this field (wave-like, diffusive, fluid-like, etc.) matters for cognitive performance.

Method: They design a dynamic internal field defined over a graph of computational modules, with its evolution governed by a family of partial differential equations (PDEs) on the graph Laplacian. This field interacts with an adaptive-depth reasoning system, modulating which modules run and for how long. They analyze the numerical integrators for this family and prove an "integrator certificate" of stability, including a new discrete Schur-Cohn criterion for the Verlet scheme with velocity coupling, requiring no commutation assumptions. Empirically, they run a preregistered, multi-seed deconfounding study comparing first- vs second-order field dynamics, capacity-matched variants, and a matched-interface GRU controller, and they use a kill-gate experiment to test whether the field behaves as an evidence accumulator.

Result: Theoretical results show that the one-step operator of the field admits exact runtime stability checks, providing strong stability certificates independent of the specific physical interpretation of the field. Experiments show that the type of field physics (wave, diffusion, mixtures, 2D Navier-Stokes) has no consistent advantage for accuracy: they perform similarly. When confounding by capacity is controlled, a second-order field offers a detectable performance gain in one model family but not in another, and a GRU-based controller matches or slightly outperforms the field when interfaces are matched. The field does not show evidence of functioning as an evidence accumulator (no meaningful AUC gain under a positive control setup). Learned recurrent controllers can also be equipped with sufficient but conservative stability certificates.

Conclusion: A dynamic internal field is a sound and practical mechanism for governing computation in intelligent systems because it allows exact, certifiable stability checks at runtime. However, it does not inherently improve the underlying reasoning capabilities: its role is modulatory rather than cognitive. The specific physical form of the field’s dynamics is largely irrelevant to performance, and alternative controllers like GRUs can match or surpass it in capability. The distinctive benefit of the field is not superior intelligence but stronger, more explicit certifiability of its computational dynamics.

Abstract: An intelligent system does not merely reason: it governs its own reasoning - how much to compute, when to stop, which module to activate. Can that role be played by a dynamic internal field - a low-dimensional homeostatic state with explicit physics and certified stability - that modulates cognition without performing it? Ours is a field on the module graph governed by a family of PDEs on the graph Laplacian, advancing with an adaptive-depth reasoner. We certify the stability of the integrator of the whole family - an integrator certificate, not a closed-loop one. New, and proved here: a discrete Schur-Cohn criterion for Verlet with velocity coupling, necessary and sufficient per latent root, with no commutation hypothesis. The answer is threefold: substance no, structure only in part, certifiability yes. The type of the field's physics is irrelevant for accuracy: wave, diffusion, gated mixtures and a 2D Navier-Stokes substrate tie. A twenty-seed preregistered deconfounding campaign bounds the structural claim: at equalized caps the second-order effect is strong in one family (+0.087 [+0.042, +0.132], t=4.0) but is not detected in the other (+0.014 [-0.013, +0.040], n.s.), so part of the original contrast was capacity, not order; and a matched-interface GRU is indistinguishable in the first and nominally exceeds the field in the second (-0.035 [-0.067, -0.002]). What distinguishes the field is not capability but that its one-step operator admits an exact runtime stability check - a difference of kind, not of existence: learned recurrences carry certificates too, sufficient and conservative ones. A kill-gate with a positive control finds no evidence for the field as evidence accumulator (Delta AUC +0.0007 [-0.0065, +0.0079] vs a 0.03 threshold). A dynamic internal field is a viable, certifiable compute governor, but not an enhancer of cognition: it modulates, it does not think.

</details>


### [135] [SonarLLM: A Native Sonar--Optical Multimodal Large Language Model for Underwater Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24325)
*Cong Su,longxuan ma,Ling Dong,Guofeng Tang,Weijie Yin,Haohui Chen,Zhengtao Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces SonarLLM, a multimodal large language model that natively supports sonar and optical sensing, plus SonarBench, a benchmark to evaluate sonar-optical complementarity under controlled optical degradation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Underwater perception is unreliable when relying only on optical cameras because visibility degrades with turbidity, while sonar provides robust geometry but has very different structure and artifacts. Existing multimodal LLMs are designed around optical encoders and cannot effectively model sonar data or adaptively fuse sonar with images as conditions change. There is no standard benchmark to study and quantify sonar-optical complementarity under varying visibility. The paper aims to enable robust underwater perception by properly representing sonar as a first-class modality and to provide a benchmark for evaluating such models.

Method: The authors design SonarLLM, a multimodal LLM with: (1) a sonar-specific encoder that respects sonar’s range-azimuth characteristics, (2) modality-specific, physics-aware feature enhancement modules for both sonar and optical data, and (3) a reliability-aware hierarchical fusion mechanism that aligns acoustic structure with optical semantics and dynamically reweights modalities as their quality changes. They also construct SonarBench, a paired sonar-optical benchmark for four tasks—recognition, counting, VQA, and captioning—under three input regimes: sonar-only, optical-only, and fusion, with controlled optical degradation while keeping the scene and sonar fixed.

Result: On SonarBench, SonarLLM attains 72.0% macro accuracy across sonar-only recognition, counting, and VQA, beating the strongest baseline by 34.4 percentage points. Under fusion input, it reaches 68.7% macro accuracy, surpassing the best baseline by 25.1 points. For recognition and counting, the performance gain of fusion over optical-only grows from 6.0 to 36.0 points as turbidity increases, empirically demonstrating that sonar becomes progressively more beneficial when optical sensing degrades.

Conclusion: The study concludes that achieving robust underwater heterogeneous perception requires more than simply adding sonar as an extra channel; the model must represent sonar in a modality-appropriate way and dynamically weight it according to its sensing characteristics and reliability. SonarLLM and SonarBench together demonstrate that physics-aware representation and reliability-aware fusion unlock substantial gains from sonar-optical complementarity, especially under poor visibility.

Abstract: Reliable underwater perception requires complementary sensing under variable visibility. Optical cameras capture appearance and semantics but degrade rapidly with turbidity, whereas imaging sonar preserves geometry while exhibiting distinct range-azimuth structure and acoustic artifacts. Existing MLLMs, built primarily on optical encoders, are therefore ill-suited to model sonar or adaptively exploit sonar-optical complementarity. We propose SonarLLM, a sonar-optical MLLM that treats sonar as a native perceptual modality. It combines a sonar-specific encoder, modality-specific physics-aware feature enhancement, and reliability-aware hierarchical fusion to align acoustic structure with optical semantics and dynamically adjust their contributions as sensing quality changes. We also introduce SonarBench, a paired benchmark that spans four tasks: recognition, counting, visual question answering, and captioning; and, across the benchmark, three input settings: sonar-only, optical-only, and fusion. By fixing the scene and sonar observation while varying optical degradation, SonarBench enables controlled measurement of cross-modal complementarity. SonarLLM achieves 72.0% macro accuracy across sonar-only recognition, counting, and VQA, outperforming the strongest baseline by 34.4 percentage points, and 68.7% under fusion, exceeding the best baseline by 25.1 points. For recognition and counting, the fusion-over-optical gain grows from 6.0 to 36.0 points as turbidity increases, indicating the increasing complementary value of sonar under controlled optical degradation. Together, these results show that robust heterogeneous perception depends not only on adding sonar, but on representing and weighting it according to its sensing characteristics.

</details>


### [136] [Selective Regenerative Decoding: Trajectory-Level Intervention for Inference-Time Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24338)
*Sophia Xiao Pu,Yumo Xu,Sailik Sengupta,Millennium Bismay,Ruixue Lian,James Gung,Yi-an Lai,Arshit Gupta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Selective Regenerative Decoding (SRD), an inference-time decoding method that improves reasoning in LLMs by selectively regenerating only low-quality suffix segments of candidate trajectories instead of discarding entire candidates, achieving better accuracy–compute tradeoffs than rejection sampling and related methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing inference-time techniques like Best-of-N or rejection sampling explore multiple reasoning trajectories but treat each as all-or-nothing: a trajectory is either fully kept or fully discarded. This wastes computation when the initial portion of a trajectory is good but the later steps are erroneous, because the useful prefix is thrown away along with the bad suffix. The authors aim to reduce this waste, improve sample efficiency, and more finely control the accuracy–compute tradeoff during LLM reasoning without requiring larger models.

Method: The authors introduce Selective Regenerative Decoding (SRD). For each sampled trajectory, a reward or evaluation model identifies whether the trajectory should be (1) discarded entirely, (2) kept as-is, or (3) partially refined. In the refinement case, SRD detects a degraded suffix segment and regenerates only that suffix while preserving the high-quality prefix, effectively performing segment-level intervention instead of whole-trajectory selection. They analyze SRD theoretically, showing under mild assumptions that it improves sample efficiency relative to rejection sampling, and empirically evaluate it across several benchmarks (MATH500, GPQA Diamond, HotpotQA, AlpacaEval) and different generation–reward model pairings, comparing against Best-of-N and speculative rejection baselines.

Result: Theoretically, SRD is shown to yield a 1.28–1.36× gain in sample efficiency over rejection sampling while achieving strictly higher expected trajectory quality, with benefits increasing with the number of candidate trajectories. Empirically, on MATH500, GPQA Diamond, HotpotQA, and AlpacaEval, SRD matches the accuracy of Best-of-N decoding while requiring substantially fewer generated tokens. It also outperforms speculative rejection baselines in low-compute settings, demonstrating an improved accuracy–compute tradeoff for reasoning tasks.

Conclusion: SRD demonstrates that reasoning-time improvements can be obtained by operating at the segment level within trajectories rather than treating each trajectory as atomic. By selectively regenerating only low-quality suffixes, SRD achieves better sample efficiency and maintains or improves accuracy compared to common multi-sample decoding methods, without needing larger models. This opens up a new region of the accuracy–compute tradeoff space for inference-time reasoning and suggests that finer-grained control over candidate trajectories is a promising direction for future decoding algorithms.

Abstract: Inference-time decoding methods improve LLM reasoning by exploring multiple candidate trajectories, yet treat each trajectory as atomic: either retaining it whole or discarding it irreversibly. This wastes computation on partially promising candidates whose high-quality prefixes are abandoned alongside degraded suffixes. We introduce Selective Regenerative Decoding (SRD), which routes each candidate to discard, keep, or refine only the degraded portion of the suffix while preserving the useful prefix of borderline candidates, without requiring a larger target model. Under mild assumptions, SRD achieves a provable 1.28-to-1.36-fold gain in sample efficiency over rejection sampling with strictly higher expected trajectory quality, with the gain growing as the candidate pool grows. Across MATH500, GPQA Diamond, HotpotQA, and AlpacaEval with multiple generation-reward model pairs, SRD matches Best-of-N accuracy with substantially fewer generated tokens and outperforms speculative rejection in low-compute regimes. By enabling segment-level intervention rather than whole-trajectory selection, SRD opens a previously underexplored region of the accuracy-compute tradeoff for inference-time reasoning.

</details>


### [137] [The Handoff Tax: Continuing Non-Native Trajectories in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24358)
*Roy Ganz,Mor Shpigel Nacson,Adi Kalyanpur,Ron Litman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how switching between cheaper/weaker and more expensive/stronger coding models during long agent runs affects quality and cost, defining a “handoff tax” when one model must continue another’s trajectory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In real coding-agent use, users often start with a cheaper, weaker model and escalate to a stronger one when tasks get hard, or later downshift to save cost. However, it is unclear how much quality is lost or gained when one model has to pick up from another’s partial reasoning trajectory, and how much of that trajectory should be shared. Understanding this is key to building cost-effective, high-quality agent workflows.

Method: The authors experiment with pairs of low-cost/low-capability (LC) and high-cost/high-capability (HC) models from Claude and GPT families. They run long coding-agent tasks and systematically vary: (1) direction of handoff (LC→HC escalation vs HC→LC downshift), (2) timing of handoff, and (3) interface, i.e., how much of the prior trajectory is passed: full trajectory, compacted summaries, or no trajectory beyond the current code/repo state. They then compare resulting task quality and monetary cost.

Result: When escalating from LC to HC with full-trajectory transfer, the gain in quality recovers less than half of the inherent quality gap between running HC from the start versus LC from the start, yet the run incurs a large additional cost; this penalty is called the “handoff tax.” Conversely, downshifting from HC to LC tends to give a more attractive quality–cost trade-off. Moreover, for escalation it is better to reduce or compress LC trajectory information, while for downshift removing HC trajectory information harms performance.

Conclusion: Model handoffs in coding agents are not neutral: inheriting another model’s full reasoning can significantly limit the benefit of escalation, leading to a sizable “handoff tax.” Strategic downshifts are more cost-effective, and the optimal way to expose prior reasoning depends on direction—less LC trajectory is better for escalation, but preserving HC trajectory is beneficial for downshift. Designing agent systems and interfaces must therefore explicitly account for direction- and interface-dependent handoff effects to optimize both quality and cost.

Abstract: Coding agents perform long-running tasks spanning dozens of model calls, tool uses, and code edits. As these runs unfold, users face a practical cost-quality trade-off: escalating to a stronger model when a cheaper one struggles, or downshifting once the hard reasoning is complete. Each switch requires the receiver to continue a non-native trajectory produced by another model. We study how this handoff affects quality and cost, and how varying the trajectory information inherited by the receiver changes the outcome. Using pairs of low-cost, low-capability (LC) and high-cost, high-capability (HC) models from the Claude and GPT families, we vary handoff direction, timing, and interface, comparing full-trajectory transfer, compaction, and trajectory removal while preserving the repository state. Across both model families, full-trajectory escalation recovers less than half of the LC-to-HC quality gap while incurring a substantial cost premium. We term this cost-quality penalty the handoff tax. By contrast, downshift offers a favorable cost-quality point. Interestingly, the preferred interface also reverses with direction: reducing LC-model trajectory information improves escalation quality, whereas removing the HC-model trajectory reduces downshift quality.

</details>


### [138] [Adaptive Influence Graphs for Failure Attribution in Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24361)
*Yarden Bakish,Amir Dudai,Roy Ganz,Oren Nuriel,Elad Ben Avraham,Mor Shpigel Nacson,Ron Litman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Adaptive Influence Graphs (AIGs), a two-stage framework that converts multi-agent LLM execution traces into structured graphs and then navigates them to automatically locate the critical error in failed runs, achieving state-of-the-art performance on a standard failure attribution benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent LLM systems are used in practical applications where failures are costly and hard to debug. Existing automated failure attribution is limited, and debugging still heavily depends on humans, who use observability tools (not raw logs) that organize information around components, actions, and dependencies. The authors want to bring a similar structured observability paradigm to LLM-based diagnosis of multi-agent failures.

Method: They introduce Adaptive Influence Graphs (AIGs), a two-stage agentic framework. In the first stage, the system converts a failed multi-agent execution trace into a structured graph representation that encodes components, actions, and their dependencies (influence). In the second stage, an LLM-based agent actively navigates this graph, following edges and focusing on influential nodes, to identify the critical error responsible for the failure. They compare different trace representations, including richer graph-based ones and adaptive graph construction, as well as various navigation strategies.

Result: Experiments across multiple LLMs show that representing traces as richer, structured graphs improves automated failure attribution compared to using raw traces. The best performance comes from the full AIG approach that combines adaptive graph construction with agent-directed graph traversal. On the Who&When benchmark, a standard dataset for multi-agent failure attribution, AIGs achieve a new state of the art in correctly localizing which agent and step caused the failure.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that successful failure attribution in multi-agent LLM systems depends not only on the diagnostic model itself but also critically on how the execution trace is represented and explored. Adaptive Influence Graphs—structured, navigable representations of traces—enable more accurate, automated localization of critical errors, reducing reliance on manual debugging and suggesting that observability-inspired tools are a promising direction for LLM system reliability.

Abstract: Multi-agent LLM systems are increasingly deployed in real-world applications, where failures can be costly and difficult to localize. Despite growing efforts to automate failure attribution, diagnosing failed runs still largely relies on human engineers. Yet engineers rarely debug complex systems by reading raw logs end to end. Instead, observability tools organize traces around components, actions, and dependencies to support targeted navigation. We hypothesize that modern LLMs can benefit from the same paradigm. To test this hypothesis, we introduce Adaptive Influence Graphs (AIGs), a two-stage agentic framework that first transforms a failed trace into a structured graph and then navigates it to identify the critical error. Across multiple models, we show that richer trace representations consistently improve failure attribution, with adaptive graph construction and agent-directed traversal yielding the strongest results. AIGs establish a new state of the art on Who&When, the standard benchmark for multi-agent failure attribution. This affirms our hypothesis that attribution depends not only on the diagnosing model, but also on how the trace is represented and explored.

</details>


### [139] [From State to Action: OODA-Tool for Reliable Multi-Turn Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24368)
*Rongfeng Guo,Yinxuan Huang,Yusen Wu,Maoqing Zhong,Yunlu Chen,Meng Tang,Teng Long,Vincent Tao Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes OODA-Tool, a structured, closed-loop policy for multi-turn tool use that separates state tracking from action generation, improving reliability and success rates, especially for smaller models and state-heavy tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-turn tool-use methods like direct function-calling and ReAct-style policies entangle state tracking and action generation in a single autoregressive process. This causes "state-action competition," where the need to produce the next tool call can corrupt or ignore previously gathered information, degrading performance on tasks requiring accurate, evolving task state. The authors aim to design a policy architecture that preserves and manages this task state more robustly across turns and tools.

Method: The authors introduce OODA-Tool, which operationalizes the Observe-Orient-Decide-Act loop for LLM tool use. The policy is typed and closed-loop: instead of generating an action directly from the raw interaction history, the model passes through four explicit stages. Observe reconstructs an explicit task state from the conversation and tool outputs; Orient checks whether execution is warranted and what kind of step is needed; Decide constructs a controller-validated, admissible action structure; Act finally realizes the external tool call or response. A controller checks and routes intermediate representations to ensure that the final action is consistent with the current state. The approach is evaluated on Qwen3 models (0.6B–14B) under multi-turn, multi-tool, and incomplete-information benchmarks, including ablations and transfer tests.

Result: Across Qwen3 model sizes from 0.6B to 14B, OODA-Tool achieves higher task success rates than both direct function-calling and ReAct-style baselines. Improvements are most pronounced for smaller models and for tasks where correct actions depend heavily on information accumulated over multiple turns and tool results. Additional experiments with controlled variants, stage-level ablations, and transfer settings show that the gains persist when individual components are modified or when the system is applied beyond its original training conditions.

Conclusion: Separating state preservation from action realization via an OODA-style closed-loop policy mitigates state-action competition in multi-turn tool use. OODA-Tool enables more reliable, state-consistent tool calls and improves task success across model sizes and task types, especially in state-intensive scenarios. The architecture appears robust under ablations and transfers, suggesting that explicit intermediate state management is a generally useful design pattern for LLM-based tool-use agents.

Abstract: Reliable multi-turn tool use requires an agent to preserve an evolving task state and ensure that each action remains consistent with it. However, direct function-calling and ReAct-style policies learn state tracking and action generation within the same autoregressive trajectory. This coupling creates state-action competition: the pressure to produce the next call can overwrite or ignore information accumulated earlier in the interaction. Inspired by Boyd's Observe-Orient-Decide-Act cycle, we introduce OODA-Tool, a typed closed-loop policy designed to mitigate this competition by separating state preservation from action realization. Rather than generating an action directly from the interaction history, OODA-Tool routes each decision through controller-checked intermediate states, ensuring that the final output remains grounded in the current task state. Specifically, Observe reconstructs the task state, Orient determines whether execution is warranted, Decide forms an admissible action structure, and Act realizes the external output. We evaluate OODA-Tool against direct function-calling and ReAct policies using Qwen3 models ranging from 0.6B to 14B across multi-turn, multi-tool, and incomplete-information settings. OODA-Tool consistently improves task success across model sizes, with larger gains on smaller models and on tasks whose actions depend strongly on information accumulated across turns and prior tool results. Controlled variants, stage-level ablations, and transfer evaluations further demonstrate the robustness of these improvements.

</details>


### [140] [Do Recipes Have Personas? Characterizing and Generating Creator Style in Attributed Procedural Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24369)
*Lei Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They study how to automatically infer and generate creators’ unique procedural styles from cooking video transcripts using graph-based models and LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs know many procedures but tend to average out and lose individual creators’ distinctive ways of doing things. The authors want to systematically model and reproduce those personalized procedural styles (“procedural personas”) from messy, real-world data like video transcripts.

Method: They build ViralRecipesTrans, a dataset of execution flow graphs extracted from viral cooking videos and tied to specific creators. They cast procedural stylometry as a graph learning and process discovery problem, compare lexical (text-based) classifiers with topological (graph-structure) metrics, and then propose a generative framework that predicts a creator’s execution graph for new dishes. Their main model is a two-stage structured generator with Markovian priors over graph structure, and they compare it with few-shot LLMs and with an ensemble that combines both.

Result: They find that text-based/lexical classifiers overfit due to semantic leakage and fail to robustly capture style, while discrete graph-topological metrics effectively encode the rigid, physical workflow constraints of each creator. In generation, few-shot LLMs are strong at assigning semantic content but weak at high-level macro-planning of procedures, whereas their two-stage structured model offers better control over the global execution topology. An ensemble that marries LLM semantics with structured topology works best overall.

Conclusion: Procedural style can be meaningfully modeled as graph structure rather than just text. A combination of graph-based structured modeling for global execution patterns and LLMs for local semantic details yields better discovery and generation of personalized procedural workflows, enabling data-driven “procedural personas” for creators.

Abstract: While large language models (LLMs) possess vast zero-shot procedural knowledge, their tendency to produce homogenized logic often obscures the unique, idiosyncratic execution processes of individual human creators. In this paper, we investigate the computational discovery of procedural personas from unstructured data. To achieve this, we introduce ViralRecipesTrans, a new dataset of procedurally aligned execution flow graphs extracted from popular culinary video transcripts and explicitly mapped to specific creators. We formulate procedural stylometry as a graph learning and process discovery task, revealing a fundamental duality: while traditional lexical classifiers overfit via semantic leakage, discrete topological metrics successfully capture the rigid physical constraints of a creator's workflow. Building upon this characterization, we extend our framework into a novel generative task--predicting a creator's exact structural execution graph for unseen dishes. We expose a fundamental dichotomy in style generation between global macro-planning and local structural execution. Our results demonstrate that few-shot LLMs dominate semantic assignment but suffer from persistent macro-planning deficits, whereas our structured two-stage model achieves superior topological control via rigid Markovian priors. Together, an ensemble approach to procedural generation combines the strengths from both sides, dynamically synthesizing global semantic reasoning with localized topological footprints to automate the discovery and generation of personalized workflows.

</details>


### [141] [ResiSpec: Enhancing Multi-Candidate Speculative Sampling via Residual Distribution Shaping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24411)
*Zhi-Kai Chen,Jun-Jie Tao,Wei-Xiang Mao,De-Chuan Zhan,Han-Jia Ye*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies a key inefficiency in multi-candidate speculative decoding for LLMs—Residual Drift—and introduces ResiSpec, a verification-stage reformulation that realigns the proposal and target distributions, greatly reducing resampling and achieving up to 1.92× speedup without changing model outputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive decoding in LLMs is slow because tokens are generated sequentially. Speculative Decoding speeds this up but is still limited by frequent candidate rejections, especially in multi-candidate schemes that try to propose more future tokens at once. Existing methods suffer when early candidate rejections make later candidates poorly aligned with the true target distribution, causing wasteful resampling and capping speed gains. The paper aims to understand this failure mode and design a method that keeps multi-candidate speculative decoding efficient and exact.

Method: The authors first analyze multi-candidate speculative decoding and formalize Residual Drift: when initial speculative tokens are rejected, the conditional (residual) target distribution over later positions diverges from what the draft model predicted, making its remaining candidates low-quality. They then propose ResiSpec, a framework that modifies the verification process itself. During verification, ResiSpec strategically reforms the proposal distribution so that the remaining target probability mass is concentrated in regions where the draft model assigns high confidence. This is done via a mathematical re-alignment of the proposal and target distributions, preserving the exactness of the final output distribution while altering how candidates are validated and resampled.

Result: ResiSpec significantly reduces the obsolescence of speculative candidates after early rejections, cutting down expensive resampling operations. Empirically, it achieves up to 1.92× speedup over state-of-the-art multi-candidate speculative decoding baselines while maintaining exact equivalence to standard LLM decoding in terms of output distribution and quality.

Conclusion: Residual Drift is an inherent bottleneck in existing multi-candidate speculative decoding schemes because early rejections distort the effective target distribution for remaining candidates. By reformulating the verification step to keep the residual target mass aligned with high-confidence regions of the draft model, ResiSpec maintains candidate usefulness, avoids costly resampling, and yields substantial speedups without sacrificing exactness. This establishes a more principled and efficient approach to multi-candidate speculative decoding for LLM serving.

Abstract: The efficiency of Large Language Model (LLM) serving is fundamentally limited by the sequential nature of autoregressive decoding. Speculative Decoding (SD) mitigates this by using a lightweight draft model to speculate future tokens, which are then validated by the LLM in a single parallel forward pass. To further boost efficiency, multi-candidate schemes propose diverse candidate sets to increase the likelihood of token acceptance. However, we show that these schemes are bottlenecked by Residual Drift: a phenomenon where the rejection of initial candidates causes the residual target distribution to diverge from the draft model's predictions. This shift renders subsequent candidates ineffective and forces the system into expensive resampling. To resolve this, we propose ResiSpec, a framework that strategically reforms the proposal distribution during verification to anchor the residual target mass within the draft model's high-confidence regions. By mathematically re-aligning the verification process without compromising output exactness, ResiSpec prevents candidate obsolescence and achieves up to 1.92$\times$ speedup over state-of-the-art multi-candidate methods. Code is available at https://github.com/Czzzk/Resispec.

</details>


### [142] [A Judge Should Know What Changed:Construct Validity for LLM-as-a-Judge Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24419)
*Jianlin Chen,Wenhui Chen,Ziyao Lin,Chi Man Vong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that current ways of evaluating LLM-as-a-judge (like agreement rates and robustness to small surface changes) miss a key notion of construct validity, and proposes a 2D framework—construct invariance and construct sensitivity—to better capture whether model judges truly track the underlying construct being evaluated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM judges are widely used to evaluate other models, but current reliability-focused metrics (e.g., inter-annotator agreement, robustness to paraphrases) do not guarantee that these judges are actually sensitive to the underlying construct of interest (e.g., answer quality, correctness). This gap means systems can appear reliable while ignoring meaningful changes to what is being judged, leading to misleading evaluation results. The paper aims to formalize and measure this missing dimension of validity.

Method: The authors define construct validity of an evaluator via two probabilities: invariance S (verdict stays the same under edits that preserve the construct) and construct sensitivity R (verdict changes under minimal edits that change the construct). They show S and R are mathematically independent and cannot be collapsed into a single scalar without losing important information. Empirically, they design intervention types that either change the construct (7 types: including scope and strength edits) or only affect surface register (5 types: style-only controls). Human annotators determine the intended direction (better/worse) of construct-changing edits. They then evaluate 7 LLM judges across 4 domains, carefully separating the models used for generation, verification, and judging to avoid leakage. Finally, they audit five public label sets by testing how well surface-only predictors can reproduce labels, including MT-Bench human votes.

Result: Across judges and domains, at a high and matched level of invariance (S ≥ 0.90), the average judge has very high invariance (S = 0.945) but low construct sensitivity (R = 0.319), indicating that judges often fail to flip their verdicts when the underlying construct changes slightly. Sensitivity differs by edit type: judges are more sensitive to scope changes than to strength changes (R_scope = 0.383 vs. R_strength = 0.262), and this +0.121 gap holds consistently across all 7 judges. In the label-set audit, simple surface-only predictors can reproduce 55–67% of labels in paired setups, including 67.4% of MT-Bench human votes, showing that many existing labels can be predicted from superficial cues rather than deep construct understanding.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that high agreement and robustness of LLM judges are compatible with poor construct sensitivity: models can look reliable while largely ignoring meaningful construct changes. Because invariance and sensitivity are independent, reporting only scalar reliability metrics is insufficient. The authors advocate characterizing judges with a 2D profile of invariance and sensitivity, and additionally auditing validation sets for surface-cue predictability to ensure that evaluations genuinely reflect the constructs they intend to measure.

Abstract: LLM-as-a-judge evaluation is usually assessed by agreement and robustness to surface perturbations, but reliability does not establish construct validity. We formalize construct validity for an evaluator as a two-dimensional profile: invariance S, the probability that a verdict is unchanged under construct-preserving edits, and construct sensitivity R, the probability that it changes under minimal construct-changing edits. We show that S and R are independent and that no scalar summary preserves all relevant comparisons. We measure the profile across 7 judges and 4 domains using 7 construct-changing intervention types and 5 register-only controls, with intervention direction determined by human annotators and generation, verification, and judging assigned to disjoint model families. At matched invariance S >= 0.90, judges average S = 0.945 but R = 0.319. Sensitivity also differs between scope and strength edits: R_scope = 0.383 versus R_strength = 0.262, a +0.121 gap with the same sign for all 7 judges. We further audit five public label sets and find that surface-only predictors reproduce 55%-67% of labels in paired mode, including 67.4% of MT-Bench human votes. These results show that high judge agreement can coexist with weak sensitivity to changes in the construct being evaluated, motivating joint reporting of invariance and sensitivity and auditing the validation set itself.

</details>


### [143] [Partial Identification under Causal Orders by Linear Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24427)
*Eric Rossetto,Alessandro Antonucci*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They provide a general method to compute sharp bounds on very general counterfactual (and nested counterfactual) quantities without needing a full causal graph, by turning the problem into a linear program based on a query-induced variable ordering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most existing methods for identifying counterfactual quantities assume a fully known causal graph, which is often unrealistic because domain knowledge is incomplete. There is a need for principled ways to reason about counterfactuals when the causal structure is only partially known or unknown, producing bounds rather than point identification.

Method: They observe that any counterfactual query itself imposes a (typically partial) topological ordering on the variables it involves. Using this order, they explicitly parameterize the query and reduce the task of finding compatible counterfactual values to a linear programming problem under constraints from observed data and the query-implied order. This extends Tian and Pearl’s bounding framework for probabilities of causation to arbitrary (including nested) counterfactuals. They also construct structural causal models that realize the extremal values of the LP to prove tightness.

Result: They obtain a general, systematic procedure to compute bounds for arbitrary counterfactual and nested counterfactual queries using only observed data and mild structural constraints implied by the query, without requiring a specified causal graph. They show that the resulting bounds are tight by constructing compatible SCMs that attain them.

Conclusion: Counterfactual reasoning does not always require a fully specified causal graph: the structure inherent in the query can be exploited to derive sharp bounds on counterfactual quantities via linear programming. This generalizes earlier bounding work on probabilities of causation and is practically useful, as demonstrated by case studies where informative bounds and insights are obtained even without an input causal graph.

Abstract: Non-parametric (partial) identification of counterfactual queries typically relies on a fully specified causal graph. Motivated by settings with incomplete domain knowledge, we challenge this requirement by leveraging structural assumptions that are inherently implied by the query itself. We show that any counterfactual inquiry induces a, mostly partial, topological ordering over relevant variables, which, in turn, enables an explicit query parametrisation reducing the identification task to a linear program. This allows bounding arbitrary counterfactual and nested counterfactual queries. Our work can be viewed as a generalisation of the classical bounding framework of Tian and Pearl (2000), originally developed for probabilities of causation. We also prove the \emph{tightness} of our bounds by constructing structural causal models that attain the bounds whilst being compatible with both the observed data and the query-implied order. To assess both the generality and practical utility of the proposed bounding procedure, we revisit several case studies from the literature, demonstrating how the derived bounds can be used to yield informative insights even in the absence of an input causal graph.

</details>


### [144] [A Behavior-Guided Online Probabilistic Forecasting Method for Electric vehicle Charging Loads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24441)
*Chenghan Li,Qingxiang Liu,Yinliang Xu,Yuxuan Liang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces an online probabilistic forecasting method for EV charging loads that accounts for both long-term station-specific behaviors and short-term behavioral changes, achieving significantly better accuracy and reliability than existing baselines, especially under evolving conditions and longer horizons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EV charging loads are highly heterogeneous across stations and vary over time, making accurate online probabilistic forecasting difficult, particularly when user behavior changes (concept drift) and when different stations have distinct persistent usage patterns. Existing models and drift-aware methods do not sufficiently capture both persistent station-specific patterns and recent behavioral changes in a unified, online, probabilistic framework, motivating a new approach that can adapt to evolving behaviors while providing reliable probabilistic forecasts.

Method: The paper proposes a behavior-guided online probabilistic forecasting framework with a dual-timescale behavior representation: one component captures persistent, long-term station-specific charging patterns, and another captures recent behavioral states and their deviations from the long-term baseline. These behavior representations are semantically encoded and used to guide a drift-aware adaptation mechanism in the forecasting model. A delayed-feedback mechanism is incorporated to perform temporally consistent online parameter updates once actual observations are available for different forecasting horizons, enabling continuous learning and adaptation under nonstationary conditions.

Result: On data from ten heterogeneous real-world EV charging stations, the proposed method consistently outperforms standard forecasting models and existing online concept-drift-aware baselines in both point and probabilistic metrics. For 1-hour-ahead forecasts, it reduces mean squared error (MSE) by 15.3% and Pinball loss by 17.8% compared with the best baselines. For 4-hour-ahead forecasts, the improvements increase to 16.8% in MSE and 22.6% in Pinball loss, indicating robust performance gains as forecasting horizons extend and behaviors evolve.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling both persistent station-specific behaviors and recent behavioral changes via a dual-timescale representation and using these to guide drift-aware, delayed-feedback online learning substantially improves the accuracy and reliability of probabilistic EV charging load forecasts. The framework is effective across heterogeneous stations and under evolving behavior patterns, and scales well to longer forecasting horizons, suggesting its practical value for real-world EV charging infrastructure management and grid operation under uncertainty.

Abstract: Electric vehicle (EV) charging loads exhibit strong behavioral heterogeneity and temporal variability, posing significant challenges for online probabilistic forecasting under evolving operating conditions. In particular, persistent charging patterns may differ substantially across stations, while recent behavioral changes can continuously alter the underlying load distributions. This paper proposes a behavior-guided online probabilistic forecasting framework that explicitly characterizes persistent station-specific patterns and recent behavioral changes. A dual-timescale behavior representation is constructed to distinguish long-term charging characteristics from recent behavioral states and quantify their deviations. These behavioral changes are further semantically encoded to guide drift-aware forecasting adaptation, while a delayed-feedback mechanism ensures temporally consistent online updates when observations become available across different forecasting horizons. Experiments on ten heterogeneous real-world charging stations demonstrate that the proposed method consistently outperforms conventional forecasting models and concept-drift-aware online baselines in forecasting accuracy and probabilistic reliability. For 1-h-ahead forecasting, the proposed method reduces MSE and Pinball loss by 15.3\% and 17.8\%, respectively, over the corresponding best baselines. For 4-h-ahead forecasting, the improvements further reach 16.8\% and 22.6\%, respectively, demonstrating consistent performance gains under evolving charging behaviors and extended forecasting horizons.

</details>


### [145] [Mahalanobis-Based Multi-Head Attention for Complex State Propagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24462)
*Xiaohe Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes Mahalanobis-Based Multi-Head Attention (MHA-CSP) that replaces dot-product attention with a Mahalanobis distance-based RBF kernel, enabling tree-structured and cross-head collaborative attention with high efficiency and strong performance on long-sequence state tracking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard Transformer attention relies on dot-products and dense attention, which can be inefficient and may struggle with structured reasoning and long-sequence state tracking. There is a need for an attention mechanism that better captures symbolic and structured relationships while remaining parameter-efficient and trainable from scratch, and that can exploit multi-head interactions more effectively.

Method: Replace dot-product attention with a Mahalanobis distance-based RBF kernel within a complex-valued state propagation (CSP) backbone, defining attention scores through accumulated Mahalanobis distances and using a LogSumExp correction to convert distances into normalized tree-like attention weights (Tree Attention). Reuse the multi-head Mahalanobis distance matrices to build an attention meshing mechanism that allows cross-head kernel collaboration, improving both accuracy and training efficiency. Apply teacher forcing only at the final hidden state in long-sequence state tracking tasks.

Result: On long-sequence state tracking benchmarks, the proposed MHA-CSP model with only 119K parameters and teacher forcing restricted to the final hidden state outperforms Transformer and GCN baselines that are trained from scratch under identical settings. It achieves better structured reasoning performance despite having fewer parameters and more constrained supervision.

Conclusion: Mahalanobis-based multi-head attention with tree attention construction and cross-head meshing, combined with complex-valued state propagation, provides an efficient yet expressive mechanism for structured reasoning and symbolic structure capture. This architecture establishes a favorable efficiency-performance trade-off compared with conventional dense attention Transformers and GCNs, particularly for long-sequence state tracking tasks.

Abstract: In this paper, we propose \textbf{Mahalanobis-Based Multi-Head Attention} (MHA-CSP), a novel attention mechanism that replaces the standard dot-product with a \textbf{Mahalanobis distance-based RBF kernel}, which effectively computes attention in an infinite-dimensional feature space without increasing the parameter count. Crucially, the positive definiteness of the Mahalanobis distance enables a \textbf{direct construction of Tree Attention}: attention scores are built directly from accumulated distances, with a LogSumExp correction that rectifies the raw distance by subtracting the log-sum of edge exponentials. Moreover, the multi-head Mahalanobis distance matrices are themselves repurposed to construct an \textbf{attention meshing mechanism}, enabling cross-head kernel collaboration that simultaneously boosts accuracy and training efficiency.
  Extensive experiments demonstrate that MHA-CSP, with only 119K parameters and \textbf{teacher forcing applied exclusively at the final hidden state}, consistently outperforms Transformer and GCN baselines trained from scratch under identical conditions on long-sequence state tracking tasks. While these baselines rely on dense attention or graph propagation, MHA-CSP achieves robust structured reasoning via synthetic distance rectification---powered by Mahalanobis-based attention---and efficient information bypass inherited from the CSP backbone.
  This result highlights the effectiveness of complex-valued state propagation with collaborative multi-head rectification in capturing symbolic structures, establishing a new efficiency-performance trade-off for structured reasoning.

</details>


### [146] [HMGCLIP: Heterogeneous Multi-Granularity Contrastive Learning for E-commerce Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24467)
*Qiuyu Zhu,Yi Gao,Zhichao Wan,Mingyang Ma*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes HMGCLIP, a multimodal embedding framework using heterogeneous hypergraphs to better capture fine-grained product attributes and outperform existing MLLMs and encoders on e-commerce benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal large language models and multimodal encoders mostly encode product information into global embeddings, which are insufficient for capturing fine-grained attributes (e.g., subtle material differences between visually similar products). This weakens performance on tasks that need precise attribute discrimination, especially in e-commerce scenarios where small differences matter. There is also a need for better fine-grained e-commerce benchmarks.

Method: Introduce HMGCLIP, a unified multimodal embedding framework based on a heterogeneous hypergraph. The model constructs a hypergraph over multimodal data and uses the hypergraph topology to mine structure-aware hard negatives, strengthening contrastive learning. It aligns multi-granular semantics at both relation level (between items/attributes) and hyperedge level (higher-order groups) to better encode attributes. A dual-granularity inference mechanism is then used to dynamically fuse attribute evidence, supporting both fine-grained and coarse-grained downstream tasks. Additionally, the authors build and release a new fine-grained e-commerce dataset for training and evaluation.

Result: On the newly released fine-grained e-commerce dataset and the public MAVE benchmark, HMGCLIP consistently outperforms strong multimodal encoders, mainstream multimodal LLMs, and specialized e-commerce baselines across evaluated tasks, indicating stronger fine-grained attribute understanding and overall multimodal representation quality.

Conclusion: Modeling multimodal product data with a heterogeneous hypergraph and performing multi-granular alignment plus structure-aware hard negative mining yields more discriminative embeddings for fine-grained attribute understanding. HMGCLIP delivers state-of-the-art performance on both a new fine-grained e-commerce dataset and MAVE, demonstrating that hypergraph-based, dual-granularity inference is a superior approach for e-commerce product representation and related downstream tasks.

Abstract: Although recent Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have advanced general product understanding, they implicitly encode product information into global embeddings, thereby limiting their ability to capture fine-grained attributes. This limitation hinders performance in tasks requiring precise attribute discrimination, such as distinguishing subtle material differences among visually similar products. To address this challenge, we propose HMGCLIP, a unified multimodal embedding framework. By constructing a heterogeneous hypergraph, we leverage hypergraph topology to mine structure-aware hard negatives and align multi-granular semantics at both relation and hyperedge levels. This design enables a dual-granularity inference mechanism that dynamically fuses attribute evidence for both fine-grained and coarse-grained downstream tasks. Furthermore, we release a comprehensive fine-grained e-commerce dataset to facilitate future benchmarking. Extensive experiments on this new dataset and the public MAVE benchmark show that HMGCLIP outperforms strong multimodal encoders, MLLMs, and e-commerce baselines, validating the superiority of HMGCLIP.

</details>


### [147] [Reinforcement Learning-Guided Evolutionary Policy Optimization for Preference-Adjustable Heterogeneous Agile Earth Observation Satellite Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24470)
*He Wang,Junyu Wu,Hui Li,Yanjie Song,Witold Pedrycz,Liang Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a reinforcement-learning-assisted evolutionary framework to schedule heterogeneous agile Earth observation satellites, improving utility and robustness over existing metaheuristics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scheduling heterogeneous agile Earth observation satellites is challenging because each satellite has different orbits, maneuvering abilities, and resource constraints, so the same observation task looks different across platforms in terms of visibility windows, transition costs, and resource usage. This heterogeneity makes unified modeling hard and degrades the performance of traditional optimization approaches, especially when multiple objectives like task gain, energy saving, and load balancing must be balanced under tight computational budgets.

Method: The authors build an evolutionary policy optimization framework that uses an assignment-based indirect encoding plus a decoder that evaluates schedules via an equivalent-cost mechanism while respecting satellite-specific constraints. They scalarize multiple objectives (task gain, energy saving, load balance) into a weighted utility function that can be tuned by user preference. In the optimization layer, they decouple schedule construction from search control: a population-based memetic evolutionary algorithm handles exploration, repair, and local improvement, while an actor–critic reinforcement learning component dynamically selects search operators (e.g., exploration, repair, refinement) online. This integrated algorithm is called RLOSMEA (Reinforcement-Learning-Assisted Operator-Selection Memetic Evolutionary Algorithm).

Result: Across multiple heterogeneous AEOS scheduling scenarios, the proposed RLOSMEA outperforms representative metaheuristic baselines by achieving higher overall weighted utility and more stable convergence within the same evaluation budget. The algorithm demonstrates better robustness across scenarios, and analyses of sensitivity and learning dynamics show that the reinforcement-learning module learns effective operator-selection policies that contribute significantly to the performance gains.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that combining indirect schedule encoding, decoder-based equivalent-cost evaluation, and reinforcement-learning-guided operator selection in a memetic evolutionary framework is an effective way to tackle complex heterogeneous AEOS scheduling. The approach not only improves solution quality and convergence stability under limited computational resources but is also robust to scenario variations and provides a tunable way to accommodate different objective preferences.

Abstract: Heterogeneous agile Earth observation satellite (AEOS) scheduling requires task selection, satellite assignment, and observation sequencing under satellite-dependent visibility windows, attitude maneuvering requirements, energy consumption, and onboard storage constraints. Since satellites differ in orbital access, maneuvering capability, and payload resources, the same task may have different feasible windows, transition costs, and resource-consumption patterns on different platforms, which increases the difficulty of unified modeling and efficient optimization. To address this problem, this paper proposes an evolutionary policy optimization framework for heterogeneous AEOS scheduling with preference-adjustable weighted objectives. In the modeling layer, assignment-based indirect encoding is combined with decoder-based equivalent-cost evaluation to retain satellite-dependent constraints while integrating task gain, energy saving, and load balance into an interpretable scalar utility. In the optimization layer, schedule decoding, population-based search, and online actor-critic operator control are decoupled, so that reinforcement learning selects high-level search operators rather than constructing schedules directly. Based on this framework, a reinforcement-learning-assisted operator-selection memetic evolutionary algorithm (RLOSMEA) is developed to coordinate global exploration, feasibility recovery, and local refinement under a limited function-evaluation budget. Experiments on different heterogeneous AEOS scenarios show that RLOSMEA achieves higher overall weighted utility and more stable convergence than representative metaheuristic baselines. Sensitivity and learning-behavior analyses further confirm the robustness of the proposed method and the effectiveness of reinforcement-learning-guided operator selection.

</details>


### [148] [Implicit Q-learning-bootstrapped ant colony optimization for maritime moving-target observation scheduling with agile satellites](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24471)
*He Wang,Junyu Wu,Yeye Liu,Yifan Zhou,Jie Zhang,Hui Li,Yanjie Song,Liang Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces IQACO, a hybrid of implicit Q-learning and ant colony optimization, to improve scheduling of agile Earth observation satellites for moving maritime targets, achieving better observation benefits and faster convergence than standard ACO.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scheduling agile satellites to observe moving maritime targets is complex due to continuous target motion, changing observation windows, and multiple operational constraints. Traditional heuristic methods like standard ant colony optimization lack adaptive control over their search behavior, leading to suboptimal performance and slower convergence across varying scenarios. There is a need for an approach that can automatically adapt search parameters to different problem scales, satellite configurations, and objective-weight settings while handling tight operational constraints and sequence dependencies.

Method: The paper formulates maritime moving-target observation scheduling as a dynamic, sequence-dependent combinatorial optimization problem with constraints on time windows, satellite attitudes, onboard resources, and cloud availability. It proposes IQACO, which embeds an offline implicit Q-learning module into a constructive ant colony optimization (ACO) framework. Instead of learning a direct task-selection policy, the Q-learning component learns to adapt key ACO control parameters—pheromone factor, heuristic factor, and evaporation rate—based on a compact representation of the search state (pheromone distribution, current and historical best solution quality, and iteration progress). During online scheduling, standard ACO constructs feasible observation sequences while the learned policy dynamically regulates exploration vs. exploitation through these parameters.

Result: Experiments across 14 scenarios with varying scales and satellite configurations show that IQACO consistently achieves the highest mean observation benefit compared to baselines, outperforming conventional ACO by 3.40%–9.40%. The method also demonstrates faster convergence of the search process and robustness to different objective-weight settings, indicating stability and adaptability of the learned parameter-control policy.

Conclusion: IQACO demonstrates that offline value-based reinforcement learning can effectively serve as an adaptive search-control mechanism within metaheuristic optimization for constrained maritime moving-target observation scheduling. By learning how to tune ACO parameters from experience, the method improves observation benefits, accelerates convergence, and maintains stability under diverse conditions, suggesting a promising direction for combining RL-based value learning with classical metaheuristics in complex satellite scheduling problems.

Abstract: Maritime moving-target observation scheduling with agile Earth observation satellites is a dynamic, sequence-dependent combinatorial optimization problem. Sea-surface targets move continuously, causing feasible observation windows to vary with target motion and satellite orbital geometry. The scheduler must jointly determine task selection, satellite assignment, observation-window selection, and observation ordering under time-window, attitude-maneuvering, onboard-resource, and cloud-affected availability constraints. This paper proposes an implicit Q-learning-bootstrapped ant colony optimization method, termed IQACO, for multi-satellite maritime moving-target observation scheduling. Rather than directly learning a task-selection policy, IQACO embeds an offline implicit Q-learning module into constructive ant colony optimization to adaptively adjust the pheromone factor, heuristic factor, and evaporation rate. A compact search-state representation captures pheromone distribution, current and historical-best solution quality, and iteration progress. During online scheduling, ant colony optimization constructs feasible observation sequences, while the learned policy regulates exploration and exploitation according to the current search state. Experiments on 14 scenarios with different scales and satellite configurations show that IQACO obtains the highest mean observation benefit in every scenario, improves the result of conventional ant colony optimization by 3.40\%--9.40\%, accelerates convergence, and remains stable under different objective-weight settings. These results demonstrate that offline value learning provides an effective adaptive search-control mechanism for constrained maritime moving-target observation scheduling.

</details>


### [149] [PeakBench: Benchmarking Resource-Aware Tool Invocation in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24509)
*Zhi-Kai Chen,Xu-Xiang Zhong,Song-Yan Li,De-Chuan Zhan,Han-Jia Ye*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PeakBench is a benchmark to evaluate how LLM agents schedule and parallelize multi-tool workflows under resource constraints, separating logical planning from physical scheduling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents increasingly use multiple tools in parallel to reduce latency, but current benchmarks focus mostly on tool choice and argument quality, assuming largely serial execution. This ignores the scheduling dimension, where naive parallelism can cause resource overflows and failures. There is a need for a benchmark that explicitly evaluates how well agents plan dependencies and schedule tool calls given resource limits.

Method: The authors build PeakBench, a collection of executable multi-tool workflows annotated with explicit execution-grounded dependency information and empirical resource usage profiles. They design a two-part evaluation framework that separates logical dependency planning (what can or must run in parallel or sequence) from physical scheduling (how to execute given resource constraints), and define metrics for each part to attribute failures to planning vs scheduling errors.

Result: Experiments using PeakBench show that agents with strong logical planning capabilities still frequently fail to execute workflows safely and efficiently when resource constraints are present. The authors find that providing agents with explicit resource information leads to fewer avoidable resource overflows and better resource utilization on the benchmark.

Conclusion: Logical planning ability alone is insufficient for robust multi-tool agent performance; resource-aware scheduling is a distinct challenge that must be evaluated and improved. PeakBench offers a standardized benchmark and evaluation methodology to study and diagnose agents’ resource-aware behavior, encouraging development of agents that both plan correctly and schedule tool invocations safely and efficiently under constraints.

Abstract: LLM agents increasingly solve tasks by invoking multiple tools, where parallel execution is essential for low latency but difficult to manage safely. Existing agent benchmarks primarily evaluate tool selection, argument generation, and end-to-end success under mostly serial execution, largely overlooking valid parallelization and resource-constrained scheduling. This missing scheduling dimension creates a practical failure mode: serial execution is safe but slow, while resource-agnostic parallel execution is fast but prone to avoidable resource overflows. To address this gap, we introduce PeakBench, a benchmark of executable multi-tool workflows with execution-grounded dependency annotations and measured resource profiles. A central challenge in evaluating such workflows is attribution: failures and inefficiencies may arise from incorrect dependency planning, poor resource-constrained scheduling, or both. PeakBench addresses this challenge with a two-part evaluation framework that disentangles logical planning from physical scheduling, with dedicated metrics for each dimension. Using this framework, we show that strong logical planning does not reliably translate into safe or efficient execution under resource constraints. We further show that exposing resource information can reduce avoidable overflows and improve resource utilization, making PeakBench a useful testbed for diagnosing resource-aware agent behavior. Code is available at https://github.com/Czzzk/Staggering-the-Peaks.

</details>


### [150] [Neurosymbolic Alignment for Physiologically-Safe Clinical Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24534)
*Abdulhady Abas Abdullah,Erik Cambria,Milena Zivkovic*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They train a clinical LLM using a physiological knowledge graph and graph neural network–based scoring so that its medical recommendations better satisfy human physiology and safety constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical LLMs often produce medically plausible but physiologically unsafe recommendations, and current safety alignment typically relies on text-only human preference data that does not leverage structured biomedical knowledge. The authors want to see whether grounding alignment in explicit physiological and pharmacological structure can significantly improve safety and reliability of clinical reasoning models.

Method: They introduce Neurosymbolic Alignment, which couples a 7B-parameter clinical LLM with a Physiological World Model implemented as an HGNN over an 847K-node biomedical knowledge graph. For each candidate response during training, they compute scores based on homeostatic physiological constraints, multi-hop path plausibility in the knowledge graph, and penalties for unsafe drug–drug interactions. These scores induce rankings used to perform iterative on-policy ORPO (offline RL-style preference optimization) updates to the LLM. They evaluate the resulting model on the Clinical Safety Benchmark (CSB), compare against baseline ORPO, SFT+SelfCorrect, and GPT-4 (5-shot), run ablations for HGNN scoring and iterative training, assess robustness to noisy EHR-like inputs, and calibrate a PhysioScore predictor against clinician labels.

Result: Compared with standard ORPO, Neurosymbolic Alignment dramatically improves multiple safety metrics on CSB: Clinical Safety Score (CSS) rises from 69.5% to 90.8% (+21.3 percentage points), physician-evaluated harmful rate (HR) drops from 14.1% to 5.1%, and a drug-interaction metric (DID) improves from 72.8% to 91.6%. An independent rule-based safety score (RSS) reaches 86.4%, which is +21.2 points over ORPO and shows high concordance with CSS (r=0.97). The method outperforms GPT-4 (5-shot) on all safety metrics despite being 10x smaller, and beats an inference-time self-correction baseline by 11.4 points in CSS. Under synthetic noisy EHR inputs, 84.2% CSS is maintained. Ablations reveal that removing HGNN-based scoring or iterative training substantially harms performance (-16.2 and -11.5 points in CSS, respectively). Their PhysioScore safety calibration against 200 clinician annotations yields low calibration error (ECE=0.038) and high inter-rater style agreement (kappa=0.91).

Conclusion: Embedding structured physiological knowledge into the training-time alignment process of a clinical LLM substantially and measurably improves its safety on controlled benchmarks. These gains are robust, independently validated by rule engines and clinician review, and surpass both larger general models and inference-time self-correction approaches. However, the authors emphasize that external validation on real-world clinical data is still needed to determine how well these safety improvements translate to practical deployment.

Abstract: Clinical LLMs can generate recommendations that are factually plausible yet physiologically unsafe. We investigate whether safety alignment can be improved by grounding preference optimization in structured physiological knowledge rather than text-only supervision. Methods: We propose Neurosymbolic Alignment, a training-time framework that couples a 7B clinical LLM with an HGNN-based Physiological World Model over an 847K-node biomedical knowledge graph. Candidate responses are scored using homeostatic constraints, multi-hop path plausibility, and drug-interaction penalties, and the resulting rankings drive iterative on-policy ORPO updates. Evaluation is performed on the Clinical Safety Benchmark (CSB), a 2,500-scenario benchmark for physiological constraint violations in generative clinical reasoning. Results: Relative to ORPO, the proposed method improves CSS from 69.5% to 90.8% (+21.3 pp), reduces physician-evaluated HR from 14.1% to 5.1% on the blinded subset, and improves DID from 72.8% to 91.6%. These gains are corroborated by an HGNN-independent Rule-Engine Safety Score (RSS: 86.4%, +21.2 pp over ORPO; r=0.97 concordance with CSS). The method also exceeds GPT-4 (5-shot) on all safety metrics despite a 10x parameter disadvantage, and outperforms an inference-time self-correction pipeline (SFT+SelfCorrect) by 11.4 pp CSS. Under synthetic EHR-style noise, 84.2% CSS is retained. Ablation analysis shows that HGNN scoring (-16.2 pp) and iterative training (-11.5 pp) are the dominant contributors. PhysioScore calibration against 200 clinician labels yielded ECE = 0.038 and kappa = 0.91. Conclusion: Training-time physiological grounding produces measurable and independently verifiable safety improvements in open-weight clinical LLMs under controlled evaluation. External validation on real clinical data is required to determine whether these gains transfer to deployment settings

</details>


### [151] [Discovering Adaptive Transmission Programs for Collective Innovation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24545)
*Cédric Colas,Jérémy Perez,Eleni Nisioti,Akhilesh Mocherla,Pierre-Yves Oudeyer,Clément Moulin-Frier,Maxime Derex*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces and studies state-aware transmission protocols—rules that adapt information sharing based on what individuals and the group currently know—and shows that these protocols can substantially improve group performance in a discovery task compared to standard, fixed network-based communication structures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human collective intelligence depends not just on who is connected to whom, but on how information and resources are routed as the group’s knowledge and state evolve. Existing work focuses mainly on static network structure, which cannot adapt transmission to content or group state. The authors want a formal and computational framework to design adaptive, state-aware protocols that can be optimized to enhance coordination and performance in complex tasks.

Method: They formalize transmission protocols as state-aware programs that condition routing decisions on both agent-level and collective states. Using a collective discovery task as a testbed, they employ LLM-guided evolutionary search to automatically generate, mutate, and select protocols that maximize group performance. They compare evolved protocols against standard baselines from the literature and run ablation studies that remove content-dependence while preserving network topology and timing, to isolate the role of state-awareness. They also test the transferability of evolved protocols across different domains and agent populations.

Result: The evolved state-aware transmission protocols improve collective performance by up to 37% over standard network-based baselines. Ablation experiments show that when content-dependence is removed—while keeping the same topology and timing—the performance advantages disappear, indicating that state-awareness is the key driver.
