Additionally, the protocols generalize well, maintaining performance benefits when applied to varied task domains and to different agent populations than those used during optimization.

Conclusion: State-aware, programmatic transmission protocols can be discovered automatically in silico and can significantly and robustly enhance collective problem-solving compared to traditional, state-agnostic network structures. The study highlights that conditioning communication on the evolving informational state of individuals and the group is crucial, and it points toward an AI-assisted approach to designing coordination infrastructures that improve human collective intelligence in a flexible and generalizable way.

Abstract: Human collective intelligence depends on transmission processes: who shares what with whom, how, and when. While these processes emerge from individual cognition, they can also be directed by deliberate top-down protocols. Prior work has studied how transmission shapes collective outcomes primarily through the lens of network structure, varying who shares with whom and when. But networks are state-agnostic: they cannot condition transmission on what agents know or on the state of the collective. Here, we formalize transmission protocols as state-aware programs that route information and resources based on agent and collective states, and we use LLM-guided evolutionary search to design effective protocols in a collective discovery task. Evolved protocols increase collective performance over standard baselines from the literature by up to 37%. Ablations confirm that state-awareness drives this advantage: removing content-dependence while preserving network topology and timing eliminates performance gains. We find that evolved protocols also transfer across domain variations and agent populations. These results demonstrate that effective and generalizable transmission protocols can be discovered in silico, suggesting a path toward AI-assisted design of coordination infrastructure that enhances human collective intelligence.

</details>


### [152] [When "Must" Becomes "Maybe": Constraint Weakening in LLM Agent Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24569)
*Yiheng Sun,Huifei Wang,Yancheng Zhu,Zhenyu Li,Zebin Zhao,Yifan Yuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates how intermediate language artifacts in LLM agent workflows often fail to preserve action-binding safety constraints, showing that common handoff transformations can deactivate safety blockers even when their content is semantically retained.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents perform complex, multi-stage tasks by passing language artifacts between roles or components (e.g., summaries, plans, tickets). For safety-critical or action-constraining information, it is not enough that the content is mentioned; it must remain binding on downstream actions. However, typical transformations like summarization or plan integration may subtly change a requirement that blocks action into a non-binding suggestion, creating safety risks. The paper aims to understand and characterize this failure mode of operational state preservation, with a focus on safety blockers as a clear testbed.

Method: The authors construct 1,296 controlled synthetic episodes where an initial state includes explicit safety blockers, each with four structured fields: prerequisite, authority, fallback, and execution consequence. Assuming that upstream identification of blockers is already correct, they systematically vary how this state is transformed into handoff artifacts (e.g., direct handoff, compressed summary, integration into plans, ownership deferral, precedent substitution). They then evaluate an executor that must act solely based on the transformed artifact, measuring whether blockers are preserved as binding constraints and whether forbidden actions occur.

Result: Direct, minimally modified handoffs perfectly preserve all blockers. In contrast, more realistic transformations such as compression, plan assimilation, convergence of multiple items, ownership deferral, and precedent-based substitution often convert binding blockers into optional caveats. Normal handoff compression leads to 100.0% deactivation of blockers and 54.2% rate of forbidden actions. When artifacts are forced to explicitly retain all four structured fields (prerequisite, authority, fallback, consequence), blocker preservation rises to 100.0% and forbidden actions drop to 0.0%. Additional interventions using fixed artifact formats show that verification at the downstream executor can prevent forbidden actions even when the artifact itself remains largely deactivating (95.3% deactivation), revealing a gap between semantic presence and operational enforcement.

Conclusion: There is a systematic state-transmission failure in LLM agent workflows between information extraction and downstream action: handoff transformations can preserve the semantic content of safety-relevant state while weakening or removing its binding force on actions. Therefore, semantic availability of safety constraints in an artifact does not ensure that they function as operational blockers. Designing safe LLM agents requires explicit mechanisms and representations that preserve not just what the state is about, but also how it constrains future actions across handoffs.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents coordinate complex tasks through multi-role and multi-stage workflows. Upstream state is repeatedly transformed into intermediate language artifacts, such as summaries, plans, tickets, memories, and handoff notes, from which downstream components act. For action-constraining state, topical retention is insufficient: an artifact may mention an unresolved condition while changing it from a requirement that must be resolved before execution into information that may merely inform the next action. We study this action-binding role as operational state preservation. Safety blockers provide a controlled instance because each source state has an explicit prerequisite, authority, fallback, and execution consequence. We condition on correct upstream identification, vary the handoff transformation, and evaluate an executor restricted to the resulting artifact. Across 1,296 controlled synthetic episodes, direct-handoff controls preserve every blocker, whereas compression, plan assimilation, convergence, ownership deferral, and precedent substitution repeatedly turn binding state into caveats or non-binding considerations. Normal handoff compression produces 100.0% deactivation and 54.2% forbidden action. Restoring all four state fields raises preservation to 100.0% and reduces forbidden action to 0.0%. Fixed-artifact interventions further separate preservation from containment: downstream verification eliminates forbidden action while artifact deactivation remains 95.3%. These results identify a state-transmission failure between information extraction and action. Handoff transformations can retain state content while weakening its constraints on downstream action. Semantic availability does not guarantee operational preservation.

</details>


### [153] [EviDx: Evidence-Aware Active Diagnosis with Scaffolded LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24570)
*Lihang Zeng,Shaoting Zhang,Xiaofan Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EviDx is a framework that turns static LLM-based medical diagnosis into an interactive, evidence-seeking process using structured environments, agent scaffolding, and a runtime controller to improve diagnostic accuracy and stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most current LLM medical diagnosis systems treat diagnosis as a one-shot prediction from a fixed case description, which does not match how clinicians actually work: they iteratively seek evidence, update hypotheses, and decide when to stop. Existing agentic LLM systems with tools are promising but vague about how to expose and control patient information during the interaction. There is a need for a principled framework that operationalizes active, evidence-aware diagnosis and governs how evidence is accessed, organized, and used in real time.

Method: The authors propose EviDx, an evidence-aware active diagnosis framework with three main components. (1) E-Synthesis: automatically converts raw clinical cases into interactive diagnostic environments where evidence can be queried and revealed over time. (2) A clinical diagnostic scaffold: defines role-specialized LLM agents, diagnostic tools for evidence retrieval, and an evolving shared evidence state that is updated as agents interact. (3) An observer-guided runtime harness: monitors diagnostic uncertainty and evidence coverage to regulate when the diagnostic process should terminate. They also design a three-level evaluation pyramid that measures execution robustness, reasoning dynamics, and final diagnostic outcomes.

Result: In experiments on clinical diagnosis tasks, EviDx leads to better diagnostic performance (e.g., higher accuracy or ranking of correct diagnoses) and more stable diagnostic processes compared with baseline LLM-based or agentic systems that lack structured evidence-aware control. The framework also exposes where different LLMs succeed or fail under active diagnosis, highlighting model-specific capability limits.

Conclusion: EviDx turns LLM-based medical diagnosis from a static prediction task into a structured active evidence-seeking process. By synthesizing interactive environments, scaffolding multi-agent reasoning, and controlling runtime via an observer, it improves both diagnostic quality and process robustness while providing insight into the strengths and weaknesses of different LLMs in clinical decision-making.

Abstract: Clinical diagnosis is an active evidence-seeking process in which clinicians acquire evidence, update competing hypotheses, and decide when the available evidence is sufficient for diagnosis. Yet many medical diagnosis systems built around large language models (LLMs) still formulate diagnosis as static case-to-answer prediction, with limited support for evidence acquisition. Agentic LLMs offer a dynamic alternative through tool use and intermediate diagnostic trajectories, but existing systems often under-specify how patient evidence should be exposed, scaffolded, and controlled at runtime. We introduce EviDx, an evidence-aware active diagnosis framework that pairs patient-specific diagnostic environments with a clinical diagnostic scaffold and an observer-guided runtime harness. In EviDx, $\mathcal{E}$-Synthesis constructs interactive environments from raw clinical cases; the scaffold organizes role-specialized agents, evidence tools, and evolving evidence states; and the harness regulates diagnostic termination by tracking uncertainty and evidence coverage. A 3-level evaluation pyramid assesses execution robustness, reasoning dynamics, and diagnostic outcomes. Experiments show that EviDx improves diagnostic performance and process stability while revealing model-dependent capability boundaries.

</details>


### [154] [Joint Optimization of Tool Creation and Use for Large Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24571)
*Zhi Rui Tam,Chieh-Yen Lin,Yun-Nung Chen,Shao-Hua Sun,Hung-yi Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes SMITH, a reinforcement learning framework that jointly trains a language model to both create and use tools, achieving strong performance on procedural reasoning benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing tool-augmented LLMs are limited by the fixed set of human-written APIs, and current automatic tool-creation methods rely on a separate, frozen LLM at inference time. This decouples the model that designs tools from the one that uses them, giving no learning signal that the generated schemas are actually usable by the inference-time model.

Method: Introduce SMITH (Schema-grounded Multi-task Iterative Tool Honing), a unified RL framework where a single policy alternates between (1) build tasks, in which it writes tools (schemas and code) from a few examples, and (2) use tasks, in which it invokes previously created tools on new questions. The training uses three distinct reward components to separately penalize schema errors, code failures, and task outcome failures so that each failure type produces its own gradient signal. The system is instantiated with a 4B-parameter Qwen3 model and trained on 13 procedural reasoning tasks that have exact verifiers.

Result: A 4B Qwen3 model trained with SMITH reaches 79.8 macro-average accuracy on held-out procedural reasoning tasks, outperforming all evaluated baselines, including an untrained 30B-A3B tool-writing model. Without any explicit visual or tabular training data, the same 4B model achieves 40.4 accuracy on TabMWP-Hard and 42.6 on out-of-domain GQA, which is +7.6 over the best inference-time tool-creation baseline using the same backbone. Additionally, tools produced by these 4B models improve the performance of smaller (LFM-2.5-350M) and larger (Qwen3-30B-A3B) models on the same reasoning tasks.

Conclusion: Jointly training a single policy to both create and use tools via schema-grounded RL leads to more effective and usable tools than inference-time tool-creation with a frozen model. SMITH’s multi-axis rewards enable targeted learning around schema design, code reliability, and task success, yielding strong gains in procedural, tabular, and visual reasoning benchmarks and producing tools that transfer beneficially to other model sizes.

Abstract: Tool-augmented language models are bounded by the APIs humans bothered to write; existing tool-creation systems patch this by prompting a frozen LLM at inference time, leaving the model that writes a tool decoupled from the one that uses it, with no signal that the schemas it produces are schemas it can invoke. We propose SMITH (Schema-grounded Multi-task Iterative Tool Honing), a reinforcement learning framework that jointly trains tool creation and tool use inside a single policy. Each rollout is either a build task (write a tool from a few examples) or a use task (invoke a pooled tool on a held-out question). Three separate reward axes catch schema, code, and outcome failures independently, so each failure mode contributes its own gradient. A 4B Qwen3 trained with SMITH on 13 procedural reasoning tasks with exact verifiers reaches 79.8 macro-average accuracy on held-out tasks, the best across all evaluated methods and ahead of an untrained 30B-A3B tool-writer. It also reaches 40.4 on TabMWP-Hard and 42.6 on out-of-domain GQA (+7.6 over the best same-backbone inference-time baseline), without any visual or tabular training data. Tools written by our 4B models also lifted the performance of LFM-2.5-350M and Qwen3-30B-A3B under same reasoning tasks.

</details>


### [155] [PhysMLLMs: Spatial Priors for Unified Referring Segmentation and Grounded Reasoning of Images and Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24574)
*Siyao Yan,Bo Han,Jisheng Dang,Bimei Wang,Shude Wang,Hong Peng,Yulan Guo,Jianhuang Lai,Bin Hu,Tat-SengChua*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PhysMLLMs introduces physics-inspired spatial continuity priors into video multimodal LLMs, significantly improving temporal consistency and segmentation quality without hurting general vision-language performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing video multimodal large language models used for language-guided video segmentation suffer from spatio-temporal inconsistencies such as jitter, drift, and identity switches, especially in challenging scenarios like occlusions, small objects, and similar distractors. A key suspected cause is the lack of explicit spatial priors during training, making it hard to maintain stable object identity, shape, and continuity across frames. The paper aims to address this core limitation while keeping inference-time cost unchanged.

Method: The authors propose PhysMLLMs, a training-stage prior injection framework that introduces physics-inspired spatial continuity priors into video MLLMs. The central mechanism, Global Representation Prior Alignment (REPA-Global), aligns the student model’s global visual representations with those of a frozen DINOv2 teacher. This is implemented via distillation from an offline embedding cache following a scheduled distillation plan. The teacher model is only used during training, so inference architecture and latency remain unchanged, while encouraging the student to form more stable, object-centered representations over time.

Result: On multiple video benchmarks for language-guided video segmentation, PhysMLLMs yields better segmentation masks and improved cross-frame temporal consistency, with particularly large gains on difficult cases such as small or fast-moving targets, occlusions, distractors, and questions requiring reasoning. On single-frame referring image segmentation and standard general vision-language model benchmarks, performance remains comparable to baselines, indicating that the added spatial prior does not degrade image-level localization or overall multimodal capabilities.

Conclusion: Injecting physics-inspired spatial continuity priors into video MLLMs at training time via global representation alignment with a strong frozen vision teacher improves temporal stability and mask consistency for video segmentation, especially in challenging scenarios, while preserving image-level grounding and general VLM performance. This suggests that carefully designed spatial prior injection is a promising direction for making video MLLMs more robust and temporally coherent without adding inference-time overhead.

Abstract: Video multimodal large language models support language guided video segmentation, but they often show spatio temporal inconsistencies, e.g., jitter, drift, and identity switches. These failures are more common when targets are partly hidden or when similar objects appear nearby.One likely reason is that current training lacks explicit spatial priors, which makes it difficult to maintain stable spatial identity and shape over time. We present PhysMLLMs, a training-stage prior injection architecture that injects physics-inspired spatial continuity priors into Video MLLMs. PhysMLLMs is designed to encourage more stable object-centered representations by aligning the student global visual representation with a frozen teacher model during training. Our core mechanism, Global Representation Prior Alignment (REPA-Global), distills global visual representations from a frozen DINOv2 teacher using an offline embedding cache and a scheduled distillation plan. This design keeps inference unchanged and does not add inference time cost. Across multiple video benchmarks, PhysMLLMs improves video segmentation mask quality and cross-frame consistency, with larger gains on challenging cases involving small targets, fast motion, occlusion, distractors, and reasoning queries. On single-frame referring image segmentation and representative general VLM benchmarks, PhysMLLMs maintains comparable performance, demonstrating that the injected spatial prior improves video consistency without compromising image-level grounding or general multimodal capability. These results suggest that physics-inspired spatial prior injection can improve temporal stability while preserving general capability. The code is available at https://github.com/tusu-code/20260121-icml2026-2.git.

</details>


### [156] [Pivot-and-Station Multi-Agent Path Finding: Solvability, Complexity, and Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24585)
*Andrea Di Nezza,Mihir Patel,Fabio Fagnani,Sara Bernardini*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Defines and studies a new multi-agent pathfinding problem (PS-MAPF) for automated storage/logistics systems, fully characterizes when instances are solvable, proves related optimization problems are NP-hard, and proposes algorithms including a practical heuristic that performs very well empirically.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In automated high-density storage and logistics environments, many robots must share scarce, task-critical locations (like workstations or transfer points) and then park without blocking future operations. Classical MAPF does not directly capture the need for agents to first visit any of several interchangeable service points (pivots) and then end at flexible parking locations (stations). The authors want a theoretical and algorithmic framework that matches this practical setting and can both guarantee solvability and optimize performance measures like makespan and flowtime.

Method: 1) Formally define the Pivot-and-Station MAPF (PS-MAPF) problem, where selected agents must visit one pivot each from a set, and all agents must end at anonymous parking stations. 2) Prove structural graph-theoretic conditions for solvability: show that any instance on a 2-edge-connected graph is solvable and derive a necessary and sufficient condition on general graphs using an effective-distance measure tied to the number of free vertices. 3) Show that optimizing key performance criteria (station-makespan and station-flowtime) is NP-hard even with only one pivot. 4) Develop three algorithms: (a) a complete but likely slow baseline, (b) a SAT-based optimal solver encoding PS-MAPF as satisfiability, and (c) Pivot-Prioritized Planning (PPP), a heuristic strategy that prioritizes planning around pivots. 5) Experimentally evaluate the algorithms on benchmarks, comparing success rate and solution quality.

Result: 1) Theoretical: full characterization of when PS-MAPF instances are solvable—always solvable on 2-edge-connected graphs, and on general connected graphs solvability is exactly captured by an effective-distance condition related to spare vertices. 2) Complexity: minimizing either station-makespan or station-flowtime is NP-hard, even with just one pivot, indicating that optimal planning is computationally difficult. 3) Empirical: PPP solves 74–89% of tested instances and produces solutions whose makespan and flowtime are orders of magnitude better than those from the complete baseline, while the SAT-based solver can find optimal solutions but is presumably more expensive.

Conclusion: PS-MAPF is a realistic and theoretically rich extension of standard MAPF for high-density automated systems with shared workstations and flexible parking. The paper provides a complete solvability theory, establishes the hardness of key optimization objectives, and proposes practical algorithms. Among them, Pivot-Prioritized Planning emerges as an effective heuristic that scales well and yields high-quality solutions, making PS-MAPF a promising model and toolkit for real-world warehouse, parking, and logistics applications.

Abstract: Automated high-density storage systems (warehouses, robotic parking, plant logistics, etc.) require fleets of agents to move through scarce task-critical resources and then park without obstructing future operations. We introduce Pivot-and-Station Multi-Agent Path Finding (PS-MAPF), a MAPF variant in which a subset of tasked agents must each visit one of a set of interchangeable pivots (e.g., workstations) before the entire fleet terminates at anonymous stations, one agent per station. We characterize solvability completely: every instance on a 2-edge-connected graph is solvable, and, on arbitrary connected graphs, a structural effective-distance measure relative to the number of unoccupied vertices gives a necessary and sufficient condition. We prove that minimizing station-makespan or station-flowtime is NP-hard already with a single pivot. We present three algorithms, a complete baseline, a SAT-based optimal solver, and Pivot-Prioritized Planning (PPP), the last solving 74-89% of benchmark instances with makespan and flowtime orders of magnitude below the baseline.

</details>


### [157] [Causal Modelling of Support Interventions for Student Competency Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24632)
*Francesca Mangili,Alessandro Antonucci,Rafael Cabañas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose using structural causal models instead of standard psychometric models for educational assessment, to support explicit reasoning about interventions (like hints) and counterfactuals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current educational assessments largely rely on probabilistic psychometric models (e.g., item response theory) that connect latent competencies to task performance. These models are mainly associative and do not natively support causal, interventional, or counterfactual reasoning that educators need for designing and evaluating targeted interventions. There is a need for a framework that can handle these richer forms of reasoning.

Method: They advocate a structural causal modelling (SCM) framework for educational assessment and propose a protocol for constructing such models. The protocol involves eliciting structural equations from domain experts, using logical (causal-structural) information rather than probabilistic assumptions. They then apply this framework to assessment data involving complex tasks aimed at measuring algorithmic skills of compulsory school students, modelling interventions such as hints and analysing related counterfactual scenarios.

Result: They show that SCM-based assessment enables forms of reasoning that are not available in standard associative psychometric models, including explicit modelling of interventions (e.g., hints) and counterfactual analysis of student performance under alternative conditions. The case study with algorithmic skills tasks demonstrates the practical relevance and feasibility of the approach.

Conclusion: Structural causal models provide a richer assessment framework than traditional psychometric models by supporting interventional and counterfactual reasoning, which are crucial for designing and evaluating educational interventions. While the approach requires expert elicitation of structural equations, this information is logical rather than probabilistic and avoids some of the stronger assumptions of standard models. The illustrative application to algorithmic skills assessment suggests that SCMs can meaningfully enhance educational assessment practice.

Abstract: Accurate assessment of student competencies is essential for enabling educators to identify individual needs, design targeted interventions, and evaluate the effectiveness of educational strategies. Empirical assessment procedures are typically grounded in psychometric models, such as item response theory, which relate student competence levels to performance on assessment tasks. In this paper, we advocate adopting a structural causal modelling approach to educational assessment, moving beyond probabilistic belief updating toward a framework that explicitly supports interventional and counterfactual reasoning. We propose a corresponding protocol for its construction and analyse the practical relevance of forms of reasoning that remain inaccessible to standard associative models, including the explicit modelling of interventions such as hints and the related counterfactual scenario analysis. Although our protocol requires the structural equations to be elicited from experts, the necessary information is purely logical and does not rely on probabilistic, less tenable assumptions. We illustrate the approach using data from an assessment that employs complex tasks designed to measure compulsory school student algorithmic skills.

</details>


### [158] [Parason: Revealing Subtask and Trial Parallelism in LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24658)
*Zhengyang Zhang,Zijian Zhang,Jiaxuan Gao,Shusheng Xu,Yi Wu,Song Han,Ligeng Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Parason, a framework that exposes and trains large language models to exploit two kinds of parallelism in their reasoning—subtask and trial parallelism—to significantly reduce wall‑clock latency while preserving accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scaling test-time (chain-of-thought) reasoning in LLMs improves problem-solving but leads to extremely long sequential reasoning traces, causing high latency (up to days or weeks) for hard tasks. Existing parallel reasoning focuses mostly on decomposing tasks into independent subtasks (subtask parallelism), ignoring the large portion of computation where models try multiple speculative solution paths or hypotheses (trial parallelism). The authors aim to systematically characterize both forms of parallelism, measure their relative importance, and design a training and inference framework that can turn theoretically parallelizable reasoning into real wall-clock speedups without losing accuracy.

Method: 1) Conceptual taxonomy: Define and analyze two distinct forms of parallelism in LLM reasoning—Subtask Parallelism (independent subtasks) and Trial Parallelism (multiple speculative attempts, verifications, and hypothesis aggregation).
2) Empirical analysis: Study DeepSeek-V4 reasoning traces in the HLE setting to measure how much of the reasoning is parallelizable, finding that trial parallelism accounts for 65.5% and grows on harder problems.
3) Parason framework: 
   - Convert sequential reasoning traces into structured parallel trajectories using a context-free grammar that explicitly encodes where subtasks and trials can be run in parallel.
   - Train LLMs with Parallelism-Aware Group Relative Policy Optimization (PA-GRPO), a reinforcement-learning style objective whose reward balances three factors: solution accuracy, latency, and the two parallelism ratios (subtask and trial).
4) Inference-time execution: Implement the learned parallel structure via tool calls that manage the actual parallel execution of reasoning branches, so that logical parallelism translates to real wall-clock speedups in practice.

Result: On mathematical reasoning benchmarks such as AIME24 and AIME25, Parason delivers an average 1.7× reduction in wall-clock time while keeping accuracy competitive with strong baselines. The analysis also shows that trial parallelism constitutes the majority (65.5%) of parallelizable reasoning steps and becomes even more dominant on harder tasks, validating the design focus on learning and exploiting trial parallelism, not just subtask decomposition.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that most parallelizable computation in LLM reasoning comes from trial parallelism rather than subtask parallelism. By explicitly modeling both, converting traces into parallel grammars, and training with a parallelism-aware RL objective, Parason can achieve substantial speedups (about 1.7×) with minimal accuracy loss. This demonstrates that making LLMs aware of and able to execute structured parallel reasoning is a practical path to scaling test-time reasoning without prohibitive latency, especially for challenging mathematical problems.

Abstract: Scaling test-time reasoning has substantially improved the problem-solving ability of large language models (LLMs), but standard autoregressive decoding still executes long reasoning traces sequentially, creating severe latency for difficult tasks (up to days and weeks). Parallel reasoning offers a natural remedy. However, prior systems primarily focus on Subtask Parallelism, where the model learns to decompose a high-level task into smaller chunks that can be solved independently. This approach overlooks another pervasive form of parallelism: Trial Parallelism, where multiple speculative attempts explore, verify, and aggregate competing hypotheses in parallel. In this paper, we introduce Parason, which reveals and learns both forms of parallelism in LLM reasoning. Our analysis identifies Trial Parallelism as the majority of parallelizable reasoning computation (65.5% in DeepSeek-V4's reasoning steps in HLE), and it becomes increasingly dominant on hard problems. Guided by this taxonomy, Parason converts sequential reasoning traces into structured parallel trajectories with a context-free grammar, then trains models with Parallelism-Aware Group Relative Policy Optimization (PA-GRPO), whose reward jointly balances accuracy, latency, and the two parallelism ratios. At inference time, Parason executes the learned parallel structure through tool calls, translating theoretical savings to real-world wall-clock acceleration. Experiments on mathematical reasoning benchmarks including AIME24 and AIME25 show that Parason achieves an average acceleration about 1.7$\times$ while maintaining competitive accuracy.

</details>


### [159] [Lifted Model Construction under Approximate Commutativity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24713)
*Malte Luttermann,Jan Speller,Tanya Braun,Marcel Gehrke,Ralf Möller*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces ε-commutativity, allowing approximate commutativity in factors to support lifted probabilistic inference when learned parameters slightly differ.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lifted inference relies on exactly commutative factors to exploit object indistinguishability, but in realistic, learned models, parameter noise breaks exact commutativity and undermines lifted representations.

Method: Define ε-commutativity as approximate invariance of factor outputs under permutations of some inputs; develop lifted model construction algorithms and probabilistic inference procedures that use ε-commutativity, along with theoretical error bounds for approximation.

Result: Show theoretically that using ε-commutative factors induces bounded approximation error in inference; empirically demonstrate that ε-lifted models keep query accuracy comparable to exact models while reducing runtime.

Conclusion: ε-commutativity enables robust, scalable lifted inference in realistic learned probabilistic models, preserving most of the efficiency benefits while guaranteeing small, controlled approximation error.

Abstract: Lifted inference algorithms enable scalable probabilistic inference even for large object domains by leveraging the indistinguishability of objects in a probability distribution. An essential prerequisite for constructing a lifted representation is to identify commutative factors, i.e., functions whose output values are invariant under permutations of a subset of their input values, in a potential-based factorisation. In practice, however, parameters learned from data inevitably deviate even if associated objects are indistinguishable, causing their corresponding factors to be only approximately commutative instead of being exactly commutative. We address this problem by introducing the concept of ε-commutativity, a relaxation of commutativity where output values are only approximately invariant under permutations of input values. Specifically, we show how ε-commutativity can be exploited for lifted model construction, downstream probabilistic inference, and prove strict bounds on the induced approximation error, thereby ensuring the practical applicability of lifted model construction while maintaining highly accurate query results. These theoretical guarantees are confirmed empirically, demonstrating comparable query accuracy at lower runtime.

</details>


### [160] [RACE: Scalable Statistical Estimation of Functional Consistency in LLM Neurons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24758)
*Runyu Wang,Bo Liu,Xiaxin Zhang,Yu Han,Jiawei Cao,Xiaoye Zhang,Zhe Zhang,Yifan Yang,Peng Ping*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces RACE, a fast forward-pass framework to measure how consistently Transformer neurons behave across an entire domain, improving domain specificity and scalability over gradient-based methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding stable, domain-specific neuron behavior across whole input domains is hard: existing work mostly uses instance-level, gradient-based point estimates that (1) miss population-level variability and (2) are computationally expensive, limiting large-scale analysis.

Method: They propose RACE (Residual Alignment for Consistency Estimation), a purely forward-pass, statistical method that uses residual alignment metrics to assess how functionally consistent each Transformer neuron is over a full domain. They compare it against gradient-based point-estimate methods and perform perturbation and token-distribution-level analyses to validate it.

Result: Perturbation experiments show RACE finds neurons that are more domain-specific than those identified by gradient-based estimates. Token distribution analyses confirm that neurons chosen by RACE are strongly associated with the target domain. RACE also reduces computation by about two orders of magnitude versus gradient-based approaches.

Conclusion: RACE offers a scalable, statistically grounded and computationally efficient way to measure domain-wide functional consistency of Transformer neurons, yielding more domain-specific neurons than prior gradient-based techniques and enabling broader mechanistic interpretability studies.

Abstract: Discovering stable neuron behavior across entire domains remains a challenge in mechanistic interpretability. Existing methods often rely on instance-level point estimates or computationally expensive procedures, which either obscure population-level variability or limit scalable domain-wide analysis. We present RACE (Residual Alignment for Consistency Estimation), a forward-pass statistical framework that evaluates the domain-wide functional consistency of Transformer neurons. Perturbation experiments demonstrate that RACE achieves superior domain specificity compared to gradient-based point estimates. Meanwhile, token-distribution-level results verify the association between the selected neurons and the target domain. Furthermore, its computational overhead is two orders of magnitude lower than that of gradient-based methods.

</details>


### [161] [Evidence Blindness in Direct Corpus Interaction: Persistent Navigation with AtlasNav](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24764)
*Hongyu Guo,Zhiyu Zheng,Zhao Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces AtlasNav, a framework that organizes a corpus once into a reusable “Corpus Atlas” to support efficient, finite-budget navigation for large language model agents, reducing evidence blindness and improving accuracy and cost over prior dynamic-workspace methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Direct Corpus Interaction (DCI) approaches let LLM agents interact directly with full corpora, but under finite interaction budgets they suffer from Evidence Blindness: key evidence may never be retrieved, retrieved documents may not be opened, or crucial fragments in opened documents may not be inspected. Moreover, both raw DCI and dynamic-workspace methods repeatedly reconstruct query-dependent structure online, which is inefficient and fragile. The authors aim to understand and mitigate this progressive loss of usable evidence and to design a reusable corpus representation that enables more reliable and efficient agentic search.

Method: The authors formalize Evidence Blindness as stage-wise evidence realization across the interaction pipeline (surface document, open document, inspect fragment). They reconceptualize large-scale agentic search as finite-budget navigation over a persistent corpus structure. They propose AtlasNav, which builds a reusable multi-view Corpus Atlas once, then allows each new query to navigate this atlas adaptively instead of reconstructing structure from scratch. AtlasNav is evaluated against prior dynamic-workspace state-of-the-art methods on benchmarks including BrowseComp-Plus, PhantomWiki (with controlled scaling from 10K to 1M documents and different corpus organizations), and heterogeneous enterprise knowledge settings.

Result: On the BrowseComp-Plus benchmark, AtlasNav achieves 92.05% strict accuracy while reducing recorded online inference cost by 30.21% relative to the previous dynamic-workspace state of the art. Under equal budget constraints, AtlasNav realizes all required evidence earlier and approaches the same LLM’s empirical performance when provided with full evidence more rapidly. Similar gains hold on PhantomWiki despite its different corpus organization and large scaling range, and AtlasNav transfers competitively to enterprise knowledge corpora, indicating robustness of the representation principle.

Conclusion: The study concludes that the effectiveness of agentic search with LLMs is governed not only by whether evidence is technically accessible but also by how the corpus is organized for navigation under limited interaction budgets. A persistent, multi-view Corpus Atlas enables agents to mitigate Evidence Blindness by turning limited interactions into targeted navigation, outperforming dynamic, query-specific workspace approaches in both accuracy and efficiency across diverse corpora and scales.

Abstract: Large language model agents are moving beyond conventional retrieval-augmented generation toward direct interaction with external corpora. Direct Corpus Interaction (DCI) keeps the full corpus accessible, yet reachable evidence can remain unusable under finite interaction budgets. Required evidence may fail to surface, a surfaced supporting document may remain unopened, or an opened document may fail to expose its decisive fragment. We call this progressive silent loss Evidence Blindness and quantify it through stage-wise evidence realization. Within the DCI paradigm, raw interaction adds little reusable corpus organization, while dynamic-workspace methods reconstruct a query-conditioned interaction space from each query and trajectory. In both cases, useful structure is recovered largely online. We instead formulate large-scale agentic search as finite-budget navigation over reusable corpus structure. We introduce AtlasNav, a persistent multi-view corpus-navigation framework that retains direct corpus interaction but organizes the corpus once into a Corpus Atlas, allowing each query to navigate adaptively rather than reconstruct shared structure. On BrowseComp-Plus, AtlasNav achieves 92.05% strict accuracy while reducing recorded online inference cost by 30.21% relative to the prior dynamic-workspace state of the art. Under matched budgets, it realizes the complete required evidence earlier and approaches the same model's evidence-supplied empirical reference more rapidly. The same representation principle remains effective under PhantomWiki's distinct corpus organization and controlled 10K-1M scaling, and transfers competitively to heterogeneous enterprise knowledge. These results show that agentic search depends not only on accessible evidence, but also on how the corpus is represented so that limited interaction becomes effective navigation.

</details>


### [162] [StepGuard: Learning Step-Level Guardrails with Scalable Supervision and Safety-Utility Balancing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24777)
*Zhijie Zheng,Yu Li,Chen Qian,Yuqian Fu,Yanwei Fu,Lu Sheng,Jing Shao,Dongrui Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces StepGuard, a step-level guard model that monitors and audits tool-using LLM agents before each action, significantly improving security with minimal utility loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents that can invoke tools and act in external environments pose security risks such as file tampering, data leakage, and unauthorized operations. Existing guardrails usually assess only completed trajectories after the fact, which misses the opportunity to block dangerous actions before execution and may fail against fine-grained attacks at specific steps. There is a need for proactive, step-level defenses that distinguish between safe and unsafe actions under the same context.

Method: The authors propose StepGuard, a guard model operating at the step level. It can both audit completed trajectories and pre-screen tool calls before execution. To train it, they design StepGen, an automatic data engine that creates pairs of safe and unsafe trajectories sharing identical context but diverging at a single risky step, giving strong supervision on what constitutes a harmful versus benign action. To prevent the model from becoming either too strict (over-defense) or too permissive (under-defense), they introduce Balance-GRPO, a training scheme that adjusts learning emphasis between safe and unsafe actions according to their empirical accuracies, encouraging a calibrated decision boundary.

Result: StepGuard attains the highest average accuracy among open-weight guard models and performs on par with a strong proprietary baseline, GPT-5.4, on relevant evaluation benchmarks. When deployed to protect agents evaluated on AgentDojo and AgentDyn security/utility benchmarks, StepGuard lowers the mean attack success rate by 77.3% compared with having no guard, while only reducing task utility by about 2.8 percentage points, indicating effective protection with limited impact on normal behavior.

Conclusion: Step-level guarding is an effective approach for securing tool-using LLM agents. By generating paired safe/unsafe examples at the step where risk occurs and using Balanced-GRPO to avoid over- or under-defensive behavior, StepGuard delivers strong security gains while preserving most of the agent’s utility. The work suggests that proactive, fine-grained action checking can be a practical and high-performing defense paradigm for real-world LLM agent systems.

Abstract: LLM-based agents can interact with external environments through tool invocation, but this capability also introduces security risks such as file modification, information leakage, and unauthorized actions. Existing guardrails often evaluate completed trajectories, leaving pre-execution monitoring of step-level actions underexplored. We propose StepGuard, a step-level guard model that can audit completed agent trajectories and check tool actions before they are executed. To train StepGuard, we introduce StepGen, an automatic data engine that generates safe and unsafe trajectories with the same context but different actions at the risky step. To further reduce over-defense and under-defense, we propose Balance-GRPO, which dynamically balances learning between safe and unsafe actions based on their observed accuracy. Experiments show that StepGuard achieves the highest average accuracy among open-weight guard models, with performance comparable to GPT-5.4. When used to guard agents on AgentDojo and AgentDyn, StepGuard reduces mean attack success rate by 77.3% relative to the no-guard setting, while mean utility drops by only 2.8 percentage points.

</details>


### [163] [Right Diagnoses, Decorative Reasoning:A Perturbation Audit of Medical Chain-of-Thought](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24790)
*Mengzhu Xu,Jifan Gao,Xia Jiang,Yaoxin Wu,Xi Long*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper audits whether large language models’ medical chain-of-thought (CoT) explanations actually guide their answers or just document them. Using a suite of clinically grounded perturbations, it finds that models’ visible reasoning is largely decoupled from their final medical decisions, providing a metric (CDR) and framework to test CoT faithfulness in healthcare settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinicians often interpret chain-of-thought outputs from LLMs as genuine evidence of underlying medical reasoning. However, most prior CoT-faithfulness work is in general domains and doesn’t account for clinical risk, while medical LLM evaluations typically take CoT at face value without probing whether it truly affects answers. This creates a safety and trust gap: if CoT is unfaithful, clinicians may be misled into overtrusting systems based on plausible but irrelevant reasoning. The paper aims to systematically and clinically meaningfully test whether medical CoT is causally tied to model predictions.

Method: The authors design a “medical perturbation audit” comprising 30 clinically motivated operators that perturb either the question, the chain-of-thought, or both. Examples include flipping disease severity, reversing negation, swapping patient demographics, and ablating key evidence. For each perturbation, they jointly track whether (1) the chain-of-thought visibly updates to reflect the edit and (2) the final answer flips when it should. This yields a taxonomy of model failure modes and a central metric, the Chain-Decoupling Rate (CDR), defined as cases where the chain does not register the edit and the answer also does not flip, despite the edit being clinically significant. They run this audit across 14 LLMs on four medical QA benchmarks, and have two board-certified clinicians re-annotate a subset of perturbed questions to verify that perturbed items still admit a defensible gold label.

Result: Across 14 models and four benchmarks, the Chain-Decoupling Rate is high: on clinically meaningful destructive edits, CDR averages 72.9%, indicating that in most such cases neither the chain-of-thought nor the answer responds appropriately to important clinical changes. They find that corrupting the chain-of-thought often leaves answer accuracy unchanged, and that disabling CoT prompting does not reduce accuracy, implying that CoT text is not functionally central to the model’s decision. In a clinician-reviewed sample of 197 perturbed questions, 98.5% of perturbations still preserved a defensible gold label, supporting that the perturbations themselves are valid tests rather than label noise. Similar decoupling patterns appear regardless of medical vs. reasoning fine-tuning and model scale. For closed-source models where CoT is unavailable, answer-side behavioral signals likewise suggest similar decoupling between any internal reasoning and outputs.

Conclusion: Medical LLMs’ chain-of-thought explanations are largely unfaithful to the underlying decision process: they often fail to reflect clinically important input changes, and model accuracy is largely unaffected by altering or removing CoT. This challenges the assumption that visible CoT can be trusted as evidence of genuine medical reasoning and highlights a risk of overtrust by clinicians. The paper contributes a reusable audit framework and the Chain-Decoupling Rate metric as practical tools to evaluate whether medical CoT is truly reasoning-supportive or merely post-hoc documentation, informing safer deployment and evaluation of LLMs in healthcare.

Abstract: Clinicians read chain-of-thought (CoT) rationales as evidence of medical reasoning, but whether the visible chain plays that role is rarely tested. General-domain CoT-faithfulness probes ignore clinical cost, and medical LLM evaluations treat the chain as a black box. We close this gap with a medical perturbation audit: a 30-operator battery edits both the chain and the question with clinically motivated operators (severity reversal, negation flip, demographic swap, evidence ablation), paired with a chain-update times answer-flip joint analysis that classifies each model by its failure mode. Applied to 14 LLMs on four medical QA benchmarks, three independent tests converge: the Chain-Decoupling Rate (CDR; chain does not register the edit and the answer does not flip) is 72.9% panel-wide on clinically meaningful destructive edits, chain corruption leaves accuracy unchanged, and removing CoT prompting does not reduce accuracy. Two board-certified clinicians re-annotate N=197 perturbed questions; 98.5% leave the gold defensible. The pattern holds across medical and reasoning fine-tuning and scale; on the closed-source tier, where the chain text is unavailable, the answer-side signals are consistent with the same decoupling. Our framework and CDR provide a reusable yardstick for auditing whether medical CoT is faithful or merely documentation.

</details>


### [164] [CAFE: Self-Improving Search Agents Need Co-Evolving Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24794)
*Boyang Liu,Senjie Jin,Peixin Wang,Zhangyue Yin,Yibo Wang,Yuhao Zhou,Xinbing Liang,Shizheng Zhu,Yuhui Wang,Jingqi Tong,Zhiheng Xi,Jiazheng Zhang,Clive Bai,Clarenceai,Blaze Chen,Tao Gui,Qi Zhang,Xuanjing Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces CAFE, a framework where an LLM-based search agent and a feedback-giving critic share parameters and co-evolve, enabling the agent to learn when to request and how to use corrective feedback during search. This improves performance, robustness, and reduces hallucinations across multiple benchmarks compared to other RL-based search agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing outcome-supervised search agents only use terminal rewards, which means they cannot localize where in the reasoning trajectory errors occurred or correct an ongoing trajectory before errors compound. Furthermore, corrective feedback is usually treated as external and fixed, rather than something that must adapt as the agent improves. The paper is motivated by the need for a framework where the agent can strategically request and exploit feedback mid-trajectory, and where the feedback mechanism itself learns from and adapts to the evolving behavior of the agent.

Method: The authors propose CAFE (Coupled Agent--Feedback Evolution), where a single shared-parameter model alternates between acting as a search agent and as a critic that provides feedback. Training proceeds in two coupled phases: (1) online RL, where the agent interacts with tasks and learns when to request feedback and how to use it, guided by a comparative feedback estimate (using success gaps between feedback and no-feedback prompts) and feedback-aware advantage shaping (reweighting token-level advantages pre- and post-feedback); and (2) offline preference-style optimization, where feedback behaviors are trained using matched successful and unsuccessful rollouts, learning which interventions would have led to better outcomes. CAFE is initialized by constructing trajectories around the base agent’s own failures, teaching the model how to recover once feedback is given, and then iteratively improves both the policy and the feedback generator through alternating updates.

Result: On seven agentic search benchmarks, CAFE achieves higher performance on average than several RL-based search baselines. The gains transfer to six out-of-domain benchmarks, indicating robustness and generalization of the learned feedback-and-search policy. The framework also reduces answer-level hallucinations relative to other search agents. Ablation experiments where only the agent or only the critic is improved show early performance plateaus, whereas alternating updates to both components continues to yield performance improvements, evidencing the benefit of co-evolution of agent and feedback.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that effective self-improving search agents require feedback mechanisms that co-evolve with the policies they steer, rather than static or hand-designed feedback. By coupling the learning of when and how to request feedback with the learning of how to provide useful corrections from outcome-confounded rollouts, CAFE enables mid-trajectory interventions that improve search quality and reduce hallucinations. The joint evolution of agent and critic leads to better and more robust performance than improving either component in isolation, highlighting the importance of integrated agent–feedback training frameworks for complex reasoning and search tasks.

Abstract: Outcome-supervised search agents learn when and how to retrieve evidence, but terminal rewards neither localize intermediate errors nor redirect an ongoing trajectory before those errors compound. Treating corrective feedback as a learned in-trajectory intervention couples the two roles: the agent must decide when to request and use feedback, while the critic must infer useful corrections from outcome-confounded rollouts whose failure patterns shift as the agent improves. We introduce CAFE (Coupled Agent--Feedback Evolution), a framework in which a shared-parameter model alternates between search-agent and critic roles. CAFE initializes feedback-conditioned recovery from trajectories built around the base agent's own failures, then couples online and offline optimization. During online RL, a comparative feedback estimate uses a prompt-level call--skip success gap to shape request returns, while feedback-aware advantage shaping reweights token advantages before and after feedback. Offline, rollout-derived preference optimization learns feedback from matched successful and unsuccessful trajectories. On seven agentic search benchmarks, CAFE outperforms the evaluated RL-based search agents on average, retains its gains across all six out-of-domain benchmarks, and reduces answer-level hallucinations. One-sided ablations show that improving only the agent or only the critic eventually plateaus, whereas alternating the two updates continues to improve performance. These findings suggest that a self-improving search agent needs feedback that co-evolves with the policy it guides.

</details>


### [165] [StarHarness: Evolving Harnesses with Stratified Search for Enterprise Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24804)
*Esakkivel Esakkiraja,Denis Akhiyarov,Vikas Yadav,Sai Rajeswar,Patrice Bechard,Sridhar Nemala,Sagar Davasam*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: StarHarness is a framework that evolves the *harness* around a fixed LLM—prompts, tools, and agent structure—to better fit specific enterprise environments, leading to large, transferable performance gains on realistic ops/IT/finance benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs often underperform on real, tool-heavy enterprise tasks not because of poor model weights but because of mismatches between the environment and the agent harness (prompts, tools, and workflows). Manually designing and tuning these harnesses is complex, brittle, and environment-specific. The paper is motivated by the need for a systematic, scalable way to automatically adapt agent setups to diverse operational environments while keeping model weights fixed, improving reliability and reducing integration overhead.

Method: The authors introduce StarHarness, a framework that *evolves* an agent harness through iterative changes rather than retraining the base model. The harness includes prompt/task framing, tool interfaces, skill definitions, MCP-backed providers, subagent structure, and agent-loop configuration. They: (1) Build a compact evolution pool by stratifying tasks based on how a baseline harness fails; (2) Separate tasks into proposer-visible search tasks and proposer-hidden selection tasks to avoid overfitting; (3) Hold out a subset of tasks to evaluate generalization. Proposed harness modifications are evaluated on the evolution pool; only beneficial changes are accepted, gradually improving the harness for each environment.

Result: On three benchmarks—ITBench SRE, EnterpriseOps-Gym ITSM, and AutomationBench Finance—evolving the harness leads to 20–35 percentage point improvements in full-benchmark performance over a default harness, after only 4–12 accepted harness changes per environment. The improvements persist on tasks not used in evolution and transfer across different model families (GPT and Qwen) without re-running the evolution. Trace analysis shows that the evolved harnesses fix interface mismatches, better encode environment conventions, and incorporate operational knowledge that reduces search, leading to fewer false positives and shorter solution trajectories.

Conclusion: StarHarness demonstrates that systematically evolving the agent harness around a fixed LLM can substantially reduce model-environment mismatch in complex, tool-rich enterprise tasks. Large performance gains can be achieved without changing model weights, and the improvements generalize to unseen tasks and different model families. This suggests that harness evolution is a practical, reusable approach for deploying LLM agents reliably in real-world operational environments.

Abstract: We present StarHarness, a framework for evolving environment-specific agent harnesses while keeping model weights fixed. The evolved harness can include prompt and task framing, tool interfaces, skills, MCP-backed providers, subagent structure, and agent-loop configuration. StarHarness constructs a compact evolution pool by stratifying tasks according to baseline failure behavior, separates proposer-visible search tasks from proposer-hidden selection tasks, and reserves held-out tasks for evaluating generalization. Across ITBench SRE, EnterpriseOps-Gym ITSM, and AutomationBench Finance, harness evolution improves full-benchmark performance by 20-35 percentage points over the default harness after 4-12 accepted changes per environment. These gains persist on tasks excluded from evolution and transfer without re-evolution across GPT and Qwen model families. Trace analysis links the improvements to interface repairs, environment conventions, and operational knowledge that compresses search, with fewer false-positive diagnoses and shorter trajectories in several settings. StarHarness therefore offers a practical way to reduce persistent model-environment mismatch in tool-rich enterprise tasks.

</details>


### [166] [Strictly Causal Streaming Video Anomaly Detection with a Theoretically-Grounded State-Space Core](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24810)
*Yogesh Kumar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a causal, streaming Mamba-style state space model for video anomaly detection with constant-time updates and analyzes how its temporal memory affects detection delay and minimum detectable anomaly length, while also reporting real edge-device latency; accuracy lags non-causal baselines, and future work targets closing this gap and scaling benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Mamba-style SSM methods for video anomaly detection still buffer clips/windows, lack theory linking temporal memory to detection latency, and report efficiency mainly via GPU throughput instead of real edge devices. There is a need for a purely streaming, causal method with a principled understanding of time scales and true on-device performance.

Method: They design a strictly causal streaming anomaly detector whose state is a diagonal linear state space recurrence with an input- and state-dependent decay gate. The model updates a fixed-size state in O(1) per frame with no lookahead or clip buffering. It is trained self-supervised by causal next-embedding prediction on top of a frozen visual backbone. They analytically derive a relationship between the recurrence decay spectrum and both detection delay and minimum anomaly duration, and then empirically validate and ablate decay rate, state size, and gating on UCSD Ped2 and CUHK Avenue.

Result: Theoretical analysis yields a closed-form link between SSM decay parameters, detection delay, and minimum anomaly length. Empirically on UCSD Ped2 and CUHK Avenue, the predicted settling delay from base decay (57–59 frames) is much larger than observed detection delay (1.6 and 18.4 frames), indicating the learned gate mainly controls responsiveness. On Apple M3 Pro they measure ~0.74–0.77 ms per frame (>1300 FPS). In an untuned setup, frame-level AUC is 67.9% on Ped2 and 70.2% on Avenue, worse than non-causal SSM baselines but with strong efficiency.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates that a purely streaming, causal Mamba-style SSM can achieve extremely low-latency video anomaly detection on real edge hardware and offers a theoretical understanding of its temporal behavior. However, its anomaly detection accuracy currently trails non-causal baselines, with gating effects depending on dataset size. Improving accuracy and validating on larger benchmarks are identified as next steps.

Abstract: Recent work has applied Mamba style state space models (SSMs) to video anomaly detection, yet existing approaches still rely on buffering clips or windows internally, lack a theoretical account of how temporal memory relates to detection latency, and benchmark efficiency only through GPU throughput rather than the edge hardware these methods are intended to target. We introduce a strictly causal streaming anomaly detector whose fixed size state is updated in O(1) time and memory per incoming frame, with no lookahead and no clip buffering. Its temporal core is a diagonal linear state space recurrence with an input and state dependent decay gate, trained self supervised through causal next embedding prediction on a frozen visual backbone. We derive a closed form relationship between the recurrence decay spectrum and both detection delay and the shortest anomaly it can reliably capture, then validate empirically on UCSD Ped2 and CUHK Avenue. The settling delay bound predicted from the learned base decay (57 to 59 frames) sits far above the measured detection delay (1.6 and 18.4 frames), showing that the event boundary gate, not the base decay, governs responsiveness. We further report end to end latency and throughput measured directly on Apple M3 Pro hardware, 0.74 ms and 0.77 ms per frame (over 1300 FPS), rather than simulated GPU numbers. With an untuned initial configuration the method reaches 67.9 percent and 70.2 percent frame level AUC on Ped2 and Avenue, trailing prior non causal SSM baselines in accuracy. Ablations over decay rate, state size, and gating reveal that the gate contribution is dataset size dependent, hurting accuracy on the smaller Ped2 training set but helping on the larger Avenue one. Closing this accuracy gap and extending evaluation to a third, larger benchmark are immediate next steps.

</details>


### [167] [Constrained Entity Selection under Partial Knowledge for LLM-Based Knowledge Graph QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24824)
*Emanuel Kitzelmann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a way to verify LLM-generated answers to knowledge graph questions using symbolic constraints, improving precision without hurting recall, even when the knowledge graph is incomplete.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based KG question answering often fails to correctly ground answers in the graph. Full semantic parsing to queries like SPARQL is brittle with complex or incomplete real-world knowledge graphs, while pure LLLM reasoning lacks formal guarantees and symbolic evidence. There is a need for a middle-ground approach that can check and support answers without requiring full logical forms.

Method: They define a new problem, Constrained Entity Selection under Partial Knowledge (CES-PK), where an LLM first generates candidate answers from a KG, and then lightweight symbolic constraints (types, relations, exclusions) are derived from the question and used to filter and re-rank candidates. They use a three-valued semantics (satisfied / violated / unknown) to evaluate constraints over incomplete KGs, avoiding wrongful rejection due to missing facts. The framework is instantiated on the Hetionet biomedical KG.

Result: On the Hetionet biomedical KG, applying type, relation, and exclusion constraints on LLM-generated candidate answers increases precision by removing candidates that violate constraints while preserving recall by keeping candidates whose constraints are unknown (not provably violated). Satisfied constraints provide positive evidence for ranking among remaining candidates.

Conclusion: Lightweight symbolic verification of LLM-generated KGQA answers—formalized as CES-PK with three-valued constraint semantics—can effectively improve answer quality in real-world, incomplete KGs. The approach avoids the brittleness of full semantic parsing while adding formal guarantees and symbolic support that are missing from unconstrained LLM reasoning.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used for knowledge graph question answering (KGQA), but can fail to correctly ground answers in the underlying graph. Current approaches to LLM-based KGQA either rely on full semantic parsing into executable queries such as SPARQL, which is brittle in practice due to complex schemas or incompleteness of real-world KGs, or on LLM-reasoning and answer generation over KGs, which can be more robust but lacks formal guarantees. In this work, we study a complementary setting in which \emph{candidate} answers are generated by an LLM-based system and subsequently verified using lightweight symbolic constraints derived from the question. We introduce \emph{Constrained Entity Selection under Partial Knowledge (CES-PK)}, a problem formulation that focuses on eliminating invalid answers and providing symbolic support for valid ones without requiring construction of executable logical forms. To account for incomplete KGs, we employ a three-valued constraint semantics (\emph{satisfied, violated, unknown}) that avoids incorrect rejections under open-world assumptions. To demonstrate the effects of our method, we instantiate this framework over the Hetionet biomedical knowledge graph and evaluate the impact of type, relation, and exclusion constraints. Experiments show that precision improves by filtering invalid candidates, while recall is preserved due to retaining candidates whose constraints are not explicitly violated. Satisfied constraints provide additional positive symbolic evidence to rank remaining candidates.

</details>


### [168] [A Dual-Dimensional LLM Framework for Automated Item Incidental Content Similarity Analysis in Large-Scale Assessments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24825)
*Jing Huang,Jihong Zhang,Hua-Hua Chang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose and validate an LLM-based system to measure similarity between test items more accurately than traditional text metrics, and show it improves computerized adaptive testing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large-scale assessments and automatic item generation create many test questions that may be unintentionally redundant in wording or context, causing construct-irrelevant local dependence and psychometric problems. Existing similarity metrics (BLEU, cosine) poorly capture nuanced semantic and structural redundancy, so better automated tools are needed to detect and manage item similarity for bank curation and adaptive testing.

Method: They design an Automated Item Similarity Analysis (AISA) framework using large language models with two dimensions: (1) Structured Decomposition, capturing structural/format features of items, and (2) Semantic Relatedness, capturing deeper meaning relationships. They compute LLM-based similarity metrics, then conduct psychometric validation by comparing these metrics to indicators of construct-irrelevant local dependence and item parameter groupings. They also embed the similarity constraints into a Computerized Adaptive Testing (CAT) item selection algorithm and run simulations to compare performance with CAT using traditional similarity metrics.

Result: LLM-derived similarity measures show stronger alignment with psychometric indicators of construct-irrelevant local dependence and produce more coherent clusters of item parameters than conventional text-based metrics. In CAT simulations, adding LLM-based similarity constraints to item selection improves ability-estimate stability and reduces bias, while maintaining efficiency and outperforming constraints based on BLEU/cosine-like measures.

Conclusion: The proposed LLM-powered AISA framework provides a more psychometrically meaningful way to quantify item similarity than traditional methods. When used in CAT, it improves measurement quality with minimal loss of efficiency. This suggests LLM-based similarity can support scalable item bank management, more content-aware and locally independent test assembly, and better test-taker experiences across multiple assessment contexts.

Abstract: The rapid expansion of large-scale assessments and the growing adoption of automatic item generation have intensified concerns about incidental content redundancy, where construct-irrelevant elements such as wording or contextual framing become unintentionally repetitive across items. Traditional similarity metrics like BLEU or cosine similarity, often fail to capture the nuanced structural and semantic layers that drive perceived redundancy simultaneously. This study proposes a dual-dimensional framework for Automated Item Similarity Analysis (AISA) powered by Large Language Models (LLMs), operationalizing similarity through Structured Decomposition and Semantic Relatedness. Psychometric validation indicates that LLM-derived metrics align more closely with indicators of construct-irrelevant local dependence and yield more coherent item parameter groupings than traditional text-based measures. The framework is further evaluated through its application in Computerized Adaptive Testing (CAT). Simulations reveal that incorporating LLM-based similarity constraints into item selection improves estimation stability and reduces bias with minimal efficiency trade-offs, outperforming constraints based on conventional metrics. These findings highlight the potential of LLM-powered AISA to support scalable bank curation, content-aware test assembly, and experience-sensitive adaptive testing across diverse assessment contexts.

</details>


### [169] [FedV-KGQA: Multi-Hop Question Answering over Vertically Partitioned Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24846)
*Md Saikat Islam Khan Bappy,Oshani Seneviratne*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces FedV-KGQA, a framework for answering multi-hop questions over vertically partitioned knowledge graphs without centralizing data or sharing raw triples/parameters across organizations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In many real-world scenarios, knowledge relevant to question answering is spread across multiple organizations that cannot fully share data due to governance and data sovereignty constraints. Existing centralized KGQA systems assume access to a unified knowledge graph and therefore fail when facts needed for multi-hop reasoning are split across vertically partitioned silos owned by different parties. There is a need for a method that can perform multi-hop reasoning and achieve near-centralized performance while respecting strict data boundaries and avoiding inter-organizational sharing of sensitive graph structure or model parameters.

Method: The authors propose FedV-KGQA, a federated framework for knowledge graph question answering over vertically partitioned graphs where organizations share entities but each owns a disjoint subset of relations. The method combines (1) local graph enrichment at each silo to expand its partial view of the graph, and (2) knowledge graph embeddings that are trained and used locally so that raw triples and relation parameters never leave the silo. This sets a structural data boundary while enabling multi-hop reasoning across the distributed data. Additionally, a topic entity anchoring mechanism is introduced to correctly ground questions into the appropriate local graph neighborhood, enabling reasoning without any runtime inter-silo communication.

Result: The framework is evaluated with 12 different model configurations on three benchmark datasets. Experiments show that FedV-KGQA achieves strong performance that remains close to that of a fully centralized system, successfully generalizes to 3-hop reasoning tasks, and shows robustness when the embeddings are perturbed, indicating stability in real-world settings where embeddings may be noisy or imperfect.

Conclusion: FedV-KGQA enables effective multi-hop question answering over vertically partitioned knowledge graphs while preserving organizational data sovereignty. By combining local graph enrichment, local KG embeddings, and topic entity anchoring, it avoids sharing raw data or relation parameters and removes the need for runtime inter-silo communication, yet still attains performance comparable to centralized approaches and scales to more complex multi-hop reasoning tasks.

Abstract: Real-world data for knowledge graph question answering is often distributed across different organizations due to governance and data sovereignty constraints. While centralized systems exist, they cannot answer multi-hop questions when the required facts are split across vertically partitioned silos. In this paper, we propose FedV-KGQA, a framework for multi-hop reasoning over knowledge graphs in which organizations share entities but own disjoint sets of relations. Our approach combines local graph enrichment and knowledge graph embeddings to ensure raw triples and relation parameters never leave each silo, establishing a structural data boundary without requiring centralized graph access. We further introduce a topic entity anchoring mechanism that grounds questions in the correct graph neighborhood without any runtime inter-silo communication. We evaluate 12 model configurations across three benchmarks and show that FedV-KGQA performs strongly, remains close to centralized performance, generalizes to 3-hop reasoning, and is robust to embedding perturbations.

</details>


### [170] [SPO++: Stream-Aligned Policy Optimization for Asynchronous Agentic RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24870)
*Kai Ruan,Jinghao Lin,Qianshan Wei,Ziqi Zhou,Zihe Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes SPO++, an improved reinforcement learning method for language models that optimizes policy updates at the token level without waiting for sibling rollouts, leading to better online learning efficiency than prior SPO.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing group-relative reinforcement learning methods require multiple sibling rollouts of the same prompt to compute relative advantages, which is inefficient for long, variable tool-use trajectories. Single-stream Policy Optimization (SPO) removes the need for sibling rollouts but has a mismatch between how advantages are centered and how the actor loss is computed, potentially weakening learning efficiency.

Method: The authors analyze SPO’s advantage normalization procedure and show that whitening a single trajectory-level advantage does not correctly center the token-weighted quantity actually used by the token-mean actor loss. They propose SPO++, which standardizes terminal-outcome advantages under the action-token measure (i.e., normalizing with respect to the distribution over action tokens rather than trajectories) and reorganizes how prompt evidence is batched, grouping it by the policy event that generated the data instead of by when the learner receives it. They then evaluate SPO++ on ALFWorld at two different model scales and on the Math-TIR benchmark, including ablations to isolate contributions of different components.

Result: On ALFWorld (two model sizes) and Math-TIR, SPO++ yields higher online learning efficiency than the original SPO, meaning faster or better performance for the same interaction budget. Ablation studies show that the main performance gains come from the proposed action-token-measure normalization, while other changes are less impactful.

Conclusion: Fixing the mismatch between trajectory-level whitening and token-level actor optimization in SPO by normalizing advantages under the action-token measure substantially improves online reinforcement learning for tool-use and reasoning tasks. Organizing data by policy event further helps, resulting in SPO++, a more sample-efficient variant of SPO that better leverages long, variable trajectories.

Abstract: Group-relative reinforcement learning waits for sibling rollouts of the same prompt, which is costly for long and variable tool-use trajectories. Single-stream Policy Optimization (SPO) removes this dependency with a persistent prompt-level value estimate, but its recipe whitens one advantage per trajectory before optimizing a token-mean actor loss. We show that trajectory centering generally does not center the token-weighted quantity consumed by the actor, and fix the mismatch by standardizing terminal-outcome advantages under the action-token measure. We additionally organize prompt evidence by the policy event that generated it rather than learner receipt order. Across matched runs on ALFWorld at two model scales and on Math-TIR, SPO++ improves online learning efficiency over SPO. A paired ablation identifies action-token-measure normalization as the strongest tested component.

</details>
